This is the same logic as 6:43, "so when they forgot what they had been reminded of, We opened to them the gates of all things, until — when they rejoiced in what they had been given — We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair," and as 61:5, "so when they deviated, God deviated their hearts," with 9:115's own rule for the sequence: "God does not misguide a people after He has guided them, until He makes clear to them what they should guard against" — the sealing always follows a choice made manifest, the principle al-Ṭabarsī draws from these verses. The verse thus refuses both self-reliance ("my faith is my achievement") and fatalism ("my crime is God's design"): the Reminder is sent (v. 9), hearts receive it, and what they become in the receiving — believe or mock — is real, and God's threading of mockery is the *second* act that crowns the first, as a judge's sentence crowns the crime the convict chose.

**The Social Science Inside the Verse**

Read beyond the individual, *naslukuhu fī qulūb* describes a mechanism the modern world recognizes under other names: ideas threaded through a social body until they are "in" it, invisible, felt as common sense. Mockery of conscience, cynicism toward reform, the reflex that treats sincerity as comedy — the Qur'an says these are not merely absorbed but *installed* by divine threading as the discipline of a society that rejected its reminders; hence the sūrah's own calm in vv. 88, 97: the Prophet ﷺ is told not to agonize, because the laughter in the hearts is a placement, not a verdict on his mission — and placements, unlike verdicts, expire with the Day of the Known Term (vv. 4–5). The same analysis makes the verse a warning to every era's "mujrimūn" who think ridicule their invention: the mechanism that made Pharaoh's court laugh (54:9, "this is a possessed man — repel him with a word"; 43:47-54, the "show me" sneers at Mūsā; 11:38, the chiefs mocking Nūḥ's ship-building — "What! do you build the ark when we pass by you?" — and drowning in the mockery's own flood) is still running.

**Application: What Threads Into Your Heart Today?**

Practically, the verse teaches an asceticism of reception: the heart is a needle's eye, and what passes through it once lodges along the channel it wore. Every repeated mockery — of prayer, of modesty, of the Qur'an, of the preacher in the marketplace (25:20-21's answer to the objection "why was not an angel sent down on him?" — "You only impose upon a people; you are but a warner") — is an act of threading; and the sūrah's theology turns that into both awe and responsibility: awe, because God is the final threader of what a heart holds; responsibility, because He threads only where the channel has already been cut by love of the thing. The next verse, "they believe not in it," closes the unit with the diagnosis: the threaded thing and the refusing heart are one case, judged by the ancient *sunna* (v. 13) — which is why this verse has been classically recited not to despair of guidance but to fear hardening: guidance, says Ibn Rajāʾ later in the same channel, is God's threading of *tawfīq* into the heart that humbly asked — and this sūrah's own list of the saved (Lot's family, "all of them," v. 59; Abraham's certainty, v. 56; "My purified servants," v. 40) is the mirror-image proof that hearts, threaded either way, are still God's, still malleable in His hands until the Day that closes the eye of the needle — "repentance is not for those who go on doing evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, 'now I repent'" (4:18). The verse is thus both an explanation and an alarm: an explanation of the mocker, an alarm for the one who merely enjoys the joke.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:13

> **They believe not in it, though the wont of those of old has already passed away.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Clauses, One Verdict**

*Lā yuʾminūna bihi wa-qad māḍat sunnatu'l-awwalīn* — "They believe not in it, and the way of the former ones has already passed on." The conjunction *wa-qad* between a present refusal and a finished precedent is the whole meaning of the verse: their disbelief today is not an open event; it is a repeat of a closed case whose sentence is on file. Al-Ṭabarī explains the *sunna* here precisely as "the way God took with the nations of old when they denied His messengers — destruction," and he adds the point that gives the clause its terror: *qad māḍat* — it passed — meaning the precedent is established, not revoked, and the mockers are not starting a new negotiation but finishing an old one. The present-tense verb *lā yuʾminūna* ("they [go on] not believing," the imperfect of habit) paints an ongoing refusal; the perfect *māḍat* paints a done deal; the clash of the tenses is the sermon: you are refusing repeatedly a Book, into a history already closed on the question.

**"It" — The Reminder They Hear and Deny**

The source commentary (Bḍ, R, Ṭb, Ṭs) resolves the pronoun *bihi* to the Qur'an — tying the verse back to v. 9's Reminder and to v. 12's threading — so the picture is coherent: the Reminder has been sent down, guarded, and even made to pass into the guilty hearts (they have heard it, recited it, memorized its bits, quoted it in court, as v. 90-91's *taqṣīm* will show: they divided it into "soothsaying," "poetry," "ancients' fables"); yet *lā yuʾminūn*, they settle into non-belief. Al-Rāzī adds the nuance that the negation is comprehensive because the denial is volitional: their "not believing" is the *continuation* of a refusal already enacted, like the People of the Book who "knew him as they knew their own sons, yet a party of them concealed the truth knowingly" (2:146). The Qur'an elsewhere describes exactly this condition — hearing without believing as a *completed character*: 2:7, "God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and upon their sight is a covering; and for them is a great punishment"; 22:53, "so that those in whose hearts is disease may say, 'these — their religion has deluded them'" — yet the same book also keeps a window open: "they believe not" is a report on their *present*, and the sūrah itself (v. 96) will address the *future*, "soon they will know." The verse therefore states facts, not the foreclosing decree — a distinction the commentators of vv. 4-5 prepared us for: the *ajal*, not this verse, is the lock.

**The Sunna of the Former Ones: What Actually "Passed"**

The phrase recurs in the Qur'an as a technical term — *sunnat Allāh fī man arsalnā qablaika min rusulina* is 17:77's expansion: "the Sunna [of God] regarding those of Our messengers We sent before thee, and thou wilt find no changing [against] the Sunna" — and 40:85: "So their belief was not to profit them when they saw Our might — the Sunna of God that has gone by among His servants." To these the source cites 8:38c and 35:43c: 35:43, "and thou wilt find no changing for God's Sunna; and thou wilt find no turning for God's Sunna." Two attributes of the *sunna* are affirmed in those verses: *immutability* (no substitution — God does not change the rule when a new lobby appears) and *impersonality* (it is not a grudge; it is physics). That is why v. 13 places it after v. 12: mockery threaded (v. 12), refusal fixed (v. 13a), and the mechanism running on rails (v. 13b). Ibn Kathīr's summary: "the punishment descended upon the deniers of former nations; this Qur'an is the same proof as was given them, so God's Sunna has already passed: what happened to those who received a book and denied it is the measure for what comes to those who receive this one and divide it (cf. vv. 90-91)."

**The Historical Exhibit of the Verse**

Al-Ḥijr itself is the exhibit hall for this *sunna*. The sūrah will narrate, in order, the four cases the verse compresses: Iblīs, the first denier, granted a respite till a dated end (vv. 36-38) — the "passing" deferred by request and fixed to "the Day of the Time Known" (v. 38) so that the precedent would be *demonstrated*, not merely asserted; Lot's city, warned, laughed at, upturned at sunrise (vv. 67-73) — "so We made its highest its lowest" (v. 74); the Companions of the Wood (vv. 78-79), the commercial sinners given the same short arc; Thamūd, who demanded and received the she-camel, then hamstrung it, then were seized "in the morning" (vv. 80-83). Each story ends with the moral formula the sūrah uses as a stamp: "Truly in that are signs for those who discern," "that which they used to earn availed them not" (v. 84; cf. 3:10 and 58:17: "their wealth and their children will not avail them against God in the least"). One may add that the sūrah's present audience, Makkah, sits inside v. 13 as a *fifth* case, with the same four beats available on its clock: warning, mockery, respite, *ṣabāḥ*. The known decree of v. 4 was the date; this verse is the law that fills the date with the right content: only those communities get the *sunna* that got the Reminder and denied it (v. 9's guarantee means the denial is never excuse-less).

**The Verse Applied: The Danger of "Nothing Happened"**

The psychological lesson of *wa-qad māḍat* is that un-happened punishment is not un-decided punishment, and a society's most dangerous reading of its own security is "we mocked, nothing fell" — a reading every fallen city also maintained until its *ṣabāḥ* (v. 83). The prophetic tradition supplies the metric for the believer's private case: "When you see God giving the servant from the world what he loves while he persists in disobedience, that is istidrāj — gradual enticement," then the Prophet recited 6:44 (Aḥmad, ḥasan; al-Bukhārī has the shorter form, "God gives the wrongdoer respite until, when He takes him, He does not let him go," Bukhārī 4685). Conversely, the verse's comfort side: the *sunna* works for the small faithful, too — "We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger…" (2:155), and in this sūrah's own terms, the mockery is threaded into the criminals but the believers are *left* on the "straight path to Me" (vv. 41-42), for which the *sunna* is the *salvation of the family of Lot* — "all of them" (v. 59), the saved exception that proves the rule of the punishing precedent — and the "beautiful forbearance" of v. 85. So verse 13, which looks like an epitaph, is read correctly as a summons: before the *sunna* completes its sentence, *ūmanū bihi* — believe in it; the Book is still here, the *dhikr* still guaranteed (v. 9), the eyes still in the head, and the morning of the punished is not yet your morning. The window is the whole sermon.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:14

> **Were We to open for them a gate unto Heaven, that they might continue to ascend through it,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Thought Experiment That Cuts Off the Last Excuse**

*Wa-law fattahnā ʿalayhim bāban min al-samāʾ fahum fīhi yaʿrujūn* — "And if We were to open for them a gate from the heaven, so that they were going up through it, ascending…" The construction is the pure *law* of the unreal condition: *if* — and it is not so, will not be so — the verse builds an impossible elevator and lets the mockers stand in it. The sūrah, remember, is answering v. 7's demand ("Why do you not bring us the angels?"), and this is the sharpest possible refutation: it grants the request in fantasy, in full detail — not a rumor, not a vision, not a single angel, but an *open gate* in the sky, a visible *door*, and continuous ascent (*yaʿrujūn*, the imperfect of sustained climbing), the kind of public, repeatable, science-friendly miracle every skeptic claims is his price. And the next verse reports their answer to *that*: they will deny the elevator. The logic is ironclad and completes the argument of vv. 8-13: if a sign as big as a stairway to heaven would not produce faith, then the absence of signs is not the cause of unfaith, and v. 13's verdict ("they believe not in it") is confirmed as a condition of the heart, not a shortage of evidence. Ibn Kathīr records the transmission from Ibn ʿAbbās and the others on the purpose of such hypotheticals — *li-yuʿarrafa hum* — to expose them: God sometimes describes the mockers' future refusal of a granted sign to prove, before He ever grants, that the granting was never the issue (cf. 32:13, "had We willed, We could have given every soul its guidance," and 6:35, "but God guides whom He wills").

**Al-Bāb: The Architecture of the Image**

A *bāb* in the sky — the choice is not ornamental. Gates in the Qur'an are always functional thresholds of descent or ascent: "the gates of heaven" open with rain (54:12, "We opened the gates of the heaven with water pouring down" — the flood), the angels ascend and descend through them (32:5, "He manages the affair from heaven to earth; then it ascends to Him"), and the gate of judgment closes like a door ("the Gate of the Hour," 42:17 — *fa-lā yumkin* — no). Thamūd were offered their own miracle through rock — the she-camel *emerging* (v. 81c; 7:73) — like an elevator shaft in reverse: a creature exiting a stone door. The sūrah's point with v. 14's image: give these men even the sky's own gate and they will re-describe it (v. 15, "our eyes are dazed"). A gate is only a gate to those who intend to walk through; for the hostile it is "a trick of light." The grammatical note of al-Zamakhsharī sharpens the scene: *fahum fīhi yaʿrujūn* — "they *were* climbing in it" — a second protasis layered on the first, as if the verse grants even the *ongoing* climb, day after day of visible, testable ascent, not a one-time miracle photo; every modern verification the mockers could devise is already inside the hypothesis, and their denial is already inside the answer.

**The Other Half: 44:14's Twin Verse**

The near-identical passage in al-Dukhān (44:12-14) is the source's companion: Moses came "clear" and they doubted; "And if We had opened for them a gate of the heaven so that they ascend therein, they would only say, 'Only our eyes are dazed — nay, we are a people bewitched'" — the *ḥikāya*, the narrated hypothetical, repeats in a Makkan sūrah about a *previous* nation (Pharaoh's) the same refusal of the same imagined proof, which is what v. 10-11's "universal mockery" doctrine needed: the hypothetical gate is a laboratory test run across nations, and the results are invariant. The sūrah thus does something subtle with v. 14: it *foretells the foretelling* — Quraysh already know what their answer to a sky-gate would be, because the book they call "sorcery" has already told them (Mūsa's case), and they will still mock. That is the exact mechanism of v. 12's threading: a people who have the archive of all failed demands can manufacture nothing new except the excuse.

**Why "They Would Say" — The Epistemology of the Hardened Heart**

The epistemic point the classical commentators press: even *ʿiyān* — naked eye-witness — does not create certainty for a soul that has chosen an outcome; it only relocates the denial from proposition to perception. Instead of "it is not true," they say "I did not see it truly" (*sakhranā bi-abṣārinā*, v. 15's "our eyes are dazed/sorcered") — an almost modern move: the mind defends its conclusions by editing the data. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's comment on these verses (in the source note) is that they demonstrate that denial here is *bi'l-ʿadawān wa'l-ḥasad*, from enmity and envy, not from evidence: when a person's refusal can absorb *any* possible sign, his refusal has never been about signs. The Qur'an says it plainly about other hearts, 7:40, "to them will not be opened the gates of heaven, nor will they enter Paradise until the camel passes through the eye of the needle" — the gate-vocabulary again, now for the inverted case: the gate that the mockers demand becomes, at the end, the gate closed on them, and their ascent (v. 14, *yaʿrujūn*) becomes the ascent "they will not be ascending" of 7:40. The hypothetical gate, read against that verse, is the actual gate of the next world: one opens for the believer's prayers (the ascent that *is* granted to the humble, without spectacle — "to Him ascend the good words, and the righteous deed He raises," 35:10), and the same gate for the proud is a sealed ceiling (7:40, 15:14-15).

**Modern Echoes of the Open Gate**

Contemporary readers can feel the force of the hypothetical without reaching for the sky: we have literally opened gates — telescopes that photograph the edges of visible time, stations continuously orbited in full public view, space-walks streamed like the ascent this verse imagines. Did faith arrive on schedule? What arrived instead is precisely the sūrah's forecast: a genre of "dazed-eyes" theories — state illusions, mass hypnosis, hoax-sickness — the *sakhranā* vocabulary now turned from prophecy into press-release. The verse is thus not a defensible-prediction stunt but a standing lesson in the order of the faculties: hearts are not convinced by eyes; eyes are read through hearts; and until the heart is soft, every gate opened becomes evidence that God does not answer demands — not because He cannot, but because, as v. 8 already said, the granted demand ends the reprieve, and this people, this Prophet's people, are in the years of the Reminder, not of the spectacle (57:16, "Has the time not come for those who believe that their hearts should be humbled at the remembrance of God and what has come down of the truth?"). The "open gate" of this sūrah is the Reminder itself; v. 15 tells us what they will call it, and the rest of the sūrah — the gardens, the constellations, the stories, "the seven oft-repeated" (v. 87) — is the inventory of the gates they did not need to ascend, because they were already given the ascent: "the good word ascends to Him" (35:10), and a single prostration — v. 98's last command — climbs higher than the ladder of vv. 14-15 ever could.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:15

> **they would say, "Our eyes are merely spellbound. Nay, we are a people bewitched!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punchline: Anatomy of a Perfect Denial**

*Laqāla innamā sakharat abṣārunā bal ḥunā qawmun mashūr* — "They would say: our eyes are only dazed; nay, we are a people bewitched." Two sentences, and between them a *bal* — the Arabic particle of *iḍrāb*, self-correction — and that tiny pivot is the entire psychology of the verse. First they reach for the sensory excuse: our *eyes* are at fault (*sakhrat*, from *sukhra*, a mocking trick or delusion — "we were duped optically," al-Ṭabarī: *dhulilat abṣārunā*, our sight was led astray, as one is made to see a mirage). Then, as if the excuse is too fragile, they upgrade to the metaphysical one: no, this is not an eye-glitch, this is *siḥr* — someone has *bewitched* us. The sūrah compresses the whole denial-machine into one breath: first distrust of the senses, then distrust of the cause, and never — in either breath — the slightest willingness to check the content. For the mockers of vv. 6-7, a sky-gate would have to be wrong twice over (once in the retina, once in the cosmos) to remain safe. The verse is thus the empirical close of the argument begun at v. 7: the demand for a sign is structurally unfalsifiable by any sign.

**"Spellbound" and "Bewitched": The Words Quraysh Had Actually Chosen**

*Mashūr* is from *sh-w-r* — to *turn around*, to render (the mind) disordered — a word the tafsīr tradition glosses "turned inside out, deranged" (S, Ṭs: *muhayyad majnūn*), and the source notes Aj's point that the *siḥr* accusation was the Makkans' standing second charge alongside *jinna* (v. 6). Both terms recur across the prophetic file: to Mūsā — "Mūsā said, ‘Woe to you! Do not forge a lie against God lest He destroy you with a punishment; and the forger has indeed failed’" (20:61), sealed by the Qur'an's maxim for every miracle-contest, "the sorcerer will not succeed wherever he comes" (20:69); and Pharaoh's court: "this is indeed a master sorcerer," 7:109-110; 26:34-35, "this is nothing but the sorcery of a man," and 2:102, the *siḥr* slander in Israel's own file. Even Jesus, the most miracle-laden messenger in the book, meets the same rebuttal-mechanism — "when the son of Mary was cited as an example, thy people turned away from it" (43:57) — with 5:110's "I strengthened thee with the Holy Spirit" standing behind the very miracle the charge would deny. The Qur'an's list at the source — 10:2; 38:4 ("the unbelievers are in dissent and in far-off schism," 38:12 also "the people of Noah… ʿĀd… Pharaoh… the Companions of the Stone," all in the *siḥr*-cluster of 50:25, "they say, 'this is a lie, a sorcery'") — shows the charge is not an incident in this sūrah; it is *the* perennial counter-receipt for unsought evidence.

**Sukhrat: The Theology of Dazed Eyes**

The choice of *sakhrat* is quietly precise, because the *s-k-h-r* root is the Qur'an's word for *mockery* itself (v. 11's *yastahziʾūn*; the chiefs "passing by" the shipyard while Nūḥ built, 11:38). The mockers' excuse is thus worded — under inspiration's irony — in their own sin's vocabulary: "our eyes have been mocked." The one who deals in mockery ends up attributing mockery to the cosmos: not God opened the gate, but reality pulled a gag on my retina. Al-Rāzī's observation fits perfectly here (already cited on v. 14): a denial that can metabolize *any* witness is a denial of the faculty of witness, i.e., a heart problem presenting as an epistemology. The Qur'an generalizes it: "it is not the eyes that are blinded, but the hearts within the breasts" (22:46), and the horizons-signs verse — the end of evidence-accumulation is not belief but *recognition*, and the unsoftened heart converts even gates to jokes.

**Where the Gate Really Opens: Reading vv. 14–15 With the Whole Sūrah**

Structurally this verse-pair returns the sūrah to its own v. 8 and closes a circle. v. 8 had said: the angels do not come down except in truth, and then you get no respite; vv. 14–15 say: and if they did — if the door in the sky opened for ascent — it would be answered not even with "we believed when the angels came" but with "we are dazed," meaning: God never loses a sign to a mocker; He only stores it up. The verse thus explains the *inaction* of God in the face of the Makkans' demands (8:32, "O God, if this is the truth from Thee, rain upon us stones from heaven, or bring us a painful punishment") — an "answered" prayer in that exact form would have looked like a granted demand, and this sūrah says it would have looked, to *these* men, like a daze. That is why the sūrah turns, from v. 16 onward, to the signs He has already put where no *siḥr*-excuse survives: constellations (v. 16), the earth (v. 19), rain (v. 22), the clay and the breath (vv. 26-29), the saved families (vv. 45-60). vv. 14–15 are the *via negativa* that clears the ground: you cannot believe your way past a heart that can survive anything; you must first change the eye, not the sky. And the sūrah's own prescription, its ending, is exactly that surgery: praise, prostration, and worship until certainty (vv. 98–99) — the ascent not through a gate but through a forehead on the ground (cf. Muslim 479, "the nearest a servant is to his Lord is when he prostrates; so increase supplication therein"). The mockers of vv. 14–15 imagined the sky as the address of proof; this sūrah answers: proof walks the ground, the gate is already open at Mecca's dust — "lower thy wing to the believers" (v. 88).

**Application: The Inner Quraysh**

The verse's lasting use is diagnostic, not apologetic. Every reader should, at least once, run the *sakhrat* check against himself: when some truth begins to rise visibly in my life — a prayer I meant to quit, a debt I meant to pay, a habit the Book exposes — what is my first sentence? "The data is contaminated," "the scholars are biased," "that's emotional manipulation"? The structure of the excuse will rhyme with v. 15: first the daze, then the spell. The sūrah invites instead the opposite reflex: when something true is ascending before your eyes, do not audit your retina — audit your will. "Say: He is able to send punishment upon you… see how We turn about the signs, and they are a people who do not reason" (6:65) — and the open road of debate, "say: you are not questioned about what we commit, nor are we questioned about what you do" (34:24), remains open only until the file closes: the mockers were told, at the end of the sūrah's logic, that their excuse would not be accepted on the Day because the hypothetical gate, and the real Reminder, and the sunna of the ancients (v. 13), had all been filed; "they will say: we were not listening or understanding; if we had been, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze," 67:10 — the final transcript of v. 15's voice, fifty years later, in the fire itself, where the excuse at last runs out of adjectives.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:16

> **We have set constellations in the sky, and We have adorned them for the onlookers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Dazed Eyes to Open Skies: The Sūrah Changes the Court of Evidence**

The verses of the imagined heaven-gate (vv. 14–15) ended with eyes declared untrustworthy; this verse replies by pointing the same eyes at what they already trust — the night sky — and calling it, first, architecture (*jaʿalnā*, "We made/constituted") and second, ornament (*zayyannā*, "We adorned"). The pivot is the sūrah's method in one line: since mockers can disown a spectacle, God directs them to the spectacle no *siḥr*-excuse has ever removed: the sky that every traveler, farmer, sailor and child already reads. *Al-Burūj* is the verse's keyword and the source notes its dominant meaning (Kl): the twelve constellations of the zodiac — the great "towers" through which the sun and moon pass, the stations that divide the year (the same word is used in 4:78, "you are in strong towers and fortified," of citadels). The root *b-r-j* means to be high, broad, and conspicuous; *burj* is a tower, a castle bastion, any sign that cannot be missed. Al-Ṭabarī widens the referent to the stars' great groupings and to the planets themselves, while some reciters' glosses (Qatāda) took them as the fixed stations of the sun and moon for the counting of time — every sense shares one quality: *visible order*.

**"Adorned for the onlookers" — Aesthetics as Evidence**

*Wa zayyannāhā li'n-nāẓirīn* — the adornment is stated as purposeful and as *for observers*: the verse welds beauty to function in a single clause, and the sūrah's theology of nature lives in that weld. Two classical readings of the *li* (for) cover it: (a) the stars are *for* the viewers as an ornament is for its audience — the sky exists partly to be *enjoyed*, and delight in beauty is built into the cosmos as a mode of remembrance; cf. 41:12, "We adorned the nearest heaven with lamps," and 67:5, "We adorned the nearest heaven with lamps and made them missiles for the devils" — the lamp-missile double-duty that the next two verses will detail; and (b) *li-n-nāẓirīn* as those who *contemplate* (n-ẓ-r being reflection, not mere glazing): the adornment is a screen; only the reflector sees it as adornment rather than wallpaper. Ibn Kathīr brings in 37:6, "We have adorned the lowest heaven with an adornment, the stars," noting the stars are at once decoration, navigation, and defense — three uses, one sky, no spare parts. The same triple appears, beautifully, in a report the mufassirūn transmit from the Prophet ﷺ: "God created the stars for three things: as an adornment of the heaven of the world, as missiles for the devils, and as signs by which the directions are known" (a report cited by Ibn Kathīr on 67:5 from the earlier collections of ʿAbd al-Razzāq and Ibn Jarīr; its chain has been debated, but its content is pure sūrah: verse 16 = adornment, verses 17-18 = missiles, and navigation, "and by the stars they are [rightly] guided" (16:16); three verses answering one hadith — the sūrah's own triptych.)

**Against the Star-Worshippers: What "Burūj" Refutes**

The Makkan setting thickens the verse's argument. The same sky the Qur'an here hands to the "onlookers" was, for some of the Arabs, an audience to be feared or courted: the Ṣābiʾa of Ḥarrān and fringe Arabian sects read fate in the constellations; the sūrah's response is to *re-describe* the burūj twice over: (a) they are *made* — "We set them," so they are created, not ruling; and (b) they are *for* you — adorned *for the onlookers*, so they exist to serve the observer's wonder and wayfaring, not the other way round. This is the exact argument of the famous passage at 37:88-89, when Abraham "glanced a glance at the stars and said, 'I am sick'" — the stars dismissed as a screen behind which the Only One stands; and of 41:37, "Do not prostrate to the sun or to the moon, but prostrate to God Who created them." The sūrah will also, at vv. 17-18, give the sky a moral police-force (the stars as anti-demonic), i.e., the constellations are *guardians*, and guardians do not rule their charges. Ibn Taymiyyah, in *Darʾ taʿāruḍ al-ʿaql wa'l-naql*, leans on exactly such verses in his campaign against *ʿilm al-aḥkām*, the judicial astrology the philosophers had imported: the same stars that rule nothing in the sūrah are, by His mercy, *useful* at every level — the middle path between superstitious reading and cynical dismissal is the Qur'anic one: look up, admire, navigate, and let the Maker have the glory.

**The Verse as Daily Liturgy**

There is a practical theology of the night-sky in this sūrah that the Prophet ﷺ's own sunna operationalized. Every one of the five prayers is bound to a sky-event — the sun's decline, its shadow, sunset, twilight, dawn — as this sūrah's own closing pair will command worship at day's ends (cf. 11:114, "and in some hours of the night," 20:130, "glorify with the praise of thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting, and during hours of the night glorify Him, and at the flanks of the day, that thou mayest be pleased"), and there are authentic supplications at moonrise and at new moons (the new-moon duʿāʾ of the sunna: "O God, bring it upon us with safety, faith, security and Islam — my Lord and your Lord is God"), and the night-prayer of the stars' setting in the Quranic Abraham-narrative (37:88) which the *salaf* read as teaching that created lights are not lords. The verse's *nāẓirīn* are thus not only astronomers: every worshipper checking the horizon for maghrib is an "onlooker" the adornment was for. And for the modern city-dweller whose sky is erased by light-pollution, the verse quietly legislates a season: travel where the burūj return, and let the built-in evidence re-imprint the heart — the sūrah's own cure for the "dazed eyes" of vv. 14-15 is precisely this: not a new gate, but the old sky, seen as adornment, which is the same sky seen with the heart re-opened. "Adorned for the onlookers" — the universe is dressed for an audience; to be an audience is the first office of man.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:17

> **And We have preserved them from every satan outcast,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sky Has Walls: The Guarded Heavens**

*Wa ḥafiznāhā min kulli shayṭānin marīd* — "And We guarded them — every one of those towers — from every outcast devil." The verse follows the adornment clause with a security clause, and the sequence is a lesson in how revelation travels: before the sūrah reasserts its own claim (the Reminder sent down and preserved, v. 9), it shows that the *channel* through which revelation comes down was never open. *Ḥafiẓa* (from *ḥ-f-ẓ*, guarding — the very root of v. 9's *ḥāfiẓūn* and 5:67's "God will protect thee from the people") is used here for a *place*: the heaven is a guarded city, with the burūj its towers; and the guarded object is ambiguous at the level of grammar — "them" can be the constellations (so: the stars are kept from every rebellious devil, who cannot seize or pollute them) or the heaven as the domain of descent (the majority reading, per the source's cross-referenced 37:7-8: "We made it a guard against every accursed devil; and they cannot approach the highest assembly, for they are pelted from every side"). Either way, the theology: the sky through which the Reminder came down is a corridor under armed watch.

**Marīd: The Devil Whose Name Describes His Case**

Al-Ṭabarī records the exegetes' split on *marīd*: (a) from *m-r-d*, rebellion — "every deviant, refractory devil," Qatāda's gloss *ʿarīd* "remote," drawing on *marada* as a tree far gone in dryness, and (b) a *mārid*, one cast out — the passive face of Iblīs's own plea in v. 39, "since Thou hast caused me to err," rebellion and expulsion being two sides of one clause. Both readings fit the context like the two flaps of a door: the devil is *in himself* crooked (rebellion is his nature, cf. 17:27, "the wasteful are brethren of the devils," *m-b-dh-r* squanderers), and he is *in his status* expelled (cf. this sūrah's own sentence, v. 35: "surely the curse shall be upon thee till the Day of Judgment"). The phrase recurs with rajīm and *marīd* nearly interchangeably — 7:200, "if an evil-prompting from Satan prompts thee, seek refuge in God," and 41:36, "an evil-prompting from Satan," — and in the *istiʿādha* the Prophet ﷺ taught (the *taʿawwudh* taught at the start of the prayer — "I seek refuge in God the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing, from the accursed Satan, his evil-prompting, his blown-breath and his knotted-braid" — transmitted by Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī and graded ḥasan by several imams) the same triad of the outcast's arsenal — *nafkh* (inspiration, as in 114:5, "who whispers in the breasts of men"), *ʿaqd* (the knot of the sorcerer, 113:4), and *khinzab*. The sūrah's *marīd*, in that light, is not folklore: it names the operational enemy of the channel.

**Why Revelation's Credibility Depends on This Verse**

Here the sūrah's argumentative architecture is at its most subtle. vv. 6–9 defended the Reminder: *sent* by God and *guarded* by God; vv. 16–18 defend the *route*: the Reminder descends from the highest assembly (and the Prophet ﷺ's night-ascent was that route lived, not described) through skies where eavesdroppers are hunted. The pagan objection that vv. 6-7 voiced — perhaps your information is jinn-intercepted, mixed with stolen, corrupted sky-news, the product of the soothsayer's trade — is cut off not by a denial but by a description of security protocols: the heavens are guarded so that what descends descends clean, and what ascends (the *malāʾikah* carrying the Reminder) ascends with escorts. The very structure of 72:1-9, the jinn's own testimony — "I heard a wondrous Recitation; We found the heaven filled with mighty angels and lamps; we used to sit in it for eavesdropping seats but now whoever listens finds a watching flame" — is the verse's twin; and 26:210-212 removes the last doubt from the human side: "the devils did not bring it down, nor is it fitting for them, nor can they; they are from the hearing banished" — *mahjūrūn*, the very security clause of our verse. The logic of faith in this sūrah is thus complete: an honest God (v. 8, *bi'l-ḥaqq*), a preserved text (v. 9), a monitored channel (v. 17), and a hostile-listener quarantine (v. 18): nothing in the system is left to chance except the human yes or no.

**Cosmic Security in the Two Other Heavens of the Tradition**

The idea of the guarded heaven was so fixed in the earliest Muslims that their historians and hadith-masters narrate the whole career of the eavesdropper as public knowledge: ʿĀʾisha's report — "people used to ask God's Messenger about the soothsayers; he said, 'they are nothing'; they said, 'O Messenger of God, their talk comes true sometimes!' He said, 'That word of truth — the jinn seizes it and pours it into the ear of his client, and the client adds to it a hundred lies'" (al-Bukhārī 5757 (Kitāb al-Ṭibb, the chapter on soothsaying), with Muslim 2229's parallel "the devils descend from the sky, snatch a word of the news and pour it into the mouths of their soothsayers, and they add to it a hundred lies from themselves"). The sūrah's *ḥafiẓnāhā* is the divine side of exactly this report: preservation of the channel explains why *siḥr*-culture produced a Qur'an with a hundred lies, while the Reminder produced zero — the falsifiable claim of 4:82 again. And on the Day, the same guarded heaven reappears, opened in honor of the truthful and sealed against the accusers (78:19, "the heaven shall be opened and become gates"; 82:10-11, "over you are guardians, noble recorders"; and 50:16-18, "We know what the soul whispers while We are nearer to it than its jugular vein — no word does he utter but with him a watcher, ready" — one continuous *ḥ-f-ẓ* family: the angels who guard the man, the heaven that guards the word, the God who guards the book, v. 9).

**The Believer's Application: A Universe Under Guard — and Under Orders**

Two practical rays. First, *humility of reception*: the verse teaches that the revelation reaching you has passed through more vetting than any news in human hands — which makes it the only report in one's life safe to stake eternity on, and the rest of the media (including, for the 7th-century Arab, the market-poetry and soothsayer news-lines, and for us the feed) comparatively unprotected. Second, *security practice for the soul*: the sūrah describes a guarded heaven, and the believer, in the Prophet's sunna, keeps a personal sky — the morning and evening *adhkār* are the private *ḥifẓ* protocol ("whoever says in the morning and evening 'In the name of God, with Whose name nothing on earth or heaven can harm, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing,' three times — nothing will harm him," Abū Dāwūd 5088, Tirmidhī 3388), and the two-guardian-angels (Bukhārī-Muslim, *muʿāqibāt*, the alternating angels of night and day) and the sleep-verse of 2:255 (the *āyat al-kursī* — with 114's "from the evil of the whisperer" as its nightly mirror). The sūrah's cosmology and the believer's liturgy are one system in this book: the cosmos has a border police; so does your bedroom, at the end of every verse you recite before sleep — "God appoints a guardian over him, and no devil approaches him until dawn" (al-Bukhārī, from Abū Hurayra). Every devil in this verse is *outcast* — a status, not a power: the sūrah will say it again in v. 42, "My servants — you have no authority over them" — and so the real news in v. 17 is not the devil's malice but the sky's security; from which the reader borrows, by God's promise, the same: guarded, in the guarded book, for the guarded.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:18

> **save he who gains a hearing by stealth, and then a manifest flaming star pursues him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Proves the Lock**

*Illa man istaraṣa al-samʿa fa-atbaʿahu shihābun ṣaqib* — "except whoever snatches a hearing, then there follows him a piercing flame." The grammatical *istithnāʾ* (exception) here is, as al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī both note, *munqaṭiʿ*-like in effect — "but whoever tries to steal a hearing…" — for the exception does not so much limit the guarding as *demonstrate* it: the devil is not permitted to roam; he is permitted to *attempt*, and the attempt is his prosecution. *Istarṭa* is itself a mini-lesson: *istaṭaʿa*, to demand a loan (*qarḍ*) — here, to ask for a hearing, meaning: he can only get what he borrows; nothing in the guarded heaven is *given* to him. And *al-samʿa* is the verbal noun for the *heard content*, not the sense of hearing: he snatches, at most, the outer edge of a decree about to be executed on earth (as the jinn testify: "We found the heaven filled with mighty strong ones; we used to sit for it seats of eavesdropping, but whoever listens now finds a watching flame" (72:8-9); and as al-Bukhārī reports, when God decrees a word in the heaven "the angels beat their wings in submission to His word, until, when that dread is struck from their hearts, they say: What has your Lord said? The others say: the truth — and He is the Most High, the Great" — the soothsayer-chain of v. 17's note closes the circuit). The system is one-way: the descent of the Reminder is escorted; the descent of stolen fragments is shot down mid-flight.

**Shihāb Ṣaqib: The Physics of the Pursuit**

*Shihāb* is the fireball, the meteor's tongue of flame; *ṣāqib* is from *ṣ-q-b*, "to pierce" — al-Ṭabarī: "a flame that pierces and burns; some say it is the star that follows in his wake, burning him, and the one struck does not quite die but passes the word on, half-scared, to his client." Ibn Kathīr records the Salaf's precise descriptions: the meteors that streak at night (the Arabs' *kusūf* of falling stars they watched in the desert) were, for them, evidence of the sūrah's claim as much as astronomy; 37:10's fuller version — "and they are pelted with a punishment of a blazing flame" (with 37:6-9's lamps as *shihāb*) is the twin, and 67:5 adds that the missiles are *made* ("We made them missiles for the devils"). The pursuit's result (37:8-9 with 72:9: "and they are pelted from every side, with a punishment of a blazing flame, and cast into an everlasting — except whoever snatches; then he finds himself pursued by a blazing flame") completes the jurisprudence: the snatch is answered by the flame, and the flame is *ṣaqib* — penetrating — so the interception is total except for one designed residual: the fragment reaching the soothsayer arrives already terrorized, half-burnt, and mixed by the client with a hundred lies (Bukhārī 7090, Muslim 2229 — "that word — the devil drips it into his soothsayer's mouth — and they mix with it a hundred lies from themselves") — the sūrah's own answer to the Quraysh's *siḥr*-taunt at vv. 15 and 6: the soothsayer-channel works, but it *adds*; the Gabriel-channel is guarded and adds nothing (v. 9).

**Reading the Verse Historically: As-Sādiq wa'l-Makdhūb**

The Prophet ﷺ's own biography gives the verse its 7th-century datum. the report of ʿĀʾisha: people asked the Messenger of God ﷺ about the soothsayers, and he said, "They are nothing." They said: Messenger of God, sometimes they tell us something and it comes true! He said: "That word of truth — the jinn snatches it and pours it into the ear of his client like a hen's cluck, and they mix into it more than a hundred lies." (al-Bukhārī 5757; cf. Muslim 2229) The tradition-names for the phenomenon — *al-ṣādiq wa'l-makdhūb*, "the truther and liar" — belonged to the Arabs' own proverb, and the sūrah's genius is its refusal to *deny* the soothsayers' hits (which would have looked ignorant) while *explaining* them (which destroys their authority). The same verse thus underwrites an epistemology of partial truths — an enemy's accurate fragment does not validate his system; and the Qur'an, which never needed to snatch anything, is by the sūrah's own logic the *only* sky-report with no *siḥr* contamination: the Reminder from the Lord of the burūj, guarded as the burūj are guarded.

**The Verse's Cosmology and the Modern Sky**

The exegetes have long discussed how far the *shihāb* imagery is *literal* (real meteors launched against real listeners) or *imagistic* (the guarding described in the idiom the desert understands). Al-Ṭabarsī transmits the literalist reports — ʿAbdullāh ibn az-Zubayr was asked of the shooting stars and answered: "they are the missiles of the angels" — and Ibn Kathīr affirms the reality without pretending to measure it. What the modern reader brings — the atmosphere's meteor-shield, the ionosphere's opacity to most radiation, the very fact that the sky *is* a wall against the unseen's raw energies as against physical debris — is not proof of the Qur'an in any naive way, but a resonance the sūrah has been preparing since v. 16: three functions in one sky, and "guarded" remains, per the sūrah, a *juridical* description, not a physical discovery: the heavens police the traffic of information, so that the question "whose channel is clean?" is answered for all time by the divine oath of v. 9 rather than by v. 18's flames — the flames serve to make the channel's integrity visible. There is, however, an ethical astronomy the commentators press: al-Rāzī notes (and Ibn al-Qayyim later develops in *al-Fawāʾid*) that the outer sky's guard mirrors the believer's inner sky — the heart, too, is *maḥfūẓ* against the *shihāb*-less thieves: the "whisper that comes and goes" (the Prophet ﷺ's description of *khinzab*, Muslim 2203, the "devil called Khinzab who interferes between a man and his heart — when you sense him, seek refuge and spit dry-spittle to your left three times") is chased off by the flame of remembrance: "the devil flees from a house in which the Qur'an is recited" (Muslim 780), and "when the call to prayer is sounded they retreat with windulence" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra). As the burūj hunt the eavesdropper, dhikr hunts the whisper: the sūrah's cosmic security is, in practice, a household protocol, and the *ṣāqib* has an inner twin — the piercing verse that lands in a soft heart and burns out what the devil tried to plant (cf. our source's note: "the Qur'an's order is a wall round it like a city's"). A truth, once snatched, is pursued; a truth, once *received*, purifies. The difference between v. 18's devil and the believer is the one word the whole surah exists to teach: not *istaṭaʿa* — *āmala*, hoped, and believed, and preserved.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:19

> **And the earth We spread out, and cast therein firm mountains, and We caused to grow therein all manner of things in due balance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Courtroom: From the Sky He Adorned to the Ground He Spread**

The sūrah's evidence-train has run from the Book (v. 1), through the mockery (vv. 6-15), to the sky (vv. 16-18); now it lands — literally. *Wa'l-arḍa maddahnāhā* — the fronting of *al-arḍ* (the object before the verb) marks the contrast: *and the earth — We spread it*. *Madda* is to stretch out a hide on pegs, to unroll a mat: the same verb used for Pharaoh's boast ("is not the kingdom of Egypt mine, and these rivers flowing beneath me?" — 43:51 — *yanbfiqu*, while *madd* is here God's answer), and for the sky itself elsewhere ("the heaven — We built it with might, and We are expanding it," 51:47, *mūsiʿūn*). The image is not a flat-earth claim but a *bed* claim: the earth made a spread-out surface on which life can be laid — 2:22, "Who made the earth a carpet for you and the heaven a structure." Al-Ṭabarī cites Qatāda's gloss: He spread it for you, so you travel its roads and graze its pastures — the "spread" is measured to the human need, which is the whole verse's theme: the earth is not a planet we stumbled onto; it is a floor built for the feet on it (cf. 67:15, "it is He who made the earth tame for you — walk in its flanks and eat of His provision," *dhullūlan* from *dh-l-l*, subjection, the same word used for a docile camel).

**Rawāsī: The Mountains That Answer Two Charges**

*Wa alqaynā fīhā rawāsiya* — "and We cast in it anchor-mountains." *Rawāsiya* is the plural of *rāsiya* — fixed, rooted (root *r-s-w*, the anchoring of a ship, the settling of a debt), and the Qur'an consistently pairs the mountains with *an* tawīda — "lest it shake with you" (16:15, 21:31, 31:10, 78:7, "and the mountains — pegs," *awtād*). Two charges are answered by the single image. (a) Against the dazed-eye mockers of vv. 14-15: you ask for a sign that moves — a gate to climb; here are signs that *refuse to move*, mountains cast like sinkers into the earth's fabric so that your bed does not heave while you sleep; the stability you call "nature" is, in the sūrah's grammar, an *alqāʾ*, a "casting-into," a verb with an Actor. (b) Against the geography of Makkah itself, the *Bakkah* of stone in which nothing grows — the valley Abraham described as "uncultivated" (14:37), and which v. 20 says was then planted with "means of livelihood": God says, the same hand that anchors the soil anchors the *revelation* — a point the sūrah's first verses already made in reverse: the Book is *rāsiya* in the hearts it chooses (cf. Ibn ʿAbbās on the mountains as parables of the Law and prophecy, noted in the source: "mountains = God's signs, the Sacred Law, and prophethood, because they are fixed and rooted and keep human life rooted in truth," citing the sūrah of Ibrāhīm's same pairing: "He cast in the earth firm mountains, lest it should shake with you" (14:46)). Modern earth-science has popularized the old description (mountains' deep roots, the *isostasy* that stabilizes crustal plates) — the sūrah's readers should note that the classical exegetes never needed that; the point is *teleology*, not physics: the verse's *an* *tawīda* — "that it not quake with you" — states a purpose, and purposes are known by the One who made them, not by the instruments He used.

**Bi-Qadr: All Things in Measure — the Balance-Built World**

*Wa anbatnā fīhā min kulli shayʾin mawzūn* — "and We grew in it everything of measured kind." The adjective *mawzūn* (passive participle of *wazana*, weighed) carries the verse's load, and the source's double gloss covers it: (a) "measured upon the scales of God's Wisdom and apportioned accordingly" (Z), and (b) the botanical sense: species in balance — every growth weighed to its soil, its season, its use (Bg), so that, as 13:3 continues, "of every fruit He placed therein two spouses" and 55:7-9 lays down the law the physical order illustrates: "and the heaven He raised and He placed the balance — that you transgress not in the balance; and establish weight with justice, and do not short the balance." The same *m-w-z-n* root thus links the *earth's* measured growth to the *scales of judgment* — "and We place the scales of justice for the Day" (21:47) — a semantic bridge the sūrah itself crosses in v. 21 ("nothing exists but its treasuries are with Us, and We send it down only by a known measure") and closes again in v. 26's clay to v. 44's apportioned gates: as everything in the earth is apportioned, everyone in the Fire is apportioned ("for every gate a portion, already promised," *mauʿūd*). The balance of nature is the moral balance, taught in the grain of the wheat.

**From Spread Earth to Spread Table: The Verse's Human Lesson**

The three verbs — spread, cast, grew — describe, in order, a floor, a foundation, and a banquet; the sūrah's hearers, Makkah's traders in a stony land that grew nothing and anchored nothing, are being asked to see that their trade itself is a copy of a deeper provision: they measured goods at their scales (and vv. 78-79's sin of the *mufaṭṭif*, the short-measure people of the Wood, will convict their successors); the earth they cross is a scale God Himself balanced, every species weighed into place, every aquifer and pollinating wind and sown date-palm "in due measure" (v. 21's *qadr maʿlūm* echoing here). The Prophet ﷺ's law of husbandry belongs to this verse's light: "Whoever plants a crop and bird or beast or human eats from it, it is charity for him" (al-Bukhārī 2320); "If the Hour stands and in your hand a shoot — plant it" (Aḥmad, Ṣaḥīḥ al-Jāmiʿ). A world weighed with purpose teaches the *worker* to weigh his work and the *consumer* to taste thanks: the soil is a madrasa, says Ibn al-Qayyim, whose *Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn* reads the natural order as God's "customary legislation": He sends measured rain over measured earth to grow measured grain, and "then woe to those who give short measure" (83:1) of all that measure, from the grain to the grace. And for the *modern* reader the verse's "bi-qadr" answers the two poles of our age's nature-rhetoric: the world is neither raw material for unlimited exploitation (the balance forbids *t-gh-y*, transgressing, as the same verb is used of the scales and of God's limits — "whoever transgresses the limits of God," 2:229) nor a deity to be worshipped (the sky is guarded *from* the devils, and the earth is *spread for* us: nature is a service, not a god). To stand on it, anchored, balanced, growing, is the everyday miracle the mockers asked to have *ascended* — and the sūrah's quiet verdict: they were standing on the miracle while demanding a spaceship.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:20

> **And We placed therein means of livelihood for you and for those for whom you provide not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Maʿāsh: The Word That Turns Survival into Design**

*Wa jaʿalnā lakum fīhā maʿāshiya* — "and We placed for you therein livings." *Maʿāsh* (from *ʿ-sh*, to live, to seek a life; the *mafʿal* of living) is the Qur'an's careful word for the machinery of sustenance: not *niʿma* (blessing) but *maʿāsh* (livelihood), the plural forms naming *the things by which you earn* (al-Ṭabarī: "what you live by — crafts, trades, and tillage"). The verse thus descends from the mountains and the balanced growth into the *work* that draws them out: God spreads the floor, then places on it the *livelihoods* — every plough-line, caravan-route (Quraysh's own winter and summer journeys, 106:1-2!), forge, fish-trap and loom is, in the sūrah's grammar, *jaʿala*, a placement by deliberate hands. Al-Qurṭubī cites 20:131's parallel, "your Lord's provision is better and more lasting," and 62:10, "and when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the lands and seek God's bounty" — the two-testament rule of *kasb*: work is worship-shaped, but provision is God-shaped (cf. ʿUmar's verdict on the man who sat in the mosque waiting: "the sky does not rain gold and silver" — cited in al-Bayhaqī's *al-Zuhd*, and ʿUmar's *hisba* office as the state's *maʿāsh* custodian).

**"And for those you do not feed" — The Verse's Silent Partner**

*Wa man lam tarzuqūhu* — literally, "and for those whom you do not feed." The source's double gloss (Ṭ: cattle and beasts of burden; Z: dependents — the weak, infants, elders) covers the full circle of mouths not in your pay-roll. This is one of the Qur'an's great *economies of compassion*: the earth was spread with means of life for creatures whose upkeep no human budget includes — the sparrow on the wall, the beetle under the stone, the neighbor's orphan no one registered. The doctrine is spelled out at 11:6, the sister-verse: "There is no creature that crawls but God takes it by its forelock — my Lord is on a straight path," and "there is no moving creature on earth but upon God is its provision" (11:6 *wa-mā min dābbatin fi'l-arḍi illā ʿalā Allāhi rizquhā*). The sūrah here quietly redefines ownership: your *maʿāsh* — your income stream — is a *branch* of a universal provision-line that includes the animals, so no wealth of yours was ever *yours* in the first place, and the *ghayr marzūqīn* — your un-fed dependents (servants, donkeys, cats, the poor whose name you forgot at the zakāt count) are co-heirs of the spread earth. The sharīʿa drew the legal tooth from precisely this clause: "a woman entered the Fire because of a cat she had caged — she neither fed it nor released it to eat from the vermin of the earth" (al-Bukhārī 3318; Muslim 2242): "those for whom you provide not" includes the mute, and their hunger is booked against the capable.

**Al-Zamakhsharī's Knife: Who Really Provides?**

The source preserves the Muʿtazilī master's line (al-Kashshāf on 15:20): "the one who thinks he feeds his dependents is mistaken — God is the Provider (*al-Rāziq*), and He feeds him along with them," cross-referenced with 51:58, "Truly God — He is the Provider, the Lord of Power, the Firm," and 29:60, "how many a creature does not carry its own provision — God gives it and you its provision." The point is not fatalism — the *kasb* remains, the plough remains, the paycheck remains — but the *deed of ownership* shifts: the verse's structure (*jaʿalnā lakum… wa man lam tarzuqū*) says God *made*, and men *think they make*. Al-Rāzī, quoting his father's line, adds the ethics that grow from it: the provider who knows he is a channel is neither arrogant to the one he feeds nor anxious about the feeding; the hadith that anchors it all: "if you relied upon God with true reliance, you would be provided as the birds are provided: they go out empty-stomached and return full" (Tirmidhī 2344, from ʿUmar, with the note: "they go out *ghudūwan* — early — and return *rūḥan* — late — they *go*, they do not sit!"), which is why the *ʿulamāʾ* called this verse the *āyat al-tawakkul*: birds fly without portfolios; they do not fly without wings, and the wings are the *maʿāsh*.

**The Livelihood and the Lord of the Hour**

The verse is doing sūrah-work, not just sermon-work: vv. 16-19 built the stage; vv. 20-23 will show that the stage is owned by the One Who gives life and death (v. 23), and so the *maʿāsh* is a trust with a deadline. Three consequences the classical exegetes draw out: (1) *gratitude* — every wage is a receipt from a Giver, which is why the *duʿāʾ* of earning (al-Tirmidhī, from ʿAlī: "O God, suffice me with what is lawful against what is forbidden, and enrich me with Thy bounty beyond need of anyone besides Thee") is the sunna's liturgy of the workplace; (2) *justice* — if the *maʿāsh* was placed for me *and* for those I do not feed, the market is a shared room, and the sūrah will convict the *mufaṭṭifūn* (83:1-3) of the Wood (v. 78) at its climax, making v. 20 the *premise* of v. 79's punishment — fraud against the short-weighted is fraud against the verse's "those you do not feed," whose provision God is personally underwriting; and (3) *contentment* — 4:32, "do not wish for what God has given some over others — for men a portion of what they earned, and for women a portion" — read against the verse's "for you *and for those you don't feed*": your portion is your *maʿāsh*; the other's portion is *their* provider, not your appetite. A modern application closes: an age of GDP argues over shares; the sūrah says the earth's *maʿāsh* was placed for the whole table — the ones who eat (you) and the ones you don't feed (eight billion futures, one billion present) — and the same hand that spread the earth guarantees the meal: so weigh honestly, earn actively, and pray the prayer of the birds — *ghudūwan*: out early, *rūḥan*: back at dusk, with full wings and full stomachs, and gratitude for both.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:21

> **Naught is there, but that its treasuries lie with Us, and We do not send it down, save in a known measure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Ends Panic and Ends Arrogance Together**

*Wa in min shayʾin illā ʿindā khazāʾinuhu wa-mā nunazziluhu illā bi-qadrin maʿlūm* — "And there is nothing — but that its treasuries are with Us; and We do not send it down except with a known measure." The verse is one of the Qur'an's total-claims, and its two halves are aimed at the two diseases of the human heart under pressure. Half one kills anxiety: whatever you need — rain, wealth, a child, victory, healing — has a storehouse, and the storehouse has an Owner, and the Owner says *ʿindanā*, "with Us": the keys are not lost, the shelves are not empty, and nothing is "out of stock" except by His leaving it out. Half two kills presumption: He does not *send it down* at your tempo or in your quantity — *illā bi-qadrin maʿlūn*, "save with a measure that is known," to Him first, and to you, if you read the signs, eventually. Al-Ṭabarī's summary: "nothing that exists — of all that the creatures use — but its source and store are with God, and He gives it to whom He wills, in the amount He wills, at the time He wills" — a full *theodicy of distribution* in a single breath.

**Khazāʾin: The Word, the Image, the Metaphysics**

*Khazīna* is the strong-store (the Persian loan *khāzēn* was current in Ḥijāzī markets — the vaults of grain and coin that every Quraysh merchant knew); the plural *khazāʾin* makes the sky-earth system into a treasury whose teller is God. The source commentary maps the readings: (a) specific: the treasuries of *rain* (Kl, R), since rain is the one commodity everyone wants and no one controls, and the verse is the answer to 56:68-70: "have you seen the water you drink? Is it you who send it down from the clouds, or are We the senders? If We willed, We could make it salt — so why do you not thank?" (56:68-69, cited on v. 22); (b) universal (IJ, Bḍ): every existent thing's *origin* and *cause* is with Him. Al-Zamakhsharī adds the Muʿtazilī nuance — the *khazāʾin* of a thing are its *asbāb*, its means: God's treasury of rain is wind and cloud; of wealth, effort and exchange — the treasuries are *the systems*, so that the verse forbids neither work nor science, only the worship of them. And the source preserves the Akbari reading: the treasuries are the *aʿyān thābita*, the fixèd essences of all things in God's knowledge — "unmanifested archetypes," so that "sending down" (*tanzīl*) is creation itself: what was latent in the treasury of knowledge becomes manifest in the world's market, "with a measure" — because every manifestation must fit the container it was weighed for. Ibn ʿArabī's school will say: the outflow never empties the treasury and the measure never misses the need; the sūrah's own vocabulary — *nunazzil*, the verb of revelation in v. 9 — makes the treasury and the Book one economy: as the Reminder was "sent down" in surahs and verses on a timed schedule (v. 87's "the seven oft-repeated and the Mighty Qur'an"), so rain, so bread, so life: the *tanzīl* of all things is measured, staged, and guarded.

**Bi-Qadrin Maʿlūm: The Measure as Mercy, Not Scarcity**

The clause's function is to redefine *enough*. The Qur'an's own glosses: 54:49, "and everything they did is in the scrolls — and every small and great thing is written," and 42:27, "and had God apportioned provision lavishly to His servants, they would have committed tyranny on the earth; but He sends down what He wills, with a measure — indeed, He is of His servants Aware, Seeing" — the *qadr* is here explicitly *protection against tyranny*: unlimited supply from unlimited will, in unlimited amounts, would produce unlimited Pharaohs (cf. Qārūn, 28:81, whose un-measured treasury the sūrah just dismantled: "his treasure's keys were a burden for the strong party," v. 28:76). The verse then teaches that measure is not stinginess but *craftsmanship*: the known measure of a sown field, a prescribed fast, an apportioned zakāt, a fixed *ajal*. The spiritual psychology of *qadr* is captured by al-Kharrāz (Su in the source): whoever truly believes this verse will not rely on other than God in *any* affair, worldly or otherworldly — because both are in the same treasury and both are released on the same schedule. Hence the two great prophetic sayings on the ledger of the measured:

> "Know that what was going to miss you could not hit you, and what was going to hit you could not miss you, and know that the pen has dried with the knowledge of the Lord of the worlds." (transmitted by Aḥmad and al-Tirmidhī (2516) from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, in the spirit of al-Bukhārī's chapter on divine decree; the Qur'an's own gloss is 57:22-23: "no calamity befalls on the earth nor in your selves but it is in a Book before We bring it into being — so that you may not grieve over what escaped you nor exult over what He gave you," with 57:22-23 as its *taʿlīl* — the *ḥikma* being the double therapy of grief and pride)

The *qadr maʿlūm* thus has *known* — and the known measure is the believer's *permission to plan without panic*: the market crash, the failed exam, the drought, the diagnosis — all are debits from a treasury whose Owner has already told you the ledger's rule: it is *known*, which means it is *not random*, and it is *with Us*, which means it is *not hostile*.

**The Verse Against Two Modern Idols: Scarcity and Abundance**

Read as public theology, v. 21 cuts both of the world's economic theologies. Against *scarcity-anxiety* (the fear-grammar that hoards, corners markets, and wages wars on granaries: "the treasuries are with Us" — the storehouse is open; what is withheld is withheld by a Wise One, cf. 17:100 — "say, were you to hold the treasuries of my Lord's mercy, you would withhold for fear of spending them," the exact twin: the verse says the *withholding* is *their* imagined sin or *our* mercy, never a shortage in the Real): the believer is freed to build, give, and consume as one who shops in an open house. Against *abundance-worship* (the "growth without limit" religion — the *qadr maʿlūm* says every download, every harvest, every gallon is metered, and 41:10's four-day measure of the earth's food — "He blessed it therein and apportioned therein its sustenances in four days, equal for those who ask" — *waqaddara fīhā aqwātahā*: the same verb *q-d-r* for the earth's caloric budget!): every act of over-drafting the earth is *tuğyān* in the balance (v. 19's *bi-qadr* and 55:8-9) and ends where Quraysh ended — with the known decree (v. 4). The verse's personal application remains the simple one the Salaf practiced, crystallized in the Prophet's reply to a man who asked whether to tether his camel or leave it trusting: "Tie her, and rely." (al-Tirmidhī 2517, from Anas; graded ḥasan): the treasury releases through *tethered* causes; the measure includes your rope. And the same sunna's *qunūt*-duʿāʾ for the oppressed and *istisqāʾ* for rain (the sūrah's v. 22) show the system whole: ask the Owner, work the cause, receive the measure, and read the *known* clause as the verse that forgives you for not being God — and holds you to being His servant, to whom every "what if" was answered before the earth was spread.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:22

> **And We sent forth the winds, fertilizing. And We sent down water from the sky, providing you with sufficient drink thereby, and you are not the keepers of its stores.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Treasury's Delivery Service**

*Wa arsalnā al-riyāḥ lawāqiḥa fa-anzalnā min al-samāʾ māʾan fa-asqaynākumu* — after the treasuries and the measure (v. 21), the verse opens the mechanism: winds dispatched (*arsala*, the verb of sending, used for messengers and for rain alike — the whole verse reads like a courier's log), water *sent down* (the *tanzīl* vocabulary again — the sky's economy and the Book's are one system of dispatched gifts), and the purpose clause: "so that We gave you drink" — *fa-asqaynākum*, the Form IV for "We gave to drink," the causer of the drinking: the grammar itself hands the act back to God — you drank; *We* caused the drinking, through winds you did not send and a cloud you do not own. The source cites 56:68-70 as the verse's twin court: "Have you seen the water you drink — did you send it down from the cloud, or are We the senders? Had We willed, We could have made it salt: so will you not thank?" — the same three-step proof (the gift, the question of who gave, the counterfactual) run as an interrogation.

**Lawāqiḥ: The Winds That Conceive the Rain**

*Lawāqiḥ* is the plural of *lāqiḥa* — the one that *laqqaḥa*, impregnates — and the commentators split in a way that enriches rather than divides. (a) Al-Ṭabarī, Qatāda: winds that carry the clouds and *fertilize* them, driving the male wind against the cloud so it "conceives" water (the Arabs' own meteorology: a *riḥ laqūḥ* was the wind that struck a cloud and left it pregnant; and 15:22's parallel 51:41-42 — the *ṣarṣar* wind that destroyed ʿĀd, "not leaving anything it comes upon but it makes it like crumbling dust" — the barren, lethal wind held against the fertilizing one, so that wind is *mood*, not mere motion). (b) Some (Ṭs) read it as the winds that pollinate *trees* — and both are true to the Arabic, since *talqīḥ* is used for both cloud-gravidity and date-palm pollination, and the Prophet ﷺ's famous agricultural ruling belongs here: "you know better the affairs of your world" (Muslim 2363, the *talqīḥ* of the palms at Medina — the passage where the Companions' expert pollination *corrected* a well-meant estimate, exactly the sunna of "the causes are yours, the measure is His," v. 21). The word thus welds the verse's whole lesson: life is made by *encounters* God arranges — wind meeting cloud, male palm meeting female spadix, rain meeting seed — and every "natural" fertility is a dispatched *irsāl*, a sending, i.e., a mission: the winds are couriers in a logistics of grace, and the *known measure* of v. 21 passes through them like water through a pipe that did not dig itself.

**"You Are Not Its Keepers" — The Property Lesson of the Century**

*Wa mā antum lahā bi-khāzinīn* — "and you are not for it (the water) store-keepers." The clause closes the rain's case like a judge's line: you did not dispatch the wind, you did not send the water, and — note — you do not even *hold* the water: the *khazīna* of v. 21 was God's; here the *khawāzin* of the rain are explicitly *not* yours, i.e., the verse answers the Makkans' (and every empire's) claim over water: the cisterns of the Ḥaram, the wells of the caravans, the *sāqiyas* of the sultans — all are *custody*, not property; you are not even its *warehouse*: the water you "store" is stored by the One whose treasury it was before it fell and after it evaporates. Al-Qurṭubī reads the clause against *ḥima*, the pre-Islamic chieftains' water-exclusivity, and cites the Prophet ﷺ's abolition: "no prohibiting the surplus water" (al-Bukhārī, Kitāb al-Muzāraʿa — "the Muslims are partners in three: water, pasture, and fire" — with Aḥmad's and Abū Dāwūd's fuller wording, and the ruler's duty to compel its delivery to those in need, laid down by the imams of the *ḥisba* on the strength of the same reports — the sharīʿa's water law is this verse made statute: the verse's "you are not its keepers" is the fatwa's *ʿilla*).

**The Hydrologic Creed: Rain, Politics, and Gratitude**

The verse's theology is applied by its own sūrah's sequel (vv. 23-25: life, death, inheritance, and the gathering of the swift and slow), and by the Prophet ﷺ's rain-liturgy, which made the Qur'an's hydrology into daily practice: when the wind blew he said, "O God, I ask Thee for its good, the good of what it is sent with, and the good of what is ordered in it; and I seek refuge in Thee from its evil…" (Muslim 899); when the rain fell, "O God, make it a *mughīth*, a nourishing rain," uncovering an arm so the drops touched the skin (Abū Dāwūd's chapter of the rain-prayer: he bared his body until the rain ran down it, saying, "it has just come from its Lord"); when the clouds gathered darkly, his face changed and he rushed between the pulpit and the people, "perhaps it is near," praying *istisqāʾ* — the *salāt al-istisqāʾ* itself, the sūrah's v. 22 enacted in the mosque's open air (al-Bukhārī 1009-1023: the Prophet went out to the *musallā*, turned the *ridāʾ* inside-out, and prayed, and raised his hands until the whites of his armpits showed, and God gave them rain till the next Friday). From the verse to the sunna the pattern is the same: *the sky is not yours; ask.* And the modern application is exactly as literal: over two billion people live in water-stressed lands; desalination, pipelines, and "cloud-seeding" are *lawāqiḥ* technology, not treasury ownership — the sūrah's line *mā antum lahā bi-khāzinīn* reads, in the 21st century, as a charter against the privatization of thirst and a rule of public *fiqh*: surplus water is shared by divine title, and hoarding it is not "market logic" but *ghasb*, usurpation of what was never in your store. The *known measure* of v. 21 keeps even this verse's water: every watershed, every aquifer, every monsoon — apportioned, dispatched, and *not yours*; the only rational posture toward such a world is the one the verse ends with by implication and 56:70 names outright: *afalā tashkurūn* — "will you not then thank?" — a thankfulness that, in this sūrah's order, will become, by the Day, a garden (v. 45) or a gate (v. 44), the two distributions the one *Khāzin* alone measures.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:23

> **Surely it is We Who give life and cause death, and We are the Inheritor.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Claims, One Ledger**

*Wa innā la-naḥnu nuḥyī wa-numītu wa-naḥnu'l-wārit* — the verse stacks three assertions, each a *jumla ismiyya* of oath-weight: We — *We* — give life; We cause death; and We are the Inheritor. The rhythm is a summary of everything the previous six verses demonstrated: the winds and rain that *gave drink* (v. 22) are the verb *nuḥyī*; the *ajal* of the nations (vv. 4-5) and the mockers' "soon they will know" (v. 3) is the verb *numītu*; and the *wārith* names what remains when both verbs finish — the Owner of all estates after all heirs die. The sūrah's natural theology (vv. 16-22) is here crowned with its *eschatology*: the same God who dispatches rain dispatches souls, and both flows run toward one balance-sheet: "to God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and the earth" (3:180 with 57:10: "to God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and the earth; and God is aware of what you do" — the inheritance-title welded to the all-seeing clause: the Wārith knows what the tenants did with the lease).

**Nuḥyī wa-Numītu: The Double Verb That Demythologizes Nature**

The present-tense pair — "We give life and We give death" — is deliberately continuous: not "We did it once," but "We are doing it," every germination after the rain (v. 22's *fa-asqaynākum… li-yublija* — the sprouting of the dead earth, cf. 30:50, "then look at the traces of God's mercy, how He revives the earth after its death; that One is reviver of the dead, *ḥayyu'l-mawtā bi'l-fiʿl*" — 35:9 and Fāṭir's "the One who gave life to the earth after its death" — given to *the human* as well), and every death that is the *numīt*, the same verb, applied to sleep (the prayer on waking: "Praise be to God who gave us life after He caused us to die, and to Him is the resurrection," al-Bukhārī 6325: sleep as nightly estate, *nuḥyā* as inheritance returned). To this double verb the verse adds its polemic edge: Makkah's *dahr* — "nothing destroys us but time" (45:24, *wa-mā yuhlikunā illā'd-dahr*) — is answered by naming the two hands Time does not own: life and death are *acts*, and acts have actors; the "chance of the atoms" and "the turning of the ages" both lose their verdicts here. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī on this verse notes that the order is *nuḥyī* first to answer those who credit nature with life; *numītu* second to answer those who credit fate with death; and *al-wārit* third to answer those who credit heirs with ownership at all: the estate returns not to the son but to the Sovereign; "the children's portion is *after* the Inheritor's title, not beside it" (cf. the *farāʾiḍ* jurists' rule: no property of any deceased is theirs — the very science of *mīrāth*, inheritance-law, is 15:23 written into the courts: *wa'l-ākhirona lā yumkin… al-māl mālu'llāh*: the estate is God's and the heirs are tenants, the sūrah's *wārith* the hidden clause of every *farīḍa* in Islam).

**Al-Wārith: The Name's Meaning and Its Marketplace**

*Al-Wārit* — the Inheritor — is the name by which 19:40 speaks: "We inherit the earth and whoever is upon it, and to Us they are returned" (*wa-ʿalaynā yurjaʿūn*, the return that makes every leaving a *lease*), and 28:58 (cited in the source): "and thy Lord would not destroy the towns until He had sent in their Mother-Town a messenger… nor did We destroy towns except for a people wrong-doing — and what was given to you is the enjoyment and adornment of the worldly life; and what is with God is better and more lasting — do you not then reason?" — the *wārith* clause is thus, in the Qur'an, always a *valuation* clause: the world's goods are *matāʿ*, a use that passes; the Akhira's are *mā ʿind Allāh*, what is *with* Him, and "with Him" is the same idiom as v. 21's *ʿindanā khazāʾinuhū*: the treasuries and the inheritance are one *ʿind*. The Prophet ﷺ applied the name to the human pocket's psychology (al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Mājah: "the best of you in Islam are those whose *ṣalāh* the people feel safe from, and the *muhājir* is not the one who leaves what God forbade — and whoever leaves two garments for the inheritance, God will clothe him of the garments of Paradise," a report of the early *zuhd* tradition — a garment set aside beyond one's need belongs to the estate, and the verse's logic turns that into an estate-plan: what you bequeath is being inherited by you from yourself tomorrow). The most cited legal reflection is on 57:10: "why do you not spend in God's cause — while to God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and earth? Not equal among you are those who spent before the conquest and fought…" — the *wārith*-doctrine as the foundation of *infāq*: you are handing back what will be handed away from you, so *giving it early is the only way to own the credit*.

**The Believer's Short Estate Law**

Three lines of application close the verse, all native to it. (1) *Detachment without monasticism*: the sūrah did not say "die to the world" — it said *nuḥyī* then *numītu* — you live first; the *wārith* is a reason to earn and plant (v. 20's *maʿāsh* stands) but not to cling, because the last act of every estate is *not probate but return*: *wa-naḥnu* — to *Us* — *al-wārit*. (2) *Justice between heirs*: the Islamic law of *mīrāth* — the widows', orphans', daughters' shares revealed in this very Makkan-Medinan arc (the *kalāla* verses, 4:11-12 and 4:176, whose just division is our vv. 4-5's *kitāb maʿlūm* written into the courts) — reads as 15:23's statute: God divides before death so the Inheritor is not insulted after it; and ʿĀʾisha's transmitted maxim under this name — that the worth of a man's wealth is what he sends ahead of it, not what he leaves behind, and (3) the *duʿāʾ* the verse licenses: when the sūrah of vv. 20-22's treasuries reaches the *wārith*, the believer's worry — "what will my children eat?" (the exact fear answered at 17:31: "kill not your children for fear of poverty — We provide for them and for you" — the *rizq*-clause of Sūrat al-Isrāʾ, the twin Makkan passage of our sūrah) — is answered in the sūrah's own order: the Provider of v. 20 and the Inheritor of v. 23 are the same *naḥnu*: "We are its Preserver" (v. 9) was the book's guarantee; *wa-naḥnu'l-wārit* is the man's: your estate is not in the market's hands at the end of the day; it is in the hands of the One who gives life, gives death, and keeps the books — so *spend*, the verse says, and *plant*, and *weigh honestly*, and when the pen dries (v. 21), you will find the Inheritor more faithful than any heir, since "perhaps you hate a thing while God places much good in it" (4:19).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:24

> **We indeed know those among you who advance, and We indeed know those who lag behind,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Roll-Call Before the Roll**

*Wa laqad ʿalimna'l-muqaddimīna minkum wa-ʿalimna'l-muʾakhkharīn* — "We have already known among you those who advance, and We have known those who put back." The verse's two perfect verbs (*ʿalimna*) answer the *ajal* clause of v. 5: since no nation can hasten or delay its term, God states that the *hasteners and the laggards within* it are already known — the roll is called before the parade arrives. The double *la-…-qad* oath gives it the ring of a courtroom notice; and the participles are not abstract: *muqaddim* and *muʾakhkhar* are the ones who *advance* and *retard* a thing — the vocabulary of *schedules*, of rows in a prayer-line, of ranks in a march.
