The sūrah's audience heard *advancing and lagging* first as *prayer*: they knew the rows, since the *muqaddimūn* are the ones ahead in the *ṣaff* and the *muʾakhkharūn* those who keep falling back from it, and the Prophet ﷺ's commands bind the two participles to the row: "straighten your rows, for the straightening of the rows is part of the perfection of prayer" (al-Bukhārī 723; Muslim 433), and "when you hear the *iqāma*, proceed with *sakīna*; whatever you catch, pray, and whatever escapes you, complete" (al-Bukhārī 636; Muslim 602) — the *muʾakhkhar* given a door back.

**What the Two Groups Are: The Exegetes' Pair of Readings**

The source records Rāzī's two pairs, and both are kept by the Salaf's reports. (a) *Moral* reading (majority): the *muqaddimūn* are those hastening to obedience and the good — the ones who were first to Islam, to the *ṣaff*, to the call of the *āzān* — and the *muʾakhkharūn* those who lag in negligence, the *yarṭabirūna* of 4:143 — "the hypocrites who wait and see; if victory comes to you from God they say, 'were we not with you?'" — the *laggard* with a claim at the till. (b) *Cosmic* reading: the *muqaddimūn* are those already dead (sent forward to the Reservoir), and the *muʾakhkharūn* the living still on earth, and the reading transmitted in al-Ṭabarsī's *Jāmiʿ* that the verse addresses the people of the *ajal* — God knows the ones He has *advanced* in death and the ones He has *deferred* to their term: "and no soul is going to die except by God's permission, in a writing dated (kitāban mujjalan)" (3:145). And there is the eschatological sense too, on which the two readings merge: at the *ḥashr* all are marshalled — the *ṣāffah* of 18:47, "and they will be exposed to thy Lord in rows" — and God's knowledge of who is forward and who is back at that muster is the verse's final frame. The verse thus covers a human being's *entire* distribution: he is *known* in his schedule, his death, and his rank; the *mīzān* (v. 19's *mawzūn*) runs on *ʿilm*, not guesswork.

**The Hadith of the Row-Angels: Why "Advancing" Is a Cosmic Verb**

The pair *taqdim/tāʾkhīr* in this verse is best glossed by the verse it belongs beside: Ṣāffāt — "We are surely those who range in rows, who glorify" (37:165-166), and the Prophet ﷺ's report that the heaven *creaks* and has no hand's-span without an angel prostrating or standing (Tirmidhī — *al-sanad*: "the heaven creaks and rightfully creaks; there is no place of four fingers in it without an angel placing his forehead to God in prostration or glorifying Him" — at-Tirmidhī and Aḥmad): "the ranks" of heaven, "as the ranks of prayer" — the famous saying: "do you not line up as the angels line up before their Lord?" They said: and how do the angels line up before their Lord? He said: "they complete the front ranks, and they straighten one another" (Muslim 430, from Jābir ibn Samura): *taqdim* is thus not only the mosque's floor-plan but the cosmos's *order of merit*, and the *sūrah* quietly teaches: the *muqaddimūn* are not those who *got ahead of others* — the world's definition — but those who got ahead *of their own laziness*, who arrived *before* their selves.

**"We Know" — Not "We Will Find Out": Anti-Fatalist Reading of a Determinist Verse**

The verse is one of the *ʿilm*-verses the theological schools fought over, and its careful reading defuses the fight. The *Qadariyya* said: God's foreknowledge would make the *muʾakhkhar* a victim; the *Jabriyya* said: the knowing *causes*; the sūrah's grammar says neither: God *knows* (ʿalima, a cognitive verb) — the *ʿilm*-verses do not attach *irāda* to the servant's act as its *ʿilla*; the next clause pairs the *ʿilm* with *wisdom* and *ʿadl*: "and thy Lord will gather them — He is Wise, Knowing" (v. 25), the *ḥakīm* and the *ʿalīm* at once: so the gathering is *judgment of what they chose*, not of what He imposed (cf. 18:29, "the truth is from your Lord — whoever wills let him believe, whoever wills let him disbelieve," and 76:29-30, "you do not will except that God wills — God is Knowing, Wise"). The *known* schedule is thus not an excuse: al-Ṭabarī states it as plain *adab* — "God has informed you that He knows who advances and who lags, so *be* of the *muqaddimūn*, since the knowledge is not the deed and the register is not the reward." The pastoral reading: the verse's job is to make *every* moment visible — no hidden *sincere* and no hidden *cheat* (the *khātif* of the *nifāq*), and the *ʿilm* that covers both is, for the sincere, the verse they will read ten thousand times: "He knows the secret and what is hidden" (20:7; 67:13-14, "whether you conceal what is in your breasts or reveal it — God knows what is in the breasts. Should not He who created know? He is the Subtle, Aware").

**Application: The Audit of One's Own Tempo**

The practical verse is a mirror: what is my *tempo* with God? The *muqaddim* and the *muʾakhkhar* are not castes but *rhythms*, and the sūrah tells you the rhythm is already *on record* — which is either terror or therapy, depending on whether you are willing to *change tempo while the record is still being written*. Ibn al-Qayyim, whose *al-Fawāʾid* lives on exactly this axis of knowledge and return, draws the lesson plain: the foreknown division is not the fatal one — the gathering will embrace *all* of them (v. 25), and the Wisdom will divide them again by what each carried, not by what was carried about him. And in the mosque’s row the lesson is daily: whoever catches the *takbīr* and stands first in everything — first *ṣalāh*, first *ṣaff*, first in the queue of good works, first to apologize, first to give — has made this sūrah’s vocabulary into his biography. The *muʾakhkharūn* of this verse are not the late-comers of a single Friday; they are the ones whose whole life runs in the tense of *tomorrow*; and the verse’s last kindness is that it is written *before* the *ḥashr*, so the *taʾkhīr* can yet become *taqaddum* — "for God does not change a people’s condition until they change what is in themselves" (13:11) — which is why the *ḥadīth* closes this unit: "Whoever repents before the sun rises from the west, God will turn toward him in repentance" (Muslim 2703; al-Tirmidhī 3537 adds: "before the death-rattle") — until that sunrise the Day’s *taʾkhīr* is not final, the *ʿilm* stands before the *ʿamal*, and the *ʿamal* can still *arrive*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:25

> **and verily it is thy Lord who will gather them. Truly He is Wise, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Adds the Court to the Clock**

*Wa inna rabbaka huwa yajmaʿuhum* — "and thy Lord — He it is who will gather them." The *ḥashr*, the mustering, turns v. 24's foreknowledge into procedure: the God who *knows* the advancing and the lagging is the One who physically *collects* them — "a Day about which there is no doubt" (40:59), when "We shall gather the guilty that Day, bleary-eyed" (20:102), when "every soul will come with a driver and a witness" (50:21). The pronoun *hum* spans both classes of v. 24 — the dead already advanced and the living still deferred: "Wherever you may be, death will overtake you, even if you should be in towers built up and fortified" (4:78). The gathering is thus not merely re-creation but *roll-call*: the same book that measured each community's *ajal* (v. 4) now reads out the ranks within it.

**Gathering, Not Herding: The Two Names at the Verse's End**

The verse closes by yoking two divine names: *al-Ḥakīm al-ʿAlīm* — "Wise, Knowing." Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Ṭabarī both weigh the pairing: knowledge without wisdom would be a bare register of the *muqaddimūn* and *muʾakhkharūn*, a census with no verdict; wisdom without knowledge would be a judgment pronounced on the unseen. Welded together, they promise that the gathering is a *sorting into proper places*. The root *ḥ-k-m* carries the sense of *placement* — *aḥkama al-dābba*, to fit the horse with a bit, guiding it to its lane — and Ibn ʿAbbās glossed *al-Ḥakīm* as "the One who puts every thing in its place (*mawḍiʿ*)." Thus vv. 24-25 form one movement: the *taqdim* and *taʾkhīr* known now, the *jamʿ* coming then, and the *ḥikma* that will assign, on the Day, exactly the positions the people assigned themselves by their tempo — the sūrah's own architecture will show it: for Hell, "seven gates, and for each gate a portion appointed" (v. 44); for the reverent, "gardens and springs" (v. 45).

**Why the Believer May Love This Verse More Than Its Warning**

Notice the kindness latent in the *jamʿ* within this sūrah's context. The mockers (vv. 6-7) received no public spectacle; the Reminder was kept, yet denied; injustice laughed and nothing fell. Verse 25 answers the *disorder itself*: every unjudged case, every wrong that walked free past a grave, every sincere late-comer passed over and every prominent corrupt man promoted — the gathering is the re-arrangement: "That is the Day when every soul will be recompensed for what it earned; no injustice this Day, for God is Swift in Reckoning" (40:17; cf. 45:22, "and the Hour is coming — of this no doubt — that God may judge between the servants"). Al-Rāzī adds that naming the Judge here as *Rabb* — "thy Lord" — personalizes the summons for the Prophet ﷺ: the same Lord who raised, fed, and guarded him (vv. 9, 20, 22) is the One who gathers; the verdict therefore cannot be hostile to the man He has already kept.

**The Prophet's Depictions of the Gathering**

The *jamʿ* of this verse stood before the Prophet's eyes when he preached, and his descriptions fix the sūrah's two classes in a single image: "People will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised" (al-Bukhārī 6527; Muslim 2860, from ʿĀʾisha). She objected: "Men and women — will they not see one another?" He answered: "The matter is too weighty for that" — the *ḥashr* is an audit, not a spectacle; every soul *ḥasbuhā*, sufficient to its own account (cf. 80:37, "each of them that Day will have enough to occupy him"). And he tied the audit to the ledger of v. 24's knowledge: "No one will stand in that gathering except that God will uncover for him his deeds, and one will be overcome by the greatness of them — that is the one for whom the Book is given in his right hand" — and the saying of ʿĀʾisha on that plain: "Whoever is questioned in the Reckoning that Day will be ruined" (al-Bukhārī), answered by the companion-report in both Ṣaḥīḥs of the private audience: "God brings the believer near, draws His covering over him, and makes him confess his sins — and when he has acknowledged them, He says: I concealed them for you in the world, and I forgive them for you today." Even the *order* of the gathering is answered by v. 24: the march of the swift and the lagging into one plain.

**Application: Living Under the Audit and the Bit**

Three rays close the unit. (1) *For the ambitious*: the *ḥashr* relativizes every ranking won in this life's crooked tables — "the day whose length is fifty thousand years" (70:4) is not survived by the fast runners of a single season; and "the disbelievers see what they are promised as far off, but the believers see it as near" (70:6-7), so the *muqaddim* of the sūrah is the man who lives at a near-Hour's tempo, unhurried and un-lagging. (2) *For the sluggish*: foreknowledge is not predestination to ruin; the Wise-Knowing pair means God's knowledge encloses even the *muʾakhkhar* who turns — "except those who repent, believe, and do righteous deeds — perhaps God will restore them to Himself; and God is Forgiving, Merciful" (the verse's own twin, 25:70: "whoever repents and believes and does righteous deeds — God will change his evil deeds into good deeds; and God is Forgiving, Merciful"). (3) *For the community*: a *jamʿ* that gathers all — advance and lagging, martyr and tax-collector, master and slave, the family of Lot saved whole and the wife who lagged separated (vv. 59-60) — teaches that the final sorting runs on obedience, not biography. This is why the pious law of the row in prayer (v. 24's *ṣuff*) is not decoration: the believer rehearses the *ḥashr* five times a day — feet aligned, gaps closed, ranks perfected — so that the gathering of verse 25 will find him already standing where he should. "So hurry to forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden as wide as the heavens and the earth, prepared for the reverent" (3:133) is the sūrah's own sequel to this verse; the clock and the court are one, and their Keeper is described, here, by two names only — Wise, Knowing — which is the last thing a frightened heart needs and the first thing a presumptuous one must hear.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:26

> **And We indeed created man from dried clay, made of molded mud,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns from Cosmos to Clay**

From the treasuries, winds, and inherited earth (vv. 21-23) the sūrah now zooms to the material of the one who inherits: *Wa laqad khalaqnā'l-insāna min ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn* — "and We created man from sounding clay, from mud molded into shape." The verb is *khalaqa*, not the *jaʿala* of the earlier verses: measuring, fashioning, creating with proportion — the same verb that will describe Iblīs's boast and, in v. 28, God's own announcement, "I am *creating* (khāliqu) a human being." The sūrah has placed the anthropology of man at the center of the chapter between its cosmology and its history; and it places it twice over, here as the general creation of *al-insān* and, in vv. 28ff., as the particular creation of Adam — the two being one event in the sūrah's eyes, since "We created man" speaks of the species by naming its first clay.

**Ṣalṣāl: The Clay That Rang**

*Ṣalṣāl* is onomatopoeia elevated to doctrine: al-Ṭabarī transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās and the Salaf, "clay that became dry so that it *ṣaṣṣala* — made a sound," like the *ṣalṣala* of pebbles in a vessel, or the ring of fired pottery when struck (cf. 55:14, "He created man like the pottery, like clay"; the *fanāʾ* of Kiln, in Arab speech, is *ṣalṣāl*). The image is *vitrified* mud: not wet earth, but earth dried to the point of ringing — a substance that has passed from softness to brittleness, from malleability to a state that no longer bends. Al-Rāzī's spiritual gloss follows the physics: the *ṣalṣāl* stage is the moment when the material is fixed, and it is fixed *before* the Spirit is breathed in — the sūrah's order teaches that what gives you the ring is not the clay but the breath, and the clay's part is only to *resonate*, which is what the Arabic ear hears in *ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn*: even the consonants of the word sound the striking of pottery.

**Ḥamaʾ Masnūn: Dark Mud, Poured Into Shape**

*Ḥamaʾ* is mud — specifically, per the lexicons cited by al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, *black* mud, dark earth; and *masnūn* has two great readings, each enriching the verse. (a) From *s-n-n*, "poured, cast": shaped mud, or mud poured into a mold — Ibn Zayd's gloss the source alludes to: "shaped and fashioned," as metal is poured into its form, so that *masnūn* is "given a figure." (b) From the older usage, *sanā* the meat "aged, changed": al-Bukhārī's *Ṣaḥīḥ* chapter title in *Badʾ al-Khalq* runs "the *ḥamaʾ masnūn* is the aged, changed mud," and Ibn Qutayba explains *sanā* as "changed, spoiled" — the blackness of long-settled silt. The exegetes happily combine: clay kneaded with water (the *ṭīn* of 6:2 and 23:12), left until it darkens and changes, then dries until it rings, then — in some readings — shaped. The sequence is itself a small theology of the stages the human paste went through, matching 22:5, "O people, if you are in doubt about the Resurrection — We created you from dust, then from a drop, then from a clot, then from a lump of flesh," and 23:12-14, the *aṭwār*, the stages: "a drop in a firm lodging, then We made the drop a clot, then the clot a lump, then the lump bones, then We clothed the bones with flesh, then We brought him forth another creation — blessed be God, the Best of creators." Al-Zamakhsharī's comment on our verse: the elaborate staging — dust, mud, blackness, drying, molding, firing — is not chemistry; it is *taʿjīb*, astonishment-production: every one of these names, taken from ordinary artisanal Arabic, is meant to make the listener ask how so many opposites were married into one ringing vessel — soft and hard, dark and bright, mud and spirit — a *khalq jadīd*, "another creation."

**Why the Qur'an Insists on the Low Material**

The sūrah's auditors were proud by lineage; the answer is that their clay is the same clay. Against the Quraysh's genealogical aristocracy — Abū Lahab's "who is this that the tribes of Fihr are commanded to follow?" — Ibn Kathīr notes that God opened the story of the human with the material of *khubz*, bread — the stuff of every household: "you were created from the lowliest of earths so that pride finds no footing in you." And against the Iblīs theology about to be dramatized (vv. 28-44), v. 26 is the exhibit: fire glows, rises, and scorches; clay is cold, lies low, nourishes, and, when dried, *receives a soul*. The entire fall of the next twelve verses turns on an evaluation of these two origins, and this verse has already, before the argument begins, stated the divine verdict: the low material was not an obstacle to dignity but the *condition* of it — a vessel that must be empty, shaped, and humble before the Breath. Ibn Taymiyyah, in his moral epistles, uses precisely this pairing against the *falāsifa*'s elevation of the intellect: the human's rank is not in his substrate — every man shares the *ḥamaʾ* — but in what God *adds*: the Spirit, the commands, the teaching of the names (2:31); matter confers no nobility, and only divine address does, which is why the angels were commanded to prostrate to the clay and not to anything that had been made of better stuff.

**Application: The Self-Knowledge the Verse Prescribes**

The Prophetic sunna took the verse as a daily medicine against two diseases it cures — arrogance and self-contempt. From Abū Hurayra: "God does not look at your bodies or your appearances, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds" (Muslim 2564) — the ring of the *ṣalṣāl* is what is seen, but the Heart-Knower sees the Breath. And the sūrah itself pairs the origin-verse with the destiny-verses around it: you are *ḥamaʾ* (v. 26), you are gathered (v. 25), you are *known* in your advancing and lagging (v. 24), and you will be "proportioned" by an address (v. 29) — so neither despair of the mud nor trust in it; know the material, then look to what was breathed into it. ʿAlī's famous counsel to the proud genealogist belongs to the same surgery: "People are two: a brother in faith, or a creature in creation" — from the mud, all one kin; by the Spirit, all one address; and the whole surah's history from Iblīs to Thamūd is the catalogue of what happens when the first half of that sentence is forgotten.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:27

> **and the jinn We created earlier from scorching fire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Species in the Room**

*Wa'l-jānna khalaqnāhu min qablu min nāri's-samūmin min qablu* — the clause is parenthetical in form but central in function: before the sūrah lets Iblīs speak, it tells us what he is made of. *Al-Jānn* here is the father of the jinn (the source, with the bulk of the commentators, reads *al-jānn* as the progenitor or genus, and 37:158 testifies to the pre-Islamic belief in a kinship of jinn and God), created *min qablu*, "before" — before man, the chronology that will matter in v. 39's quarrel, and, some read, before the *drying* stages of v. 26, so that the jinn are the older workmanship. The pairing of the two creation-verses is therefore *comparative anatomy*: clay versus fire, and the moral history of the world will be an argument between those two materials — one that hardens into a shape when poured and fired, one that never holds a shape at all.

**Samūm: The Fire That Pierces**

*Nāru's-samūm* — the lexicons (al-Ṭabarī's and al-Zamakhsharī's both) transmit the variants: the *samūm* is the hot wind that pierces (its *s-m-m* root is piercing — *samma al-rīḥu*, the wind pierced the skin), the midday heat, or the flame whose heat reaches the vital parts. So the phrase is not "a fire" but "a *puncturing* fire": heat that enters, scorches from within, consumes without remaining (Ṭs: "a fire of pure flame without smoke," others "the smokeless flame," cf. 55:15, *mārijin min nār*, "a flame of fire without smoke"). The sūrah gives the jinn a nature matched to their conduct: just as their father's crime in vv. 31-33 is an act of *hot* contempt, and his war in v. 39 will be a *piercing* campaign ("I will sit for them on Thy straight path, from before them and behind them…"), so his stuff is the element that pierces. Al-Rāzī observes that the Qur'an never says fire was *shaped* (*masnūn*): the jinn were created *from* fire, the man was created *as* clay *and* breathed into — the asymmetry is the whole argument of the Iblīs scene: Iblīs mistakes the brilliance of his material for a superiority of form, while the human's low material will receive a divine form ("I *proportioned* him," v. 29).

**What the Jinn Are — the Verse's Theology**

From this verse and its twins (55:15; 51:56; 72:1-13) the creedal outline is fixed: (1) the jinn are *another created kind*, not gods, not God's kin — the sūrah's immediate refutation of 37:158, "they made a kinship between Him and the jinn, but the jinn know they are brought up"; (2) they are *moral agents* like men — "I created jinn and mankind only to worship Me" (51:56) — with their own messengers among them (6:130; 46:29-32, the band of jinn who heard the Qur'an and *believed*, the whole of Sūrat al-Jinn, and the sūrah's introduction to ch. 72 as cited in our source for vv. 17-18); (3) they *share the test and the judgment*: 55:31 addresses "thāqalān," the two weighty species — "We will attend to you, O two orders"; (4) they can *hear* but not *know* the unseen — v. 17's guarded heaven and v. 18's scorched eavesdropper make the epistemic ceiling explicit, and 72:26-27 closes it: "Knower of the unseen — He reveals not to anyone of His unseen, except to a messenger He has chosen, for He sends forth watchful guards from before and behind him." This is why the sūrah can mention the jinn *once* here and never soften its later verdicts: the unseen-world beings are not sources of information but fellow defendants — which is the sharpest anti-soothsayer theology in the Qur'an, and it sits exactly one verse before Iblīs takes the stand.

**The Authentic Frame: Light, Flame, Dust**

The Prophet ﷺ summed the three kinds of intelligent creation in one sentence the Muslims have never stopped memorizing: "The angels were created from light, the jinn were created from a flame of fire (*mārijin min nār*), and Ādam was given his description" — i.e., from the clay — (Muslim 2996, from ʿĀʾisha). The hadith matters here for its *sequence* — light (the servants who do not disobey, 66:6), flame (the beings whose nature is to rise and sting), clay (the creature who can be shaped, fall, and repent) — the moral ladder running *opposite* to the material ladder: fire glitters, but the clay is the one God breathes into, teaches the names (2:31), and makes His vicegerent (2:30). Ibn Kathīr, on our verse, adds the tradition that the jinn preceded man by a long ages — some transmissions say *two thousand years* before, cited by the historians from Ibn ʿAbbās — and that the mischief for which the disorder attributed to the jinn before Adam — the *fasād* and bloodshedding the angels mention in 2:30, explained in the Salaf's reports as the jinn's pre-Adamic history — is why "the jinn were created *before*" is not idle: the old disorder predates the new appointment, and Iblīs's fall is the elder species losing, by its own hot arithmetic, the contest it had been running for eons. And in the prophetic sunna the jinn remain *in* the human world: "there is a companion (*qarīn*) appointed for each of you" — jinn and angel — and when the Prophet ﷺ said, "There is none of you but has a companion (*qarīn*) among the jinn appointed to him; and among the angels appointed to him" — they said, "not even you, Messenger of God?" He said, "not even me, except that God helped me against it and it only commands me to good" (Muslim 2812-2813), the duʿāʾ of entering the lavatory — "O God, I seek refuge in Thee from the male and female devils" (al-Bukhārī 142; Muslim 375) — and the report of the devil running in the son of Adam "as blood runs in the veins" (al-Bukhārī 2038; Muslim 2174): the jinn are near, tested, dangerous, defeated, and — in the verse's grammar — *created*: a being with a beginning is a being with a Lord, and nothing that *began in flame* deserves worship.

**Application: Two Materials, One Question**

The verse asks its reader to locate himself in the pair: fire or clay? The temperament of *samūm* — the piercing, quick-tempered, shapeless pride that scorches everything it touches — is a *moral* category every human can fall into, and the jinn's story is its portrait; the temperament of *ḥamaʾ masnūn* — slow, dark, lowly, but *pourable into a form that will one day ring* — is the portrait of the believer. The jinn were created first, and man was made the qibla of the angels' homage: precedence in time is not rank in value — the lesson that every genealogist and every elder sibling must relearn. And since the jinn share the test, the sūrah's ethics extend sideways: the "family of those who provide not" (v. 20) and the neighbors of the unseen are part of the one household of *ʿibāda*; the enmity is only toward "the devils from among them" — and toward the same temperament whenever it burns in one's own chest. "The jinn were created before" — and will answer, as we will: "That Day We shall surround hell with of the disbelievers one by one" (19:68, *kulluhum* — both species, one line at the seven gates, v. 44).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:28

> **And [remember] when thy Lord said unto the angels, "Behold! I am creating a human being from dried clay, made of molded mud;"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Announcement: Why He Speaks Before He Makes**

*Wa-idh rabbuka li'l-malāʾikati innī khāliqu basharan min ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn* — "and when thy Lord said to the angels: I am about to create a human being from dried clay." The scene is God's *declaration*, not His act: the creating has not yet happened, and the announcement precedes it — the one thing the verse shares with 2:30's "I am placing upon the earth a vicegerent" and 38:71's "I am creating a human being with My Two Hands." The *wa-udhkur* ("remember") of the sūrah's *idh*-clauses frames it as a lesson to be recalled in Makkah's present: as God told the angels what He was about to do with the clay, He now tells the mockers what He has done with the Reminder (v. 9) and what He will do with the mockers (v. 95). Al-Ṭabarī opens his discussion here with the theological commonplace that God's speech to the angels is real speech, "of the attribute of His Essence," and that the announcement is *taʿlīm* for the angels and for every reciter: the divine pedagogy begins by informing the *obedient* before executing, so that they, at least, know the *why* of what they will be commanded to do (prostrate) and why they will never dispute it, while the *disobedient* (Iblīs, listening in) will get the same information and reveal, by their reaction, what was already latent in them. The creation of man was thus, before anything else, the *examination* of the intelligent world, announced aloud: vv. 16-18's guarded heaven, v. 26's clay, v. 27's flame — the sūrah has been seating the examinees.

**"A Human Being (Bashar)" — the Word Says More Than "Man"**

*Bashar* — and not *insān* here, and not yet *Adam*. The commentators (al-Rāzī; al-Zamakhsharī) note the skin-root of *bashar* — that which has outer skin, the fleshy, the *apparent*, and, in some lexicons, "one who *bushira* with good and evil," one who seeks the *bishāra*. The choice does work: God says "a *skin-bearing* being" precisely where He will say "and I breathed into him of My Spirit" — the two halves of the human contract in one sentence: the low, visible, tactile *bashar* and the hidden, honored *rūḥ*. And since the sūrah's *insān* in v. 26 was generic and the *bashar* here is the individual Adam while also representing the species (as the source notes, citing 7:11c), the two words are a diptych of origin and nature: *insān* from *uns*, the taming and kinship that makes man *familiar* to himself and others (and *nisyān*, forgetfulness, which is his other name — as in "We have created for Hell many of the jinn and men: they have hearts with which they understand not" (7:179) — the *nisyān*-fate of the God-forgetter); *bashar* from the skin, the body's claim. Name him and you have described his war: the forgetting, familiar flesh.

**The Address to the Angels: A Court of Witnesses**

*Li'l-malāʾikata* — to the angels. The source commentary stresses that this announcement is the scene of 2:30 (the *khalīfa*, the question, the teaching of the names), 38:71-72 (here, *biyadayya* — "with My Two Hands," the honor of direct fashioning cited by every mufassir as the ground of the prostration), and 17:61 and 7:11 — and adds that "the human being or vicegerent refers to Adam but also represents humanity as such." Why the angels are addressed rather than left to observe: (a) their prostration is a *command*, and commands in the divine court are given as announcements, so the prostration in v. 29 is a *response* to an *address* — the angels bow to God, at Adam, because God said so, which is the sūrah's own definition of worship: *obedience with an understanding of the why*, in contrast to Iblīs's "worship" of his own arithmetic; (b) the announcement makes the angels and *all the later rational creation* witnesses that the dignity of Adam was *conferred*, not earned: the clay had no prior claim, so every honor that comes to man afterwards is *gift* — the grammar of "minnatan," grace, which is why the sūrah will say to the saved family of Lot, "a bounty from Us, and a favor — thus We recompense him who is thankful" (the Lord's own gloss on Lot's rescue: "a favor from Us — thus We reward whoever is grateful," 54:35), and why it says to the mockers, "your Lord's provision is better and more lasting" (v. 87's sequel).

**The Decree Structure — and the Question of "Consultation"**

Classical theology paused on *innī khāliqu*: does the announcement imply a divine "consultation," an *istishār*? Ibn Kathīr's answer, citing the *salaf*: the angels did not object but *inquired*, as 2:30's "will You place in it one who will shed blood" shows; al-Jubbāʾī's Muʿtazilī claim — that the angels' question was a *rebuke* God accepted — was rejected by the Sunnī majority, since the angels do not command, and their speech in 2:30 was an *istiʿrāḍ*, a seeking-to-understand, followed immediately by submission: "Glory be to Thee, we have no knowledge except what Thou hast taught us" (2:32). This is the sūrah's quiet model for all *lower intellects* before the divine decree: the angels' sequence — question, doxology, submission, prostration — is the etiquette of the creature; Iblīs's sequence — silence, then *qiyās*, then refusal, then blame — is the anatomy of the apostate. The same contrast returns in the sūrah's own last narrative beat, when the saved family of Lot "go forth" without a word of question (v. 65), and when Abraham, told of the destruction, does not argue about the decree but *negotiates for its mercy* (vv. 51-56): the three manners of the three types of soul before the divine announcement: submit, ask-mercy, or refuse.

**For the Reader: Announced Honors, Producible Nothing**

The verse's practical light falls on the "announcement-first" economy of grace. God tells what He will do before He does it, and the announcement outruns every qualification: Adam had no virtue at the moment of the address; he was not yet. The honor is therefore entirely *ibtidāʾ*, initiative — which is why the believer's gratitude here has no ceiling: the whole created order, angels included, was *informed* of his making before he himself existed, and the only rank Iblīs could point at (older, brighter) was the one that did *not* get the announcement. To know oneself is "a *bashar* from ṣalṣāl" is humility; to know that such a *bashar* was the subject of a divine declaration to the heavens is dignity; to hold both — the sūrah's method — is the balance that its Garden (v. 45) and its Fire (v. 43) are separated by. And for the *dāʿī*, the one who carries this Reminder to the proud: the sūrah has placed the announcement of Adam's honor *after* the catalogue of the mockers (vv. 6-15), i.e., the divine response to a mocking world is not to answer its questions but to *re-state the origin and the destiny*: you come from mud; you are headed to a gathering (v. 25); between the two, bow, and you will be in the Garden of those who discern (vv. 45-48).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:29

> **so when I have proportioned him and breathed into him of My Spirit, fall down before him prostrating.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Divine Verbs Before the Command**

*Fa-idhā sawwaytuhu wa-nafakhtu fīhi min rūḥī fa-waqiʿū lahu sājidīn* — the prostration-command is *hinged* on two prior acts: "when I have proportioned him" (*sawwā*, Form II of *s-w-y*: made him level, symmetrical, a completed measure) and "breathed into him of My Spirit." The order is the whole anthropology: first the *shape*, then the *Breath*; first the vessel, then the ring; first the "most beautiful form" (95:4, "We created man in the best of molds"), then the divine addition. The source commentary on *sawwaytuhu* cites Z: "created in the most perfect manner to receive His Spirit." The *tawīd* of the verb (the intensive form) means the proportioning is not rough-hewing but finishing — the clay of vv. 26-28 has been, by this point, dried, molded, and *leveled* to the exact capacity of the Spirit to be poured. Al-Rāzī: the shaping must be *perfect* before the bestowing, "for the outpouring of a great gift requires a prepared recipient" — the same logic as the sūrah's *ḥifẓ*: heaven is guarded (v. 17) before revelation descends (v. 9), the earth is spread and anchored (v. 19) before the *maʿāsh* is placed in it (v. 20): preparation precedes gift, everywhere, in this book.

**"Of My Spirit" — The Grammar of Honor Without Incarnation**

The phrase *min rūḥī*, "of My Spirit," is the genitive of *tafdīl*, specification and honor — as in "the house of God," "the camel of God" (the she-camel of v. 81! — *nāqatu'llāhi*: the added thing is not a *part* of the Adder but a *sign* of His honor), and the Prophet's own usage, above all the ḥadīth qudsī of the supererogatory: "My servant draws near to Me by supererogatory deeds until I love him, and when I love him I am his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees…" (al-Bukhārī 6502) — the language of *nearness and honor*, not mixture. The source records the split: the majority take "My Spirit" as *the created Spirit* (the *rūḥ* whose reality 17:85 fences: "they ask thee of the Spirit — say, the Spirit is of the command of my Lord, and you have not been given of knowledge except a little"), and some, like the theologians after them, as God's command and power breathed into the clay. Against every reading that would *conflate* the creature with the Creator, Ibn Taymiyyah (in *al-Ḥadīth al-Wāfi* and his fatwas) insists the genitive is one of *ikrām*, not *baʿḍiyya* — "of My Spirit" as "of My creation, My command," exactly as "the blessed *night*" (44:3) is "of My command" though it is created, and the Kaʿba is "My house" though it is stone. The Christian doctrine of incarnation is thus excluded by the *grammar of Arabic possession* the sūrah uses; and the angelic, spiritual world is *admitted* to kinship with man by the same particle: the being with *rūḥ* and clay is a *third* thing, neither angel alone nor jinn alone — which is why the angels prostrate and Iblīs does not: the composite has more to answer for, and more to receive.

**The Breath and the Sneeze — the Salaf's Scene**

The commentators' pictures of the moment come from a sound report: the Prophet ﷺ said that when God breathed the spirit into Adam, Adam sneezed — and the first thing out of the new creature's mouth was *al-ḥamdu li'llāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn*, and God answered *yarḥamuka rabbuka*, "may your Lord have mercy on you" (al-Tirmidhī, from Abū Hurayra). Al-Tirmidhī's wording continues: God then said to him, "Go to that company of the angels, and greet them with *al-salāmu ʿalaykum*," and when he did, they returned it, and God said, "…your greeting to your progeny until the Day of Resurrection, and every sneezing believer is answered with mercy" — the hadith in al-Bukhārī (from Sahl ibn Saʿd, the *tahiyya* of Adam) fixes the same scene: Adam's greeting is "the *tahiyya* of your posterity," and the angels' reply "and upon you be the peace" is the *pattern* for the whole community's greeting (al-Bukhārī 6251). Read inside our sūrah, this is more than lore: the *rūḥ* makes a *speaker*. The mud that was silent through its stages now opens with *praaise*, then with *salām* — and the Garden, its destination, will be greeted with the same word: "Enter them in peace, secure" (v. 46). The verse thus brackets the human's whole career between two *salāms*: the first syllable of his speech and the last sentence of his journey, and the sneeze — the body's small, ungovernable convulsion — is made by the tradition into the hinge of gratitude: the creature whose *first* act is thanks has been told how to *keep* thanking, and how to answer, until the Day.

**"Fall Down Before Him, Prostrating" — the Command That Tests the Whole Court**

*Fa-waqiʿū lahu sājidīn* — "fall prostrate to him." The verb of *wuqūʿ* (falling upon) plus *sujūd* makes the prostration an act of *descent*, the motion of the honored One (v. 26's clay) lifted into the reverence of the *honorer*: to bow is normally the lower to the higher, and here the higher (angels, beings of light who never tire, never sin, never marry, never die) are sent *downward* in obeisance to a *naked* being who will yet hunger and fall. Al-Ṭabarī records the two readings of the direction of the prostration — to Adam, in *taʿẓīm* by God's command (the majority; prostration of *greeting*, as Yaʿqūb's sons fell to Yusuf, "and his parents fell prostrate before him" — 12:100, the source's own cross-reference), or *to God* with Adam as their *qibla* (some Salaf, since prostration to other than God is abrogated in our law) — and the sūrah's syntax tolerates both because the *obedience* is to God in either reading: "the bowing was to Adam as a *command*, and to God as *worship*." The logic is why Iblīs has no ground to stand on: if prostration were about *the object's essence*, fire could argue with clay; but it is about *the Commander's will*, and to *that* there is nothing to argue — a point Iblīs's reply in v. 33 will miss exactly, and which the sūrah will re-teach in v. 40's exception, "save Thy purified servants," the ones who prostrate when told to prostrate, *to whom* ever it be.

**The Prostration That Never Was: Why the Law Changed**

The Sharīʿa of Muḥammad ﷺ keeps the *form* of this verse and seals its *direction*: prostration of obeisance to creatures was lawful for earlier communities — Jacob's sons prostrated to Yusuf (12:100, in Joseph's *sharīʿa*) — and was closed with our Prophet, whose answer to Muʿādh's bowing was decisive: "Do not prostrate to me; had I ordered anyone to prostrate to anyone other than God, I would have ordered the wife to prostrate to her husband" — "it is not fitting that anyone prostrates to anyone other than God" (the reports in Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, and Aḥmad from Anas and Imrān b. Ḥuṣayn; the meaning is in Muslim’s *Salām* chapter). In any case the *ʿibāda* was abrogated, not the *obedience*: "lower thy wing to the believers" (v. 88) remains; *sujūd* for God alone remains). This closes a beautiful circle: Adam's *honor* was the prostration of the angels; the ummah's honor is the prostration *to God* — five times a day the forehead, "nearness" (96:19, "prostrate and draw near"), re-enacts the first scene: *fall down, prostrating*; and the enemy who lost everything by *not* descending is answered each time by the servant's descent (v. 18's meteor has its twin: "when the Qur'an is recited, listen and fall silent — that you may receive mercy," 7:204; and Iblīs "oozes through the son of Adam like blood — so dam him with hunger, the small prayer, and dhikr" — according to the counsel of the Prophet ﷺ, "tighten the pathways of Shayṭān with hunger" (a report transmitted in Musnad Aḥmad, whose meaning stands on the Qur'an's own promise that fasting is restraint, 2:183): the sūrah's anthropology is, in practice, a *sujūd*-centric life: clay raised by Breath, proved by prostration, and guarded against the only crime that ever mattered at the first.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:30

> **Thereupon the angels prostrated, all of them together,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whole Court Rises to Obey — the Sūrah's Silent Argument**

*Fa-sajada al-malāʾikatu kulluhum ajmaʿūn* — "then the angels prostrated, all of them, all together." Two words of total emphasis (*kulluhum ajmaʿūn*) do more than describe a scene: they settle its jurisprudence. The command was given to a class, not to individuals, and the response was unanimous, instantaneous, and complete — the angels "do not grow weary of obedience, nor do they tires" (cf. 21:19-20, "They glorify [Him] night and day, never flagging"; 39:75, "and you see the angels surrounding the Throne, glorifying with the praise of their Lord"). The source commentary notes the deeper ruling of the prostration: it was *to* Adam in honor, *for* God in worship — either the angels bowed to the man as their *qibla* of obeisance, or they bowed to God with Adam as the direction — and either reading is a fence against idolatry: a prostration that is an act of obedience to God's command can be directed toward a creature's honor without deifying him, because the *intent* is servitude to the Commander. This is precisely why the sharīʿa of Muḥammad ﷺ closed the gesture while keeping the principle: the people were shown that prostration to an honored one was once an act of obedience, and were then told, "do not prostrate to me" — the *form* abrogated, the *lesson* universalized: honor belongs to whom God honors, worship to God alone.

**"Thereupon" — the Swiftness of the Good**

The *fa* of sequence (*fa-sajadū*) makes the prostration immediate: no caucus, no deliberation, no "let us consider the wisdom first." The swiftness is the sūrah's reproach and its school. Where Iblīs paused to *derive* — fire versus clay — the angels *moved*. Al-Zamakhsharī delights in the scene's rhetoric: the most knowing of creation (the angels) obeyed faster than the least (the man), though the man is the being to whom *knowledge of the names* was given (2:31); obedience, in other words, is not the wage of rank but of orientation. The same *fa* of promptness returns in this sūrah's other saved ones: "So Lot set out with his family in a part of the night" (v. 65, *fa-atbiʿ* — "and follow behind them," no turning back, no dawdling); while the mockers of Makkah are given the opposite grammar of delay, "leave them to eat and be beguiled by hopes" (v. 3). Two speeds, two ends — the sūrah is measuring its audience by how fast they move when God says *fall down*. And the Prophet ﷺ gave the community the same virtue in the words of ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd: "which deed is best? He said: prayer at its time; I said: what next? He said: kindness to parents; I said: what next? He said: jiḥād in God's way" (al-Bukhārī 527; Muslim 85) — *timing*, again: swiftness at the appointed moment is the mark of the sincere heart.

**The Unanimity Proves the Command's Fairness**

The verse's most subtle function is evidentiary. When the sūrah stages the trial of Adam's honor, the *whole* of an intelligent, morally responsible creation — the angels — casts its verdict on the plaintiff's side. If the prostration had been *unreasonable*, the angels could have hesitated, as men hesitate; they did not, because the reason was plain: God had spoken. Al-Rāzī, gathering the thread, states the rule: "When all the reasonable ones act on a judgment without dispute, its correctness is established" — the ijmaʿ of the heaven as the model of the ijmaʿ of the ummah. This is also why the next verse isolates Iblīs with such surgical finality: when the only dissent in a unanimous court is *one*, the defect cannot be in the command; it is in the dissenter — the same logic the sūrah applies to the mockers of v. 6: a message the whole sane world of revelation receives, that one city alone spurns, indicts the city and not the message.

**A Prostration Worth Imitating**

The Companions asked the Prophet ﷺ about the verse's echo in their worship — the two prostrations of gratitude (the *sujūd al-shukr*, of which the reports in Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah from Abū Bakra, al-Mughīra, and others, say the Prophet ﷺ would prostrate when news of mercy came to him or a threat was averted, and say in prostration: "O God, You made me prostrate — so accept my prostration," the wording in Aḥmad). The gesture of the angels was a *sujūd* to a man in honor of God; the ummah's *sujūd al-shukr* is a prostration to God in honor of what He has done — the direction of reverence always repaired to its Owner. And the sūrah will close its own argument with the same posture: "So hymn the praise of thy Lord, and be among those who prostrate" (v. 98) — the Makkan asked to produce a sign of proof is answered: the proof was decreed to prostrate; now *you* prostrate, and you will find the court of heaven already in the row with you, *kulluhum ajmaʿūn*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:31

> **save Iblīs. He refused to be with those who prostrated.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Convicts**

*Illa Iblīs — aba an yakūna mina's-sājidīn*. In Arabic grammar the exception from an emphasized whole (*kulluhum ajmaʿūn*) is called *istinthaʾ munqiṭiʿ*-like and rhetorically *iqṭiʿ*-like: a single thread pulled out of the cloth to show what the cloth was made of. The sūrah's choice is surgical: not "he did not prostrate" but *aba*, "he *refused*" — the verb of obstinacy, used in the Qur'an for those who can but will not (7:11, "he refused and waxed proud, and was of the disbelievers"; 2:34). And not "he refused to prostrate" but "to *be with* (an yakūna min) those who prostrated" — a phrase the commentators (Ibn ʿAbbās in Ṭabarsī; al-Qurṭubī after him) note is deeper than the bare act: his sin was *separating himself from the congregation of the obedient*, pride of standing apart. To be "among" the prostrators was the whole of righteousness in that moment; he wanted to be *superior to* them more than he wanted to obey God. This is the first *firaq* — the first schism — in the history of moral creatures, and every later "I am not one with them" in this sūrah (the mockers who will not sit with the believers, the gate-people of v. 44 sorted by their parties) replays it.

**Why Is Iblīs in the Angelic Court? — the Question the Verse Forces**

The source states the classical debate: "that the angels prostrated… save Iblīs suggests that Iblīs was among the angels, although in 18:50 he is said to be *of the jinn*." The solutions the tradition offered are three, and the sūrah itself adjudicates: (a) he was an angel by nature and disobedient — rejected, because the Qur'an elsewhere defines angels as "who cry not above Him, nor do they grow weary" (21:19-20) and "over the gates — angels thereof, mighty, severe, who disobey God's command not" (66:6), so a disobedient being cannot be an angel in the Qur'anic sense; (b) he was a *jinn* who had been *raised* into the company of the angels by his worship — the famous reading of 18:50, "he was of the jinn, so he transgressed his Lord's command," and the reports in Ṭabarī that Iblīs ("who was called ʿAzāzīl among the angels" — a name transmitted without a sound chain, as Ibn Kathīr cautions) had been exalted for his devotion until pride revealed what the clay test revealed; (c) "angel" is used loosely of any of the luminous heavenly company. The sūrah's own verdicts are decisive and, on reading, kind: *he was of the jinn* — meaning moral capability, not nature, decided him; and *he refused* — meaning the act was chosen; and, in v. 34, he is *expelled* — meaning his presence among the angels was by permission of worship, not by essence, and the permission was his chance, not his pedigree. Ibn Taymiyyah compresses the point: "The angels are sinless of what they are charged; the jinn and men may disobey — so Iblīs's presence proves not that he was an angel but that worship elevates even him, until the heart bares its own bottom" (*al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn* in spirit, and his *Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā* repeatedly marshals 18:50 against the angelic-nature reading).

**The Name: *Iblīs*, the Despairer**

The source preserves the two etymologies: from *ablas*, "to be dumbfounded, struck despairing" — the root also gives *balisa*, "to be dumbfounded," and *ablasa*, "to despair," near-synonymous in the lexicon with the *yāʾisa* of "despair not of God's breath" (12:87); and the Greek *diabolos*, "slanderer," which had entered Arabic before the Qur'an. Either way the name does the theology: he is *the one who, when God's mercy opened toward the clay, despaired* — despair being, for the Qur'an, the true origin of arrogance (Abū al-Ḥusayn's gloss cited by al-Rāzī: "he despaired of mercy, so he became a seeker of vengeance rather than of forgiveness"). Which is the sūrah's own proof-text, three verses later: "Who despairs of the Mercy of his Lord, save those who are astray?" (v. 56) — Abraham, the man made from the same clay, states the rule from which Iblīs is the sole eternal exception. Pride and despair are one disease at its two moments: Iblīs thought he was too good to bow; when the door slammed, he was too hopeless to knock.

**The Hadith of the Refusal**

The Prophet ﷺ gave the community the moral of the verse in its sharpest single sentence: "God is gentle, loves gentleness, and gives for gentleness what He gives not for harshness" (Muslim 2593) — while the *harshness* of Iblīs's "I am better than him" (7:12) made him the archetype of the opposite court. And on the very word *aba*, the tradition's most cited ḥadīth on *kibr*: "No one who has a mustard-seed of arrogance in his heart enters Paradise; and no one who has a mustard-seed of faith in his heart enters the Fire. Arrogance is rejecting the truth and scorning people" (Muslim 91; Abū Dāwūd 4091 — and observe that Iblīs committed both halves: he *rejected* the command as truth, and *scorned* the clay-man). The commentators note the sūrah's economy: the entire second act of verse 31 is *one* past tense — *aba*, "he refused" — the grammar of a decision that *is still a decision*: his descendants ("his brood and his followers," "will you then take him and his progeny as allies besides Me, though they are enemies to you?" (18:50-51 — the sūrah's own sequel to the story)) inherit the *aba* until they, like Pharaoh, say "I am your lord, the most high" (79:23) and are "overtaken by the punishment of the Day" (20:39 — actually "so We overtook him…," 79:24-25).

**A Verse for the One Who Stands Apart**

Finally, the sūrah reads to its Makkan audience like a diagnosis of v. 6: Quraysh "refused to be with those who believed" — the same *aba*, the same separateness, the same honor-arithmetic that finds prayer-with-slaves-and-orphans beneath dignity (the chiefs' demand to expel the poor believers — "shall we believe you, followed only by the lowest of them?" (26:111) — and the command that answered it: "be not impatient with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, desiring His face; the reckoning of such people falls on no one else, so you would drive them away and be unjust" (6:52; cf. 18:28)). The verse tells every community: the court's unanimity is the evidence — when all the good prostrate and one man will not "be among them," he has not reasoned past the truth; he has refused to *be with* it. And the exception, here as in the sūrah's saved families, has two kinds: the exception who *repents and returns* — the ummah's door — and the exception who *demands a delay to finish his spite* — which is the next verse of our sūrah (v. 36), and the last word of Iblīs: not "forgive me" but "let me."

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:32

> **He said, "O Iblīs! What ails you that you are not with those who prostrate?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Asks — Not for Information, but for the Record**

*Mā laka allā takūna mina's-sājidīn* — "what is the matter with you, that you are not among those who prostrate?" The sūrah's most quietly astonishing line: the All-Knowing puts a *question* to the accused. The commentators (al-Ṭabarsī; al-Qurṭubī; Ibn Kathīr citing "the men of insight") fix the function: it is *iḥjāj*, the procedure of establishing proof — God who needs no answer grants the defendant a podium so that the verdict shall carry the weight of the excuse *offered*, not merely the crime committed. The same divine etiquette runs through the Qur'an's court-scenes: "Did we not raise you among us as a child, and you stayed of your years among us?" (26:18), "did We not give you a long life so that whoever would reflect could reflect therein — and the warner came to you?" (35:37), "how can you disbelieve in God — and you were dead, and He gave you life?" (2:28) — and in our sūrah, v. 33's answer does the rest of the case. The question is thus not a gap in God's knowledge but an expansion of the sinner's responsibility: what he says *becomes* the record, and the record becomes the evidence against him. To make a criminal speak is the deepest justice: he is convicted by his own voice, not by the Judge's omniscience alone. And for the reader, the verse teaches the ethics of interrogation: the right to answer is built into divine procedure; no ḥadd and no *taʿzīr* in the sunna's courts was ever held valid where the accused was not heard.

**"What Ails You" — the Wording Names the Disease, Not the Man**

The phrase *mā laka* — "what is the matter with you" — is Arabic for *something has changed you*, not *something is you*. The question locates the fault as an affliction ("what has altered you from the state in which you stood with the prostrators?") — the same idiom as Mary's people's question, "O sister, no sister of yours was ever like this" (19:28), and the Qur'an's general rhetoric toward the sinner's *state* rather than his *essence*. Iblīs had been — the reports in Ṭabarī preserve this reading of Ibn ʿAbbās and others — *elevated with the angels* for his worship; so the question is precisely historical: *what changed you?* The wording thus embeds the sūrah's theology of moral catastrophe — no intelligent being falls except through a *conversion* of will, and the question invites the confession of that conversion. And when Iblīs answers (v. 33) with a *comparison* — clay versus fire — he answers the wrong question: *mā laka* asks what ails you; he replies "what is he." The sūrah lets the mismatch stand as evidence: the sick man defending his sickness by insulting the healthy. Al-Rāzī, with al-Zamakhsharī before him, both mark the rhetorical point: God "asked him not from ignorance but to expose the excuse's ugliness after he himself pronounced it."

**The Echo of 38:75 — the Command's Honor in the Question**

In Ṣād, the same question comes with the addition that makes the trial complete: *mā manaʿaka an tasjuda li-mā khalaqtu bi-yadayya* — "what prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My two hands?" (38:75). Our sūrah keeps the plain form; Ṣād keeps the emphatic, and the two are one event told twice (the "bi-yadayya," as the Salaf unanimously insisted — Ibn Taymiyyah devoting a long section against the philosophers and the anthropomorphists both — is "an attribute befitting His majesty without likeness to creatures," its meaning in the *honor* of the direct workmanship, not the *craft* of hands). The cross-reference does real work for v. 32: the *question itself* contains the counter-evidence — "I created *him* with My two hands; you, I made from fire, and *you* — what prevented you from prostrating to what *I* fashioned?" The being to whom God attributes *hands* cannot be an accident of clay; and the being who sneers at him is answering a question whose wording already condemns him. Ibn Kathīr's summary of the two versions: "In this is the demonstration that the fault of Iblīs was arrogance against God and against His honored creature, and that the divine question stands to every mocker's dare: *mā laka* — what is the matter with you — that you do not do what even the clay deserved?"

**The Etiquette of Argument, Extracted by the Scholars**

Because the verse is the *opening question* of the sūrah's longest disputation, the adab-literature treated it as a rule-book. Its extracted maxims: (a) *start the exchange with the question, not the verdict* — God did not say "you are proud," He asked what ails him; the accused arrives at his own confession; (b) *address the defect, not the person's annihilation* — he is still called by name and spoken to with a form of inquiry, even as he falls; the sūrah will never again speak to him directly (vv. 34-38 are the verdict and his counter-demands, and then the file closes, v. 40, "say, this is your path and My servants — you have no authority over them" 15:42-43 — the *dialogue with God* ends, and Iblīs is left to the quarrel he chose: with *Adam*); (c) *let the excuse be recorded before the sentence is executed* — v. 34 (expulsion) follows v. 33 (the excuse) exactly in order. Fakhr al-Dīn sees here "the whole fiqh of disputation: premise-claim (v. 33), divine proof (v. 32 with 38:75), and verdict (v. 34) — with the sentence deferred by the very exchange." These are not ornament: the sūrah argues by *how it argues* — the form of verse 32 is itself the first lesson of verse 32.

**Why Every Preacher Should Read This Verse Before verse 33**

The verse also disciplines God's spokespeople. When Quraysh spat on the Prophet ﷺ's back (the event of v. 11's mockery), when they called him *majnūn* (v. 6), the sūrah did not license a rebuttal in kind; it *put the question*: "do you not then reason?" (2:44), and "what is the matter with you — how do you judge?" (68:36) — the very *mā lakum* of v. 32, pointed at a whole nation. The *mā laka* form is the Qur'an's standing gesture of interrogation toward the mocker: make him say it aloud. Ibn al-Qayyim, whose *al-Ṭuruq al-ḥukmiyya* legislates on this principle for the Islamic court, cites the scene: "the imams did not punish the speaker before the interrogated question, because the divine Sunna is that *mā laka* before *ukhruj*." The whole verse, then, is the sūrah's *constitution of dialogue*: ask before verdict, hear before sentence, and let the excuse itself prosecute the heart. And the man whose excuse is a comparison of elements — "I am better than him" (7:12) — will teach us, in v. 33, what every argument of bigotry sounds like when God makes it *public*: not a syllogism, a confession.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:33

> **He said, "I am not one to prostrate to a human being whom Thou hast created from dried clay, made of molded mud."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer That Is a Confession**

*qāla laysa lī an asjuda li-basharin khalaqtahu min ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn* — "I am not one who should prostrate to a human You created from dried clay of molded mud." Al-Ṭabarī opens with the grammarians on *laysa lī*: "it is not for me, nor befits me" — a construction that places the prostration *beneath* his dignity, as one says "it is not for me to sit with the lowly," confessing the very disease he claims to be too pure to catch. And observe the theft at the heart of the sentence: he quotes God's own description — *min ṣalṣālin min ḥamaʾin masnūn*, verbatim from v. 28 — and turns the divine words into a charge. The Creator said "clay" as the material of honor ("I proportioned him and breathed into him," v. 29); the sinner repeats "clay" as if honor were determined by raw stuff. This is the whole science of *tazyīn*, making the false seem fine, in its first laboratory case: the same words, differently weighed, produce a blasphemy — and every later corruption of revelation will follow the pattern: quotation without context as the instrument of refusal.

**The First Qiyās — and the Flaw That Was Never a Flaw of Logic Alone**

The commentators are unanimous that Iblīs *reasoned by analogy*: "I am better than him; You created me from fire and created him from clay" (7:12; 38:76) is the syllogism's full statement, and our verse is its applied form. The transmitted verdict is sharp: "the first who introduced qiyās into religion was Iblīs" — the report from Ibn ʿAbbās carried in ʿAbd al-Razzāq's *Muṣannaf* — and Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim both make *qiyās* without knowledge the Iblīs-type error as such. For the analogy is formally *plausible*: fire is nobler than clay by an ordinary physics (light, heat, ascent, subtlety against weight, darkness, lowness); a natural hierarchy is asserted; and the conclusion *should* follow — if the question had been "what is the more excellent material?" But the question was not that. The command was "prostrate to *what I made with My two hands and breathed into*" (38:72-75) — the relevant variable is divine intention, not elemental pedigree. Iblīs's defect, in the logicians' own idiom, is that he ran his analogy in the wrong court: he measured by *mādda* (matter) what God had measured by *murād* (will). Ibn Taymiyyah generalizes the verse into a law of heresy: every innovation of this kind "is a qiyās of the Iblīs-type — a true premise extended over a ruling to which it does not belong," a physics of merit smuggled into a courtroom of obedience. So the sūrah is not warning against reasoning — it is nothing but argument — but against reasoning on the wrong axis, which is precisely what v. 32's patient question invited him to expose.

**The Theology of Fire and Clay: What the Elements Rehearse**

The source preserves al-Maybudī's luminous gloss (on 5:6): fire rises, scours, and scorches — by nature arrogant; clay is pressed down, soaked, shaped, nourishes — by nature humble; and God chose for the vicegerent, and for the one prostrated-to, the material that *receives*. The argument has a second, ethical half: if nobility were elemental, the All-Wise would not have made the honored one from the lowliest stuff at all — He would simply have made the honored one *from gold*. The lowliness of the material is the stage on which the dignity of obedience is displayed; and when the display is refused, the material is not the crime — the *reading* of it is. This is the Qur'an's standing refutation of every aristocratic arithmetic, stated once and finally: "the most noble of you in the sight of God is the most righteous of you" (49:13); and in the Prophet's sermon at Minā: "no Arab has superiority over a non-Arab, nor a non-Arab over an Arab; no white over a black, nor a black over a white — except by piety" (Aḥmad). The verse then also explains why the trial took the form of *sujūd*: prostration is the voluntary descent of the noble to what he is commanded — so the fire-animal who refuses it is not stating a physics; he is announcing that he, not the Author of rank, is the judge of rank.

**"A Human (Bashar)": The Sneer Inside the Word-Choice**

He says *li-bashar*, not *li-Ādam*: the species-word with its skin-root (v. 28c), uttered as a put-down — "mere flesh." The etiquette of the insolent is here for study: they name the target by his material or his office, so that the sneer itself does the arguing — as Nūḥ's people said of their messenger, "he is only a man possessed; repel him with a word" (54:9), and as the Qur'an reports of Pharaoh's court before Mūsā, "this is nothing but sorcery handed down; why were not bracelets of gold dropped on him, or angels come with him in a procession?" (43:52-53) — belittle the bearer, retire the message. And the irony is structural: the *bashar* whose very skin makes him touchable, eatable, mortal — that being is the one God *proportioned* and *breathed into*, while the being of intangible flame received no breath at all. The Prophet ﷺ sealed the matter in the ḥadīth this sūrah was partly made to expound: "God does not look at your bodies or your appearances; He looks at your hearts and your deeds" (Muslim 2564) — the divine gaze does not audit the material; it audits the *taswiya*, the shaping (v. 29), and the submission inside it. Iblīs read the record of creation backwards and called it an insult; the verse lets him say it aloud, so all the ages can see what the court already saw: the excuse *is* the crime, spoken.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:34

> **He said, "Go forth from it! Surely thou art outcast,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict in Two Words**

*Qāla fakhruj fa-innaka rajīm* — "Go forth! You are outcast." The imperative *ukhruj* is the verb of *exit without return-tickets*: the same verb that executed the fall of Adam and Ḥawwāʾ — "descend" (*ihbiṭū*, 2:36) — where the sentence that expelled them became at once their title and their repentance (2:37); while for Abraham, the same power of expulsion reverses into welcome: "We said, O fire, be coolness and peace for Abraham" (21:69). What is exile to the proud is embrace to the submitted — the sūrah's two-directional grammar of place. The grammar is bare: no deliberation clause, no appeal window; the case v. 32-33 was opened, heard, and is now closed, exactly as the divine procedure promised: *mā laka* before *ukhruj* (see v. 32). And the verdict is not a new sentence; it is an *announcement of his place*: the Qur'an's justice never punishes into a state the sinner has not already chosen. Iblīs made himself foreign to the company of the prostrators (v. 31's *aba an yakūna min*); "go forth from it" states his own separation as residence.

**Rajīm: Cursed, Stoned, and Cast-Out-Three-Ways**

*Fa-innaka rajīm* — the source commentary records the double gloss (Ṭabarsī's collection): "expelled and cursed," and the reading of ʿĀṣim's companion, *marjūm* — "stoned," from *rajm*, pelting. The two are not alternatives but strata: the *rājim* is the outcast *as such* (one driven out with stones, the ancient Arab rite of banishing the madman or the pollutant from the tribe), the *marjūm* is the one *actively pelted* — and the sūrah has just, in v. 18, described the actual pelting: *shihāb ṣāqib*, the piercing flame that follows every eavesdropper who snatches a hearing. So Iblīs is *rajīm* in the same way his soldiers are: their meteorology of fire is his curse made visible, the sky's own verdict enforced each time a devil tries to trade in the unseen. The etymological root, *r-j-m*, also yields *ar-rajm bi'l-ẓann* — "the stoning by guesswork" (a phrase the ʿulamāʾ use, citing 45:32's parallel "they follow nothing but conjecture," *inn yatbaʿūna illā ẓannā*): the outcast is the one whose every word is *ẓann*, conjecture; and Iblīs's entire speech — from "I am better" to the respite-request to his oath of vengeance — is, from v. 34 onward, the testimony of *one whose epistemic status is officially guess*. The sharīʿa drew the practical line from this: *rajm* is the punishment *with stones* for the married adulterer — the penalty the community executes mirrors, in its material, the expulsion the cosmos executes; and the fuqahāʾ add the wisdom (Ibn al-Qayyim's *Iʿlām*, and before him Ibn Taymiyyah's *al-Siyāsa*): stoning *returns the offender to the ground* the way the outcast fell from the sky — the penal system as applied anthropology of vv. 31-34.

**"Go Forth *From It*": The Loss of the One Location**

The pronoun *minhā* — "from it" — is left deliberately open, and the commentators harvest: from the heaven, from the garden, from the *maqām* (station) of the angelic assembly. The single thing all agree on: the loss is *locational* precisely because the whole drama has been about *place* — where one stands relative to the prostrators. Paradise is a *place* because the righteous, too, will be told *udkhulū* — "enter" the gardens (v. 46) — and the sūrah's entire geography of salvation and ruin is this pair: *ukhruj* for the cursed, *udkhulū* for the reverent. The same two verbs frame the two families of the sūrah's endings, and the Qur'an generalizes: "Whoever does righteousness, male or female, being a believer — those shall enter Paradise and are not wronged a speck" (4:124, *yadkhulūna'l-janna*), and "the disbelievers' abode is the Fire" (3:197). Ibn Kathīr, on our verse, brings out the balance of mercy that the expulsion does not erase: the text never says "despair" — it says *ilā yawmi'd-dīn* (v. 35) and *fa-innaka min al-munẓarīn* (v. 37): even the outcast keeps a *term*, and a term is time; and time, until the Day, is a door kept open for *every* intelligent creature except the one who closes it.

**The Ḥadīth of the Mantle: Why This Is the Only Unforgiveable Move**

The source quotes, in its note on vv. 34-35, the ḥadīth qudsī that Makkah's mockers were warned by: "Pride is My mantle and greatness My garment; whoever contests Me for either of them, I shall cast him into the Fire" (Muslim, from Abū Hurayra). The qudsī is the *ʿilla* (effective cause) of *ukhruj* in one image: God's "garments" are the attributes no creature may wear; Iblīs's excuse (v. 33) was, translated, "the nobler material should be prostrated to" — i.e., the *distribution of honor is his to decide* — and that claim *is* the contest for the mantle. The punishment (expulsion, then the curse till the Day) is simply the *exposure* of what wearing it does to a creature: clay cannot wear infinity; it cracks. The ḥadīth's second half — the Fire — arrives here not as added rage but as the only possible *residence* for one who insists on a garment whose size is God. This is why, in this sūrah's theology, no sin is compared to pride: lust repents — in the authentic story a woman of Banū Isrāʾīl who traded her body for bread saw a dog panting at a well's rim, filled her shoe with water for it, and was forgiven for that single mercy (al-Bukhārī and Muslim); theft repents; murder repents (the man who killed ninety-nine — Bukhārī and Muslim); but the refusal to bow, kept to the end, kept its *māna* (meaning) as the one unrepented sin — the one whose confession (v. 33) came as a *defense*. The sūrah's first narrative thus supplies the criterion for all the others: the difference between Ādam's fall and Iblīs's was not the fall; it was the *answer to the question*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:35

> **and surely the curse shall be upon thee till the Day of Judgment!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Curse: What the Word *Laʿna* Removes**

*Wa-inna ʿalayka laʿnatī ilā yawmi'd-dīn* — "and My curse is upon thee until the Day of Recompense." The root *l-ʿ-n* is *removal*: the *malʿūn* is the one *expelled from* the range of mercy's nearness, as the *lāʿin* (curser) is expelled from the etiquette of the community; the lexicons (al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī both open here) gloss it: "banishment and distancing." The verse's weight is not in the naming of a punishment but in the naming of a *relation*: what Iblīs lost is not comfort — he was never owed comfort — but *proximity*; the curse is the formal record of a distance he had already elected in v. 31 ("he refused to be *with* those who prostrated"). The commentators press this symmetry: the *laʿna* is not arbitrary branding; it is the sky's own verdict on the one who found the company of the obedient unbecoming: "then remain where you put yourself." And the possessive *laʿnatī* — "My curse" — as al-Zamakhsharī notes on 38:78's identical phrase, lends the threat its utmost terror in the Qur'an's idiom of direct attribution, precisely as "My hands" (38:75) lent the honor of Adam its utmost elevation: the two verses mirror — "what I made with My two hands, you scorned; so My curse is upon thee" — the closeness he refused becoming the closeness he is told of now, only from the other side.

**"Until the Day of Judgment" — a Limit with Two Readings**

The *ilā* (until) obliges the exegetes, and the source cites the problem: does the curse *end* at the Day? The dominant answers are two, and both serve the sūrah. (a) Al-Ṭabarsī and Ibn Kathīr: the delimiting is about the *period of respite and the scene of contest*, not about the curse's cessation — after the Day the malʿūn enters what the *laʿna* itself only announces: the permanent abode (v. 43's tryst; the "eternal abode" of 4:169, 9:68, "God has cursed them and prepared for them a blazing Fire, therein to abide forever"). The "until" marks where the *warning* stops being address and becomes report: on the Day, the cursing is executed, no longer announced — as the Qur'an's people of the Fire will hear: "and those whom God curses will be cast into it," 33:60-61's idiom, and 38:78's pairing in the very same breath, "till the Day of Recompense," is read by al-Qurṭubī as "its curse *completes* at the Day." (b) Some of the ʿulamāʾ add a subtler reading: the *laʿna* here is the curse of *expulsion from acceptance* — and it is *bounded* in this world precisely because the sinner's fate is not yet fixed until the Day; the text never curses *by name* a living individual to his face in this ummah for exactly this reason (the Prophet ﷺ forbade: "do not invoke the curse of God on your servants" in the qunūt incident — "do not supplicate against your children, do not supplicate against your servants, do not supplicate against your wealth, lest you coincide with a time when God is asked and He answers" — Muslim 2726, from Jābir — the etiquette of *not* executing the laʿna early). So "until the Day of Recompense" is simultaneously a warning of permanence and the sūrah's own rule of restraint: cursing belongs to the Judge; the court of this life pronounces none.

**The Day of *Dīn*: The Word That Ties the Verse to the Whole Sūrah**

*Yawmu'd-dīn* — the same expression as the Fātiḥah's third verse (1:4), and in this sūrah's architecture it carries three threads at once. *Dīn* is *recompense* ("it is recompense — is it that we shall be returned to a low state?" 79:12; "He is not asked about what He does" 21:23), *debt* ("on him is the *dayn* of God," its root in the commercial ledger the sūrah has been keeping since the treasuries of v. 21), and *obedience* (the *dāllūna* of 11:57's "you have come as one who *bids to a dīn*?"). The curse "until the Day of *Dīn*" therefore says: the expulsion runs until the settlement of accounts, the end of the *dayn*, the closure of the *dīn* of testing. The sūrah's mockers live under the same syntax: "soon they will know" (vv. 3, 96) is the same *ilā*; "no community can hasten its term nor delay it" (v. 5) is the same bounded respite; the Day the *mujrimūn* will wish they had been dust is the same Day the curse completes. Reading v. 35 as the sūrah's own law, the "until" is a *mercy-frame*: everything in this sūrah before that word is a chance, and the chance is precisely why the verse is not addressed to Makkah's dead but to Makkah's living.

**The Iblīs of Every Age: Why the Curse Is Cosmological, Not Personal**

The classical ʿulamāʾ turn the verse into a rule for the community in two careful strokes. First: the *laʿna* here is the *state*, not the biography — whatever in creation is the seat of refusing-honor *is* rajīm, "outcast, cursed," and Iblīs is its archetype; so the Qur'an curses *the attitude*, "for the one who wears pride is Iblīs's kinsman whether born of fire or clay," the saying of the Salaf cited by al-Ṭabarsī (and ʿAlī's maxim that the *nafs al-ammāra* — the commanding self — "is the Iblīs of every man" in the ḥadīth literature: 12:53's "the soul is commanding to evil" is the sūrah's sequel). Second: since the curse is *until* the Day, the ummah's courts are not to run the sky's verdict ahead of its time: "do not curse what He has cursed *in a man* — the door is still hinged," in the idiom of the *ḥisba*-writers; enmity is owed to the *deed*, invitation is owed to the *agent*, and no believer is made the executor of the *laʿna* — which is exactly what the sūrah's *own* ethics will show in v. 42 ("you have no authority over My servants") and in the Prophet's treatment of the mocker who spat, whom he entrusted to God's *ḥifẓ*, not his hand (v. 95's "We suffice thee against the mockers" is the community's charter of *not* executing the curse before its time). And thus v. 35, which looks like a hanging of Iblīs, is in practice a *restraining order on the ummah* and a *window on the sinner*: the curse is real, its execution is postponed by divine decree to the Day of Recompense — and the postponement is the whole meaning of the sūrah's *soon*, the same word that will end it, "for soon they will know."

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:36

> **He said, "My Lord! Grant me respite till the Day they are resurrected."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wrong Request at the Right Address**

*Qāla rabbi anzirnī ilā yawmi yubʿathūn* — "My Lord, respite me until the Day they are raised." Two details constitute the verse's whole argument, and the source commentary has already placed them side by side: he says *rabbi* — the address of lordship is intact; and he asks *anzirnī* — the request of delay is chosen, not the request of pardon. Iblīs does not disbelieve in his Lord; he disbelieves in *forgiveness being available to him*, and so he asks for *time* instead of *return*. The commentators make the contrast the point: Ādam and Ḥawwāʾ, deceived by this same enemy, said "our Lord, we have wronged ourselves; and if You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we shall surely be of the lost" (7:23); and Ādam "received *words* from his Lord, and He turned toward him" (2:37) — those words, "the most widely transmitted report in the mufassirūn (via Ibn ʿAbbās) is exactly 7:23." The sūrah sets the twin scenes three verses apart on purpose: one sinner falls and *asks forgiveness*; the other falls and *asks respite*; the one is taught the names, the other is left to his arithmetic; and the sūrah's verdict on the mockers of Makkah hangs on which pair they resemble — because Quraysh, like Iblīs, were not accusing the Prophet ﷺ of teaching falsehood, but of being *beneath them*: the disease is identical, and so the verse warns every reader that the truly catastrophic prayer is "give me time," when it is spoken in order to keep sinning, not to quit.

**"Grant Me Respite" — The Grammar of the Reprieved**

*Anzir* is Form IV of *n-ẓ-r*, "to give delay, to put off," the verb of the *ajal* and the same root that named the angels' *munẓarīn* in v. 8 ("and then you would not be reprieved") — the sūrah has thus already told us the economy in which Iblīs is bargaining: v. 8 was the announcement that no spectacle buys extension of the reprieve; v. 36 is the sinner *asking the reprieve itself*, nakedly, and being answered — which is the terrifying gentleness of God in this book: even the cursed one who asks for time is *given* it (v. 37, "you are among the reprieved"), without the delay being approval, and without the asking being acceptance.
