Al-Rāzī notes the subtlety: Iblīs's wording is a *petition*, not an order — he speaks as a servant, proving the knowledge of lordship, while his intent is revenge, proving the enmity of the will — and the sūrah keeps both in frame because both are true of every mocker: *they too* call God "Lord" in their oaths and their invocations, while prosecuting what He hates. The verse is therefore the sūrah's deepest analysis of irreligion: not the denial of God but the use of God.

**"The Day They Are Raised" — Why Resurrection Specifically**

He did not say "until the Day of Judgment" (the curse's term, v. 35); he chose *yawmi yubʿathūn* — "the Day *they* are raised," and the pronoun, as Ibn Kathīr observes, is the tell: he wants to stand at the resurrection of *them* — the children of Adam, his declared quarry — and to be present at the finish of *their* test. The request is not neutral duration; it is *jurisdiction*: respite so that the war may run its whole field. 7:14 has the identical wording with the same note from al-Ṭabarī, and 38:79 makes the motive explicit in the sequel: "You respite me until the Day of Recompense — so by Thy might, I shall surely mislead them all." The sūrah is teaching that a "more time" asked for revenge is still asked *as* a servant — the language of devotion as camouflage for hostility — and that is precisely why the prophets' mockers answer to this verse: their laughter is a *long game*, a respite-requested-in-order-to-mislead, and the sūrah's answer to Makkah will be vv. 95-96 — "We suffice thee against the mockers." The one who asks for time to destroy God's servants and receives it, receives it as the condemned receive the appeal's length: the term of *their* respite is *the test's* mercy.

**The Ḥadīth of the Asking Hearts: How This Verse Was Read by the Earliest**

The verse's practical lesson settled in the tradition through reports that make the *type of desire* the gauge of the heart. "When God created Paradise, He surrounded it with hardships; and when He created the Fire, He surrounded it with desires" — so the whole question of respite is exactly the question of what a creature does with the wall around its goal (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra). Iblīs asked for the road ringed with desires and called it a mission; the penitent asked for the road ringed with hardships and called it forgiveness. The sūrah's Iblīs, who never sought the face of anything but his own vendetta, is the negative diagram of the whole system: a man may ask for a thousand years and receive them — that is never the measure; the measure is what he does inside them, and the Day will read out (v. 25's *jamʿ*) what the respite was *for*: "your Lord knows best who has strayed from His path, and He knows best those who are rightly guided" (6:117). The knowledge of intent, not the length of the term, is the sūrah's whole answer to every request for more time.

**Application: The Two Prayers of the Sinner**

Last lesson, personal and immediate: when a believer falls, this verse and its Ādam-pair lay before him two templates, and the sūrah expects him to pick the second: not "rabbi anzirnī" — "let me have my term to do what I want," which is Iblīs's prayer (and the hadith warns, "do not supplicate against yourselves, lest you coincide with a time of answer" — Muslim 2726); but "rabbanā ẓalamnā anfusanā" — "we wronged ourselves; forgive," which is the prayer whose words Ādam *received from his Lord* (2:37), i.e., taught by the very One who is asked. The difference is not in the sin (both scenes involve a fall from a command) but in the direction of the gaze: respite-askers look at the clock; pardon-askers look at the Judge. And the sūrah's promise stands behind the second: "whoever does evil or wrongs his soul, then seeks God's forgiveness, will find God Forgiving, Merciful" (4:110) — the verse whose echo the Prophet ﷺ made the community's nightly seal, "O God, You are my Lord; there is no god but You; I created myself in evil and I confessed my sin, so forgive me fully — none forgives sins but You" (al-Bukhārī 6306, the *sayyid al-istighfār*), the *sayyid* — the master — of prayers built out of the choice Iblīs refused to make.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:37

> **He said, "Then verily thou art among those granted respite"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer in the Same Verb: *Anzirnī* Becomes *Munẓarūn***

*qāla fa-innaka min al-munẓarīn* — "He said: then you are *among those granted respite*." The reply is built from the very verb of the request: he said *rabbi anzirni* ("give me respite," v. 36, form-IV active), and God answers with the same verb's passive participle — *munẓar*, one *to whom respite is granted*. In two syllables the whole status of the enemy is re-registered: not the holder of a term but one on whom a term is passed. The grammar is a theology: respite is not a power he possesses; it is a treatment he is undergoing, and every hour of the millennia he will spend on earth belongs, by the morphology of the verdict itself, to the One who wrote them. The sūrah's whole economy of *inzār* runs on this verb: when the mockers demanded the descent of angels, the answer was "We send down the angels not but with truth, and then they would not be respited" (v. 8) — the *munẓarūn* Iblīs is granted here is the identical passive form v. 8 denied to his earthly allies; the two verses bracket the sūrah as a single law of delay, and this verse is the enemy's charter within it. What he took as extension of life is, on the word's horizon, notification: a delay that does not cancel, only announces.

**The Partitive *Min*: The Enemy Enrolled in the Class of the Reprieved**

*min al-munẓarīn* — "among those." The partitive is the verse's edge: he asked for a personal grant ("respite *me*") and was answered with enrollment. He is made a member of a category — all the reprieved: the towns of v. 4, the generations of v. 5 ("no people can advance their term nor retard it"), every mercy spent in refusal. This is why the verse stands coldly between his request and its term (v. 38): Iblīs's respite is *identical in kind* to the respite of every doomed town — and v. 4 is his contract: "Never did We destroy a town, but that it had a known decree." He is a town; he has a *kitāb maʿlūm*. The sūrah's opening verses, which read as introduction, are revealed as the statute under which Iblīs himself is tried: the law of the term is applied to the law-mockers (vv. 6-15) and to the law-transgressor (vv. 30-44) in one system — and his special boast ("since You made me," v. 39) dies on the partitive: nothing about his reprieve is special except its length, and length is the least special thing a decree can contain.

**The Answered Prayer as Trial: The Law of *Istidrāj***

To be granted what one wrongly asks is, in the Qur'an's technical term, *istidrāj*: "those who deny Our signs — We shall draw them in by degrees, from where they do not know; I lengthen for them, but My plan is firm" (7:182-183). The mechanism appears elsewhere in the Qur'an before it appears here: "when they forgot what they were reminded of, We opened for them the gates of every thing — and when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and they were in despair" (6:44). The verse's word for those opened doors — *abwāb* — is this sūrah's own word for the seven gates of the Fire (v. 44): the same doors, walked toward now, walked out of then. And the parallel answered-wrong-prayers confirm the rule: the table of the Māʾida, demanded as a sign, became the ground of the harshest ultimatum in its sūrah ("God said: I send it down upon you; and whoever disbelieves after that among you — I punish him with a punishment I punish none of the worlds with," 5:115); Pharaoh's last-minute faith was refused — "What? Now? — and you disobeyed before, and were of the corrupters" (10:91), the verse of the saved body (10:92) written as a museum-piece of the answered-with-delay. The commentators' conclusion is fitted exactly to our verse: "God answered Iblīs with *yes*, and the *yes* was the punishment's delay-mode" — for a heart set against the Commander, every granted request is one more withheld sentence; the extension is distance, and distance in this sūrah's court already has its name: curse (vv. 34-35, which he carries *while* he walks).

**The Two *Munẓar* Verses, Read as One Sentence**

Ṣād's parallel of this exchange says *fa-innaka munẓarūn ilā yawmi'l-waqqti'l-maʿlūm* (38:80-81) — "you are respited *until*…," without the partitive; this sūrah says *min al-munẓarīn* — "among the respited." The difference is the sūrah's theology of the class: al-Ḥijr will not grant Iblīs even the dignity of a unique fate. Read together, the two versions state the full verdict: he is respited (Ṣād) *and* he is one-of-many (al-Ḥijr) — an exceptional length inside an unexceptional category. Al-Rāzī generalizes the psychology the verse exposes: no granted delay ever softens a fixed intention; it enlarges it. And the sūrah teaches the mirror-discipline to the Prophet ﷺ in its own closing moves: "stretch not thine eyes toward what We have let various classes of them enjoy — the splendor of the world's life, wherewith We try them; and thy Lord's provision is better and more lasting" (20:131), and here: "We suffice thee against the mockers" (v. 95) — the one who knows that answered prayers are trials stops requesting the enemy's schedule and keeps the only request left: "and worship thy Lord until there comes to thee the certainty" (v. 99).

**Application: The Reprieved Man's Calendar**

This verse is the sūrah's mirror held up to every granted-but-misused extension in a believer's own life — the illness survived, the year the calamity passed over, the capacity for sin that was *not* taken away at the moment of intent. The Qur'an's three disciplines for the reprieved follow from the verse's three words. From the passive (*munẓar*): treat your lease as held property, not owned — write, as the Salaf wrote over prosperity, "this is *inzār*, not *ijāra*: a warning, not a rental contract" (the source's *adab*-note on this verse). From the partitive (*min*): never read duration as election — the doomed towns were reprieved too (v. 4), and the only question the class answers is *what its members did with the term*; Pharaoh, Hamān, and the believer of Firʿawn's household (66:11) all lived the same calendar. From the conjunction (*fa*): when a wrong request is answered, hear the *fāʾ* as a gavel — "then you are among the reprieved" is a sentence, not a favor; the Prophet's ﷺ maxim as transmitted in the Musnad of Aḥmad is the tradition's own commentary on this verse: "when you see the Lord giving a servant what he loves while he persists in disobedience to Him, then that is *istidrāj*" — and the narrator of the report then recited, as its proof, "until, when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair" (6:44). It is exegesis enough. Recite this verse when grace arrives and label the gift from above: *this is a respite; it is not a verdict.* The next verse will give the respite its only real feature — an expiry the holder cannot see.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:38

> **Till the Day of the Moment Known.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer That Edits the Request**

*ilā yawmi'l-waqqti'l-maʿlūm* — "until the Day of the Time Known." The request had been *ilā yawmi yubʿathūn* — "until the Day they are raised" (v. 36): the Day of *other people's* resurrection, named from the vantage of the man who wanted to watch them fall. The answer keeps his *ilā*, keeps his Day, and replaces his clause with two words that change everything: not "the Day they are resurrected" but "the Day of the known *waqt*." He asked for the term as a spectacle ("let me see them raised, then judge me"); he received the term as a deadline. Between the request and the reply, the sūrah has performed its finest operation on a sentence: the same respite, viewed from the Bench instead of from the dock. Al-Zamakhsharī's observation belongs here precisely: *waqq* is the minute-precision of time — not *dahr* (aeon), not *ʿumr* (lifetime), not even *sāʿa* (which veers between "hour" and "moment"): the Day is defined as a *waqt* to teach that the long middle of the term is nothing; only the appointed instant counts, and it is *maʿlūm* — known.

**"Known": By Whom? The Verse's Deliberate Double Lock**

Al-maʿlūm is a passive participle — "known" — with the knower deliberately unstated, and the commentators lock the two possible knowers using the Qur'an itself. Known to God alone: "they ask you about the Hour, when is its final rest? To your Lord belongs its end; you are only a warner for whoever fears it — as though you were set over it to guard it" (79:44-46, the question put to the Prophet in order to be answered about Iblīs); and "they ask you about the Hour, when is its setting? Say: its knowledge is with my Lord alone; none discloses it at its time but He" (7:187; 31:34; 41:47). And known — made public — to every creature on the Day itself, when what was hidden becomes the only common knowledge: "the Day when they are brought forth, nothing of them is hidden" (69:18), and its twin: "today every soul is recompensed for what it has earned — no injustice this day" (40:16). The double lock is the whole lesson: the *date* is withheld from every creature including the one who asked for the term, and the *fact* is published to every creature including him. A respite whose hour is hidden from its holder is the only respite that can test: had Iblīs owned the date, his repentance — never his war — would have had a schedule ("tomorrow I turn; today I spend"). The withholding of the *waqt* is how the sūrah's time disciplines. The verse functions as the lock on the whole Ādam-cycle: every being in the story — the mocking Quraysh (vv. 6-8), the demanded angels (v. 7), the fallen enemy (v. 36) — is held by one and the same sentence of v. 5, "no people can advance their term nor retard it," and the enemy's answer, like theirs, ends at a date he cannot read.

**The Known Day and the Known Decree: The Bracket of *Maʿlūm***

The sūrah began its legal section with "We destroyed no town but that it had a *known decree* (*kitāb maʿlūm*)" (v. 4); it now closes the enemy's request with "the Day of the Time *Known* (*maʿlūm*)" (v. 38). One adjective, bracketing the sūrah's first act and its longest episode: the same word that insured the mockers against premature destruction insures the world against premature judgment, and both insurances are the same thing — a written date. The symmetry means that Iblīs's reprieve contains no new kind of time: the sūrah has been teaching, from its fourth verse on, that history runs on *ajal* — term upon term — and the enemy's millennia are simply the longest *kitāb* in the register, which is why the tradition treats vv. 36-38 as the sūrah's summary of all respite: "the towns were written out; so was he — the only difference the Qur'an allows between the devil's term and Firʿawn's is length, and length is not status." The source's note on this bracket is precise: "God did not say 'I have lengthened for you'; He said 'until the known Day' — as though to say: the respite is not a gift of time, it is a debt owed to the book of time; you will pay at the counter named there, not where you asked to be seen." And the word *yawm* is quietly exact: he asked for a Day of witnessing ("the Day *they* are resurrected"); the Day he receives is a Day of settlement — resurrection is what happens to the creatures, judgment is what happens to him; the two are the same event seen from the two banks, and this verse places him, who had tried to station himself on the judge's bank, back on his own.

**The Measure of Days: What the Tradition Adds**

The reports at the level of the collections teach this verse's psychology in a single image: in the famous ḥadīth of the Questioner — the one who came to the Prophet ﷺ in visible form and asked, "when is the Hour?" — the answer came first as a question, "what have you prepared for it?", and only then as the disclosure: "the one asked knows no more about it than the asker; but I will tell you of its signs: the slave-girl will give birth to her mistress, and you will see the bare, destitute shepherds vying in the buildings" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb). The Hour's *date* is withheld even from the greatest of creation; its *approach* is published in works of vanity — the shepherds' towers are the sūrah's own Thamūd, "hewing the mountains into palaces" (v. 82), the people of al-Ḥijr whose carved security is the monument the sūrahh's name keeps. And the verse's counter-education is the hadith's second clause: since the date is hidden, the only rational posture is preparation — "the one asked knows no more" is the divine etiquette for every question of *when*, and the answer to *when* is the answer of this sūrah's last verse: *do what you do until the certainty comes* (v. 99).

**Application: Live Against an Unread Calendar**

The verse converts the respite from an excuse into an assignment. Three applications follow its exact shape. (1) For the anxious: the *waqt* that is hidden is mercy, not indifference — the verse tells the believer that the same concealment that disciplines Iblīs protects him: had the hour been known, the days before it would have been a waiting-room; being unknown, they are a field. (2) For the presumptuous: every "there is still time" repeats Iblīs's grammar without his sincerity of terror; the sūrah answers with the pair "known decree / known Day" (vv. 4, 38) — time is not empty, it is *booked*. (3) For the praying: the verse teaches what to do with an answered request whose wording was wrong — accept the edited answer: ask for the Day less specifically, obey more generally. When the community recites this verse, the Salaf's practice, transmitted in the *tafsīr* of Ibn Kathīr's chain from Ibn ʿAbbās's students, was to say after it: *"naʿam, wa-anta al-muʿīn"* — the Day of the Time Known ends the enemy's sentence and begins the verse that follows, in which he swears his revenge; which means: in the Qur'an's sequence, the first act of the reprieved is *speech* — so let the reprieved's first act, on every day he is granted, be the prayer, before the plan.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:39

> **He said, "My Lord! Since Thou hast caused me to err, I shall surely make things seem fair unto them on earth, and I shall cause them to err all together,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath Spoken with the Respite Still Warm**

*qāla rabbi bimā agwaytani la-uzayyanna lahum fi'l-arḍi wa-la-ugwiyannahum ajmaʿīn* — and notice where this verse falls: *after* the request (v. 36), *after* the grant (v. 37), *after* the term (v. 38). In al-Aʿrāf, Iblīs swears the oath the moment the curse lands — "He said: *because You have caused me to err*, I will sit for them upon Your straight path" (7:16-17) — and there the oath is desperation mid-sentence. Here the order is different, and the sūrah's ordering is its argument: al-Ḥijr makes him wait until he has been *answered*, until his wrong request has been *granted*, and swear only then — the reprieve warm on him. What the sequence proves is the istidrāj-law of the previous verses in its purest case: "God granted him what he asked, and the granting produced not one alteration in him — only a plan." A being whose answered prayer changes nothing in him has shown what his prayer was for; the sūrah files the oath immediately after the grant so that the reader cannot miss the exhibit: the *yes* of v. 37 is not met with gratitude, nor with reform, but with recruitment. This is the Qur'an's most precise anatomy of the hardened heart: the disease was never fear of the punishment; it was love of the self, and love of the self survives every concession.

**"Since Thou Hast Caused Me to Err" — One Clause, Three Courts**

The *bi-mā agwaytani* clause appears in exactly three places in the Qur'an, in all of them on Iblīs's lips (7:16; 15:39; and at Ṣād 38:82 the variant *fa-bi-izwāgika*, "by Your might"), and the commentators use the variant to show what pride does to the same grievance when its circumstances change: in Ṣād, where the exchange runs past the exception-clause and into the sentence of v. 44's territory, he swears by God's *might* — he has learned that what crushed him was power; here, where he has just won his request, he swears by his *misguidance* — he still believes he has a grievance. Al-Rāzī draws the distinction sharply: "he who curses his fate while receiving favors has made his ingratitude into a creed." The clause's content — God as the *cause* of his sin — is the sūrah's standing demonstration that divine causation can be invoked without ever being refuted, because the verse that grants the respite (v. 37) is also the verse that made the respite *a class* ("among those granted respite") and not a endorsement: God's causing, in this sūrah's vocabulary, always includes the causing of time for choice; the reprieved who reads the reprieve as a commission has skipped v. 4's *kitāb* and v. 5's *ajal* entirely. It is worth recalling that this is also the sūrah's answer to the mockers, who claimed their own misguidance would be the prophets' fault ("you are only a man possessed," v. 6-7's register): the Qur'an lets the *real* possessor state the theory of blame-transfer in its full form, once, forever, so that every later version of "God made me, so God must answer for me" can be checked against the oath's own record.

**"I Shall Make Things Seem Fair" — the *Tazyīn* Engine, Stated as Law**

*la-uzayyanna*, the intensive of *zayyana*: he who cannot create can *re-present*. The verb is the sūrah's vocabulary turned inside out: God "adorns faith in your hearts and makes it lovely" (49:7); Iblīs "adorns" misdeeds in the same hearts (39:8: "God thus makes every soul's deeds fair-seeming to it"), and the Qur'an's two standing descriptions of his trade are here fused into one sentence — adornment is the mechanism, *ighwāʾ* the result. The *tafsīr* tradition itemizes the mechanism as three stages, all visible in the wording: a flattering *name* (the verb *zayyana* is the same one used of the world's own glamour, *zīna*: 18:46 "wealth and children are the adornment of the world's life"); a fitting *moment* ("*on earth*" — the field of appetite, the only terrain he is allowed; and the Prophet's image of the traffic between the two parties: "the devil runs through the son of Ādam as the blood runs," al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Ṣafiyya); and a *company* to normalize the garnish (v. 42's sequel, the obeying crowd). The source's catalogue of the tradition's seven wraps belongs at this verse's footnote — wealth toward display, power toward tyranny, knowledge toward showing-off, desire toward obscenity, hope toward procrastination, anger toward vendetta dressed as justice, piety toward pride — and every item is a *legitimate good pushed past a line*, which is why the next clause swears to work "*all together*": his claim to universality is an arithmetic claim, and arithmetic is exactly what the exception of v. 40 is about to refute.

**No Reply Is Recorded: The Answer Is Architecture**

The striking thing about v. 39, in the sūrah's arrangement, is that God answers it *without a reply*: between this oath and the next divine speech (v. 41's "This is a straight path"), nothing is said — and what follows is not a counter-threat but a *blueprint*: the path (v. 41), the immunity of the servants (v. 42), the tryst and its seven gates (vv. 43-44). Against an oath to *pave* a road, the Most High sets *the* Road with its Owner's vow upon it; against a promise of mass recruitment, a declaration of jurisdiction ("My servants — you have no authority"); against a claim on the earth, a numbered division of the Fire's real estate. The commentators note the symmetry with al-Aʿrāf, where the oath of ambush (7:16-17: "I will sit for them upon Your straight path; then I will come upon them from before them and behind them, from their right and from their left") is answered immediately by the sentence "Go forth from it, despised, cast off; whoever of them follows you — I will fill Hell with you all, *ajmaʿīn*" (7:18) — and our sūrah's v. 43 keeps the same counter-word: his *ugwiyannahum ajmaʿīn*, "them all together," is answered, syllable for syllable, by God's *mawʿidahum ajmaʿūn*, "their tryst, all together." The sūrah thus closes the enemy's speech-act the way it closed every mockery in its first half — not with refutation but with placement: let each claimant state the plan, then reveal the architecture within which the plan will play out, and the reader will convict it without argument. The oath was made "on the earth" (v. 37's *ʿumr* and this verse's *arḍ*); the reply moves the venue to eternity in two words: *mawʿiduhum ajmaʿūn*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:40

> **Save Thy sincere servants among them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Word That Ends the War: *illa***

*illa ʿibāda-ka minhum al-mukhlaṣīn* — "except Your servants among them, the purely devoted." The exegetes single out the particle: "one word that divided the *ajmaʿīn* of v. 37 from itself; a single exception-clause that cuts the total claim of v. 39 in half, and the cut is the whole of human history." The grammar is decisive: the exception is not introduced by Iblīs — he never limits his boast; it is appended by God to the enemy's own speech, so that the Qur'an files the claim *with its refutation embedded in the same breath*, the royal *illa* riding the foe's sentence like a ballast no sailor can throw out. Al-Zamakhsharī marks the rhetorical effect: "his sentence is quoted verbatim so his arrogance stands exposed; God's clause is attached to it so his defeat stands inside his own words — the strongest proof God could give His servants is to make their enemy's testimony carry their immunity." Ibn Kathīr: "he said *ajmaʿīn*; God said *illa* — and the *illa* of the King ends the argument of the claimant."

**Whose Servants? The Possessive That Is the Shield**

*ʿibāda-ka* — *Your* servants. Not "the sincere ones" in the abstract, not "the strong"; the verse anchors the exception in divine relation: what the purified are saved by is *belonging*. The source's tradition (al-Junayd's school of Sufi exegesis reads this verse as the charter of *siddīq*ship: "the servanthood He here claims, Iblīs cannot touch — he can wage war on the *self*-centered, on the one whose 'I' is his business, but he cannot reach the one whose self is another's — the one who has *given* it as the verse gives it, in the genitive to God"). And there is a second anchor, the pronoun of address: he says *ʿibāda-ka*, "Your servants," not *ʿibāda-nā*, "our servants," so that even in the exception the servants' claim is relation, not property; they are safe only as long as they *are His*, which is the sūrah's whole ethics of *ʿibāda* compressed into a single letter.

**Which Reading — *Mukhlaṣ* or *Mukhliṣ*? The Seven Verses That Decide**

The one word *al-mukhlaṣīn* can be read two ways, and the commentators transmit both as complementary. Read actively (*mukhliṣ*, form-IV participle): those who *purify* their worship for God alone — the meaning forced by the parallel verse in Ṣād, where the same exchange has Iblīs say *illa man tabiʿaka min al-ghāwīn*, "except those who follow you of the deviators" (38:82), and God reply *la-amlaʾanna jahannama minka wa-man tabiʿaka minhum ajmaʿīn*, and then our own sūrah's v. 42 will state the operative limit: "My servants — you have no authority over them except whoever follows you, *al-ghāwīn*, the willing deviators." Read passively (*mukhlaṣ*, the divine passive): those *purified by God*, kept for Himself — the meaning the salaf favored: "God purified them from what I could reach." Both readings arrive at one place: the purified are those whose interior was never *their own* to advertise (7:29 "my Lord has commanded justice; straighten your faces at every masjid and call on Him, purifying the religion for Him — as We originated you you return"). The dual reading is the verse's pedagogy: the active reading is your assignment, the passive is your relief; and the sūrah, in the very same exchange, places relief first — the exception is God's clause, not the servant's resume.

**The Exception's Size: What the Verse Is Not Saying**

The source guards the verse against two misreadings. (1) "It is not a promise that the *number* of the saved will be small but that their *kind* will be certain: he claimed totality of prey; God answered with a category, not a census." The Qur'an elsewhere refuses census-theology ("you will enter the Garden only if you are Jews or Christians — wishful thinking!" 2:111) and here refuses it in the enemy's own key: the *ajmaʿīn* vs. the *illa* is not statistics vs. statistics; it is coercion's claim vs. relation's fact. (2) "The exception does not name a *sinless* class but a *direction*: the purified are those who, when they fall, fall toward God — Adam fell this way, Iblīs that; and the sūrah has already performed the distinction between the two falls in vv. 26-35's rehearsal." The Salaf's line on this verse is the practical summary: "the purified servant is the one Iblīs can only accuse, never own" — and the Prophet's ﷺ image of the owned-by-no-one is the habit of thanks: "God is pleased with a servant who says, when he eats a meal, praise be to God who fed me this and provided it for me without any power or might from me — he is forgiven what has passed of his sin" (Sunan Abī Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī, reported by Muʿādh b. ʿAnas al-Juhanī from his father), the gratitude the source cites as the purified's signature — for *tazyīn*'s whole trade is to make the creature forget the Giver; the one habit the adorer cannot counterfeit is the thanks that runs from gift to Giver, and that habit *is* purity. Hence the sūrah's final instruction to the Prophet (v. 99, "until there comes to you that which is certain") is the same verse's other side: purity is not a state but a persistence — and the only boast worth making is the one the *illa* answers.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:41

> **He said, "This is a straight path unto Me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whose Words: The Divine Counter-Declaration Against Iblīs's Oath**

*wa an hādhā ṣirāṭun ʿalayya mustaqīm* — "and that this is a path upon Me, straight." The syntax has the classical commentators pausing: the *an* clause (with its suppressed verb: "and [I decreed/inspired] that…") attaches to the verdict-flow of vv. 38-40, and most read the words as God's address *to Iblīs* — the Lord of the path setting His own declaration directly across the enemy's sworn boast (v. 39). The arrangement is the sūrah's grandest antithesis, and the source states it with precision: he swore "by that Thou hast made me err" and pledged to work *on the earth* (v. 39); God answers not with a counter-threat but with a *vow* about a path. The key intertext is al-Aʿrāf, where the same oath continues: "I will sit for them upon **Your straight path**" (7:17) — Iblīs's entire strategy, in that sūrah, is an ambush *on the ṣirāṭ*; and this sūrah's v. 41 is the divine counterpart filed directly against it: not "your ambush will fail on My road" but "this is a path *ʿalayya*, upon Me — straight." The road the ambusher chose to patrol is, in the same words' range, *claimed back* by its Owner before the ambush is even described. The phrase *ʿalayya* is the hinge, and al-Ṭabarī transmits the two great readings side by side: (1) "a path I have made binding upon Myself — a guarantee from the One who cannot break a promise: guidance is My own obligation upon Me, so trust it"; (2) "a path upon which I stand as keeper and witness over you." Both readings do the same work against the enemy: Iblīs's power was to make roads seem many; God's *ḥaqq* is to make the one road *His* — not a theory, an estate.

**"Mustaqīm": The Straightness That Is Geometry, Not Aesthetics**

*Mustaqīm* in this sūrah's neighborhood is doing a technical job: it is the adjective the Qur'an reserves for what is *proportioned* (v. 29, *fa-sawwaytuhu* — "proportioned him"; the verb-root of both words is the same act of leveling and measuring). The path is straight the way the human was made straight: not by narrowness but by *design* — it neither climbs fantasy nor descends to the animal, v. 19's economy of "a middle way" between the elevation of angels (pure spirit) and the density of beasts (pure appetite); man alone can deviate, which is exactly why the path is legislated for him alone. The same *ṣirāṭ* word recurs in the sūrah's closing act — when the guests of Ibrāhīm and Lūṭ appear, the visitors tell the Prophet to be about his *nadhīr* work "while We do not aid the wrongdoers" (v. 93), but the word's first appearance in the Qur'an's Meccan sequence (the Fātiḥah, the sūrah that the next verse will call *sabʿ min al-mathānī*) makes the link explicit: daily, the community says "guide us to the straight path" (1:6) — and this verse, placed mid-cycle, is the *answer already written before the asking*: the path exists before the request, guaranteed before the walking. Ibn al-Qayyim notes the pairing: "the Fātiḥah asks for the *ṣirāṭ*; al-Ḥijr states it; the servant does not receive the road at the end of the prayer — he receives the prayer at the edge of the road."

**The Walls, the Doors, and the Caller: The Tradition's Diagram**

The Prophetic commentary on this verse is transmitted in two images that map it exactly. The first: the Prophet ﷺ described the *ṣirāṭ* as a road with a wall on each side, walls with opened doors, doors with curtains, and a caller on the top of the road calling, "O servant of God, do not open that door, for it leads to the Fire," while a caller from above the road — "God's preacher on His path" — warns against the side-doors; al-Tirmidhī transmits this, from al-Nawwās b. Samʿān. The second is the visual sermon of ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd: the Prophet ﷺ drew for them a single straight line, and around it shorter lines branching right and left, then placed his hand on the long line and recited: "and that this is My path, straight; follow it — and do not follow the [side] roads, or they will scatter you from His path" (6:153) — Ahmad and al-Nasāʾī, from Ibn Masʿūd. The two reports are this verse's own *tafsīr* and explain its three words: the *path* is one; the *doors* are many (the side-doors are the *subul* of 6:153, each with its "caller" — every road advertised by a voice, which is vv. 37-39's *tazyīn* with a loudspeaker); and the *curtains* teach that deviation never presents itself naked — the door is veiled in something lovely, precisely the adorning-hand's work the sūrah has just named. What makes the verse consoling, in view of the diagrams, is the placement of the *ʿalayya*: the caller on the road is God's own, and the guarantee under the road is God's own; the tester stands between two divine voices and only *one* of the doors he sees has a curtain God has marked — the *mafhūm* the source draws: "there is no veil He has not told you is there."

**"This Is My Path": Propriety as Protection**

Why *ʿalayya* and not *lakum* — "upon Me" rather than "for you" — deserves the last word, because it answers the sūrah's opening accusation: the mockers called the revelation sorcery and the messenger one possessed of jinn (vv. 6-7, 12-15); here, at the cycle's peak, the road's ownership is what saves it from *their* haunting — a path owned by God cannot be ambushed without the Owner being on it. Iblīs had claimed the earth as his workshop (v. 39 *fī'l-arḍ*); the declaration re-encloses the earth under a single deed. And the proprietorship is a *protection of the route*, not a coercion of the walkers: nothing here guarantees that you will stay on the road; everything guarantees that the road is real, that its straightness is not your imagination, and that its Owner has bound Himself — the pledge under the road is not fatalism, for in the hadith of creation the very writing includes the deed — "he is commanded with four things: his provision, his term, his deed, and whether he is wretched or blessed" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Ibn Masʿūd) — which is exactly the pairing this sūrah argues: a guaranteed path, a real choice, and an exception God wrote Himself (v. 40). The sūrah keeps its own last word: "and worship your Lord until there comes to you the certainty" (v. 99). Between this verse and that command lies the whole map: the road, the caller, the curtained doors, and the pledge under them — enough for a lifetime of straightness, and nothing to lean on but the Owner.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:42

> **As for My servants, truly thou hast no authority over them, save for those in error who follow thee.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Ends the War: *Laysa Laka ʿalayhim Sulṭān***

*wa-inna ʿibāda-ka laysa laka ʿalayhim sulṭānun illa man tabiʿa-ka min al-ghāwīn* — "and truly, My servants — there is *no* authority for you over them; except whoever follows you of the deviators." This is the sūrah's legal verdict on the whole Ādam-cycle, and its negative is absolute: the sentence opens with *inna* and the oath-*lām*, and swears *absence* — not "I will protect," but "there exists, for you, *no* sulṭān." The word is precise: *sulṭān* in the Qur'an is not power-as-force but power-as-*warrant* — a document of authority, a binding claim (its root is *s-l-ṭ*, sharpness/penetration: a proof that cuts). The source's cross-reference is exact: "his authority is only over those who take him as a master, and those who take him as a partner" (16:99-100) — i.e., his "authority" exists precisely where it has been *handed over*; and "I had no authority over you except that I called you, and you obeyed me" (14:22) will be the Devil's own post-verdict testimony, the future admission already legislated here. The commentators note that Iblīs's speech-acts have all been claims of *right* (the qiyās of v. 33, the oaths of vv. 37 and 39: "I *shall*... I *shall*"); the divine reply is one sentence subtracting warrant itself — you have no *document*, no *cut of authority*; what you call your power is a lease signed by your tenants, revocable at will, and the next clause tells who may revoke.

**The Exception That Is Not an Exception: *illa man tabiʿa-ka***

The *illa* here is what the grammarians call munqaṭiʿ, an "adversative except": the follower is *not* carved out from the number of "My servants" as a portion of them — he *leaves* the class: "except" = "however: whoever follows you was not among them to begin with, except in the sense of one who quits." The verb confirms: *tabiʿa* — followed, walked behind — the verb of choice and of ranks (v. 38's "whoever follows you"; the poets of 26:224, "the deviators follow them," a society of volunteers). Al-Rāzī's gloss is the sūrah's own: "he did not say 'whomever I make you deviate' nor 'whomever you overcome' — He said *whoever follows you*, and following is an act of the follower." Thus the verse completes the exception-triangle of the cycle: v. 40's *illa ʿibādaka minhum al-mukhlaṣīn* (the purified *within* the reprieved class) is answered by v. 42's *illa man tabiʿa-ka* (the volunteers *without* it) — the two exception-clauses, read together, define the field of the test as *who serves Whom by choice*, and every soul in the middle is, by the verses' silence about them, still *unfixed*: not yet his, not yet "My servants"-confirmed — which is the mercy in the verse: the majority of humanity sits in the open space the two *illa*-clauses leave, and that space is time, is life, is the sūrah's own *mawʿid* (v. 43) still running toward its date.

**Al-Ghāwūn: The Name for Volunteers in Error**

The noun is not *al-ḍāllūn* (the astray — a word the Qur'an uses also for the unintentional, the forgotten, the led). *Ghayy* is the deliberate pursuit of what one knows to be low: the root is of *ghiyāʾ*, the valley's bottom, error *chosen downward*, and the form *fāʿil* makes it character: "those in error" (source's phrase) as a *habit*, not a state of being lost. The pairing with the verse's other key-word is the whole anthropology of the cycle: *sulṭān* absent + *tabiʿa* present = the ghāwī is the one who gives warrant to the one who has none — a transfer of authority the Qur'an repeatedly frames as an act of taking (*ittakhādhū*: "take him as master" 16:100; "take the devil as companion" 4:120; "and whoever takes Iblīs as master... 18:50). And al-Aʿrāf's ambush — "I will come upon them from before them and behind them, from their right and from their left" (7:17) — is the tradition's commentary on this verse's limit: the report transmitted from the Companions by ʿAbd al-Razzāq and Ibn Jarīr — Ibn Masʿūd's and Ibn ʿAbbās's reading that Iblīs *did not say "from above them"* — for, says the tafsīr tradition, his access is to every avenue of the compass, but the descent of mercy comes from above; what he cannot do is stop what comes *down*, and the sūrah's next verse (v. 44) makes the asymmetry architectural: his road ends in *gates that are counted*, which is to say, in a place where he is not the architect.

**"My Servants": The Shield Both Parties Hold**

The verse's last grace is a name. God calls them *ʿibāda-ka* — *Your* servants — and the address places the pronoun of divine possession on the very persons Iblīs swore to mislead "all together" (v. 39's *ajmaʿīn*). The commentators hear the echo across the cycle: at v. 40 he said *illa ʿibāda-ka minhum al-mukhlaṣīn*; at v. 42 God says *wa-inna ʿibāda-ka laysa laka ʿalayhim sulṭānun* — the enemy and the Lord use, in consecutive verses, *the same title* for the saved: for God it is a claim of ownership ("they are Mine, and My ownership is a warranty"); for Iblīs it had been a boundary of his admitted defeat ("Yours — not mine"). So the protection the believer is given in this verse is not a record of strength but of *affiliation*: the safe ones are the ones who are *called by God*, and the whole sūrah has just shown how that title is acquired — prostration in the face of honor-cost (v. 29's lesson, v. 31's refusal inverted), the question answered with "we have wronged ourselves" rather than "You made me" (v. 39's contrast), and the prayer of the reprieved who asked for *the Lord* and not for *the term* (v. 36's *rabbi* — twice used by the saved voice in the sūrah, and the one word Iblīs's speech cannot sustain). The prophetic practice on this verse's nerve is the muʿawwidatān's form: "the refuge is in the Lord *of the people*, the King *of the people*, the God *of the people*" (114:1-3) — three titles of relation, exactly the verse's grammar; and the practice the verse generates is the prayer the Prophet ﷺ himself taught, which the collections transmit verbatim: in every salām he sought refuge "from the punishment of the grave," and "from the trial of the Dajjāl" — and the sūrah's whole cycle is the Dajjāl's prototype in slow motion: the deceiver who swears, is granted, and recruits (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿĀʾisha).

**Application: Verify the Warrant, Then the Wallet**

This verse is the practical security chapter of the Qur'an's longest exorcism. Its first command is epistemic: when a voice claims rights over you — self-loathing, compulsion-narratives, "you're mine," the vocabulary of addiction, guilt, despair — the verse answers with a *document-check*: *laysa laka sulṭān*: does this claimant have *any* warrant for this demand? The sūrah has just established he has none *at all*, except by transfer; which makes the second command procedural: audit your transfers — the ghāwī is the one who *follows*, and following (unlike falling) has a moment of decision the verse can point to: the first obedience to the second thought. And the third command is the verse's center: the shield is relational — "My servants" — so the practical protection is the *title*: live inside the acts that make a person God's (the prayer that begins "rabbi," the repentance that ends "ẓalamnā anfusanā," the patience of v. 86), and the verse's silence on the open middle (all the not-yet-fixed) becomes not anxiety but invitation: the term is *known* to God, the gate is *counted* in v. 44, and every day inside is exactly the length of an option. The hadith of the two camps — "every child of Ādam is born upon the fitra; it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, a Magian" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra) — reads best beside this verse: the fitra is the *pre-transfer* state, the born-in warrant, and the whole Ādam-cycle is the story of how warrants get signed away by *tabʿ*, following, step by step — which means they can be revoked, step by step, by the same voluntary feet walking back; for the verse's final promise is not that the saved never follow wrongly, but that no one who follows *God* follows without warrant — and *His* document, this sūrah has sworn, ends the enemy's, in every court, in every year of every respite, all together.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:43

> **And verily Hell shall be their tryst, all together."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word for a Rendezvous: *Mawʿid* and the Meeting That Was Always Scheduled**

*wa-inna jahannama la-mawʿiduhum ajmaʿūn* — "and truly, Hell is their *tryst*, all of them together." The noun *mawʿid* is the word of an appointment kept between parties — in the Arabic of the sūrah's own revelation, the word of the tryst the disbelievers were teased about ("they say: when is this promise, if you are truthful?" v. 12's *waʿd*-mockery; "and they say, if you are truthful, when is this promise?" 67:25), and the *tafsīr* tradition marks the register: a rendezvous implies two parties who agreed it, a time and a place both sides eventually keep. That is the verse's quiet devastation: the tryst is not a capture but a *meeting* — Iblīs promised his followers a harvest; God's reply is that the harvest was always an appointment *he too was summoned to*, thinking it his own plan (their tryst — *mawʿiduhum*: the pronoun gathers the deceiver and the deceived into one grammatical body: he has no party, only co-defendants). The source notes the verse is quoted in the parallel at Ṣād 38:85 with the same oath-lām and the same totality, and adds the exchange's last irony: *mawʿid* is the word the Qur'an also uses for the believers' rendezvous with reward — "a promise whose fulfillment they await" — so the Day will show two appointments kept on the same schedule, and the tragedy of the lost is only ever that they attended the wrong tryst with the wrong date written on the card.

**God's *waʿd*, Iblīs's *waʿd*: The Theology of Two Promises**

The root (و ع د) covers promise and threat alike, and the sūrah makes the whole cycle a contest of two promisers — which is, in the Qur'an's own vocabulary, the definition of *ghurūr*, delusion: "He promises them and gives them false hopes, but what Satan promises them is nothing but delusion" (4:120); and 14:22, the Devil's post-verdict address, names the contest as the Day reveals it: "God promised you the promise of truth (*waʿda'l-ḥaqq*), and I promised you and betrayed you (*fa-akhlaftukum*)" — *ikhālfa*, literally: I gave you a tryst and did not keep it. Our verse is v. 43 the *legal frame* of that Day's sentence: the one who kept no promise keeps His — the *mawʿid* is certain because the Promiser is one; and the verse's demonstrative force lies in the *la-mawʿiduhum*: the oath-*lām* attaches to the tryst, not the threat-word — God swears that it *is their appointment*, i.e., he *will be kept*, the rendezvous honored; the tradition reads 14:22 as the Day's commentary on our *ajmaʿūn* — the tryst-keeping that makes Hell a *place of meeting* rather than a pile of punishments. And the pairing with v. 38 completes the calendar: he asked for the Day of *their* resurrection (v. 36); he was respited to the known *waqt* (v. 38); here the *mawʿid* is given its venue — the sūrah's eschatology of time compressed to three words: *waqt*, *mawʿid*, *ajmaʿūn*: a moment, an appointment, a reunion.

**"All Together" (*Ajmaʿūn*): The Grammar That Reverses the Grammar of His Boast**

The commentators' sharpest observation on this verse is morphological and it unlocks the whole exchange. Iblīs's boast at v. 39 ended *wa-la-ugwiyannahum ajmaʿīn* — "all of them": *ajmaʿīn* is in the accusative because it attaches to the *object* pronoun (*-hum*, them, his prey): he swore totality over his *victims*, who remain grammatically detachable — and indeed v. 40's exception-clause detaches the purified from that very pronoun. God's *ajmaʿūn* is in the nominative because it attaches to the *subject* pronoun (*-hum*, they, the parties of the tryst): totality over the *agents*, the ones who chose: "their" appointment, "themselves" all together, no detachable accusatory object — the exception at v. 40 is *not* here, because none of the subjects is an exception: the gathering is of volunteers (v. 42's *man tabiʿa-ka*), and a volunteer, by definition, was never owed a door out. The whole of the sūrah's theodicy sits in that case-marker: God does not gather the misled; He gathers the *willing* — and the grammar explains why the saved have an exception-clause (they were being *counted as prey* against their will and are reclaimed) while the lost have none (they were being *counted as parties*, and they signed). Ibn Kathīr, transmitting the salaf on this point: "the totality here is the totality of the *ajmaʿūn* — each of them came knowing; his was the foot, and the foot was never compelled."

**The Tryst That Is a Reunion: Who Will Be There**

The word *mawʿid* implies the Beloved is expected — and the tradition reads the verse with a shudder that is not punishment but *meeting*: the people of the Fire will find at their tryst, face to face, the one they followed — "and when they see him whom they used to associate with Him, they will say, our Lord, these are our partners whom we used to invoke besides you — and they will throw back to them the word: you are liars" (16:86); the leaders disown the led, the led disown the leaders (2:166-167: "the ones who were followed disown the followers, they see the punishment and their ties are severed; the followers say: would that we had a return — we would disown them as they have disowned us"; and the verse of no-escape's twin: "it is the same for them whether they endure or not: you are only being recompensed for what you used to do" — 52:16), and the verse's *ajmaʿūn* makes the reunion total: no partial attendance, no delegation, no proxy — "and for each a separate portion" (v. 44) will say: together in the place, alone in the share — which is the sūrah's last word on crowds: the crowd is a venue, not a defendant; on the Day the "all together" means *everyone present*, and the "separate portion" means *nobody covered*. The source's quotation of the hadith that frames this — "God will speak to every one of you with no intermediary" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿAdī b. Ḥātim) — belongs on the verse's last syllable: the tryst is real, the appointment is one-to-one, and the only thing that makes it Hell or Garden is which party the worshipper *expected* when the date arrived.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:44

> **Seven gates it has, and for each gate is appointed a separate portion of them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Seven Doors Downward: The Answer to the Ladder Up**

*lahā sabʿatu abwābin li-kulli bābin minhum juzʾun maqṭūʿ* — "it has seven gates; for each gate, a portion of them, cut off." The verse answers the sūrah's own opening fantasy. Quraysh had dared the messenger with a demand for ascent — show us angels, or climb: "We would not believe in your ascent save that you drop down upon us a book we can read" (17:93's taunt is the same register as v. 14's demand), and the sūrah's first act closed the sky: "We made the heavens a fortified roof, and a guard against every rebellious devil; they eavesdrop but are pelted by a piercing flame" (vv. 14-18). Here is the reversal stated in architecture: the sky whose seven strata were *sealed* against him has, below, its mirror — seven gates *open* for his party, and the sūrah's geometry is its sermon: ascent by pride is refused; descent by following is numbered. The *abwāb* (gates) of v. 44 are even the same noun as the "gates of every thing" that *istidrāj* opens before the seizure (6:44) — the doors of the reprieve are the doors of the Fire, seen from the inside and the outside; and of the gate-image of 7:40 ("the gates of heaven do not open for the deniers") this verse is the completion: the denial that forfeits every upper door does not forfeit the lower ones — they are appointed.

**"Seven Gates": The Names the Tradition Arranges**

The Qur'an itself never lists the seven; the sūrah states the number, and the *tafsīr* of al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Abī Ḥātim preserves the exercise the verses invite: the commentators collect the seven names God uses for the Abode in His own speech — *Jahannam* (named in this very verse), *al-Laẓā* (70:15-16 "a fire that strips the scalp"), *al-Ḥuṭama* (104:4-7 "that which crushes... which rises over the hearts"), *al-Saʿīr* (37:5-6, the blaze that "envelops"), *Saqar* (74:15-30 "that leaves nothing, that scorches the skin, over which nineteen attend"), *al-Jaḥīm* (the blazing, e.g. 18:29), and *al-Ḥāwiya* (101:9-11 "a pit whose mother is a blast of fire") — and arrange them as levels matched to classes of sin: the tradition's schema runs from the lowest for the outright deniers up to *Jahannam* reserved, in the reports Ibn Jarīr transmits from ʿIkrima and Ibn Zayd, for the *muwaḥḥidūn* whose faith outweighs their crimes. The exegetical honesty this sūrah trains requires one note: the *arrangement* is reading, not revelation; no ḥadīth of the Prophet ﷺ fixes the order, and the source flags the lists (Bg, JJ, Kl, Ṭ) as "part of traditional Islamic eschatological belief," i.e., the community's diagram of what its Book states in numbers. What the *number* does state — as the source's note says, "levels in accordance with the degree to which they followed him" — is the doctrine the sūrah has been building from v. 32's record: nobody is sent to a place he did not help build.

**"A Portion, Cut Off" (*Juzʾun Maqṭūʿ*): Contract Language on the Day of Meeting**

The two words are legal. *Juzʾ* is a *share* — the same term the Qur'an uses when it says of the angels' partisans, "assign them a portion of the earth," and when the mockers' contract with God is quoted in v. 4's idiom; *maqṭūʿ* — cut, severed, decided — is the Arabic of a *settled* share: and the Qur'an uses its cousin-verb for the end of litigation: when the wronged people of every sūrah meet their term, "the last trace of the people who did wrong was *cut off* (*quṭiʿa dābuhum*), and praise be to God, Lord of the worlds" (6:45) — the same *qaṭʿ* that severs the share, in this sūrah's own idiom, closes the case, and the verse's hamd after the cut is the Ādam-cycle's first word returned: the prostration of praise stands where the prostration of pride was refused. Al-Rāzī's gloss names the point: a *cut* portion admits no increase and no appeal, and it is *individuated* — the crowd enters through a gate, but the share is personal; hence the tradition's consolation threaded even through this verse's severity: at the lowest level of the lowest gate, the hadith the collections transmit ensures the last exits — "there will come forth from the Fire a man whom God will turn His face toward; when God turns toward him, he will say: my Lord, whatever You turned me away from, You have forgiven" — the report in Muslim, from Anas b. Mālik, belongs precisely to the *muwaḥḥidūn's* gate, and it proves the sūrah's whole exception-theology to the last soul: the portions are cut for the *followers* — the *following* was the crime, and the *turning* dissolves it.

**The Justice of Mirrors: Degrees Downward Answer Degrees Upward**

The verse's symmetry with what follows is the sūrah's final act of balance: seven gates, each with its cut portion below; and immediately, v. 45 — "the reverent are amid gardens and springs," which the next verses will furnish with couches, removed rancor, no fatigue and no expulsion. The architecture mirrors, and the *tafsīr* tradition (the source's note on vv. 43-44: ʿAjāj's "in accordance with the degree to which they followed him") gives the law of the mirror: as the downward shares are cut by degree of following, so the upward ones are given by degree of turning — "to each are degrees of what they have done" (6:132, the verse that makes both buildings one blueprint); and the Fātiḥah's phrase for the topmost floor and this sūrah's phrase for the bottommost are the same grammar of apportionment: *juzʾun maqṭūʿ* is the punishment's "appointed portion," and the Garden's people too "have a *juzʾun* — an appointed share of the fruit" (52:27, the sūrah that pairs itself with this one). The lesson of the two mirrors is the whole Ādam-cycle's: the gates are not a torture's inventory but a *receipts* system — each door records a class of choices, and the verse's silent mercy is the tradition's loudest reading: nobody is at a gate he did not choose, and the lowest gate's chosen crowd is, by the Qur'an's own law of names — "his authority is only over those who take him as master and those who take him as partner" (16:100, quoted at the last verse) — *volunteers*, which is exactly why each of them can, while the term lasts, walk back out through the only door that was ever cut *open* rather than cut *off*: "except Your servants, the purified" (v. 40).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:45

> **Truly the reverent shall be amidst gardens and springs:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Verb in the Sentence: The Grammar of Already-Being-There**

*Inna al-muttaqīna fī jannātin wa-ʿuyūn* — "the reverent, truly, are amid gardens and springs." The sentence contains no verb: the prepositional phrase of place carries the whole clause, and the *tafsīr* tradition's first observation is that the verse does not say "We will admit them" or "they shall enter" — it says *they are in*. The tenselessness is doctrine: the Fire was introduced through a *tryst* still ahead (v. 43's *mawʿid*, a date on the calendar of the reprieved), while the Garden is stated as a state already holding its people, as though the verdict for the reverent were never a sentence passed but a homecoming recognized. The grammarians' idiom — the nominal sentence expressing *thubūt*, permanence — marks the contrast the sūrah has engineered: everything said of the lost in this cycle has been future-tilted (oaths, terms, appointments, the Day's verdicts), while the first word of the saved is a present. The source's note that the verse is verbatim 51:15 ("indeed the reverent are in gardens and springs") belongs here too: the two sūrahs that open with the *ḍāḍ*-oaths of revelation's authenticity (al-Dhāriyāt swears "by the winds that scatter," this one "by the Book that makes things clear," v. 1) close their Meccan arguments with the same sentence — the reward-line is shared because the argument is one: revelation is true, therefore the tryst is real, therefore the reverent *are*.

**The Plural That Reverses the Seven: Gardens Without Gates**

Set the verse against v. 44 and the architecture answers itself. There: one abode (*jahannam*), seven gates, and "for each gate a cut-off portion" — real estate, apportioned, counted, sealed. Here: "gardens and springs" — plural, unnumbered, no gate, no parcel, no division: the verse's two nouns are *jannāt* (the plural of garden, each an enclosed whole) and *ʿuyūn* (the plural of *ʿayn*, spring-eye: the sources themselves, not channels). The economy of shares is replaced by the economy of presence: what needed a *juzʾ maqṭūʿ* below is, above, simply *being among* — the commentators note that even the preposition differs: the people of the Fire are *assigned to* gates (*lahā*), the people of the Garden are *in* gardens (*fī*), containment exchanged for assignment: one is placed *into* a verdict, the other lives *inside* a gift. And the springs answer the sūrah's own hydrology: "We sent the winds fertilizing and sent down from the sky water and gave you drink from it — and you are not the treasurers of it" (vv. 22-23): there, water came by measure, through sky, withheld or given as test, the treasuries belonging to God (v. 21); here, water rises under them, source-eyes with no sky above them and no drought in the plumbing — the final refutation of the starvation-theology of vv. 6-15's mockers, who asked for pieces of heaven dropped on them: the Garden needs no weather, and the One who was not their treasurer in the world is, in the next abode, simply their taps.

**"The Reverent" — The Class the Whole Sūrah Has Been Building**

Who are *al-muttaqūn*, arriving unannounced at v. 45? The tradition reads the sūrah's whole first movement as their portrait, and the word carries its derivation: *waqā*, to shield — the reverent are those who built a cover between themselves and what burns; which is exactly what this Ādam-cycle has been about from the clay onward. The verse's placement lets the sūrah define them without definition — a class named, not listed — and the previous twenty verses have already done the listing by negation: the reverent are not the refuser-who-reasoned (vv. 30-33), not the oathsman (vv. 37-39), not the follower (*man tabiʿa-ka*, v. 42), and they *are* the exception-class of v. 40 (*mukhlaṣūn*) under their other name: *muttaqūn*, because purity and protection are one road's two directions, the inward-facing name and the outward-facing. This is why the verse needs no entry-ceremony: the reverent were the sūrah's subject all along — vv. 1-2's "the verses of the Book and a Qur'an that makes clear" — a Book whose own opening sūrah had named its single purpose "guidance for the reverent" (2:2) — and the moment the cycle of the enemy closes, the sentence that opened the sūrahh's rhetoric of the Book is simply *executed*: what was announced as the Book's purpose (v. 1-2's "verses of the Book and a Qur'an that makes clear") turns out to have been a deed already done for the reverent, v. 45 reading as v. 1's fulfillment, the *āyāt* ending where they began, in the plural: gardens, springs, verses — all enclosed, all watered, all already true.

**Application: The Present Tense of Promise**

The verse trains one reflex: read reward as *thubūt*, not as *tarqīb* — permanence, not escalation. The psychology the sūrah has just dissected (the reprieved who treat duration as possession, v. 37's *min al-munẓarīn*) has its cure here: the reverent do not *accumulate* gardens; they are placed *among* them, which means the believer's posture toward the promise is not the miser's (counting parcels) but the guest's (accepting presence). Two practices follow the verse's grammar. First, when fear of the Fire's gates rises in recitation, close the pair the way the sūrah closed it — never v. 44 without v. 45: the counted doors below, the uncounted gardens above, and in between exactly one life of *taqwā*, which is the name of the *choice*, not of the perfection (the verse says "the reverent," not "the never-wrong," and v. 40's exception already taught that the purified are the turning, not the stainless). Second, the springs' quiet theology — sustenance without means — is a lesson for *now*: the same God whose treasuries v. 21 withheld from human hands is teaching, by this verse's water-from-below, that provision was never the sky's or the soil's but a courtesy that can be discontinued without weather; gratitude (*shukr*, the sūrah's own closing worship, v. 99) is the recognition of that courtesy — and gratitude is a spring: it rises from under a person, and needs no permission from above. The Garden is stated without a verb, and so the verse's final instruction is the strangest and the kindest: *live as if already in* — the preposition, not the parade.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:46

> **"Enter them in peace, secure"—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Imperatives, Opposite Directions: *Udkhū* Against *Ukhruj***

*udkhūhā bi-salāmin āminīn* — "Enter them in peace, secure." The cycle turns on a pair of imperatives, and a reader moving verse by verse feels the hinge before the commentators name it: at v. 34 the only verb of motion God had ever addressed to a rational being in this sūrah was *ukhruj* — "Go forth!" — exit, without return-ticket; here the same divine speech issues *udkhū* — "enter," with the pronoun of the gardens attached (*-hā*): enter *them*. One word rewrites the whole scene of the fall: Ādam's descent was an exit-with-hope, Iblīs's an exit-without; the Garden's entrance is announced in the grammatical voice of return, and the source's orbit preserves the salaf's line on it: "the one who was sent down is invited up; the Door he lost is where he is met, and the greeting he was denied is spoken first." The verb takes no object of fear: not "enter after judgment," not "enter if weighed" — the entry is stated as a *word*, because the sūrah has already spent nineteen verses on the courtroom; v. 46 is what a courtroom is *for*: the dismissal of the defendants into a home. And the pronoun's plurality — *udkhū*, enter *them* (the gardens plural) — matches v. 45's unnumbered abundance: gates were cut off and numbered below (v. 44); entrances here are as many as the gardens, and none of them says "go forth."

**"In Peace": *Salām* as Word, Name, and Welcome**

*bi-salāmin*: the *bāʾ* is of attendant circumstance — entering *in* peace, clothed in it — and the tradition treats the word as an address with three senders, which the source's note catalogues: God Himself greeting them ("the King saluting them," the report in Muslim that when the people of the Garden have entered, God asks them, "Do you wish me to increase you?" — "And what could increase you after You have lit your faces toward You?" — and the lifting of the veil is the greeting's own final form, the increase being the Face); the angels as the carriers of the address, "those who die in goodness — the angels enter upon them: *salāmun ʿalaykum* by what you used to do" (16:32); and the self-generated peace of the exited world — "their greeting therein: *salām*, and its end: *al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*" (10:10). Root-wise, *salām* and *islām* and *salāma* (soundness, freedom from defect) are one family: the entry is *into safety* from everything the sūrah has enumerated as the human condition under respite — the devil's adornment (v. 39), the tryst (v. 43), fatigue and expulsion (v. 48), and above all the *khawf*: the fear that made the reverent reverent. The source's note on the Abode of Peace (6:127, 10:25) completes the name-theology: *al-Salām* is one of God's names (59:23), Paradise is "His house of peace," and the greeting they are met with is, in the deepest reading, an echo of the Greeter: "in the company of those they love, receiving the greeting of the King of Peace" is the tradition's *tafsīr* phrase for 25:75's *salāman bi-mā ṣabartum*.

**"Secure" (*Āminīn*): The Word That Ends the Devil's Entire Business**

*Āminīn* — the plural active participle of *amina*, to be in trust, free of fear — is this cycle's final refutation, aimed at the one clause of Iblīs's oath that still hung in the air: "I will *mislead* them" (*la-ugwiyannahum*, v. 39) — a threat whose entire force is *fear*: the ghayy works by making the future terrifying and the present a shelter. The security-word answers it with a totality that matches his totality: his was *ajmaʿīn* (all of them); the reply is *āminīn* (all-safely, permanently — the form of the participle, unlike his verb, names a *state*, not an act). The commentators note the pairing with v. 48 as a two-lock: *āminīn* here is security *from* what may come (the future), and "they will not be expelled" there is security *in* what has come (the abode) — entry and tenancy both guaranteed, the only lease in creation without landlord or term. And the fear-loop is already drawn in the sūrah's own middle: when the angels entered Abraham's house and he conceived fear of them (*innī minkum wijl*, v. 53), their first word was a greeting — *salāman*, "we greet you with peace" — and their second the negation of despair, *lā tablā*, "do not despair" — and their third the news of a knowing boy (vv. 53-54). The eschatological *āminīn* is that angelic sentence spoken at the last gate: fear negated, news given, the house made a threshold of the Garden. The reverent were promised that "no fear upon them, and they shall not grieve" (2:38); the entering are *told* they are safe — and the meeting of the two verses is the sūrah's whole psychology of *taqwā*: it was never terror of punishment, but the discipline that ends in being finished with fear.

**The Greeting as Geography: How the Saved Re-enter**

The traditions of the Hour describe the entry itself with a detail this verse licenses, at the level of the collections the sūrah's commentators cite: when the people of the Garden pass the Bridge (*ṣirāṭ*, this verse's road now crossed), they are held at a place between Paradise and Fire where lingering wrongs between them are settled — "until, when they are purified, they are admitted to the Garden" (al-Bukhārī, from Abū Hurayra — the *qaṇṭara* report, in which they are purified ["huddhibū"] before admission) — which is our v. 47's *nazaʿ al-ghill* placed, by the tradition, *before* the entrance our verse announces: the "Enter them in peace" is spoken to hearts from which the rancor has already been drawn, so that the greeting finds no wound to heal and becomes pure welcome. The security-word then reads as the answer to the very first society of the sūrah — Ādam's children, the quarrel of the two sons (5:27-31, the first murder under the respite): the Garden is the place where the story of Cain ends, because *āminīn* forbids the only plot its people could stage. This is the verse's application to the reader before it is anyone's future: the road the sūrah calls straight (v. 41) is walked toward a door whose frame already says, to the one who enters it, the two words that every human heart has waited to hear from every mouth that ever wronged it: *in peace. secure.* The hadith's one phrase on the last threshold — "the people of Paradise will enter Paradise and the people of the Fire the Fire" — is the Day's *ukhruj/udkhū* completed; and the sūrah, which heard the first word spoken in anger at the beginning of the human story, saves the last for a greeting, which is how the Qur'an's longest Ādam-cycle can be summarized in its two final verbs: *go forth* — *enter*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:47

> **We shall remove whatever rancor lies within their breasts—as brothers, upon couches, facing one another.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Surgery First: What the Garden Is Mostly Made Of**

*wa-nazaʿnā mā fī ṣudūrihim min ghillin* — "and We shall draw out whatever is in their breasts of rancor." The verse's architecture startles the reader into its doctrine: the first act enumerated inside the Garden is not a giving but a *taking-away*, and the thing removed is not a punishment but a sickness — the *ghill*, the clenched resentment that keeps its shape in the chest (the root gh-l-l is of twisted cords and bridles that cut the mouth: hatred coiled and tightening). Al-Rāzī's observation, quoted in the source, is the hinge: the people of the Garden are cured of the *memory of being wronged* — because the real barrier to joy is never the absence of pleasures but the presence of the ledger, and every human happiness is leak-proof only until someone else's good arrives; so the All-Merciful empties the chest before filling the cup. And the verb *nazaʿa* (a pulling-out of what has *adhered*) treats the rancor as if it had grown there — which, the moralists of the tradition say, is exactly what rancor does, silently adding a ring each year the grudge is kept. The Qur'an's prior diagnosis of the same disease is the verse the source pairs here: "whoever is protected against his own *shuḥḥ* — the greediness of the soul — those are the successful" (59:9; and 64:16), the Anṣār's generosity with the Muhājirūn being, in the sūrah's vocabulary, the surgery *pre-performed* in this life.

**"As Brothers": The State-Word That Ends the First Story**

*ikhwānan* — brothers, in the accusative of manner: the condition they enter *as*. The word closes the sūrah's entire argument about the Ādam-family by naming its cure. The story after the fall began with two sons and a murder over acceptance (5:27-31), and every destroyed people in this sūrah died of the same pathology read upward: envy of the chosen (the *bashar*-sneer of v. 33 was envy dressed as physics), the elite's rage at the prophet followed by the weak (vv. 10-13), the lust-rage of Lot's town at his guests (vv. 67-70): each *qawm* here is a brotherhood that failed. So the Garden's definition — brothers, upon couches, facing one another — is the anti-thesis of the whole sūrah: the couches (*surur*, thrones raised on the garden's plain) give elevation *without* domination, which is the precise reversal of Iblīs's claim (vv. 31-33: "I am better; rank is a ladder and obedience a degradation"). A class of raised thrones with no ladder on it — the tradition reads the state-word *ikhwānan* as the verse's answer to every "better than" in the Ādam-cycle: the comparison that damned the fire-animal is, in the Garden, not forbidden but *obsolete*, its material removed with the *ghill*.

**"Facing One Another": The Geometry of the Healed Heart**

*mutaqābilūn* — the participle of mutual facing, and the *tafsīr* tradition's finest note is that in every worldly gathering, faces are spent or defended: one looks *at* the rival, *past* the stranger, *down* on the inferior, *up* at the richer — sight itself is a hierarchy, which is why the *ghill* and the *surur* share a single verse: grudges live in the corners of vision, and the Garden removes the corners along with the arithmetic. Al-Rāzī extends the point theophanically (the source quotes him): freed from the limitations of the body, the souls become lights reflecting the divine lights upon one another, each receiving from the Face and giving to the faces, so that no one stands *between* anyone and the Source — horizontal equality, vertical glory, both fully true, the geometry every human community wanted and no community ever built. And the sūrah adds its own detail for the builders of the *next* verse: "facing one another" also cancels the sūrah's *own* first society's body-language — the turning-away of the mockers (v. 12's *muʿriḍ*, he who turns his back: "We remove the veil of disdain," the source's phrase belongs here) and the backs of Lot's men (v. 61-77's scene of guests besieged, a house whose doors all faced outward against the mob): the Garden's faces face *in*, and the mob is outside the wall of the chest itself, disinvited forever.

**Application: The Foretaste Prescription**

Because the Garden's first gift is a *removal*, the tradition treats this verse as a prescription for this life, and three medicines of the *ghill* are prophetic and explicit. First, the law: "do not envy one another, do not outbid one another, do not hate one another, do not turn your backs on one another — and be, O servants of God, brothers" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas b. Mālik): the hadith's last word is our verse's state-word, commanding the believer to *begin* here what the Garden completes there — note also that its four prohibitions match the sūrah's four scenes of failure (envy, hoarding, rage, turning-away), so the hadith reads as the *tafsīr* of our verse in negative form. Second, the duʿāʾ the Qur'an itself prescribes for the healed generation: "those who came after them say: our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in the faith, and place not in our hearts *ghill* for those who believe — truly You are most kind, most merciful" (59:10): the verse that *asks* for the *nazaʿ* is already the *nazaʿ* begun, and whoever prays this daily, the sūrah promises, dies on the operating table's safe side. Third, the discipline of forgetting: the scholars note that *ghill* survives only where the wrong is re-remembered, and the Garden's people are given the oblivion of injury without the loss of justice — which is why the *mā* of the verse is total ("*whatever* is in their breasts"): the cure is thorough, not partial, and partial cures are what worldly communities keep mistaking for forgiveness. And the sūrah's own practical summary of the verse is this: in the Garden no one will ever again need to be told "he is not above you" — because no one will be able to say it at all, even in his chest.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:48

> **No weariness shall befall them therein; nor shall they be expelled therefrom.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Locks on the Door: What the Verse Denies**

*lā yamassuhum hāhiha naṣabun wa-mā hum minhā bi-mukhrajiyn* — "no fatigue touches them *here*, and they will not be driven out of it." The verse is a double negation with the structure of a deed of guarantee, and it closes the two fears every tenant in history has known: the fear of one's own body (fatigue, *naṣab*: the exhaustion that accumulates in the limbs and the mind until pleasure itself becomes labor), and the fear of the landlord (eviction: the eviction-notice that hangs over every worldly habitation, from the tent to the palace). The first clause cancels the former: *naṣab* is the noun of spent effort — the same word the sūrah's cousin-verse puts in the mouth of the sūrah's own patient, Ayyub: "the devil has *touched* me with fatigue and chastisement" (*massaniya'sh-shayṭānu bi-naṣabin wa-ʿadhāb*, 38:41) — and the Garden's sentence is the mirror of that complaint, noun for noun and verb for verb: "no fatigue shall *touch* them" (*lā yamassuhum*): what the devil could do to the body of the prophet in this life is, in the next, physically impossible: the verb of *touch* (mass) itself retired. The second clause cancels the latter: *bi-mukhrajiyn* — the passive participle of the very verb with which the enemy was cast out at v. 34 (*ukhruj*) — the grammar making the point: the people of the Garden are not *strong enough* to stay; the Owner of the house is *not the kind* to expel, which is the sūrah's whole doctrine of security (v. 46's *āminīn*) in its legal form: the security of the Garden is not a fact about them, it is a fact about Him, sealed in the verse's next-door neighbor in another sūrah: "the word is not changed before Me, and I never wrong the servants" (50:29).

**"No Fatigue *Here*": The Near-Pointer at the Far Gate**

*Hāhiha* — "here," the feminine near-demonstrative ("this place") — is the word the commentators stop on: the verse is describing an abode whose arrival is still, for the reciter, future and far, yet the pointer is *near*: "in *this* place," as though the one speaking were standing at the gate and the listeners were already seated. Al-Zamakhsharī's note on such usages ("the far made near in depiction, so that it is beheld and pointed at") applies perfectly to this sūrah's rhetoric — the whole Ādam-cycle has been narrated in the present-tense of the divine voice (*qāla*, *qāla*, *qāla*) — and here the verse breaks time with a pointer: the Garden is *this* place, not *that* place.
