The *tafsīr* of al-Ṭabarī adds the scene the pointer frames: at the entry, the people of the Garden hear the address of welcome before they hear anything of their own, so that the nearness in *hāhiha* is the nearness of the Welcomer — a guest is shown *this* room, and the room is named while he is being brought in. And the near-pointer works its practical miracle on the reader of the sūrahh: the two negations do not describe a distant reward but a *visited* one, the fatigue already lifting for whoever recites it standing in prayer — the sūrah that began "these are the verses of the Book" ends its Paradise section with the verse that makes the Book a door held open.

**The Third Death: What Else the Verse Excludes by Silence**

The sūrah's neighbors complete this verse's inventory, and the pairing is the tradition's: the people of the Garden will not taste *death* except the first one (44:56: "they will not taste therein of death except the first death — and He will protect them from the Fire"), will not *die* nor be *asked to leave* (38:50: "abiding forever — no death for them therein, ever, nor are they to be expelled"), and will not feel *fatigue or faintness* (35:35: "no fatigue touches them therein, and no utterance of complaint [lughab] — the word of *mental weariness* — touches them"), and they say: "praise be to God who removed grief from us." So our verse is the *middle* of the three-fold charter — the three enemies are death, exhaustion, eviction, and the Garden's deed excludes all three at once, each sūrah naming them in its own company of witnesses. The tradition's consolation at this point is famous and belongs at the verse: when the people of the Garden have been settled, death itself will be brought forth in the form of a ram and *slaughtered between Paradise and the Fire* — al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra — which is the image this verse's two negations demand: the sentence "they will not be driven out" requires that *driving out* cease to exist, and "no fatigue touches them" requires that the thing fatigue comes from — the long wear down of *time in a body* — be ended forever; the slaughtered ram is the verse's final verb.

**Application: Rest Is a Promise, Not a Reward**

The verse's first instruction is about the human condition itself: *naṣab* is universal here, and the sūrah names it as the *first* gift's negation because every worker in this life is a tenant of the double fear this verse dissolves — of failing strength and of being moved out. The tradition's reading of the two negations gives the believer a discipline for each. Against the fear of eviction: "you are, even now, in a house you did not build and cannot lose by lease" — the source's phrase, and it lands on the sūrah's own economy: the treasuries are His (v. 21), the abode is His, and the only eviction in the system is the one the resident signs himself (v. 42's *man tabiʿa-ka*) — which means permanence, here as in the Garden, is a *relation*, not a structure: whoever is *His* is not *expellable*, because expulsion requires a hand and the Hand is on both sides of the door. Against the fear of fatigue: the sūrah teaches the *ethics of rest* rather than the economics of it — rest in this life is not the opposite of work but the *promise's down payment*, which is why the believers' worldly worship is the pattern: Ayyub — whose complaint gave our verse its vocabulary — is sealed in Ṣād with the words that make tiredness irrelevant: "We found him patient; excellent was the servant, ever-turning (*awwāb*)" (38:44), and the verse's promise reads as the answer to exactly that style of exhaustion: fatigue is *not* the measure — endurance is; the tiredness the Garden cancels is the *despair* of tiredness, the wear that says "I cannot go on," which is what the slaughtered death was made to take away: *hāhiha* — recite the pointer here, in the place of your own labor, and let it convert the exhaustion of a tenant into the rest of a guest.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:49

> **Tell My servants that I am indeed the Forgiving, the Merciful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Answers the One Before It: The Source's Quote of al-Qushayrī**

*nabbiʾ ʿibādi annī ana al-Ghafūru'r-Raḥīm* — "inform My servants that I, I Myself, am the Forgiving, the Merciful." The verse's placement is its first word: v. 48 has just told the sinners of the age that the Garden is a place where no fatigue touches and no eviction reaches — and the hearts of those who heard it are, the source records, the broken ones: everyone who counted their own tiredness and their own record against that offer. Al-Qushayrī's phrase, quoted in the commentary, answers them directly: "if I am thankful and generous toward the obedient, then I am *forgiving and merciful* toward the sinful — the door is not the same door, and it is not locked." The verse is thus the sūrah's own *transition of register*: from the third person of the law (vv. 43-48, *they* shall feel, *they* shall not be expelled) to the first person of the invitation — "tell *My servants*" — and in that shift the sūrah does what the whole Qur'an does at its highest moments: the description becomes address, the courtroom becomes the anteroom of the King.

**"ʿIbādī": The Possessive of Tenderness**

The verse says *ʿibādi*, not *ʿibādi-hum*: the servants are *His*, in the Prophet's own mouth being told to announce it — and the commentators hear the intimacy of the ʿibād with the possessive of closeness, as in the most visited verse of the Book: "say: O My servants who have transgressed against yourselves — do not despair of God's mercy; He forgives sins all together — surely He is the Forgiving, the Merciful" (39:53). The *yāʾ* of *ʿibādi* is here — that is the tradition's insistence — the same letter that Iblīs never achieved in this sūrah: he said *ʿibāda-ka* twice (vv. 40, 42), "Your servants," speaking of the elect as a class he cannot touch; God says *ʿibādi* about the *sinful*, as a class He will not release. The two possessives, read across the cycle, are the sūrah's entire gospel in miniature: the enemy concedes a limit ("*Your* servants are out of my reach"); the Lord removes the limit ("*My* servants — even the sinners — are within My pardon"). And the imperative *nabbiʾ* carries the weight of *nabaʾ*: news — the word the sūrah has used for the destructions it came to warn about (v. 46's "the news of the guests" is the same noun; the *anbaʾ* of v. 16-18's guarding) — now turned to the only news the broken can hear: not "be forgiven if," but *inform them that I am*: the names are stated as *present possession*, not future offer — the grammar of *ana al-Ghafūr*: "I *am* the Forgiving," without a condition clause attached, the two divine names (Ghafūr, Raḥīm) both in the intensive patterns: much-forgiving, continuously-mercifying — *faʿūl* for the covering of sins, *faʿīl* for their non-punishment — and the pair covers the double want of the guilty soul: to be *erased*, and to be *gently treated after erasure*.

**"Ana": The Emphatic Separation the Reciters Felt**

*annī ana* — "that I — *Myself* — am." The redundant independent pronoun after the first-person pronoun is the sūrah's rhetoric of exclusivity, and the exegetes read it in two directions at once: (1) *I, and none other*: the gods of v. 14's demand and the lords of the *shirk* mocked in vv. 6-15 cannot forgive, cannot do mercy: exclusivity of *attribute* answering the exclusivity of the *path* (v. 41: "this is a straight path *upon Me*"; the pardon, too, is *upon Me* — a promise, and the Just One does not break His promise); and (2) *I Myself and not My angels*: in the master-supplication of seeking pardon , the assurance the Prophet ﷺ put in the mouth of every seeker: "O God, You are my Lord; there is no god but You; You created me and I am Your servant; I am in Your covenant as much as I am able; I seek refuge in You from the evil of what I have done; I know the favor You have given me, and I know my sin — forgive me, for none forgives sins but You" (al-Bukhārī, from Shaddād b. Aws), the prayer whose closing line is this verse's whole content in a single voice: the *ana al-Ghafūr* said back to Him as "lā yaghfiru'dh-dhunūba illā ant." The *tafsīr* tradition adds the reciter's observation  that the *lām*-oath of v. 49's partner (v. 50's *wa-inna ʿadhābī*) frames the two verses as a *single commission* — "inform them of *both*" — and that the second half arrives *unannounced*, without *nabbiʾ*: which is the subject of the next verse's commentary; here the point stands: the verse that opens the sūrah's final act is the only news the Prophet ﷺ was ever commanded to deliver with the *First-Person* as its content.

**Application: The Sentence to Recite Standing Up**

The report the source carries here is the hadith of the ninety-nine — "if the children of Ādam did not commit sin, God would remove that people and bring a people who sin, then seek forgiveness, and He would forgive them" (Muslim, from Anas) — the tradition's sharpest correction of the perfectionism the Garden-verses might have bred: the *ʿibād* whose security the sūrah has just sworn are, in this life, *those who ask*; the difference between Ādam and Iblīs was enacted, not invented, and v. 49 is God's line in that enactment. The practical of the verse is its own duʿāʾ-legacy: the Prophet ﷺ taught ʿĀʾisha the words for the Night of Power — "O God, You are Pardoning, You love pardon, so pardon me" (al-Tirmidhī, from ʿĀʾisha) — and the verse's grammar sits behind every form of it: the request is addressed to a *Name* ("You are the Pardoner"), grounded in the *love* of the act — "God loves those who turn in repentance, and loves those who purify themselves" (2:222), and closed with the bare verb ("pardon me") that leaves no condition: *nabbiʾ* says the same in three words: *tell them: I am.* So the application this verse writes on the believer's day: the sūrah ends its Paradise section with the fatigue cancelled (v. 48) and its *human* section begins here with the *record* cancelled — which means every entry in the ledger of the tired is now answerable with one verse: the one in which God introduces, in His own first person, the two names that the broken were made to hear. He said it first; we only say it after.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:50

> **And that My Punishment is the painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Without Its Own Command: Why *Nabbiʾ* Is Not Repeated**

*wa-anna ʿadhābī huwa al-ʿadhābu al-alīm* — "and that My punishment is the painful punishment." The balāgha scholars count this as one of the sūrah's great silences: the verb of announcement ("tell My servants," v. 49) is *not* renewed for v. 50 — the warning rides the command to bring the good news, appended, not commissioned. The tradition draws the rule of divine speech from it: God *orders* the announcement of mercy and *permits* the mention of punishment; the promise is a mission, the threat a safeguard on the mission — set there so that no one converts v. 49 into a license, never so that v. 49 is forgotten. Ibn ʿĀshūr's reading of the pairing belongs at this verse's head: the two verses are one *khabar* with one verb of reporting, "as the physician who prescribes the sweet syrup and lets the medicine follow in the same glass." And the sūrah's own architecture confirms the arrangement: these are the fiftieth and forty-ninth verses of a sūrah of ninety-nine — the two lines stand at the *center* of the whole, mercy first, warning second, at the heart of a text whose two ends are the Book's clarity (v. 1) and the worship that lasts until certainty (v. 99): whoever maps the sūrah finds its axis neither in threat nor in bliss but in the pair — and a pair whose *order* is itself the message.

**"My Punishment Is *the* Punishment": The Exclusive Sentence**

*huwa al-ʿadhābu al-alīm* — the pronoun of separation plus the double definite gives the grammarians' *ḥaṣr*: "it — He — is *the* painful punishment, and every other pain is its prelude." The sentence relativizes the world: every suffering in sūrah al-Ḥijr — the siege of Lot's guests (vv. 67-70), the Cry that took Thamūd at sunrise (v. 83), the thirst that the mockers' withheld rain will end (v. 22) — is here demoted to a *figure*, and the idiom of the tradition lands the point with the Prophet's ﷺ own scale: "the mildest person of punishment in the Fire on the Day of Resurrection will be told: if you possessed everything under the sun, would you ransom yourself with it? — he will say yes; and He will say: you were upon something easier than that — that you not associate anything with Me — and I would have admitted you into Paradise" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Ibn Masʿūd): the *lightest* of the verse's "punishment" out-weighs the heaviest of the world's — which is the verse's exclusive article made empirical. The converse mercy of the same hadith is the sūrah's whole news (v. 49) stated from the other side: one clause of association, one clause of admission — the *only* free thing in the ledger.

**Al-Alīm: The Punishment That Knows**

The final word is a *tajnīs* the tradition never tires of pointing out: the root of *alīm* here is read by the lexicographers as pain (*ʿalam*, the wound that burns — "a punishment that *feels* itself"), but the same root is *ʿilm*, knowledge — and this sūrah has already sworn its own verdict on the Knower of the minutes of every soul: "truly it is thy Lord who will gather them — assuredly He is Wise, Knowing" (v. 25, ten verses upstream) — making the ambiguity a sentence of justice: *ʿadhābun ʿalīm* is a punishment that *knows*: no blast falls blind, no torment is misdirected — every stroke lands on a hand that chose it (v. 42's *man tabiʿa-ka*, the only door to the Fire). Grammarians call the device *majāz al-isnād* — the quality of the Inflictor is transferred onto the infliction, as Arabic says "a knowing poem" for "a poem of the knowing one": the punishment is called *ʿalīm* because its Lord is; and this is the Qur'an's standing answer to every accusation of divine cruelty in the cycle just closed: the curse, the exile, the seven gates — all of them bear the adjective of *knowledge*, which is to say: none of them is arbitrary; the sentence of v. 32 ("what is the matter with you?") was an *investigation* before judgment from the first, and it ends, in this verse, as an attribute of the judgment itself: *knowing*.

**The Pair As Read by the Salaf: The Two Wings**

The source's note quotes the verse's frame: v. 49 "brings comfort and hope to God's sinful servants" — and the pair (vv. 49-50) became in the hands of the tradition the charter of the believer's *two wings*: "some of the Salaf said: whoever worships God in love alone is a heretic, in hope alone a corrupter, in fear alone a despairer — and the servant of God worships in hope and fear, love and awe," a dictum the Sufi handbooks carry under the name of Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq — and its scriptural anchors are precisely these two verses, in God's own ordering: *hope first, caution second* — because "My mercy precedes My wrath" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra) is a *priority*, not an equality, and the verse that carries the sūrah's last word is 7:156: "My punishment — I visit it upon whom I will; and My mercy encompasses all things": punishment *limited by a will*, mercy *of universal scope*, and the two lines of our pair quoted verbatim in al-Aʿrāf's single verse, the Book glossing itself. The *tafsīr* on our verses adds the pastoral detail the source's orbit preserves: v. 50 was never read without v. 49 preceding, nor v. 49 without v. 50 — and its practical rule for the reciter is the most human of the sūrah's laws: *hope with the verse of pardon, fear with the verse of pain, and neither verse alone* — for they are one news in one mouth: mercy unbounded (7:156), punishment never unjust (v. 25), both announced under a single divine command.

**Application: Reading the Sūrah's Heart**

The verse trains the reader's *sequence*: the warning exists to protect the promise from abuse, not to compete with it — which means the discipline is *order*, not proportion: you do not dilute v. 49 with v. 50 (that is the despair the dictum names as the heresy of the fear-worshipper); you carry v. 50 as the *guard-rail* of v. 49's road (that is the "not-in-vain" the Qur'an teaches: "and whoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord, let him do righteous work and associate none in the worship of his Lord," 18:110 — hope *with* works, the pair again). Concretely: when sin arrives in a believer's life, the sūrah's center says the order of operations — *first* the announcement of the King's name ("I am the Forgiving"), *then* the boundary ("My punishment is the pain"), because the first makes return possible and the second makes it necessary; and when presumption arrives (the "I'll repent later" of the reprieved, the v. 36-39 pathology), the center says *the second* aloud, to save the first from the abuse that would kill it. The two verses, said at the heart of the sūrah, are the two directions of one road: *toward* mercy in hope; *away* from pain in fear — and the sūrah, which opened by declaring that the Book "makes clear," has now finished making its own psychology clear, and will spend the next fifty verses on its demonstration: the guests of Abraham, the town of Lot, the day of gathering — every story to follow is v. 49 and v. 50 *in narration form*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:51

> **And tell them of the guests of Abraham,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second *Nabbiʾ*: Stories as the Execution of the News**

*wa-nabbiʾ-hum ʿan ḍayfi ibrāhīm* — "and inform them about the guests of Abraham." The verb is the sūrah's own: the center-verse commanded *nabbiʾ ʿibādi annī ana al-Ghafūru'r-Raḥīm* (v. 49) — "inform My servants of what I am" — and now, without a break, the same imperative turns from creed to chronicle: inform them *of the guests*. The structure tells the reader how to read all that follows: the three stories of this sūrah's second half (Abraham's guests, Lot's visitors, the people of al-Ḥijr) are not decoration after the theology; they are the theology *performed* — v. 49 (mercy announced) and v. 50 (punishment declared) are precisely what the angels of v. 51 carry in their two hands, and every narrative to vv. 77 and 84 will restate the central pair in plot. This is why the tradition calls these verses *the guests of both news*: the same company of angels brings the glad tiding of a son and the destruction of a city (11:76: "O Abraham, leave this — the command of your Lord has come, and there will come upon them a punishment that cannot be averted" — the sentence in which consolation and annihilation share one angelic mouth), and the believer who has just heard "tell them *that* I am Forgiving" and "and that *My* punishment" now sees *whom* the angels visit and *why*: one pair of feet, two houses, two news — the sūrah's doctrine of *rajaʾ* and *khawf* delivered as a travelogue.

**"Guests": The Most Dangerous and Most Sacred of Visitors**

*ḍayf* — a singular collective: Abraham's "guests," unnamed and unnumbered in this verse, angels wearing human hunger-less bodies, and the source records the tradition's census (three to twelve, with the three of *Dhāriyāt* — Gabriel, Mikāʾīl, Isrāfīl in the *tafsīr* of the two verses — standing over against the twelve of other reports). The word-choice does the ethical work: God's messengers arrive in the *form* of the guest, which is, in the Law the Prophet ﷺ will later seal, the most honored and the most testing of visits: "whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honor his guest according to its right," and "whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him say a good word or remain silent" are the two wings of the sūrah's social chapter, both transmitted in al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayra — and here the sūrah has just *narrated* it: the visit of the unknown guest, the roasted calf brought in haste (11:69), the danger and the blessing sharing one doorstep. The commentators draw the lesson of the *disguise*: God sends His news in the clothes of need, so that the honor a person shows to the stranger at the door is, without his knowing, the honor he pays to the command at the heart — which is why *ḍiyāfa* in the prophetic law is not courtesy but *ʿaqīda*: the guest is the Day of Judgment in miniature, come to the house early.

**Why Abraham, and Why Now: The Friend in the Sūrah of the Enemy**

The sūrah has spent its longest movement on the *enemy* of the human race — his refusal, his oath, his reprieve, his seven gates — and at the first verse of the new movement it names *khalīlu'r-Raḥmān*, the friend of the All-Merciful (4:125: "and God took Abraham as a friend"), as if to complete the portrait-gallery by the counter-face. The choice is architectural: Abraham is the man who in this very sūrah will be told "do not be among the despairs" (v. 55) — the virtue that answers Iblīs's final state (the etymology the source notes for his name: *ablasa* — to despair), and the tradition of *mawadda* — friendship — is precisely a bond *without despair*: the friend was shown the fire and the flood and the knife and the guests, and at every visit he was given news. So the sūrah invites the readers of vv. 30-50 to learn *how to be visited*: Iblīs was in the assembly of the angels and turned it into a court; Abraham was in the presence of the angels and turned it into a house of bread and welcome; the same heavenly visitors, two radically different receptions — and the difference between the two is the whole *ʿilm* of this sūrah compressed: the court asks "what is the matter with you?" (v. 32) and the friend asks "what is your errand?" (v. 57), and the second question is asked *from within* hospitality, the first *from outside* it. The source's cross-references to the four tellings of the guests (11:69-76; 14:35-41; 37:83-113; 51:24-36) are the Qur'an's insistence that this scene *must* be retold until every generation knows its own doorstep is the scene.

**Application: The Theology of the Knock at the Door**

This verse, in the tradition's practice, became the charter of *ḍiyāfa* — and its two lessons are for the reader's ordinary week. First, *read the pair before the news*: since every angelic visit in this sūrah carries glad-tiding and warning together, every real event that arrives at a believer's door should be read as a double-message: what is it *giving*, and what is it *asking* — the calf that was brought for the guests was, unknown to the servants of the house, the receipt for the destruction of a city, and the child promised at this table grows into a prophet, father of prophets (51:28: "We give you the glad tidings of a knowing boy" — the very announcement the guests make in our next verses). The discipline is not mystical: it is the prophetic command cited above — say good or be silent *at the moment of the knock* — because the first words to a guest are the hadith's test of faith and Last-Day. Second, honor the *unnumbered* guest: the verse's refusal to name or count the visitors is the tradition's license against judging the visitor's worth before serving: the sūrah's God honored the disguised messengers in the house of His friend, and the Prophet's law measures the right precisely — "his day and his night; hospitality is three days; and whatever is beyond that is charity" (al-Bukhārī, from Abū Shurayḥ al-Kuʿābī) — which is to say: the surplus is *ṣadaqa*, never a burden to be resented means the reader's practical takeaway: every table laid is a court sitting, every guest a verdict on the host's unseen creed, and every "Peace" at the door answered with a good word or silence begins, at the smallest scale of the sūrah's whole geography, the road that ends at "Enter them in peace, secure" (v. 46).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:52

> **when they entered upon him and said, "Peace!" He said, "Verily of you we are afraid."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Greeting First: How Heaven Opens a Door**

*idh dakhalū ʿalayhi fa-qālū salāman* — "when they entered upon him and said: peace." The verse records the etiquette before the errand, and the *tafsīr* tradition makes the order into a law: the visitors identify themselves with a greeting, not with a credentials — heaven's first word at the door of the friend of God is *salām*, the same word the angels will speak at the doors of the Garden (v. 46: "Enter them *in peace*, secure") and the same the community is taught to plant before every threshold ("spread the greeting among yourselves," Muslim, from ʿAbdullāh b. Salām, in the hadith that joins it to feeding the hungry and prayer by night: "O people, spread peace, feed food, join kinships, and pray in the night while people sleep and enter Paradise in peace"). The sūrah is writing the *adab* of God's own guests so that the reader cannot miss it: every announcement in this book — of a son, of a destruction — begins with "peace," and the prophetic law generalizes the verse: no message of weight is handed over without first securing the person of the recipient, which is why the scholars made *salām* the opening of all speech and its closing ("the prayer ends with the salām" is the Prophet's ﷺ rule, Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī, from ʿAlī), and why the greeting the angels bring here is, in the technical sense, *amān* — safety-passage — as the Qur'an itself glosses in the story's other telling: "their greeting therein is *salām*" (33:44; 36:58).

**"I Am Frightened of You": The Truthful Fear of the Guest-House**

*qāla innī minkum wijl* — "he said: I am, of you, a frightened one." *Wijl* is not ordinary fear but the Qur'an's word for awe — the tremble of the reverent ("those who give what they give with hearts *wājil* — trembling, knowing they will return to their Lord," 23:60, the verse the Prophet ﷺ expounded to ʿĀʾisha: "they are the ones who fast and pray and give *while fearing*," al-Tirmidhī and Mālik's *Muwaṭṭaʾ*, in her report). Abraham's fear here has the specific cause the parallel verses supply: "when he saw their hands reaching not toward it, he formed suspicion of them and felt fear toward them" (51:27: *fa-ankara-hum wa-awjasa min-hum khīfatan*), and in Hūd's telling "they said, do not fear; we are sent to the people of Lot" (11:70), the glad tiding following in the next verse (11:71): the guests refused the roasted calf, and in the society of the desert, a guest who will not eat the host's bread is a man come with an intention hidden under the refusal — the source records the custom exactly: non-participation in the meal was read as hostility. The verse therefore teaches two things at once: the *fear is real* (the prophets feel, they do not dissemble), and the fear is *right* (the man who is given the news of a son and the destruction of a city should tremble at strangers who fast at a feast); what makes it a lesson of *adab* is that the fear is spoken openly and honestly — "I am frightened of you" — not concealed until it misbehaves: the tradition reads the verse as the model of naming one's state at the door, which is why the scholars said the guest may decline what he fears and say why.

**The Refusal of Food: The Angels Who Did Not Eat**

The center of the scene — the untouched calf — the other tellings spin out to its edges, and the sūrah leaves it *between the lines*: the angels came "with a form of men" in the reports of Ibn ʿAbbās transmitted in the *tafsīr*, and they did not eat because, as the commentators say, "they had no need" and, more exactly in the moral reading, because the *eating* of the hosted and the *abstaining* of the host are the whole grammar of the test this sūrah runs on every table: Iblīs's first act against Ādam was food (the tree, vv. 20-26's long economy of "eat of it wherever you will"), and Lot's town will be destroyed at a scene whose first act is the *feeding of guests* he cannot protect (v. 68: "would that I had against you power — or a strong support!"), while Abraham's house is saved through a *refused* meal: the verse classifies the two economies of bread — hospitality that receives, and lust that consumes — and puts the angels' non-eating at the hinge: heaven eats *nothing*, and brings *everything*. The Prophet's ﷺ practice on refusal belongs at the verse's footnote: "when food was brought to him and he knew it had been given as charity, he took a bite; when he knew it was a *gift*, he ate with them" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas's report in the *ṣaḥīḥayn*'s chapter) — the etiquette of *why* the bread came, which is precisely what Abraham read in the silence of the hands that did not reach.

**Application: Read the Guest, Then the Message**

The verse trains the believer's *door-handling* in three moves. First, accept the greeting before demanding the mission: the angels said "peace," and the friend said "welcome" in effect, before "who are you" — the law of the guest hadith above is not politeness but the order of *ʿaqīda* (honor the guest *as* belief in God and the Last Day), and the sūrah has made the order divine: news enters only through a secured person. Second, name the unease honestly and early — "I am frightened of you" is the prophetic etiquette of discomfort at a table where something is wrong: the verse licenses the spoken fear that prevents the concealed suspicion, which is why the scholars read the scene as *the end of private judgment about guests and the beginning of open questioning*. Third, watch what the visitors do *with the food*: the sūrah's theology of the meal — where the enemy of man first worked, and where these angels deliberately declined to work — means the reader's practical verdict on any invitation: what is being *fed* here, and what is being *withheld*? Abraham knew by the hour that the guests who refused his calf were the bearers of two verdicts — a son for him, a ruin for a town: the greatest news in history arrived at a dinner table under an uncomfortable silence, and the friend's response in the next verse — hearing "peace, do not fear" — is the verse's last instruction to every host whose meal goes cold with worry: answer the fear with the greeting you are given, and let the calf wait.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:53

> **They said, "Be not afraid. Truly we bring thee glad tidings of a knowing son."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words for the Same Tremble: *Lā tablā***

*qālū lā tablā* — "they said: do not *tablā*." The word is the sūrah's chosen key, and the lexicographers of the *tafsīr* record both senses the verb carries: "do not be afraid" and "do not despair" — and the commentators of this verse agree the ambiguity is intentional: the guests answer Abraham's *fear* of the strangers (v. 52) and, in the same breath, pre-answer the *despair* the next two verses will confront ("be not among the despairers," v. 55). This double-range is the verse's pedagogy: fear and despair are one disease at two ages — fear is despair in its beginning (the tremble before an unknown at the table), despair is fear grown permanent (the verdict that the future is closed) — and the angelic sentence negates both with one prohibition. The sūrah of Iblīs, who despaired and called his despair a plan, now has, at its first human scene, the command that unmade him possible: *do not despair* — and the source's etymology at vv. 39-40's note (Iblīs from *ablasa*: to be cast into despair) makes the pairing structural: the enemy is the one who *heard* "prostrate" and answered with the long war of the desperate; the friend is the one who *heard* "do not be afraid" and received the impossible son — two hearings of one divine address, two eternities: the verse is the sūrah's hinge between them.

**"A Knowing Son": The Gift Specified by Its Interior**

*innā nubashshiruka bi-ghulāmin ʿalīm* — "we bring you the glad tiding of a boy who *knows*." The wonder of the announcement is not the biology — old age is a hundred obstacles in the other tellings (Sarah's astonishment in 11:71-72; the hundred-and-nineteen-year shoulders in the traditions the source cites at 51:29) — but the adjective: God does not announce "a son" but "a *knowing* son," and the commentators fix the force of *ʿalīm* as the boy's *quality of knowledge* exceeding the years (the form *faʿīl* of constancy: "knowing" as his settled state) and, in the prophetic reading, as the child's *ʿilm* of prophethood and of the covenant he will carry: the promised Isaac is the line through which the *ʿahd* continues, and the sūrah names the heir by the attribute the whole Book is about — knowledge — which is also how the same sūrah defines where revelation is kept: "rather, it is clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge" (29:49). And the *bashshara*-form of the verb carries the tradition's whole psychology of news: *tabshīr* is announcement that *gleams* — the verb of the face brightening at what it has heard — and the hadith the commentators attach to this verse's register is the Prophet's ﷺ commission to every preacher: "make things easy, and do not make them hard; give good tiding, and do not repel" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas) — the angels, the first bearers of *bishāra*, model the command that becomes the umma's law of daʿwa.

**Why the Promise Lands at the Table of Refused Food**

The scene's placement answers a question the verse does not ask: why does the announcement of the covenant's continuation come to a man in the discomfort of an un-eaten meal? The *tafsīr* tradition's answer is that the verse teaches the *etiquette of receiving*: the calf was brought, the guests refused it, the fear was spoken, the fear was answered, the news was given — the entire protocol of how to *be told something enormous*: first the courtesy (salām), then the truthfulness (the fear named), then the correction ("do not tablā"), then the gift — and Abraham, the host of heaven, is the last man on earth to receive news without the meal preceding it, because the meal *is* the preparation: the sūrah has just spent twenty-five verses on provision as *test* (the winds, the water, the treasuries, vv. 19-25), and here provision-as-refused arrives exactly so that provision-as-gift (the son) can be separated from provision-as-transaction. The commentators add the typology the later tradition will build on: the refused table prefigures the refusal of the people of Lot — the town that consumed was destroyed at the same hour the house that gave (and asked nothing of the guests' appetite) received the promise; two cities, two economies of the belly, two verdicts — and the *ʿalīm*-boy is the seed of the third city (the sūrah of the House built by his sons, 2:127), so that the whole redemptive line of the Book runs, in this verse, through one father's *patience at an awkward dinner*.

**Application: The Discipline of the Bright Face**

Three practicals sit on the verse. For the one who bears news: the angelic order is the preacher's manual — greeting, then honesty, then *bishāra*; the hadith "give good tiding and do not repel" is not, in this sūrah's reading, a permission to soften verdicts but a command about *sequence*: fear first neutralized (lā tablā), truth after delivered, because news told to a terrified heart is never heard. For the one who receives terrible or impossible news: the fear is not denial, and the fear need not be hidden — the friend himself said "I am afraid," and was answered rather than rebuked; the tradition makes this the charter of the *duʿāʾ of astonishment*: to be told of grace too large for one's case and to say "how?" is not faithlessness (Abraham's own question in the next verse will carry the sūrah's seal) but the first movement of a heart that takes the news seriously. For the one in despair: the verb's second sense is the verse's standing cure — *lā tablā* over the closed door — and the sūrah has now named the disease's two faces twice in four verses (the despairer is *al-ḍāllūn* of v. 56, Iblīs's etymology of vv. 34-40, the *ghāwī* of v. 42): whoever stops *knowing* is who despairs; whoever keeps the *ʿilm* that God can, keeps the glad tiding possible — which is why the promised son is called *knowing*, and why the prohibition of despair in this book is, grammatically, a command about knowledge.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:54

> **He said, "Do you bring me glad tidings when old age has befallen me? So of what do you bring me glad tidings?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Old Age Has Touched Me": The Same Verb as the Devil's Touch**

*a-tabshshirūna qaḍ massaniya'l-kibr fa-bimā tabshshirūn* — "do you give me glad tiding when old age has *touched* me? — then with what are you giving glad tiding?" The first thing the ear should catch is the verb: *massa* — touch — the very word this sūrah has been weighing: the devil *touching* Ādam's progeny with the reeling of usury (2:275's *yatakhabbatahu'sh-shayṭānu min al-mass*, "Satan deranges him with a touch"), Ayyub's complaint in Ṣād — "the devil has *touched* me with fatigue and chastisement" (38:41, quoted at our v. 48), and the Garden's verdict "no fatigue shall *touch* them" (v. 48's *lā yamassuhum*). Now: old age *touches* — and the sūrah's secret taxonomy of touches is complete: there are touches from the enemy (the whisper, the derangement), touches from the body's contract with time (fatigue, *naṣab*, *kibr*), and there is the touch *absent* in the Garden: what Abraham names here, with perfect theology, is the second kind: he does not doubt the Giver; he states the case — old age has laid its hand on me. The commentators' psychology of the question is precise: it is the astonishment of the impossible, not the interrogation of the improbable; and the parallel verse lets Sarah voice the other half of the same sentence: "shall I bear a child when I am an old woman, and this husband of mine an old man? — that is a thing, indeed, absurd!" (11:72): the sūrah lets astonishment be *spoken* by prophets and their wives and *answers* it (v. 55: "We give you the glad tiding *with truth*"), which is the Qur'an's whole correction of a religion of stoic pretense: faith does not forbid the question; it forbids only despair *after* the question (v. 55's second clause, and see its commentary).

**"Then With What Do You Give Glad Tiding?": The Question About the How**

The second clause — *fa-bimā tabshshirūn* — the interrogative with the preposition of instrument: "with *what sort of* glad tiding do you bring it?" The salaf's reading (the source transmits al-Zajjāj and the grammarians) is that the question is *not* whether but *how*: the childlessness at ninety-nine and one-hundred-and-twenty years of age (the source's chronology at 51:29) has put the *manner* past every natural handle, and Abraham is asking for the *instrument* — is it the re-marriage of the impossible, a renewal of youth, a child from a different bond? The parallel tellings supply what the sūrah compresses the entire inquiry into three words so that the reader may hear the prophet asking *the question of fact* while already assenting to the *fact-giver*: which is, in the *uṣūl* of *ʿaqīda*, the definition of the sound heart: astonishment at the *kayf*, resignation at the *man*. Al-Rāzī draws out the epistemic etiquette: "the question about *how* the impossible will happen does not touch trust in the One who makes it happen; the question about *whether* it will — and he, God's friend, asks neither kind — is where trust leaks"; and the sūrah's twin of this scene makes its law plain across its two impossible children: Maryam's astonishment at the annunciation — "how shall this be for me when no man has touched me, nor am I unchaste?" (19:20) — is built on the identical verb the angels here answer: *kadhāliki qāla rabbuka huwa ʿalayya hayyin* — "thus; it is upon your Lord easy" (19:21; and at the annunciation of Yaḥyā, 19:9): the *how*-question is asked by both the old man and the chaste girl, and both are answered not with mechanism but with *bi-kadhaliki qāla rabbuki huwa ʿalayya hayyinun* — "thus; and it is upon your Lord easy" (19:9, 21) — the verse that closes this sūrah's theology of *kibr* and *ʿajuz* with the only sentence that can: the *ease* of the All-Powerful is the answer to every *how* of the aged.

**"While Old Age Has Befallen Me": What the Sūrah Does With a Body**

The clause is the sūrah's single, frank acceptance of the body's calendar, and the *tafsīr* tradition sees it as an *adab*-lesson written by a prophet: Abraham did not say "you are lying" nor "this cannot be"; he said "look at me" — the body as honest witness — and let the angels correct the witness. The point earns its place in the *munājāt* of this sūrah because of what it *refutes*: the whole Ādam-cycle's two failures were, at bottom, refusals of the body's truth — Iblīs refused that clay could be honored, and the mockers refused that *bashar* could carry revelation — and here, the *old body itself* becomes the occasion not for doubt but for magnified tiding: the weakness is not hidden from the promise; it is *mentioned to* it. This is the tradition's ground for reading the scene against the ascetic's lie and the materialist's lie at once: the body is neither an obstacle to God's news nor the measure of it; it is the place where the news is *made astonishing*: which is why, in the sūrah's own terms, the Garden was defined as the abode where the body's calendar is canceled — "no fatigue touches them, and they shall not be expelled" (v. 48) — a verse now read with new ears: *kibr* was the touch Abraham named; *lā yamassuhum* is the answer written for the same hand.

**Application: Ask the How, Never Abandon the Who**

The verse gives the believer permission and a fence. The permission: to bring astonishment into the presence — "do you give me glad tiding while old age has touched me?" is a sentence the Qur'an does not delete from a prophet's mouth, and the scholars make it the charter of *duʿāʾ* that states the case: complain *to* God *about* your capacity (the Prophet's ﷺ concessions for the tired and the sick are well known in the law; and the Salaf, in prayer, pleaded the same body's calendar the Qur'an records here). The fence: the astonishment ends where despair begins (v. 55 will say "then be not among those who cut themselves off"), and the sūrah, by placing *lā tablā* before the question and the divine answer *bi'l-ḥaqqi* after it, makes the sequence law: *receive the tiding, question the manner, do not question the Giver* — and it is this sequence, not stoicism, that the hadith "give glad tiding and do not repel" is for: whoever brings news to a *massahu'l-kibr* — an old-age-touched person, a capacity-touched one — first *lifts the fear*, then *states the fact*, then leaves the *how* to the One for whom it is "easy."

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:55

> **They said, "We bring thee glad tidings in truth; so be not among those who despair."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"In Truth": The Three-Word Answer to Every Astonishment**

*qālū nubashshiruka bi'l-ḥaqqi fa-lā takūna min al-jāhirīn* — "We give you glad tidings *with truth*; so be not among those who despair." The phrase *bi'l-ḥaqqi* is the angels' entire reply to the old-age question, and it is the sūrah's own keynote returned: the same Book will state, twenty verses later, "We created not the heavens and the earth and all between them *but in truth*" (v. 85), and the Cry that takes the guilty at sunrise (v. 73) is the same truth's other face. The tradition hears, in the three words, an answer that refuses to supply a mechanism: the angels do not say "we will renew your youth" — they say *with truth*: the tiding is not a hypothesis but an event whose Author has already made it good. And the sūrah's placement makes the law plain across its two impossible announcements (the son of the hundred-year-old, the virgin's child in the verse of the *kayf*, 19:20-21): each astonishment is met, in the Book, by a single sentence — *it is upon your Lord easy* — and the glad tidings here is *bi'l-ḥaqqi* precisely as the destruction that the same visitors carry is *bi'l-ḥaqqi*: the honesty of the universe is one, and the promise to Abraham and the sentence on his nephew's town are two clauses of the same veracity.

**The Word for Despair Is a Word for Cutting: *al-Jāhirīn***

*fa-lā takūna min al-jāhirīn* — the noun is *yaʾs*, and the dictionaries' image is the camel that, in a barren stretch, *sits down and will not rise*: despair is the *cut rope* (the root's idiom is severing), not the sadness that weeps and walks on. This is why the verse's prohibition is aimed at *being among* a class (*lā takūna min*) — the same grammar of enrollment that ran through the enemy episode (*min al-munẓarīn*, v. 37; *min al-ghāwīn*, v. 42): the danger is not feeling but *joining*, not the tear but the seat in the dust. Read against this sūrah's roads, the despairer is the man who has halted in the middle of the *ṣirāṭ* (v. 41) and called the halt a verdict — the exact posture of the reprieved enemy who took his term as a venue and swore his war from a standstill (vv. 37-39): *jayus* is Iblīs's seated position, and the verse forbids the friend's household what the cast-away chose; and the *min*-partitive repeats, for the third time in this sūrah, that every heart in the story belongs to a company, and the companies are chosen while the road is still under foot.

**The Command As Comfort: the Two Ḥadīths the Commentators Bring**

The *tafsīr* of this verse's command rides on two reports. The first answers the question despair always asks — *will He take me back?* — with the image of joy: "God rejoices more at the turning of one of you than one of you who rides out into a land of death, loses his mount with his food, and despairs of it — then finds it lying at his foot" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas b. Mālik): the Lord of the tiding is *glad* at the return, and the sūrah has just said the tiding is *in truth* — the two sentences belong together because joy is the truth's guarantee; a king who rejoices does not cheat his guests. The second report fences the command around the last breath: "none of you should die save while thinking well of God" (Muslim, from Jābir b. ʿAbdullāh) — *ḥusn al-ẓann*, the obligatory estimate of the Teller, valid even when the body is failing — and the source's cross-reference, Jacob's words over the second news of the pit — "do not despair of God's *rawḥ*; none despairs save the rejecting people" (12:87) — stands as the Qur'an's own gloss: *rawḥ* is the breath of ease, and in a sūrah whose winds are the fertilizers of v. 22, despair is the refusal of the air: to be *min al-jāhirīn* is to close one's own mouth in the wind that is moving toward one.

**Application: The Cut-Rope Check**

The verse hands the believer a single diagnostic, and the word *jāhirīn* frames it: at every closed door — the barren years, the unanswered *duʿāʾ*, the long delay of v. 4's *kitāb* — ask not "do I feel hopeless?" (feeling is not the crime; the friend himself asked) but "have I *cut*?" The tradition's list of cuts is exact. The cut of the command: abandoning the prayer because it seems unanswered is despair wearing the clothes of patience — which is why the sūrah's own later order runs against it: "proclaim openly what you are commanded" (v. 94, *fa-ṣdiʿ bimā tuʾmar*) — and the sūrah's own cousin-command in Hūd, "be steadfast as you are commanded" (11:112) — the straightness of the *ṣirāṭ* measured in the refusal to stop walking. The cut of verdicts over persons: "this one is saved, that one is lost" — and the sūrah's oath to question *them all* (v. 92) protects every name from the sentence only the Owner of the tryst may pass. The cut of the world's future: the despair that will not plant — answered by the report in Aḥmad, graded good by its judges: "if the Hour rises while a seedling is in the hand of one of you, let him plant it" — the command to sow at the moment of the final silence, the anti-*jayūs* in its most physical form. And to the one who cannot rise, the verse says what the angels said to the hundred-year-old, in the order that is the sūrah's whole psychology: *first* the tiding is true — *then* be not among the cut-off; hope is not a mood the believer manufactures, it is a fact he consents to: *nubashshiruka bi'l-ḥaqqi*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:56

> **He said, "Who despairs of the Mercy of his Lord, save those who are astray?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Whose Answer Is a Wall**

*qāla man yaqnaṭu min raḥmati rabbihī illā'l-ḍāllūn* — "who despairs of the mercy of his Lord, save the people of going astray?" The form is interrogative, the content is a wall: no member of the category of mercy's despairs exists except the astray, and Abraham, whose biography the whole Qur'an testifies to, is not among them — the source cites 16:120-121 precisely here: "Abraham was *an umma unto himself*, ḥanīf, and he was not of the associaters": the man who *asked* the how-question (v. 54) now defines the class his question did *not* enter, and the sūrah lets a prophet draw the boundary of his own astonishment: ask, but do not sit down. This is the pair the angels had built in two verses — *lā tablā* (do not despair, v. 53), *fa-lā takūna min al-jāhirīn* (be not among the cut-off, v. 55) — now echoed from the human side, which is the tradition's proof-text for the *adab* of prophetic *ʿaqīda*: the command of heaven and the consent of the heart arrive at the same word; the friend does not merely receive the prohibition of despair, he *states* it, generalizes it, and hands it to every reciter for all time: the verse, the commentators note, is quoted in sermons as the *definition* of the believer's emotional law: grief, yes — despair, no; the difference, in Arabic's own morphology, is that grief keeps the rope, despair cuts it (v. 55's *jayus*).

**The Lexicographers' Paradox: *Qunūṭ* Obeys What It Despairs**

The verb *qanṭa* is one of the Qur'an's great semantic puzzles, which the old lexicography states and the *tafsīr* of this verse exploits: in the classical language *qunūṭ* is *obedience* (the standing of the qānit in prayer — the long, humble, upright compliance), and *qanṭa/qunūṭ* is *despair* — and the two, the dictionaries report, were named from the same root because "the one who despairs of God's mercy has *obeyed* his despair": the desperate soul is not free; it is a *qānit* of grief, standing in upright service to a verdict it chose. Read into the verse, the paradox completes the sūrah's Ādam-cycle of *obedience-wrongly-placed*: the Qur'an's own pre-history: "he was *from the jinn*, and he *transgressed* the command of his Lord" (18:50) — and in the language of this verse, the *fisq* from the *ṭāʿa* is the despair that learned to obey itself; the jinn's standing (qunūt) before the fall left the sūrah's records, and the moment despair entered the heart, the obedience had already gone out of it — and here the verse says the direction of the road: despair is the *worship* of the cut rope, and whoever worships it joins the *ḍāllūn* whose company is named in the sūrah two verses back (v. 42's *al-ghāwīn*, the deviators) — the two words for the two losses: *ḍalāl* (the wandering from the road) is, in the Qur'an's economy of this sūrah, the *inside* of the *ghayy* (the choice of the low), and despair is what they call, from within the house, the *mercy*: the astray do not merely miss the road; they *misname its Owner*.

**"The Mercy of *His* Lord": the Relation the Despairer Forgets**

The verse says *raḥmata rabbihī* — the mercy of *his* Lord, in the relation of *rabb* (sustainer, owner, the One who brings the thing to its term) — and the *tafsīr* tradition hears the argument within the possessive: despair of mercy is possible only if the relation has been *renamed* — if the sustainer has been demoted to a bystander; and it is impossible while He is *his* Lord, i.e., while He is the One who brought him to faith at all (the sūrah's own proof of the impossibility is 16:120's biography: *the man who knew his Lord through the stars' sinking and the sword and the fire and the migration* cannot coherently despair; and the Qur'an generalizes the proof: "and who despairs of the *raḥma* of his Lord but the rejecting people?" — the identical logic at 12:87 from Jacob's mouth). The source's note adds the pairing that makes the psychology complete: 29:23 — "those who disbelieve in the signs of God and in the meeting with Him — *those* despair of My mercy; *those* are the people of [their] portion" — despair is therefore, in the Qur'an's own ledger, a *Christology of the negative*: it denies not a feeling but the *meeting* (liqāʾ), and the sūrah that has just announced "your Lord — He is the Knowing, the Wise" who *gathers* (v. 25) stands as the refutation: the gathering *is* the mercy's venue, and whoever cuts himself off from it has mistaken, not his luck, but his Lord.

**Application: The Sentence to Say at the End of Every Sin**

The verse is the Qur'an's hand on the shoulder at the door of the mosque after the worst of weeks, and the tradition's *ʿamal* at this point is precise: the scholars made *man yaqnaṭu…* the recitation of the *qunūt* in the witr, the prayer of the last third of the night, and the duʿāʾ of the one who has slipped — because the sūrah has staged the sequence the reciter now walks: sin named (the astonishment, the question), despair refused (the *lā tablā*), mercy generalized (the *man yaqnaṭu* — *nobody*, and I am *nobody's* neighbor here). Three practicals: first, *do not die except thinking well* (Muslim's report, from Jābir) is the verse's law for the final hour, and its extension is the daily hour: each night, the *ẓann* is checked like a compass — "what did I assume of God today?" — for the hadith says: "I am as My servant thinks of Me" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra). Second, the sin is never the reason for despair; *the view of the Lord* is — so repair the view first (the verse's *rabbihī*: remember the *relation* before the *record*). Third, teach the verse to the one who has fallen: the friend of God asked "how?" and the record carries no rebuke for the asking — only the fence against despair — and the umma's rule from this exchange is the sequence itself: astonishment, then correction, then the generalized wall of the question: the sūrah that forbids despair will spend its next forty verses showing, city by destroyed city, that its mercy is not for the innocent only — it is for the *turning*, and turning, as the story of Lot's family will say before the sūrah ends, is a *door*, not a *height*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:57

> **He said, "What is your errand, O messengers?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pivot-Question of the Double Errand**

*qāla fa-mā khaṭbukum yā'l-mursalūn* — "he said: what then is your *khaṭb*, O sent ones?" The *fāʾ* is the pivot: the glad tiding is received (vv. 53-55), the astonishment fenced (v. 56) — and now the host of heaven turns from his own gift to the guests' charge. *Khaṭb* is the Arabic word for a *matter* as opposed to a *thing* — an affair with weight, consequence, and demand; Abraham's interrogative asks, in effect, "what is the *charge* folded inside the gift," and the commentators note the address's *register*: not "what do you bring" but "what is the *business*" — Abraham asks as a man who has already understood that a second commission is folded inside the first, for a visit that carries both a son and a sentence is *one errand with two mouths*, and the prophet's question asks after the *mouth* it has not yet heard: "the glad tiding had no need to be asked about — it had been given; the *khaṭb* was asked about, for the destruction had not yet been spoken, and he knew, by the strangeness of their fast at the feast, that a *khaṭb* was in the house" (the logic of al-Ṭabarī's sequence at 11:70's *nakira-hum*: he *recognized* in their non-eating the shape of an errand). The verse thus teaches the second half of the etiquette of news: *receive the tiding, then ask the burden* — the gladness does not license the guest to leave the host wondering what the visit *costs*; the sūrah makes its own prophet ask so the umma learns to ask after every mercy: what does this *khaṭb* require of me.

**"O Messengers": the Title That Replaces the Suspicion**

He had called them, at the door of fear, *minkum wijl* — "I am afraid *of you*" (v. 52); now he addresses them by their office: *yā'l-mursalūn* — "O *sent ones*." The change of address is the verse's quiet education in how a corrected fear becomes a corrected title, and the commentators of the parallel passage mark the same progression in the house of Lot: there the angels are, to the host, "my *guests*" (v. 69) and to the mob only "a people strange" (11:77) — strangeness and sin crowding the same doorstep — and once the errand speaks, they are simply "the sent ones," and the town's true name comes out in the accusation it hurls at Lot: "you *cut off the road*" (29:29) — the road-cutters of the plain meet, in this sūrah's vocabulary, the rope-cutter of despair (v. 55's *jayus*) and the ambusher of the *ṣirāṭ* (7:17): three *qaṭʿ*s of the way, and the messengers' one straight road ends their all, and the verse licenses the reader's own etiquette with the ambiguous: until the *khaṭb* is asked, every strange guest wears the mask of the unknown; after it is asked, the guest is either a *mursal* or a *ghāwī*, and nothing between — which is the sūrah's whole warning about discernment, dressed as a courtesy. And the vocative plural — the first in the sūrah since the court of vv. 29-31 — binds the scene to the angels of the prostration: the same *class* of beings whose arrival at Ādam's honor split history now stands in the house of his son's son, and the double errand they carry (the son, the stone-rain) is the *whole* of that split's after-life: the obedient angels are the messengers of mercy *and* of the Cry — the sūrah has refused, from its beginning, every theology that would divide the office.

**Why the Destruction Travels in a Glad Tiding's Ship**

The *tafsīr* tradition poses the question the verse's placement forces: why do the *bashshārūn*, the bringers of the son of the promise, carry, in the same saddlebag, the ruin of a city that *descended from the same promise's family* — Lot, Abraham's nephew, whose people's destruction will consume vv. 61-77? The answer the commentators draw from our verse is procedural and theological at once: the *khaṭb*-question *is* the answer — the double news is not a contradiction to be resolved but a *commission* to be received, and the sūrah, which has stood at its own center on the pairing of "the Forgiving, the Merciful" and "My punishment is the painful pain" (vv. 49-50), now *stages* the pair as one delegation: the same angels greet Abraham with a son and ride to Lot's town with stones, and the verse's *mursalūn* is the word that makes the picture orthodox: *one Sender, two errands, one book of truth* — and the glad tiding's ship carries the destruction because, in this sūrah's economy, the *preservation of the line* (the son) and the *cleansing of the land* (the rain) are the two operations of one mercy: the promise could not walk forward *through* Sodom unharmed; the son's line and the nephews' rescue (v. 59: "the family of Lot, We shall save them") are the same act seen from the tent and from the wall of the besieged house — a point the sūrah's next verses will turn into fire.

**Application: Ask After the Cost of the Gift**

The verse's *fiqh* for the reader is short and sharp: *no mercy in this book arrives without a khaṭb attached, and the etiquette is to ask.* Practically: (1) at every answered duʿāʾ, complete the prophet's sentence aloud — "what is your errand?" — i.e., what is this gift *for*; the tradition's fear, voiced in the *tafsīr* of the story, is that the *ʿaṭāʾ* that is not received as a *khaṭb* turns, on the Day, into the one question the blessed will not be asked (v. 92's "We shall question them *all*" includes the question of what the gift produced: the verse stands at the sūrah's end as the accounting of every *khaṭb* that went unasked). (2) When a *fear* of someone is corrected by clearer knowledge, change the address as Abraham changed his: "of you" becomes "O sent ones" — the verse is the charter of *renaming* the once-suspect once the *khaṭb* is known, which the scholars apply above all to God's judges and God's preachers whose strangeness offends: ask the errand before the verdict on the visitor. (3) Accept double-news as *office*, not as *mixed signals*: the community's rule drawn from the delegation of vv. 51-60 is that every true bearer of the Book will bring the *bishāra* and the *indhar* in one mouth (v. 8's "clear warner" and v. 49's "Glad Tidings" share the sūrah's one command), and whoever demands a messenger of only one of the two is asking, in Abraham's words, the wrong *khaṭb* — the verse closes with the correction of that question for every age: *the messengers go where they are sent, and the errand is not the guest's to divide.*

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:58

> **They said, "We have been sent unto a guilty people,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unnamed City: *Qawmin Mujrimīn* and the Suspense of the Sūrah**

*qālū innā ursilnā ilā qawmin mujrimīn* — "we have been sent to a people *deeply guilty*." The sūrah withholds the city's name for fifteen verses more (the first explicit mention comes only at v. 61's "the family of Lot"), and the withholding is the verse's first art: the guilty are described, not pointed at, and the description is *juridical* — *mujrimūn*: the people of *ijrām*, crime-earned guilt (the root *j-r-m*: to cut into, to incur; the *mujrim* is the one whose deed *attaches* to him as a debt; the classical dictionaries note *jarīma*: an act whose doer cannot shake it off). Read against the sūrah's own courtroom-progress (the *iḥjāj* of v. 32, the record of v. 92), the namelessness is deliberate: the errand is stated in the *genus* of its crime, so that every reader first asks *which* people, and then hears the answer in v. 70-77's anatomy (the assault on the guests, the road-cutting of 29:29, the *fāḥisha* "which none in the worlds preceded you with," 29:28) — and the genus is what makes the verse a warning to the reciter rather than a report about antiquity: *every* city that meets the description is, in the sūrah's tense, the *qawmun mujrimūn* to which the sent ones are even now on the road. Al-Rāzī draws the rule of the tense explicitly: the past narration of the divine verdicts is, in the Qur'an, always *is'āl* — an interrogation *of the present*.

**"We Have Been Sent": the Passive of the Sending and the Dignity Even of Ruin**

The verb is divine-passive: *ursilnā* — "We were sent" — the angels do not say "we ride to destroy" but *we are sent*, and the *tafsīr* tradition notes the etiquette embedded in the passive: even destruction, in this book, is an *office*, not an initiative; the angels of the Cry carry the stones the way the angels of the promise carry the tidings — *commissioned*, not spontaneous — and the verse is thus the sūrah's own theodicy of force: nothing happens in the economy of heaven except through a *sending*, which is why the same passive governs the punishment of Pharaoh (the *arsal*-verbs of 7:133's plagues: "We *sent* upon them the flood"), the rain on Thamūd, and the wind on 'Ād. And the *nūn al-ʿiẓama* of *ursilnā* — the royal We — sets the errand in the mouth that has said *bi-yadayya* in Ṣād's parallel (38:75): the sending that ruins a city and the Hands that shaped a clay-man belong to one dignity, and the sūrah's center (vv. 49-50) has already bound the two: *Ghafūr, Raḥīm* — and *My punishment is the painful pain*, in one commission. The reader who flinches at the verse's second line has, the tradition says, flinched at the pair at the center: the flinch is the point of the pedagogy.

**The Crime's Grammar: Indefinite People, Definite Sin**

The construction — *qawmin mujrimīn*, indefinite "a people," with the crime as their adjective — is what the grammarians of the *tafsīr* call the verse's *ithbāt al-waṣf qabla dhikr al-mawṣūf*: the quality is fixed before the name is given, so the verdict is *about the act*, not the ancestry: God's messengers do not say "to the people of Sodom" but "to a people of guilt," and the city, when it arrives, is *known by* that description, not the other way around (the source's chronology: "the mention of the town is withheld until its crime is established"). The theology of the indefinite is universal judgment and universal mercy at once: universal judgment, because *any* people that is *mujrimūn* answers to this sentence (vv. 71-77 will show the act's anatomy — the mob at the door — and it is reproducible in every generation, which is why the jurists treated the act, wherever it recurs, under the name its first city earned — the *fāḥisha* of the people of Lot — the archetype, in the law's own vocabulary, of corruption that makes itself public), and universal mercy, because the description is *mujrimūn* and not *lā yumkinu ghayra-hum* — the people are *guilty*, not *guilt by nature*: and the very next verse (v. 59) will carve a *family* out of the description and save them "all together," which is the sūrah's whole grammar of exception performing, in the story, what it swore in the enemy-cycle: the *illa* cuts through every *qawm*.

**Application: Name the Act Before You Name the Town**

The verse's method is the community's school: the crime is described before the city is named, and the reader who follows that order learns the sūrah's ethics of verdicts — *judge the act as act, then the town as town* — because the reverse order (name first, then prejudice) is how the *ghil* of v. 47 enters a judgment and never leaves. Three practicals: first, when a report of corruption arrives, ask what the *description* is (*mujrim*: an act that sticks and must be paid) before asking whose child did it — the indefinite keeps the verdict honest about the genus. Second, the passive *ursilnā* fences all violence as *office or crime*: no believer carries a destruction "spontaneously"; whoever punishes in this community does so as one *sent* — under a mandate, within a law, with the name of the Sender on his lips, and the sūrah's own rule for the Prophet when the mandate ends — "so turn from the associaters, and We suffice you against the mockers" (vv. 94-95) — marks where the sent one's *own* hand stops. Third, the verse's five-word sentence — *innā ursilnā ilā qawmin mujrimīn* — is the *duʿāʾ* of every reformer in the tradition's *ādāb*: when the sūrah turns back toward the reader at its close ("clearly warning," v. 94's *nadhīrun munīr*), the warner's sentence is always the same: not "you are damned," but *we have been sent to a people of guilt* — and the difference between the two phrasings is the difference between prophecy and hatred, which is why the verse stands, unornamented, at the hinge between the tent's glad tiding and the city's stone.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:59

> **save for the family of Lot. We shall surely save them, all together,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The *Illa* Walks into the Story: "Save the Family of Lot"**

*illā āla lūṭin innā la-munjjūnahum ajmaʿīn* — "except the family of Lot: we shall surely save them, all of them." The sūrah's master-particle, the *illa* that cut Iblīs's boast to size at v. 40 ("save Your servants, the purified"), now performs the same cut inside a destroyed city: the *qawm mujrim* of v. 58 is not, in God's accounting, a block — the verdict that reads like totality always has an exception folded into it, and the exception here is *a family*: salvation, in this book, travels through households. The commentators mark the shift from the abstract class to the concrete house: v. 40's exception was a *quality* (the purified), v. 59's is a *door in a wall* — the sūrah's method of showing that its generalities are made of particular people, one righteous man's roof being, in the hour of the Cry, the whole remnant of a civilization. And the oath-structure repeats v. 40's exactly — *innā la-munjjūnahum* (the *inna* plus the *lām* of the surety) — so that the two verses rhyme in their grammar of certainty: what God excepts, He *guarantees*: the exception is not a hope but a sworn deliverance.

**"All Together" — the Third *Ajmaʿīn* of the Sūrah**

The word has now appeared, inside this sūrah's ten verses, in three mouths and three totalities: Iblīs's claim — "I will mislead them *all together*" (v. 39); God's recompense — "their tryst, *all together*" (v. 43); and here, God's rescue — "we shall save them *all together*." The tradition hears the third as the answer to the first: the enemy's totality was always a claim over *numbers*; the divine totalities are operations over *persons* — and this *ajmaʿīn* saves the whole *āla* at once because the deliverance is not a lottery but an *evacuation*: every member of the household exits the fall of the city together, none traded for another, no one left to explain later. And there is a fourth *ajmaʿūn* a few verses down in the destroyed-cities' sequence that makes the four-word chord complete (the mockers "all together" of v. 92-93's questioning oath): a sūrah that ends its story-cycle by swearing to question *everyone* uses *ajmaʿīn* as its accounting stamp — totality is how the Book counts, and it counts *individuals*, which is precisely why the *family* can be carved whole out of a *people*, and a wife can be cut out of the *family* (v. 60) with the same particle: the exception-excepting-within-exception is the sūrah's arithmetic of persons, verse against verse, and every *ajmaʿ* in it is finally, to its last unit, a *soul*.

**The Law of the Saved House: Kinship Carries No Verdict**

The verse's *āla lūṭ* — his *household* — is, in the *tafsīr*'s careful usage, not a biological census but a *dīn* census: "his family" means *those of his family who were upon his affair* — and the next verse (v. 60) will carve out the wife precisely to make the law unmistakable: kinship is a road toward salvation, never a claim upon it; a prophet's house is saved *through* faith, not by blood's claim upon it — and the Qur'an, in its two companion rulings, makes the census explicit: the sūrah of Prohibition sets the two believing prophets' wives in the one sentence of loss — "they were between two of Our servants, both righteous, and both betrayed them, and they availed them nothing before God" (66:10, the betrayal glossed unanimously by the commentators *in the dīn*, not the bed) — and Noah, pleading for his son in Hūd, is told the line the present sūrah here illustrates from the other side: "he is not of your family; it is a deed not righteous" (11:46). Family, in this book, is what the *illa* counts. Salvation in this book is *transmitted by light, not by blood*; the *āla* is saved as a *company*, and the company is defined by what they *believed*, and this is the reason the *illa* had to cut both ways at once — the city's exception *and* the household's remainder — or else the whole sūrah's psychology of the exception (v. 42: *illa man tabiʿaka* — the followers *leave* the immunity) would have failed its first narrative test.

**Application: The Safety of the Roof and the Peril of the Bed**

The verse and its sequel, read as a pair, are the tradition's charter for the believing *household* — and both halves of the charter are severe and sweet. The sweet: *families are how God evacuates remnant* — the saved city of every generation will be, as here, one man's roof held through the night of the fall, and the house's worship (the prayer prayed in it, the *khayr* invited into it) is the sūrah's own description of the *āla* that is "saved all together"; which is why the scholars of the *ādāb* made this verse's *ajmaʿīn* the *duʿāʾ* of the household: "our Lord, grant us in our spouses and our offspring coolness of eyes" (25:74) is the Qur'anic wording of the same hope — that the house exit *whole*. The severe: the same two verses hold a woman *beside* the prophet and *outside* the salvation, and the *illa* that cuts the city cuts, with the same knife, the marriage bed that faith did not warm — so the household's safety is never a *status*; it is the direction every one of its members is *turned*, and the practical of the verse is the tradition's famous line on 66:10-11: *the ship does not carry a body; it carries a belief that has a body* — which is, precisely, why the wife of Pharaoh rides the same two verses *into* the saved: no house damns or saves; the *āla* is saved, and the *illa* decides who is *āla*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:60

> **except for his wife; We have determined that she is indeed among those who lagged behind."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The *Illa* Inside the *Illa*: One Word Narrowing a Saved House**

*illā imraʾatahu qaddarnā innahā mina'l-ghābirīn* — "except his wife; We decreed: she is of those who remain behind." The sūrah's particle, which cut a family out of a doomed city (v. 59), now cuts *one bed* out of the saved family, and the double exception is the verse's whole politics of the soul: nothing in this book is saved by adjacency — not a town through a prophet, not a spouse through a prophet — and the *illa* therefore governs every list until the last soul's own faith is named. The grammarians note the sequence's severity: v. 59 swore the rescue with *innā la-munjjūnahum*; v. 60 swears the exclusion with the same weight — *qaddarnā innahā* — and the pairing of two oaths across the couple is the sūrah's way of saying that *both* verdicts are settled by the same act of divine knowledge, the decree (v. 4's *kitāb maʿlūm* — "known decree" — now *qaddarnā*, "We measured/decreed," its verb-form) functioning, as it did for Iblīs at v. 34, as a *record* not an *arbitrariness*: the decree's content was the wife's own settled direction, and the *qadar* names what she had become.

**"The Laggers" (*al-Ghābirūn*): Dust, Dawn, and Those Left in It**

*ghābirīn* — from *ghubar*, dust; *ghabara*, to pass by and leave it settling: the word's field holds *the dust that rises from a departing caravan*, *the past times* (*al-ghābira*, bygone generations), and, in the dictionaries read at this verse, *the residue left behind* — so the exegetes gloss: "of those whom the affair passed by and left in its dust; of those destroyed with the city, who *remained behind* when the believers departed" — and the image they prefer is almost unbearable in its plainness: the caravan of the saved moves at night toward the mountains (the story's own next verses: "take your family at a portion of the night" — v. 65), and the name of the excluded is not "the destroyed" but *the laggard*: she who, when the hour of the Exodus came, was not *with* them. And the word's second light — dawn (*ghabīr*: the dawn's dust-colored light) — enters the verse through the sūrah's own sequel (v. 73: "and We rained upon them a rain — how was the end of the warned!" and the Cry "at sunrise" — *bi-wuqū'i's-ṣubḥ*, in the parallel of Hūd 11:81 "wa-saʿʿirnā al-wādiya ṭaʿāman min hijārin manḍūd"?) — the *ghābira* of her city is its *ghabīr*: the dawn that finds the dust settled where a town had been, and the tradition's one-line moral writes itself: whoever lags when the command moves is *made of the morning's dust*.

**"We Decreed": The Theology of the Verb, Read Against the Wife's Own Decree**

The *qaddarnā* is what the *tafsīr* must handle with the most care, for the verse's surface seems to make the exclusion an act of the decree upon the woman, and the commentators close the door on that reading with the sūrah's own furniture: the decree here *names* rather than *compels* — the same *qadar*-grammar as v. 4 ("no town destroyed but by a known decree," where the decree *records* the term the town's own refusal had run out) — and the woman's history, as the Qur'an tells it, is her own settled direction: al-Aʿrāf repeats our sentence verbatim — "his wife: We decreed that she is of those who remain behind" (7:83) — and adh-Dhāriyāt counts the city's final census from the other side: "then We found in it none but one house of the Muslims" (51:36) — one house, and within it one lagging soul whose membership was nominal.  What the tradition teaches at this verse is thus the *law of the decree's direction*: God's *qadāʾ* is, in this book, the *fixing* of what a self has chosen until the choice hardens into nature — *mina'l-ghābirīn*, "of the laggers": a *class*, the sūrah's recurring *min*, and her *illa*, like Iblīs's, *is* her crime stated as her name: the verse lets the decree speak the sūrah's whole theodicy in one word — those whom He "determines" are those who determined themselves.

**Application: The Sin of Almost-Leaving**

The wife of Lot is the sūrah's final lesson in *proximity's peril* — the one figure in the Ādam-to-Lot arc who *saw everything* and *left with nothing* — and the practicals the tradition draws are, deliberately, small. First, the *lag* is how it happens: not by a revolt but by not moving when the command moves — the scholars' line on the verse: "she was not *against* the believers in the open, and she was not *with* them at the departure, and the hour sorts only the *with*" — which makes the verse the most searching question in this book for the person who *lives at the mosque and does not walk to it*: adjacency is not companionship. Second, the *bed tests the direction*: the sūrah, which has just saved a whole family *except* one marital bed, forbids the believer from counting the spouse's house as his own salvation (the rule of 66:10 read at v. 59) and equally consoles the one whose bed has not turned with them (Asiya's house in the house of Pharaoh, "build for me with You a house in Paradise," 66:11 — the *illa* cuts both ways; the same marriage law that damns the prophet's wife saves the tyrant's, because it measures neither *husband* nor *house* but *qibla*). Third, the *dawn*: whoever is caught lagging should learn the verse's one mercy — the Cry came at *ṣubḥ*, and until it comes, the road of the *munajjūn* is still moving, and to catch it *now* is to die not *mina'l-ghābirīn*: the *qaddarnā* of this verse is the sentence on the *hardened* only — and no one, the sūrah's whole promise-cycle from Abraham's impossible son to this house's single exception knows, who turns *in time*, has been hardened.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:61

> **So when the messengers came to the family of Lot,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Visitors, the Wrong Door: Continuity as Suspense**

*fa-lammā jā'a rusulu Āla lūṭin* — "and when the messengers came to the family of Lot." The definite article binds the verse to the tent: *al-rusul* — the same sent ones of v. 57, the bearers of the son's glad tiding, now arriving at the nephew's house, and the sūrah refuses to introduce them afresh: the reader must carry the double errand through the door with Lot.
