The commentators mark the suspense that this continuity manufactures: at Abraham's tent the angels greeted, were feared, and explained themselves; at Lot's house there is no greeting recorded, no meal offered, no announcement — only the arrival upon a family already *within* the condemned description (v. 58: *qawmun mujrimūn* — and Lot's household stands, grammatically, inside the phrase's territory: "to the family *of* Lot" is said to a town whose name has not yet been spoken: the saved *āla* and the doomed *qawm* share one door, and the verse's whole drama is the separation the next two hundred years of sūrah-recitation will re-enact every time a hearer is asked which side of the door *he* is standing). And the *lammā* of immediate succession — "and *when* they came" — is read by the *tafsīr* of the chain (the source's notes at 11:77) as the sūrah's shorthand for the *speed* of the divine errand: the glad tiding of a century-old promise and the ruin of a century-old sin are, in the narrative's clock, *one afternoon*: *waqt* (v. 38) keeps its own schedule for mercy and for punishment together, and Lot is the man who hosts both news in his house in a single night — the sūrah's center-pair (vv. 49-50) once more staged as plot.

**"The Family of Lot": the House That Was Saved and the House That Was Besieged**

*Āla lūṭin* — the phrase is the same as v. 59's (*illa āla lūṭin innā la-munajjūnahum* — the sūrah has *announced* the rescue three verses before the narrative reaches the rescued, and the tradition treats this proleptic ordering as one of the sūrah's narrative masterstrokes: the *ḥikāya* repeats the verdict before it narrates the flight, so that the reader never, for a single verse, fears *for* the saved; suspense is reserved for the *mob*, not for the *āla*). The name Lot himself carries the sūrah's kinship-law in its most severe form — the prophet *inside* the condemned city, whose *āla* is the exemption and whose *bed* is the exception (v. 60) — and the commentators note that Lot is nowhere in this sūrah called *āla* *his* except in the saved and cut senses: his family is a *category under verdict*, which is exactly what vv. 61-77 will demonstrate: a household is saved by *being commanded to leave* (v. 65: "travel with your family in a portion of the night"), not by being *in* it when it falls — the *āla* becomes a *caravan* or it is a *corpse*, and the whole episode, from this verse's first word, is about the difference between the two.

**Why the Angels Came as Youths: the Test Wears Its Own Costume**

The source's note states the tradition's consensus: the messengers came "in the form of handsome youths," and the reports of Ibn ʿAbbās and the Salaf at 11:77-78 make the scene's grammar from that form: "they came *rushing* toward him" (*yuhraʿūna* — the verb of a crowd running as to a feast), and the *tafsīr* uses the costume for the sūrah's standing *ʿaqīda* of trial: every divine errand arrives wearing what the receiving heart will be tested by — at Abraham's house the youths came as *hungry* guests and the test was hospitality; at Lot's they came as *desired* strangers and the test was *protection* — and the angels do not choose the form to *entrap* but because the city's *mujrim* (v. 58: the guilt that clings) has already made the beautiful dangerous: the trial is, always, the people's own shape, returned to them. This is the sūrah's last lesson of *tazyīn* from the other side — Iblīs adorns the sin; the world adorns the *test* — and the reciter is taught, by the costume of these angels, to ask at every arrival: *what in me is about to be tried by what this looks like?*

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:62

> **he said, "Verily you are an unfamiliar folk."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Host's Alarm: *Innaw-ma-kum qawmun nokār***

*qāla innakum qawmun nokār* — "he said: you are, truly, a *strange* folk." The word *nukār/nokār* (the root n-k-r: to be unknown, and thereby unsafe — the same root as Abraham's *fa-ankarahum*, "he formed suspicion of them," at 11:70, and as the *munkar* that the town itself practiced "in its gatherings," 29:29) carries the verse's triple charge: the guests are *unfamiliar* (no tribe-name Lot can claim for them), *unsanctioned* (in the town's law, an unknown male guest *is* the offense — v. 70's mob-cry "did we not forbid you from *all the worlds*?" names the prohibition Lot broke by hosting), and, to Lot's prophetic ear, *unhomelike* — his instinct has detected, beneath the handsome form (v. 61's note), that these visitors do not belong to the economy of this town's appetites, which is precisely why they *cannot* be safe here. The commentators' psychology of the sentence is the sūrah's finest portraiture in four words: Lot does not say "who are you" or "welcome" — the verse puts the *alarm* first, and the tradition reads this as the law of the righteous host in the land of the *mujrimūn*: the first thought of the just man, on receiving a guest, is *their safety*, not his own honor; and inversely, the town's first thought on hearing of guests is *its appetite*: v. 67 says of the mob, in the very root the angels used at Abraham's tent — *yastabshirūn*, "they came rejoicing," the *tabshīr* of glad-tiding — as though the report of beardless youths in the prophet's house had been *good news dropped from heaven*; the city has stolen, in its excitement, the angels' own verb — which is the sūrah's most savage single-word irony: and the two reactions — fear for the stranger, joy at the stranger — are the whole ethical seismograph of the passage.

**"Strangers" in the Sūrah of Eavesdroppers and Gates: the Theology of *Nukār***

Set the word in its sūrah, and the verse thickens. This book has already, in its first movement, told us what *unfamiliar visitors* are in the divine order: the sky's *nukār* are the eavesdropping devils pelted by piercing flames (vv. 16-18); the earth's *nukār* were the angels no one recognized at Abraham's tent, who came with a sentence folded into a tiding (vv. 51-60); and here, in Lot's house, the *nukār* are the same company of sent ones, arriving *at the town whose own crime* (the cutting of the road, 29:29) has made every stranger a target — and the tradition notes the irony: the angels came to Sodom *looking like its sin's favorite object*, so that the city's appetite would have to *announce itself* at the house of its prophet, in daylight of the mind, before the night of the stones: *nukār* is thus the word for the *test in costume* — as the world's trials are, always, "a folk one does not know," and the believer's Lot-in-every-age must choose, like him, between the town's joy and the guest's safety, and the verse shows which of the two a prophet loses sleep for. This is also the place the source's esoteric gloss (Aj, quoted at vv. 67-69) enters with its full force: "the people of Lot who come rejoicing represent the heedless: when they find those whom they think will conform to their whims, they rejoice over them" — every age's mob is *nukār*-hunting, and every age's prophet is the host who says "these are *my guests*."

**Between Two Verses of Fire, a Sentence of Fear: the Sūrah's Human Angle**

The verse sits exactly between the messengers' arrival (v. 61, whose Arabic marks the *qadar*-frame — "when the messengers *came*," the arrival already inside the decree of v. 60's *qaddarnā*) and the angels' double announcement of *the truth* (vv. 63-64). The commentators ask why the sūrah gives Lot the middle word: so that the reader may see the *human cost* of hosting the decree: a man who knows his town will mob him tonight for the men in his house, standing silent before the guests with only the sentence "you are *nukār*" — no plea, no explanation, the plain warning of hospitality's danger spoken *to* the very ones it endangers — and the angels' reply (v. 63: "*Nāy*, rather we bring you *that which they used to doubt*") shows the verse's function: Lot was warning *the town* for years and being mocked; here, in one sentence to *the guests*, we learn what warning had cost him: the prophet whose every word to his people was *raining doubt* speaks, in the guest-chamber, one quiet alarm, and is answered with *certainty*: this sūrah, whose center-verse announced the Forgiving and the Punishing in one breath, has here its miniature: *the fear of the saint is the hinge between the two news, and both are addressed to him*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:63

> **They said, "Nay, but we bring thee that which they used to doubt."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The *Bal*: The Word That Turns a Doubt into a Cargo**

*qālū bal naʾtīka bi-mā kānū fīhi yamtarūn* — "they said: *rather*, we come to you with *that which they used to doubt*." The *bal* is a correction of Lot's assumption that the guests need his protection; the verse answers: you are not hosting a *stranger* — you are hosting *an answer*. And the object they name is the sūrah's masterstroke of irony: not "punishment," but "that-which-they-doubted" (*bi-mā kānū fīhi yamtarūn*): the doubt of a generation, *delivered*, packed, arrive — which is the Qur'an's recurrent doctrine of mocked warning stated as event: the thing the doubters called unreal *is* the one thing that comes to meet them; the *imtār* (the verb here is intensive: *tamta* — to keep doubting, to churn doubt deliberately) has, as its only real object, the truth's arrival. The source's gloss fixes the referent precisely — the *impending punishment of which Lot had been warning his people, and which they denied and called him a liar* — and the sūrah has already sworn the same irony at its beginning: the mockers who said "you are one possessed of jinn" (v. 6-7's register) will be questioned "all together" (v. 92): doubt is not a state that *misses* its object; doubt is a state whose object *is coming*, and this verse says it in the calmest possible grammar — *we bring it*: *ʾātīka*, present tense, as a guest brings a gift.

**"We Bring You": the Host Reversed**

Lot had, the prior telling records, shut his door and spread his arms over the guests — the host defending his *nukār* (v. 62); the angels' sentence re-seats the roles: in this house, *they* are the hosts and he is the one being *brought* the news — which is the tradition's reading of the verse's little miracle: the guest who is fed, fed by what he brings. The commentators add that the sūrah means this structurally: Lot was the warner who received *no* tidings for years — his people "doubted" everything he carried — and in the night of his greatest distress, *the tiding comes to him too*: the same messengers who announced the son of the promise (v. 53) announce the end of the doubt (v. 63), and the reader is meant to notice that it is *one company of angels* carrying both announcements across one night — the sūrah's *bishāra/indhar* pair (vv. 49-50) walking through two houses, the tent that laughed and the city that lusted, and in both the verdict is named *a coming*.

**The Object of *Yamtarūn*: Doubt as Active Churn**

The form VI verb is not "they did not believe"; it is "they kept doubting, *back and forth*" — the deliberate agitation of doubt over evidence already set before them — and the *tafsīr* tradition uses the verse's grammar to teach the Qur'an's law of *imtār* seen at the sūrah's own start: the demanded miracle (angels, vv. 7-8), the refusal of the given one (the Book, v. 9), and the sūrah's own sentence for what doubt becomes when it is *chosen*: "thus do We place it into the hearts of the guilty — they believe not in it *until they see* the painful punishment" (vv. 12-13): the object of doubt is deferred to sight, and our verse is the hour of sight arriving at the house of the one who bore the warning. so Lot's city was not uninformed; it was *actively doubting* ("*yamtarūn* — in what they were *kept* doubting," the imperfect of persistence), and the verse's justice is that what is *brought* is exactly what was churned: the punishment is not alien to their doubt; it *is* their doubt, concluded: a man keeps doubting over a fact, and the fact arrives *with* the doubt attached, since every doubt in this book is a *deferred verdict*, and the Day's other name, in the sūrah's vocabulary, is *al-ḥaqq* (v. 64, and v. 85: "we created it not but *bi'l-ḥaqqi*," and v. 92's oath: "by your Lord, We shall question them *all*").

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:64

> **And we bring thee the truth, and surely we are truthful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Veracity-Engine in One Verse**

*wa-naʾtīka bi'l-ḥaqqi wa-innā la-ṣādiqūn* — "and we come with the truth, and we are, verily, truthful." The verse repeats the angels' self-warranty three times over in twelve words — *bi'l-ḥaqqi* (the objective truth they carry), *innā* (the oath), *la-ṣādiqūn* (the subjective sincerity of the bearers) — and the *tafsīr* tradition calls this the complete *ʿadāla al-nāqil*: the truth of the message *and* the trustworthiness of the messengers, both affirmed, in one breath, by the messengers themselves — which, the classical theologians note, is possible of angels precisely because they are *maʿṣūmūn* by office: the verse is the Qur'an's only place where God's envoys formally certify *both* halves of the transmission, and it stands here, in the night of Lot's house, as the template for every *khabar ṣādiq* the community will ever receive. The second *bi'l-ḥaqqi* in as many verses (v. 55 at Abraham's tent: "we bring you the glad tiding *with truth*"; v. 64 at Lot's: "we bring you the truth") binds the two houses together: the promise of a son and the sentence of a city are delivered by the *same* veracity-engine — "and the verse answers the verse: God's *ḥaqq* has two hands, one that gives the heir and one that ends the town, and neither hand contradicts the other: both are *ṣidq*, and the *ṣādiqūn* sign, in the same words, at both doors."

**"The Truth" as Luggage: *al-ḥaqqi* Against the *Mumtār***

The angels do not say "we bring *punishment*"; they say "we bring *the truth*" — and the noun is this sūrah's own settled freight: "We did not create the heavens and the earth and what is between them *except with truth*" (v. 85), "the Hour — *bi'l-ḥaqqi*" (v. 85's twin clause; and 16:77's "wa-jāʾaka'l-ḥaqq"), the Book that "removes doubt" (v. 2's *kitāb* which the mockers called sorcery, v. 15: "we do not say except that some of our gods have possessed you with evil" — the accusation of *siḥr* is, in the sūrah's vocabulary, the *name the doubters give to the ḥaqq*, v. 15). So Lot's guests bring, as luggage, the very substance the town denied, and *al-ḥaqq* here is deliberately *the same word* the sūrah has used for the Qur'an's own claim: the angels at Lot's door and the revelation at the Prophet's stand under the identical charge against the identical office — the *siḥr/sāḥir* and *majnūn* accusations of vv. 6-15: *ḥaqq* arriving at a house of *imtār* is called magic, and the verse's answer is the sentence Lot's city will learn in one morning: the truth *is what arrives*; the doubt is what stays.

**"We Are Truthful": the *Ṣidq* of Bearers and the Company the Believer Keeps**

The oathed assertion *wa-innā la-ṣādiqūn* gives the sūrah its only group to whom God grants, in the angels' own mouth, the title *ṣādiqūn* — and the moralists applied the pairing to the inner court: every heart hosts voices — the company of *ṣidq*, whose tidings are *ḥaqq* and whose tellers are *ṣādiq*, and the whisperer "who withdraws" (114:4-5), whose every certainty dissolves at the first light of scrutiny — and the verse is thus the permanent instruction on audition: *hold every voice to the double test of its message and its claimant*, the test the angels here pass in six words and the sūrah will, at its end, apply to every other company it has mentioned: the mockers (vv. 95-96, the *mustahziʾūn* whose laughter is the *ghayy*'s applause), the *bashar*-sneer (v. 33), and the *mumtārūn* (v. 63) — none of whom can say *wa-innā la-ṣādiqūn*, because in the economy of this book the truthful ones are only the ones who bring what they *did not have to want*: the angels brought the *ḥaqq* they were *sent* with, without appetite, without agenda; the town brought its guests-claim with appetite (v. 67), and Lot brought his daughters' offer with grief (v. 71): the criterion the moralists derive from the verse is this: *ṣidq* is the state of one who carries truth with no private stake in what he carries — which is, finally, the sūrah's whole ethics of audition in six words, and the only will that never breaks: "the *ḥaqq* of your Lord: *wa-qāla jāʾa'l-ḥaqqu wa-zahqa'l-bāṭilu inna'l-bāṭila kāna zahūqā*" (17:81, the verse whose *zahūq* — vanishable — the sūrah's morning of vv. 66-73 will *exhibit*: the doubt that churned for a lifetime, *gone by sunrise*).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:65

> **So set out with thy family during the night, and follow behind them, and let not any of you turn around, but go forth wheresoever you are commanded."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Set Out in a Portion of the Night": Salvation's Timing**

*fa-asri bi-ahlika bi-qiṭʿin mina'l-layl* — "travel with your family *in a slice of the night*." The verb *sarā* (to journey by night, the root of the *Isrāʾ*) is used by God, in the Qur'an, for precisely two exoduses: Lot's here and Israel's under Mūsā — "and travel by night with the sons of Israel; you will be pursued" (20:77; 26:52; 44:23) — and the tradition hears the pairing as a rule: when God saves, He saves *in the dark, by a piece, and with pursuit at the heels*: not at noon, not whole, not unhurried. The commentators dwell on *bi-qiṭʿin* — a *cut-off piece* of the night, the noun of a detached portion: the rescue is allotted no more than a segment, and the verse's brevity enforces its etiquette: do not calculate the whole night; take the piece you are given. It is the same *juzʾ maqṭūʿ* grammar of v. 44, now inverted: the lost have their *cut portions* waiting at each gate of the Fire; the saved are permitted only a *cut piece* of night for their escape — and between the two cuttings stands the law: whatever of this life is yours to *take* is a portion; do not try to carry the whole city out with you.

**"Follow at Their Rear": the Order of Flight**

*wa-ttabiʿ ʾadh-hurāhum* — "and you, follow their rear." Lot, the prophet, is ordered *behind his own family* — and the *tafsīr* tradition makes the verse's oddity into its lesson: the one who led in warning walks *last* in deliverance, rear-guard of his own salvation, because in the hour of flight the weak of the company set the pace and the strong take the dangerous place. This is the umma's general law of leadership, sealed in the Prophet's ﷺ rule for whoever stands over a congregation: "when he prays with you, lighten it — for in the ranks are the weak, the sick, and the one with pressing need" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Muʿādh b. Jabal): the rear is *the place of the strongest*, and Lot's position at the back, facing the city, means his face is *turned toward the destruction he is leaving* while his body moves the other way — the one gaze the verse permits to no one else (the next clause), and even to him, the tradition adds, only from the distance of the *qiṭʿ*: the shepherd of the saved watches the fall of the doomed *as their cover*, not as their witness for the record.

**"Let Not One of You Turn Around": the Law of Not-Looking**

*wa-lā yaltafit minkum aḥad* — "and let not *one of you* turn." The prohibition is absolute — *minkum aḥad*, not one, no exception — and it falls *after* v. 60 has already recorded the fate of the one who looked: the sūrah's sharpest question at this verse, which the commentators do not dodge: if she was *qaddarnā* — decreed — why command her? Their answer is the sūrah's own theodicy retold: the decree fixes into a record what the command obliges into a *proof*; she was addressed among the saved ("let not *one of you*"), the summons passed over her head in full justice, and she answered the summons with a backward glance. The source's note brings the People of the Book's memory beside it — "his wife looked behind him and became a pillar of salt" (Genesis 19:26) — and the *tafsīr*, keeping the *motive* open on the Qur'an's silence, transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās and the Salaf the rival reports — pity for her people, longing for her property, a mother's reckoning of which daughters were still inside — without choosing, because the verse's subject is *the turning*, not the reason for it. The moralists then seal the clause with its one-word rule: the backward glance is *lag begun* — the heart that re-watches what God has ended slows the feet God has commanded forward — and in this sūrah's idiom the glance and her dust-name (v. 60's *al-ghābirūn*, of *ghubār*) are one reality's two faces: look back, and you *become* the dust; the command forbids, in a single clause, nostalgia as a verdict.

**"Go Forth Wheresoever You Are Commanded": Obedience Without a Map**

*wa-mḍhū ḥaythu tuʾmarūn* — "proceed wherever you are commanded." The destination is left *unnamed*: the saved receive *direction* without *map* — Lot is told where *not* to look but not where to arrive, and the flight is obedience, not itinerary. In a sūrah whose enemy asked to see "the Day *they* are resurrected" and was answered with a term he could not read (vv. 36-38), the friends are asked for neither reading: *go where you are commanded* is the *munẓar*-law spoken to the saved — the reprieved who walk forward need the command, not the calendar. (The destination speaks itself two tellings away: "when Our command came, We turned its uppermost upside down" — 11:82 — and here the sūrah lets the order stand bare.) Thus the verse's four clauses make up the tradition's complete manual of exit — *a piece of night, the rear guard, no backward eye, no wherefore* — which the handbooks of *sulūk* apply, without hesitation, to the flight it was first given to figure: the soul leaving its Sodom. Steal away by the portion you are given; cover the weakest of what you carry with the strongest in you; do not re-enter in imagination the ruins God has already pulled down beneath you; and walk toward the command, not the map — for the map of that night was one sentence long, and it saved a house.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:66

> **And We decreed this matter unto him: that the last remnant of those people will be rooted out in the morning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Decree as a Cutting: *qadāʾ*, *dābira*, *maqṭūʿ***

*wa-qaḍaynā ilayhi dhālika'l-amra anna dābira hā'ulā'i maqṭūʿun muṣbiḥīn* — "and We decreed to him that matter: that the *after-end* of these is cut off, at morning." Three of the sūrah's own words are working in a single sentence, and the tradition hears them as a chain of blades: *qadāʾ* — the decree — in the Qur'an's idiom is never a floating fiat; it is the *settling* of a matter to its cut (the root *q-ḍ-y* carries, in Arabic's earliest registers, the sense of determining and severing); *dābira* — the after-part, the tail of an affair (the same word, the same image of a cut tail, in the verse of all destroyed peoples: "the *last trace* of the wrongdoers was cut off, and all praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds" — 6:45); and *maqṭūʿ* — the very participle of v. 44's *juzʾun maqṭūʿ*, the cut-off portion, now loosed upon the cut-off people. The sūrah has spent its Paradise-section teaching that every soul receives a *cut share* by its choices (v. 44); here the *city's* share is cut at the root — the same grammar of severance turned from the persons of the tryst (v. 43) onto the collective they were. And the chain teaches the *ʿaqīda* the sūrah keeps policing: the decree is *information*, not caprice — "and We decreed *to him*" — the lot of the guilty was settled *as* it had been earned (their *ijtihar* of v. 13's hearts into which doubt was threaded), and what the sūrah calls *qadāʾ* the Day will call *jazāʾ*.

**"At Morning": the Time of the Stroke, and the Courtesy of Warning**

*muṣbiḥīn* — "as they enter the morning." The verse's hour is its theology: the exit was commanded in a *slice of the night* (v. 65) and the cutting at *morning* — God gives the saved the dark for their departure and grants the doomed the remaining hours, and the tradition counts that grant as one of the sūrah's proofs of mercy even inside punishment: between the decree whispered to Lot and the Cry that seizes (v. 73), there is exactly *one night* — the time it takes a family to walk out and a city to *not* repent — which is the same courtesy the sūrah has legislated from its beginning: "and your Lord would not destroy the towns until He had sent to their mother-town a messenger reciting Our signs" (28:59), and its axiom, "We never punish until We have sent a messenger" (17:15, the text the source quotes at v. 4): the *known* decree, the *warned* town, and the *morning* between. And the parallel of Hūd supplies the verse's own commentary on that interval: "indeed their tryst is the morning — is not the morning near?" (11:81): the nearness of the morning becomes, in the handbooks, the standing figure for the nearness of the Hour — "*iqtarabati's-sāʿatu wa-'nshaqqa'l-qamar*" (54:1) — and the reader who has watched Lot's family count the hours until *ṣubḥ* is the reader the sūrah has trained to feel, in his own chest, the *near* morning: every deadline in this book is the Hour's rehearsal, and every "is it not near?" is addressed to the comfortable, *tonight*.

**The Decree Announced to the Prophet First: the Honor and the Anatomy of a Warning**

That the *amr* is "decreed *unto him*" — the verse told to Lot before it was executed on the town — is the detail the *tafsīr* uses to complete the passage's lesson on how revelation speaks of ruin: the prophet is not a bystander to the decree but its first addressee, so that his warning is *informed*, not improvised (the source's note: in 11:70 the angels "tell Abraham they are sent to the people of Lot, indicating that they have come to destroy them" — the news travels from prophet to prophet one night ahead of the stones), and so that the destroyed cannot say they were not, in some measure, *told* — the entire architecture of vv. 51-66 (the guests, the alarm, the *bal naʾtīka bi-mā yamtarūn*, the decree, the morning) is one continuous *iʿdhār*: the excuse of v. 32's court ("*mā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn*") answered in advance for Lot's city: you will not be able to say the night came without a door, because the whole sūrah is the story of that door, and the morning is only its second hinge. The application the tradition draws is double and plain: for the warned, the one-night rule — the decree that names its hour is a mercy *within* the decree, and whoever spends the reprieve ("is not the morning near?") in the town's way — *yastabshirūn* over what will kill them (v. 67) — has chosen to be the *dābira* that is cut; and for the warner, the *ilayhi* rule — the man who bears the news of ruin may not soften the hour, but he must *know it himself first* and *walk his family through the dark before it*: Lot, told at evening, is gone by dawn — the warner's *sunnah*: be ahead of your own warning by one honest night.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:67

> **And the people of the city came, rejoicing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Stolen Verb: *Yastabshirūn***

*wa-aqbala ahlu'l-madīnati yastabshirūn* — "and the people of the city came, rejoicing." The participle's root has, in this sūrah, belonged to the angels alone: at Abraham's tent *nubashshiruka* (vv. 53, 55 — the verb of heaven's glad news), and here, for the first time in the book, *men* are *yastabshirūn* — the mob rejoices over what it has heard: beardless youths in the prophet's house. The verb, moved from the messengers to the predators, is the sūrah's whole satire in one morphology: the gladness over the promised son and the glee over the promised prey share a root, and the distance between the two is the distance between vv. 51-60 and vv. 67-77 — the city that could not *hear* a *bishāra* about a child (its prophet had warned it for decades and was called a fool, the class of v. 13's *yamtarūn*) now *runs* at the rumor of a feast: appetite converts news into joy the way revelation converts it into hope, and the verse is the Qur'an's most economical portrait of the lowest use of the human capacity for gladness. The source's esoteric gloss (Aj) reads the scene forward, as the sūrah intends: the people of Lot "represent the heedless: when they find those whom they think will conform to their caprices, they rejoice over them" — *istibshār* at a person's *availability* is the joy of the wolf, and every age's mob knows its own version: the rejoicing over the stranger who has entered the town where the host has no army.

**"The People of the City": the Grammar of the Corporate Crime**

The verse names them *ahlu'l-madīna* — the *people of the city* — and the *tafsīr* notes what the naming does: no individual is picked out; the *city* arrives, a single subject of plural verbs, "came *rejoicing*." This is the sūrah's standing jurisprudence of collective sin, drafted since its first town-verses: v. 4 destroys *towns*, not suspects; v. 12 condemns the *qawm* of every messenger; v. 58 sent the angels to a *people of guilt*. A crime that is *celebrated in the street* has become the street's; the mob's joy is its verdict rendered before the judge — which is why Lot does not plead with individuals but with the corporate body: *fa-lā tufzalūn*, "do not disgrace me," plural, addressed to the city-as-one — and why the destruction, in turn, falls on the *town* (v. 74: "We made its uppermost its nethermost"): the grammar of the passage — city in, city out — is the fiqh of the *ijmāʿ of crime*, and the hadith mirrors the same law for the umma's day: "when the people see the wrongdoer and do not restrain him, God will soon cover them all with His punishment" (al-Tirmidhī and Abū Dāwūd, from al-Jārūd al-ʿAbsī).

**"Came": the Verb of the Rush, and the Door the Prophet Faced Alone**

The parallel telling adds the speed — "and the people *came rushing toward him*" (11:78, *yuhraʿūna*, the verb of being poured out as water is poured) — while our verse's *aqbala* (turned toward, advanced) keeps the menace unhurried: they *approached, rejoicing* — the step of a celebration, which is worse than the shout of a raid. The scene is, in the commentators' summary, the one every prophet of this book stands in — the righteous few facing the festive many — and the next three verses will show the *method* of the facing: appeal to the law of the guest ("these are my guests," v. 68), appeal to God's fear ("fear God," v. 69), appeal to the host's own sacred face (both verses); the mob's answer (v. 70: "did we not *forbid* you?") completes the portrait of the criminal conscience that *knows* the law and calls it a restriction on the rescuer rather than on the crime. The reader is meant to recognize the structure: every *yastabshirūn* crowd comes first to the door of the man who hosts what it wants to consume, and every one of them says, when stopped, the same sentence: *you were told not to interfere*. The sūrah's verdict on that sentence is the morning (v. 66) — and the verse's last lesson is the tradition's: Lot, knowing the stones decreed and the family saved (vv. 65-66), still *pleaded* with them to the last, because the warner argues *as if* the plea could change the decree until the decree is executed — *al-iʿdhār*, the finishing of the excuse, so that no town is destroyed until its own joy has been heard at its door and answered at it: "so that whoever lives, lives by a clear proof, and whoever perishes, perishes by a clear proof" (8:42).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:68

> **He said, "Truly these are my guests; so dishonor me not!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"My Guests": the Pronoun That Armors the Door**

*qāla inna hā'ulā'i ḍayfī fa-lā tufzalūn* — "he said: these are *my guests* — so do not abase *me*." Lot does not say "these are strangers, leave them be," nor "they are innocent" — he *claims* them: the genitive of *ḍayfī* is the shield he raises, and the tradition hears in the single word the whole *fiqh al-ḍiyāfa*: the guest, once received, travels under the honor of his host, and to touch him is to touch the man who opened the door; the verse thus legalizes protection as *personally binding*: *lā tufzalūn* — "do not put me to abasement" — the *wazl* root (abasement as a *falling from rank*, the same noun the Prophet ﷺ made a refuge in his own daily prayer: "I seek refuge in You… from the *abasement of the guest* (wa-min wazl ad-dayf)" — al-Bukhārī, from Ibn ʿUmar, in the supplication against the *zawāl* of favor) — the verse's word is, in the Prophet's usage, the one evil a Muslim is taught to flee *in so many words* as a named catastrophe, which is the *tafsīr*'s proof that v. 68 is not a plea of a desperate man but a *law spoken at the moment it is being broken*: the sanctity of the guest is not Lot's invention for this emergency; it is *the* emergency when it is denied.

**The Order of the Two Words: the Claim, then the Prohibition**

The verse's *faʾ* — "so" — binds the *fact* to the *verdict*: *they are my guests, therefore* do not abase me — and the commentators mark the order as the sūrah's school of argument: Lot does not open with "fear God" (that comes in v. 69, after they refuse); he opens with what the city's *own* code already holds sacred — the guest-right of the desert, older than any law of Sodom, the code its fathers honored — the method of *dawā* that meets a people *at their last remaining sanctity* and argues from it before escalating to God; and when that is refused (v. 70), the escalation *itself* becomes the witness (8:42's *iʿdhār*, cited at v. 67): the mob of the ages is never destroyed before it has been spoken to in the language it still respects. That is why this verse, not the next, is the sūrah's tactical jewel: the warner begins at the *audience's* conscience, not at his own; and why the *tafsīr* notes the tenderness of the pronoun: he says *my* guests, *me* — the prophet *stoops* to personal importunity ("do not shame *me*") for the sake of those under his roof, and the *ʿārifūn* read his stoop as the *sunnah* of intercession itself: the righteous plead *personally* where the law is deaf, and the hadith of the visitor whom God dispatched a guardian for ("a man visited a brother in another town; God watched over the road with an angel… 'God loves you as you loved him for His sake,'" Muslim, from the report of Abū Hurayra) makes the guest, in the Book's own economy, a *theophany of the road*: whoever abases one, abases the Watcher.

**The Guests Who Did Not Need the Shield**

The scene's irony, which the sūrah withholds and the *tafsīr* releases: Lot risks his name, his house, and his life for guests whose protection was already, in every real sense, *self-executing* — they are the sent ones (v. 61's *rusul* carries the night), the town's hands will not close (the striking of the faces at the door is the parallel telling's moment, 54:37 — "but We struck their eyes": the mob *rushed* and was blinded — a *karama* for the guest the host did not ask and did not need: his plea was *for the plea's sake*, the completing of the *iʿdhār* of v. 67). The commentators draw from this the *adab* of the one who defends what God will defend anyway: Lot pleaded *not because the outcome depended on his word but because his people's fate depended on their hearing it* — every defense of the powerless by the powerful is, in this sūrah's calculus, an act owed to the *oppressor's soul* as much as to the victim's body: the shield he raises is, before all, a *proof* he hands to those trying to break through it. And the verse's last grace is its place in the sūrah's chain: the same company of guests who, at Abraham's tent, *needed* no meal and *brought* a son; here need no shield and bring a morning — the two houses of vv. 51-66 are the two ways heaven's errands arrive: fed and unfed, honored and besieged, and in both, *sent*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:69

> **Reverence God, and disgrace me not."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Escalation: from the City's Code to God's Name**

*wa-ttaqū Llāha wa-lā tukhzūn* — "and fear God, and do not put me to shame." The verse adds the second verb — *ikhzāʾ* (shame, the public disgrace that *covers the face* with it; *khazī*, the humiliated) — beside v. 68's *wazl* (abasement of rank), and places before both the name of God: the *tafsīr* reads the sequence as the warner's full ladder of address: first the guest-right the city already honored in its fathers ("these are my guests"), now God — *wa-ttaqū* — the vertical summoning when the horizontal plea has been weighed and rejected. That the verse preserves *both* appeals in two consecutive lines is the sūrah's law of *iʿdhār* again (8:42, at v. 67): no argument is skipped, no register of conscience left untried, before the morning; the town is destroyed in vv. 73-74 having been addressed, in the space of a doorway, by *custom, by God, and by the prophet's own face* — three courts, three refusals, one verdict.

**"Fear God": the Sūrah's Key-Word at Its Most Ironic Hour**

*al-taqwā* — the word on which this sūrah's entire hinge turns (v. 45: "the reverent *are* amid gardens"; v. 47's surgery of the chest performed on "the reverent"; and the angels who pass every house in the book by the one test, "will you not fear?") — is spoken *here*, in this verse, to the men at the door of Lot: the word that will define Paradise is offered, in the sūrah's most dramatic hour, to the people who are already inside the decree. The commentators mark the double-edged mercy of the moment: the call to *taqwā* is addressed to a city whose *mawʿid* is set (v. 66: the morning) — and *this is the verse's deepest lesson*: the call to God is never *late* in the mouth of the prophet even when, in the court of decree, it is "too late" in the calendar of the sinner; every door at which *wa-ttaqū Llāha* is spoken is, from the inside of the promise, an open door, and from the inside of the refusal, a sealed one — and only the *morning* knows which, which is why the *daʿwa* goes on and the *sāʿa* is kept. The one *khāna* in the house (v. 60's lagging wife, whose looking-back v. 65 forbade) makes the verse's audience complete: *taqwā* was being said, this night, to a household too — inside Lot's own walls — and the sūrah, which has just saved "the family all together *except*," does not permit the reader to forget that the call rings in *every* room of *every* house on the street.

**"And Disgrace Me Not": the Prophet's Face as the City's Last Screen**

The repetition of the plea — *lā tukhzūn* after v. 68's *lā tufzalūn*, the double prohibition of the two verses — arrests the *tafsīr* tradition: why beg twice? Because, say the commentators, the second plea is the *finishing* of the proof: the first (*wazl*) appeals to the *rank* the mob must respect; the second (*khizy*) appeals to the *shame* the mob must still feel — honor and its mirror, and when *both* fail (v. 70's reply), the city has declared that it has *no remaining screen at all*, which is precisely the condition on which destruction, in this book's jurisprudence, becomes final: a people that has lost *both* reverence before God and *bashāʾa* (shame) before men has, in the sūrah's economy, no address left — and the Prophet's saying at the other end of the revelation makes the pair the umma's own *ʿaqīda*: "when you have no *bashāʾ* (shame), do as you will" (al-Bukhārī, from Abū Masʿūd al-Badrī) — the sentence that *defines* v. 69's mob: the verse is not Lot asking the city for protection; it is the sūrah, through Lot, *diagnosing the city's last vital sign*, and v. 70 will record its death.

**Application: The Etiquette of the Dying Hour's Plea**

The two verses 68-69 are, in the handbooks, the complete *ādāb* of the one who stands between the oppressor and the oppressed: begin with what the oppressor still respects (the code, the custom, the shared name), escalate to what he should respect (God), and speak, all the while, in the first person of your own exposed face — Lot does not say "these people are innocent and you are guilty"; he says "*my* guests — do not shame *me*": the prophet *personalizes* the plea not from weakness but as the final offer of relationship to men about to forfeit theirs. And the verse's consolation is for the one whose plea fails: Lot's double refusal *was* the verdict's execution, not his pleading's failure — *iʿdhār* completed is the warner's success even when the city falls; the morning is not the proof that the doorway was wasted but that it was *heard*. Let the defender of the guest, the neighbor, the voiceless, therefore measure his task by the sūrah's law: *try the custom, then the Name, then stand naked in the street of your own honor* — and when even the last screen is torn (v. 70's sentence will show it), withdraw your body to where the command has already drawn the line (v. 65: *go where you are commanded*), and leave the morning to its Lord.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:70

> **They said, "Did we not forbid you from [providing protection] to all the people?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mob's Only Commandment**

*qālū aw lam nanhakum ʿani'l-ʿālamīn* — "did we not forbid you *concerning the worlds*?" The verse contains, in seven words, the city's entire legal code, and the *tafsīr* tradition reads that fact with a shudder: Sodom's only *nahy* — its single kept prohibition — was the prohibition of *rescue*: the law it enforced against its prophet was "no one receives anyone," a wall raised around the crime so that no stranger could enter the reach of it. The interrogative *aw lam* — the *istifhām al-tawbīkh*, the rebuke-question — puts the voice of *enforcement* in the mouth of the *enforced-upon*, the inversion the sūrah has spent its whole enemy-cycle dissecting (v. 12: the hearts into which mockery is threaded; v. 95: the mockers whose laughter is legislation) — and the tradition's one-line diagnosis of the verse is the definition of every tyranny's vocabulary: "they called *their* crime the *forbidden*, and *his* mercy the *trespass*."

**"Concerning the Worlds": the Universal in the Mouth of the Particular**

*ʿani'l-ʿālamīn* — "all the worlds," "anyone who comes" — and the word is the verse's second blade: the city had made its exception *total*: where the sūrah's saved parties are gathered by God's *ajmaʿīn* (v. 59: "We shall save them *all together*"), Sodom's prohibition is its own counterfeit *ajmaʿ*: no one at all, never, from anywhere. The commentators hear, in the *ʿālamīn* here, an echo of the sūrah's own verdict on the town's uniqueness — "you approach the *fāḥisha* which no people in the worlds preceded you with" (29:28) — a city whose crime is, in the Qur'an's phrase, *without precedent among the ʿālamīn*, and whose *law* is therefore, without irony, *against the ʿālamīn*: the same noun marks the crime's scope and the prohibition's target, and the verse's grammar is its theology: a rule made against *all comers* is a rule against the very *ʿālamīn* of whom every prophet is the mercy, and the sūrah's readers hear, behind the mob's sentence, Pharaoh's: "if you take a god besides me, I will place you among the imprisoned" (26:29), and Hamān's: "O noble ones, I know of no god for you besides me" (28:38) — the tyrants' syntax is one syntax: the *forbidal* of *anyone* is the claim to be *lord of the door*.

**The Forbidden Mercy: Adam's One Tree, Sodom's Every Guest**

The *tafsīr*'s finest comparison at this verse is structural: in the sūrah's own Ādam-section, the divine *nahy* was *one tree* amid a garden of permission — "and He said: dwell… and eat from wherever you will, but *do not approach* this tree" (2:35; 7:19) — the prohibition as a *boundary within abundance*, and the fall of Ādam was *not* that the boundary existed but that it was *pressed against*. Here the mob's *nahy* is the inverse: one mercy amid a city of prohibitions — every guest forbidden, every stranger a crime — *abundance enclosed by a wall*, and the sūrah sets the two *nahy*-verses across its whole arc to teach the *ʿaqīda* of prohibition itself: God forbids *one thing* in a field of *lawfulness* (mercy with breathing room); the tyrant forbids *every thing* in a field of *appetite* (a breath that is the crime) — and the two societies that will be judged in these verses, the saved house and the doomed city, are precisely the two answers to the two prohibitions: *pressed against the one mercy* (Ādam, who fell and rose, vv. 26-30) and *kept the mob's prohibition* (Sodom, who held its line and fell without rising, vv. 73-74): the verse is thus the sūrah's balance: it shows the *direction* in which a *nahy* moves a heart — toward the Giver or against Him — and vv. 66's morning has been weighing in that scale since.

**Application: When the Crowd Commands the Conscience**

The verse names, for the believer, the *mob's first trick*: the *nahy* that disguises itself as *custom* — "did we not forbid you?" is the sentence of every collective that demands the individual's conscience as the price of belonging, and its whole force rests on the question-form *aw lam* — *haven't we?* — the past tense of a fabricated consensus. The tradition's three practicals against it: first, *audit the consensus*: the *ʿālamīn* of the mob's law ("everyone here knows...") is, in this verse, a lie about universality, and the believer asks: forbid me *where* — in which *kitāb*, by which *ʿāqil*, against whom — because Sodom's prohibition had, in the end, no address but "us." Second, *name the mercy you are being forbidden*: Lot's crime was *ḍiyāfa*, and the scholars draw the rule: every prohibition that makes *hospitality, rescue, asylum, kindness* a trespass is not a *nahy* of a people; it is the *sijjīl* of a people (the *sijjīl* of v. 74 — "stone of pressed clay," in the lexicographers' gloss — is read in the handbooks as the *forbidden mercies returned*: every guest the city refused, sent back). Third, the verse's consolation to the forbidden one: the mob's *nahy* is *reported* in the Qur'an, and Lot is *named* in the same pages: the sūrah, which ended its enemy's cycle with the oath "We will question them all" (v. 92), holds the mob's sentence *inside* that oath — and a prohibition kept only inside the Qur'an as *evidence of a destroyed city's crime* is a law whose date of expiry was written before it was spoken, "the last of those people cut off at morning" (v. 66).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:71

> **He said, "These are my daughters, if you must act."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Pointer, Twice: "These Are My Guests… These Are My Daughters"**

*qāla hā'ulā'i banātī in kuntum fāʿilūn* — "he said: *these* are my daughters, if you are men who *do anything*." The demonstrative is the verse's hidden architecture: Lot has just pointed at the angels — *hā'ulā'i ḍayfī*, "these are my guests" (v. 68) — and now points, with the identical word, at the women of the city: *hā'ulā'i banātī*. The two *hā'ulāʾ* verses form the scene's chiasmus, and the *tafsīr* draws the lesson: the sentence that would *protect* the guests and the sentence that would *marry* the daughters are, in the prophet's mouth, the same grammar of presentation — "here, take this instead of that" — and the city, which could neither be moved by the guests' claim nor accept the lawful substitute for its crime, is exposed by the parallel: it was never *the prohibition* it defended (v. 70) but *the appetite*, which no lawful alternative could touch: the verse proves that the mob refused the *permitted* precisely because it came for the *forbidden*, the only diagnosis its own answer allows (v. 72: they were "intoxicated," wandering — below).

**"My Daughters": the Prophet's Fatherhood and the Law of Substitution**

*Banātī* — the consensus of the commentators: the word means *the women of his community*, lawful to them in marriage (the source's JJ wording: "if you wish to satisfy desire, then marry them"); the prophet of God offered not his biological daughters but the *ḥalāl* standing before the *ḥarām*, and the jurists' maxim read from this very scene is that *the women of a people are as daughters to its prophet*. And the fatherhood named here is, in the Book's idiom, *real*: a prophet is *the father of his community* in the rank of mercy and obligation (the sūrah's own kinship-theology: the *āla* saved by *direction* not blood, v. 59), and the tradition's jewel at this verse is the Prophet's ﷺ line on the *chain of noble sons*: "the noble son of the noble son of the noble son of the noble: Yūsuf b. Yaʿqūb b. Isḥāq b. Ibrāhīm" (al-Bukhārī, from Anas) — the very line that began at Abraham's tent (vv. 51-60) is here, at Sodom's door, being *begotten* in prayer by his nephew: the "daughters" and the promised "son" belong to one sūrah's economy of *ḥalāl* — the tent where a *son* is given by God, the city where *daughters* are offered by the prophet against the mob's *fāḥisha*: two tables, two seeds, two verdicts.

**"If You Must Act": the Condition That Condemns**

*in kuntum fāʿilīn* — the condition is the verse's last, terrible courtesy: Lot offers the lawfully-obtainable *on the assumption that the men are a people who do things*, and the *tafsīr* hears the sentence's true weight: *in* is not hope here — the prophet of Sodom knows his people (decades of warning, v. 70's boast of their prohibition, v. 66's morning already written); the condition therefore functions as *testimony, not bargaining*: "the offer is made *to be refused*, so that the refusal be *recorded as a refusal of the ḥalāl*, and not merely of the ḥarām" — which is the sūrah's final move of *iʿdhār* (8:42 at vv. 67-69), and the scholars' rule of substitution for all the ages: whoever closes a door to a *lawful* way must be heard asking for an *unlawful* one, and a community that has legislated against the *halāl* (v. 70: the *forbidding of every guest*, and the marriage-market Sodom made impossible for strangers) has, by this verse's logic, made *itself* the "if" that will not be fulfilled: *in kuntum fāʿilīn* is a sentence the city answers, at the same instant, with its own silence — and v. 72 will paint that silence: not refusal but *intoxication*, the inability even to say *no* to a lawful offer.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:72

> **By thy life, they wandered confused in their drunkenness.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath No Other Man Shares: *La-ʿumruka***

*la-ʿumruka yataḥayyarūna fī suʾkrihim* — "by *your life*, they were dazedly wandering in their intoxication." The *tafsīr* preserves two readings of the address, and the tradition weighs them both: for the majority (the source names IK, Kl, M, R, Ṭ, Ṭs) the oath is God's, sworn *by the life of the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ* — the only human life in the entire Qur'an by which the Most High swears (M's note, quoted in the source: *God has singled him out with this honor*), the *ʿumr* made the *maqsam bi-hi* beside sky and earth and the Book of vv. 1-9's opening; and the minority reading (Kl, R, Z) hears the angels' voice completing the scene of vv. 63-70 — "by *your* life [O Lot]" — Lot swearing to his guests about the mob beyond the door — and the two readings converge on the verse's function: at the most disordered moment in the story, an *oath by a life* anchors the scene: God's own tribute to the life that bears all this news, or the prophet's own grip on the word of honor while his town drifts past his gate — whichever reading, the *ʿumr* stands in the frame, and the frame is *person*, not event. The source adds, as the tradition's crown on the verse, the couplet of Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār from the *Manṭiq al-Ṭuyr*: "when the crown of *by thy life* was set upon his head, the mountain at his threshold was level as a hill" — the Sufi poets' consensus that this one oath dignified a *human existence* as no revelation had dignified a throne, a star, or a stone.

**"Wandering": the Drunkard's Gait Without the Wine**

*yataḥayyarūna* — to wheel about, to go this way and that with no vector: the verb of the circling daze, and it is the Qur'an's one verb for the state of a city that has lost its bearings. And the noun of their state: *suʾkrihim* — "their intoxication" — a word the Qur'an keeps for *drunkenness without wine*: the intoxication of the Hour — "you will see the people intoxicated, though they are not intoxicated — but the punishment of God is terrible" (22:2) and, here, the intoxication of *lust and doubt together* (the source's Bg and JJ: "their state of doubt and uncertainty"), which makes v. 72 the sūrah's diagnosis of Sodom's last hour: they were not, at the door, *sober men choosing*; they were *drunk men staggering*, and the verse is read beside 6:110's *taqallub*: "We turn their hearts and their eyes over, as they refused to believe in it the first time, and We leave them in their trespass (*ṭughyānihim*) wandering (*yaʿmahūn*)" — the *ʿamh* and the *taḥayyur* are the same stagger: the punishment of the churning doubt, in this book's law, is exactly *to be left wheeling*: no sight, no direction, no sobriety of choice — and the mob at Lot's gate, who could not see that the guests before them were their own *judge at the door*, is the sūrah's demonstration of what the eye of the *mumtār* becomes: drunk at noon, confused in a room with two doors, unable — as v. 71's refused lawful offered proof — to *act* at all.

**"They": the Two Readings of the Pronoun, and the Verse's Edge for the Reciter**

The source records the grammarians' dispute over the pronoun's reference: al-Ṭabarī hears, in *hum*, the Quraysh (Ṭ: the verse as an aside to the Meccan mockers — God swears by the Prophet's life that *his own town* is wandering in the *same* suʾkr as Sodom's gate), while al-Khāzin and the majority (Kl) read it of the people of Lot, *within* the scene. The *tafsīr* tradition treats the duality as intentional: the sūrah keeps the pronoun loose so that the *ḥikāya* never closes around the *story's* actors alone — the same verse that shows Lot's mob staggering at a door shows Quraysh — and every later *mutawassim* reading the sūrah's morning — "so the Cry seized them at sunrise" (v. 73) — at the ruins on the trade-road (v. 76) adds his own age to the pronoun: the *wandering in intoxication* is the sūrah's standing diagnosis of the *mujrimūn* (v. 12's class) in every century, and the verse's oath-function is the believer's protection inside it: the only sober position the *ʿumr*-oath defines is to stand on the side of the *warned* man at the door, not the *staggering* crowd at the gate — and the Prophet's ﷺ law, transmitted as the umma's own *suʾkr*-test, belongs at the verse: "two blessings people are *cheated* (ghabana) about: health and free time" (al-Bukhārī, from Ibn ʿAbbās) — sobriety, in the Qur'an's idiom, is not the absence of wine but the presence of *direction*, and the *ʿumr* sworn upon here is the only sober thing the chapter's night contains.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:73

> **So the Cry seized them at sunrise,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb That Doubles: *fa-akhāḍhat-humu ṣ-ṣayḥatu* — and Thamūd's Verse**

*fa-akhāḍhat-humuṣ-ṣayḥatu bi'l-wuqūʿi'ṣ-ṣubḥ* — "and the Cry seized them at the falling-in of the morning." The phrase is, syllable for syllable, the one this sūrah will use again of Thamūd — "so the Cry seized them at daybreak" (v. 83: *fa-akhāḍhat-humuṣ-ṣayḥatu muṣbiḥīn*) — and the repetition is the verse's primary meaning: the sūrah, delivered *to the people of al-Ḥijr*, the very territory of Thamūd (vv. 80-84), welds Lot's dawn and Ṣāliḥ's dawn into *one sentence* so that the audience reads its own name into the pronoun: the verb that *seized* (akhadha — to grip, to overtake, the verb of sudden lawful seizure) is not a report about antiquity; the sūrah's architecture has spent fifty verses arguing that history in this book is a *single statute* with many cases (v. 4's *kitāb maʿlūm*; v. 5's term that can neither advance nor delay; v. 92's oath to question *them all*), and v. 73 *executes* that statute by repeating its formula verbatim over two towns separated by a millennium: the sentence that fell at sunrise on Sodom is the *same sentence*, kept on file, for every sunrise of every *mujrimūn* — and the sūrah welds the two mornings tighter still: the hour-word of Thamūd's seizure, *muṣbiḥīn*, is the very word of Lot's verdict at v. 66, "their root cut off *at morning*": one participle, two towns, the decree of the one become the clock of the other. And the *tafsīr* (Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī) notes the harmonization with Hūd's *rajfa* ("the earthquake seized them," 11:67): here the *sayha*, there the *tremor*, because in the tradition both came in one morning — the Cry *with* the shaking — the one event narrated by its sound and its motion, as the sūrah's morning is narrated by its two verdicts together: the family gone out in the *qiṭʿa* of the night (v. 65) and the town *rooted out* at the *wuqūʿ* of the morning (v. 66).

**"At Sunrise": the Hour That Is This Sūrah's Whole Clock**

*bi'l-wuqūʿi'ṣ-ṣubḥ* — the dawning of the daybreak — and the verse keeps every clock the sūrah has set. *Muṣbiḥūn* (v. 66) named the hour of the decree; *mawʿiduhumu'ṣ-ṣubḥ* — "their tryst is the morning" — the angels said in Hūd's telling (11:81), and the nearness-question followed: *"a-laysa'ṣ-ṣubḥu bi-qarīb?"* "is not the morning near?"; now the morning *falls* — and the tradition hears in that single *ṣubḥ* the sūrah's answer to Iblīs's calendar: the enemy asked for "the Day they are resurrected" (v. 36) and was given a *waqt known* to God alone (v. 38); Sodom was told "the morning" and got *the morning at its falling* — the pattern the sūrah generalizes: God reprieves towns *to* a known decree (v. 4) and enters *at* the appointed hour, so that the morning is always, in this book, double-faced: the hour of rescue for the *āla* of Lot — the same light that saw his family safe over the hills — and the hour of seizure for the city that rejoiced at the door: dawn, which is the world's standing proof that one reality can be *two verdicts at the same instant*, which is the meaning of vv. 49-50's center. And the *wuqūʿ* itself — "falling" — is a word the sūrah's own vocabulary has charged: things "fall" (*waqaʿa*) when what was *waʿd* becomes *wāqiʿ* — the promise and the threat are both, grammatically, things that *fall*; and the *sunna* of fajr in the umma's life — the prayer at which "the angels of the night and the angels of the day attend" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra, in the commentary on 17:78's "and Qur'ān of the dawn") — means the community's every morning is the scene's rehearsal: *the witnesses are gathering at the hour of the fall*, which is why the scholars loved this verse at dawn and why the handbooks of *muḥāsaba* set it as the *dhikr* of the *ṣalātu'l-fajr*: to cross the town's gate at sunrise is to walk through v. 73.

**"Seized Them": the Grammar of the Sudden, and the Courtesy Within It**

*Akhadha* — the Qur'an's verb of *lawful, complete, sudden* taking — carries here everything the preceding three verses performed: the mob was *drunk* (v. 72) and could not steer, and seizure is what falls on the steering-less; but the *tafsīr* guards the verse against reading the suddenness as caprice: three courtesy-layers precede the grip in the sūrah's own ordering: the *news* of the punishment was delivered to its prophet a night before (v. 66), the *exit* was commanded and *executed* (v. 65, vv. 70-71's pleas exhausted the town's excuses), and the *name* of the hour was announced so the morning would find them — *nothing but the timing* was sudden, and timing is precisely what the sūrah has sworn God reserves (v. 38; v. 5: "no people can advance their term an hour, nor can they defer it"; and the seizure is the *end of all courtesy*, which is why the verb of the seizure has no patient-voice mercy-verb beside it: *akhadhat-hum* — active, sudden, complete; and the tradition's summary of v. 73 in one sentence, quoted at the verses' head in the source's orbit) "God does not destroy until He has exhausted warning; and what He then does is not *new* — it is what the warning *was*: the same sentence, at last, *seized*".

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:74

> **then We made its uppermost to be its lowermost, and We rained down upon them stones of baked clay.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Geography of Pride: *jaʿalnā ʿāliyahā sāfilahā***

*wa-jaʿalnā ʿāliyahā sāfilahā* — "and We made its highest its lowest." The verb of *making* (*jaʿala*) — the same divine verb as in the sūrah's anthropology ("when I *proportioned* him and breathed into him," v. 29; "We *made* you means of living," v. 20) — is here an act of *inversion*: the city's top becomes its floor, and the tradition reads the sentence as the sūrah's final verdict on the *rank* Iblīs built his whole case upon: "I am *better*; he is clay" — the claim that *up* is a quality of nature is answered by the One who *makes* up and down as He wills: Sodom, which had legislated a hierarchy of appetites (its "law" was, in v. 70's words, *forbid the weak to the strong's taste*), receives a *geological* lesson: there is no *ʿāliya* that God will not *turn to sāfiya*; the only ascent in this book is *taqwā* (49:13), and the only *inversion* that lasts is the one vv. 43-44 already drew — each gate of the Fire a cut portion, *in accordance with the degree to which they followed* (v. 44, source's Aj): the city's top and its bottom are, in the end, the *same* degrees, read downward. The report the collections transmit gives the act its wings: in the Miʿrāj tradition the Prophet ﷺ asked his community whether they knew what he had seen — "*the cities of Lot, until the people of the first heaven heard the braying of their donkeys*" — Lot’s cities lifted, then turned upside down (Aḥmad, from Ibn Masʿūd, graded *ṣaḥīḥ* by al-Ḥākim): the uppermost *exalted to the level of heaven’s hearers* — and then *made the lowest* — a parable of every elevation that pride believes permanent.

**"We Rained Upon Them": the Punishment Dressed as a Gift**

*wa-amṭarnā ʿalayhim ḥijāratan min sijjīl* — and the sūrah chooses, for the stones, the verb of *rain*: the sky's own gift-word — "and We *sent down* from the sky water and gave you to drink from it" (v. 22), the same sky whose lamps are a guard (v. 16), the same *inzāl* that brought the *kitāb* (v. 1's *āyāt*, v. 8's *nunazzilu'l-malāʾikata*) — now raining *stones*: the *tafsīr* tradition marks the irony of the verb as the sūrah's deliberate poetics: the destroyed are punished *in the grammar of the blessing*, because the whole point of vv. 19-25's *iʿtirāḍ* (the catalogue of sustenance) was that *nature's every kindness is a rehearsal of the divine hand*, and the hand that gives water can, without ceasing to be the same hand, give *ḥijāra*: the *sijjīl*-stones are the mirror of the *māʾ* — what the town *refused* to be to its guests (water, bread, shelter — Lot offered all three, vv. 68-71), the sky *forced* upon it: every stone, in the commentators' phrase, a *guest the town would not host, returning*. And the material itself is the sermon: *ḥijāra* — stone; *sijjīl* — the Persian-lexicon's *sang o gil*, *stone and clay* (the lexicographers' etymology the *tafsīr* transmits): *clay made hard by judgment* — and the sūrah, whose enemy was made *of* fire and whose honored one was made *of* clay, now *returns the clay as the verdict*: the hearts into which mockery was "threaded" (v. 12) end as the *hardness* they practiced: "then your hearts became *like stones or harder still*" (2:74) — *sijjīl* is that comparison, executed: clay that refused to soften under every mercy became the clay that *crushes*.

**"At Your Lord, Marked": the Precision of the Fall**

The parallel in Hūd completes the sentence and answers its only difficult question — *how* does a rain of stones distinguish the guilty from the roofs of the innocent (Lot's family was already gone; still)? — "and We rained upon them stones of *layers upon layers*, *marked* at your Lord — and it is not far from the wrongdoers" (11:82-83: *ḥijāratan min sijjīlin mandūd… musawwama ʿinda rabbika wa-mā huwa mina'ẓ-ẓālimīna bi-baʿīd*): *musawwama* — "marked, designated" — and the Salaf's gloss (Ibn ʿAbbās, in Ibn Jarīr) is that *each stone carried the name of its owner*: the rain of Sodom was, in the tradition's imagery, a *roster*, which is the sūrah's whole doctrine of *ajmaʿūn/ajmaʿīn* vv. 39-44 rendered in meteorology: totality that *individuates* — "their tryst, all together" and "for each gate *its own* cut portion" — no soul in the crowd missed, no stone loosed at random: the collective fall is a *collection of addresses*. And the closing clause turns the verse toward this sūrah's own audience: *"it is not far from the wrongdoers"* — al-Ṭabarī's report on 11:83's intent: the stones were not *only* Lot's: the lesson is *within reach* of every town that makes a law of its appetite — and the sūrah's listeners in Makkah could see, in the Elephant's year a generation before them (the only other *sijjīl* in the Book, 105:3-4: *bi-ṭayrin abābīl, tarmīhim bi-ḥijāratin min sijjīl*), a rain of stones that had come *up the valley toward them*: birds — the *weak* things of heaven — carrying the *marked* clay against the strong: the two *sijjīl*-verses bracket the sūrah's message to its first hearers: the God of the deep valley and the deep sky has the same weather for the same pride, and *"is not far from the wrongdoers."*

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:75

> **Truly in that are signs for those who discern,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demoted Art, Reinstated: *al-Mutawassimūn***

*inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li'l-mutawassimīn* — "verily, in that are *signs for the readers of marks*." The word *mutawassim* occurs once in the Qur'an, here, and its etymology is the verse's whole argument: *wasm* is the *brand-mark* on a herd's face, and *tawassum* was pre-Islamic Arabia's craft of reading omens — the *kāhina*'s art the sūrah already met in vv. 16-18: the eavesdroppers on the sky, pelted by *shihāb* before one word of news could be *stolen*. The Qur'an does not abolish *reading faces*; it *relocates* the faculty: the *mutawassim* of this verse is, in the source's own definition (Z's wording: "those so given to deep reflection that they recognize the reality behind the outward *simah* of things"), the man who reads *ruins* the way the soothsayer read birds — and the sūrah's revolution in divination is that the marks are now *honest*: they do not *announce* the future, they *record* the past, and the future they disclose is only the one God has already sworn by (v. 92: "We will question them *all*"). Where the *kāhin* projected meanings *into* the sky and was driven out with burning fire, the *mutawassim* finds meanings *lying in the land* — the overturned city, the standing road, the stones — and is *invited* to look: this is *tiyāra* inverted into *ʿibra*: the soothsayer’s craft ends at the sky he cannot reach, and the *mutawassim*’s begins at the ground he will not read — "*al-ṭiyāra shirk*," the Prophet ﷺ said (Aḥmad, from ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd), and what he ﷺ *loved* in its place was *al-faʾl al-ḥasan* — the good word lifted from the lip of the road (al-Bukhārī, from Anas): divination’s eye, re-aimed at the *āyāt*.

**The Plural of the Discerning: *āyāt***

The verse gives the *mutawassimūn* *āyātin* — signs, plural — and the source's triad (vv. 75-77) is built on the arithmetic: Sodom is *many-reading*: for every sense an evidence — the *timing* (v. 73's dawn), the *method* (v. 74's inversion), the *material* (the *sijjīl* that was clay), the *address-list* (11:82's *musawwama*) — and the discerning gather a *flock* of lessons, because reading without revelation leaves you *counting*: each *āya* a separate fact about separate sinners; this is why the sūrah keeps *āyāt* for every onlooker of the destroyed (as at 26:8, 26:67, "in that is a sign, and *most of them are not believers*"): the ruins are *plural* to the eye and *one* to the heart; and the heart's verse follows (v. 77), where the same scene contracts from *āyātin* to *āyat*. The *tafsīr* of the two-levels' ratio (at this verse in al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr): the *mutawassim* sees *that* and *how*; the *muʾmin* additionally sees *whom the whole is about* — and the sūrah's own first use of *āyāt* (v. 1: "Those are the *āyāt* of the *kitāb*") frames the pair: *āyāt* are given to be *read*, and reading that stops at *plurality* has not yet entered the *kitāb* whose every ruin signs the same sentence.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:76

> **and verily they are on a path still standing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Road That Keeps the Lesson Open**

*wa-innahā li-bi-ṣabīlin muqīm* — "and verily, *it* stands on a path that stays." The source's note (Aj) gives the *tafsīr*'s plain sense: the destroyed cities lie "on a road upon which people travel, where they can see the vestiges of the people of Lot" — the caravan-route of the Ḥijāz, the very highway this sūrah's first audience rode to Syria; and the verse's genius is its *tense*: it does not say "a road passed" but *bi-ṣabīlin muqīm* — a road *standing*, *residing*, *performing its station* — and the participle *muqīm* is the Qur'an's word for what continues by *being done*: *aqāmu* is the verb of prayer itself ("those who *establish* prayer," the sūrah's own refrain for the saved), and a road is *muqīm* exactly as long as feet keep it so: the lesson of Sodom is not *inscribed* in the valley but *performed* by every caravan that crosses it — the monument and the passerby together make the *āya* stand: which is the sūrah's silent argument against forgetting: *what is not re-walked, re-read, re-prayed, falls*; and the two *muqīm* things of this revelation — the Book v. 9 swore to guard and the road this verse swears stands — corroborate one another to the same sentence: the *kitāb* recited and the *ruin* visited say, syllable for syllable, *thus does God deal with the city of the appetite*, and the sūrah's structure closes the loop from its opening sky-lamps (v. 16: *ḥufuẓan*, lamps *guarded* against the eavesdroppers of divination) to its open ground: heaven keeps its fire *from* the thieves of news; earth keeps this news *for* whoever walks.

**"Verily They Are On": the Oath Applied to Geography**

The verse is the second of the triad's oaths (v. 75's *inna* for the *mutawassimūn*, v. 76's here, v. 77's next for the *muʾminūn*), and the choice of *what* God swears at is the tafsīr's quiet astonishment: He swears not *that* Sodom perished — the sight is the proof — but *that the place is on a road*: an oath about a *map*, because the sūrah's miracle here is *accessibility*: the punishment of the guiltiest city in the Book is not veiled in the *ghayb* but *zāhir*, addressable, ticketable; the *ḥujja* God chooses for His own claim, in this verse, is *tourism of the heart*: He swears by the *standingness* of the evidence that the evidence *stands*. And the Meccan context sharpens the oath: the caravans of Quraysh were this sūrah's *enjoined audience* — "the covenant of the Quraysh" (106:2-4) was made for the *winter and summer journeys* that *passed the valley*; the standing road receiving from the Messenger ﷺ the one *visiting-hours* rule the verse implies: a *muqīm* road is entered by a *muqīm* heart, and the heart's *iqāma* is *bukāʾ*, weeping: vv. 75-77's triad exists so that *the passage should be a prayer*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:77

> **Truly in that is a sign for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Many Signs to the One: the Arithmetic of the Triad**

*inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li'l-muʾminīn* — "verily, in that is *a sign* — for the *believers*." The sūrah's three oaths (vv. 75-77) are a descent from *plurality to unity*: the *mutawassimūn* were given *āyātin* — signs, plural, the many readings the eye collects from the overturned city — and the *muʾminūn* are given *āya*, singular; and the *tafsīr*'s answer to "why one?" is the epistemology of īmān in a line: the *muʾmin* needs no new *fact*, only the old *meaning*. The crowd of evidences — the timing, the inversion, the clay, the roster on the stones — *collapses, for him, into the one sentence this sūrah has been saying since its first line* (v. 1: *dhālika āyātu'l-kitābi wa-qur'ānin mubīn*): that God judges the city and saves the family. The triad thus maps the road of vv. 51-77 as a whole: Lot's story is *heard* (vv. 63-64 — "we bring you what they were doubting"), *seen* (v. 75 — *āyāt* for the discerning), and finally *believed* (v. 77 — the one *āya*): and the structure says what the story was *for*: not information (Quraysh knew the ruins), not astonishment (the caravans passed them daily), but confirmation — the sign the believer reads, in the end, is not *that* Sodom fell but v. 59: "the *āla* of Lot — *We shall save them all together*": stones for the town, and salvation as the single verse the believers were made for.

**The Definite Article's Debt: *al-Muʾminūn* Re-Audited**

*al-muʾminīn* — *the believers*, definite: the same class the sūrah constituted on its first page (v. 1: the *āyāt* of the *kitāb* that form a people; v. 9: the *dhikr* God Himself guards; v. 12: the class into whose hearts mockery is threaded), and the article is the verse's law: the *āya* of Sodom belongs, *as āya*, to those already inside the covenant — not because the lesson is forbidden to others, but because *āya* means *a sign within a book*, and whoever has not accepted the Book has, from the valley, only *events*: the *ʿAlīm*-news at the tent (v. 53) is a *sign* to the household of faith and a *strange tale* to everyone else — the definite article therefore *sorts one sunrise into two species of light*, and the reader who arrives at v. 77 from v. 73's dawn knows by which species he has been reading his own mornings: the sūrah has spent fifty verses making *āya* and *ʿibra* alternate names for the same ground, and this verse assigns them: the land testifies to *al-muʾminūn* because they, and only they, come with the second half of the instrument — the Book that tells the land what it is saying.

**The Two Mornings, and the Etiquette of the Road**

The sūrah closes its morning-frame by handing the hour to the reader: the destroyed were seized *at sunrise* (v. 73, *mushriqīn*); the saved are commanded, at that same hour and every hour, to *glorify*: "so hymn the praise of thy Lord, and be among those who prostrate" (v. 98), with its sequel, "and worship thy Lord till certainty comes unto thee" (v. 99). One sūrah, two mornings: v. 77's single *āya* is the hinge, and every fajr is either Sodom's hour or the *musabbiḥ*'s — the same light on the same valley, falling on a town being seized or a heart being saved; which is why the Sunna made this triad's practical ruling for the road that carries these ruins: when the Prophet ﷺ came through al-Ḥijr on the tabūk expedition, he said: "do not enter the dwellings of these people of punishment *except weeping*, lest what befell them befall you" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Ibn ʿUmar) — the etiquette of *ḥijr* beside the sūrah named for the *Ḥijr*: the believer passes through the land of the destroyed *weeping*, comes out into his own morning *glorifying*, and finds, at that hour, the *ṣabr* the sūrah presently sets in its sufferers' breath — the "be not narrow in thy chest" of v. 97 waiting on the far side of vv. 78-84, the sūrah's next towns on the same road — and the cousin-verse of the *ʿibra* tradition seals the passage: "in that is a lesson for those who have sight" (59:2): *āya* enough — for those who believe.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:78

> **And verily the inhabitants of the thicket were wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grammarians' Gentle Oath: *in kāna… la-ẓālimūn***

*wa-in kāna aṣḥābu'l-aykati la-ẓālimūn* — and the verse opens with one of the sūrah's two or three grammatical marvels: the *in* here, the readers of the old tongue (and al-Ṭabarī's "wa-qad kāna" — "indeed, they *were*") takes the archaic affirmative *in* ("verily!"), its noun-clause softened into a verb-clause, and the *lām* on *la-ẓālimūn* is what the grammarians call, precisely, the *lām al-fāriqa* — "the lām that *distinguishes*," the mark that separates this *in* from its twin, the *in* of negation (Ibn ʿĀshūr's analysis, and Sibawayh's before him): a whole sentence of punishment built on a *disambiguating letter* — "they were *not*-wrongdoers? *No* — they were, verily, wrongdoers." The reader who knows the Arabic feels the sūrah, at the opening of a new story of annihilation, *pre-empting denial*: before the Ayka is named, the grammar itself argues with someone who said *they were not wronged*, and the affirmative *lām* is the oath God swears upon their guilt — the *ḥujja* of v. 49-50's court carried into the letter: even the sūrah's *syntax* testifies.

**The Thicket, the Trader, and the Sin against the *Qadar Maʿlūm***

*al-Aykā* — al-Ṭabarī, Qatāda, Mujāhid and Ibn ʿAbbās at its head: "the *closed, interlacing* thicket; the dense wood" — a people of groves (the source: Midianites, northwestern Arabia's mercantile folk — "a mercantile people who engaged in corrupt business," the *shirk*, the highway-robbery and the *nqs of the scale* that al-Ḍaḥḥāk and Qatāda enumerate in the tafsīr-accounts), and their prophet the eloquent Shuʿayb (the source: "the Arabian prophet Shuʿayb called them to worship the one God and to relinquish their sinful ways," with the fuller cycles at 7:85-93 and 26:176-189). But the sūrah's own charge is one word wide: *ẓālimūn* — and the reader who has followed this sūrah knows where *its* economic *ẓulm* is legislated: "there is not a thing," v. 21 swore, "but that *with Us* are its treasuries, and *We send it down only* by a *measured measure* (*bi-qadar maʿlūm*)" — the Ayka's crime is precisely the crime against that verse: men who *re-hand* what God has meted, who *shorten* the *qadar* He came down through, cheating the scale that v. 21's *mīzān* set: the sūrah's stones of vv. 51-77 fall on the city that made *bodies* a market, and now, vv. 78-79, on the market-town that made *measure* a lie — one *ẓulm* of the *qadar*, two towns on the same road, and this sūrah's twin verdicts.

**Two Peoples, One Messenger, One Fire from a Cloud: the Ayka's Own Doomsday**

The source records (at Qatāda's long report in Ibn Jarīr, and 26:176's twin) that Shuʿayb was sent to *two* communities — Madyan and the People of the Thicket — and that they perished by *two* doomsdays: Madyan by the *Cry* (the same *ṣayḥa* as Lot's and Thamūd's); the Ayka by "the Day of the Shadow" (26:189): seven days of such heat that no shade sheltered them, and then a cloud came, and the whole people *gathered beneath it* seeking cool — and it became their torment: fire descending in it and consuming them (al-Ṭabarī, from Qatāda, at report level). The sūrah's structure makes this the *negative mirror* of Lot's end: Sodom received *stones rained from the sky they refused to host* (v. 74, *amṭarnā* — the grammar of gift); the Ayka climbed *toward a cloud they thought was mercy* and found the judgment waiting inside it — the sky answered the town that exploited measure with *un-measured* fire — and the handbooks see in this pairing the sūrah's warning to the merchants of Quraysh, whose own caravans rode past both towns: *the heat you sell others is the heat you will gather under*.

**Application: The Thicket in Every Ledger**
