The Ayka is, in the scholars' summary, the *ẓālimūn* of the *counter*: their *ayka* — the interlacing wood — becomes, in the *mufassirūn*'s moralizing, the figure of a life whose cords braid into one another until no straight stem is visible (the source's Qatāda detail on the interlaced trees belongs here), and the *dhikr* drawn at this verse in the handbooks is threefold: audit the scale, because v. 21 made measure God's own sent-down law; do not trust a comfort that arrives *above* an unjust earning, for the cloud of the Ayka came *exactly as a mercy* — the tafsīr's sharpest sentence at v. 78: "what *shaded* them was what *burned* them"; and remember that this verse's sin was never called, in the Qur'an, *great appetite* but *ẓulm* — the same word as Sodom's — which is the sūrah's insistence that the oppressor's table and the oppressor's bed are one furniture: "verily the people of the Thicket were, in very truth, wrongdoers."

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:79

> **So We took vengeance upon them. Truly the two of them are on a clear road.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Two of Them": the Sūrah Folds Two Towns into One Address**

*fa-ntaqamnā minhum wa-innahumā la-bi-imāmin mubīn* — "so We avenged upon them, and *both of them* are on a road made clear." The dual is the verse's architecture, and the source's note (JJ) fixes its referents: the *two* are the people of Shuʿayb and the people of Lot — and Ibn Kathīr adds what the dual *does*: the two were "relatively close together in time," neighbors of age as of land, so that the sūrah may say of *them* in one pronoun what it said of *each* apart: the city of desire and the city of the false scale stand, in the same verse, *on the same road* — and the road, in the source's gloss (Bg, Q, R: "a road that is visible"), is declared *identical* with v. 76's *ṭarīq muqīm*: the sūrah's archaeology of witness is complete at v. 79: Lot's stones and the Ayka's burning cloud, Sodom's morning and Midian's noon, are *one exhibit*, one highway, one verdict — the pairing of *intiqām* (v. 79's "We took vengeance," *fa-ntaqamnā minhum* — the verb of the *settled account*, from *naqm*: the debt *paid*, the grief *sat upon*, the exact requital, in the lexicographers' sense of *antaqamu* as to take the full measure of vengeance as the avenger of a killed man takes the measure of the kill) answers v. 74's *amṭarnā* the way a balance answers a weight: Sodom was *rained upon*; the Ayka was *balanced*.

**"*Bi-Imāmin Mubīn*": the Road as a Led Prayer**

*imām* — and the word is the verse's secret generosity: the *umm*, the *imām*, is *what goes in front and is followed*: a highway, yes (the *mubīn*, the manifest, visible road the caravans of Quraysh *must* ride to Syria — you cannot miss these towns; they are *in the way*) — but the same noun that gives the prayer its *leader*, and the Day its *rosters*: "the Day We call *every people with their imām*" (17:71) — and the *tafsīr*'s double sense at v. 79 is no ornament: the destroyed cities are, in the Qur'an's own image, *imāms that are followed*: travelers *imitate* the road they walk, and the road they walk says, every mile, which end the two cities' end is; a people's *imām* on that Day and the imām of its caravans in this are, this verse swears, *the same direction*. Which is how the sūrah closes its second road-cycle: vv. 75-77 swore the *signs* to the *discerning* and *believing* on a road that *stands*; vv. 78-79 add the second town to the first and name the standingness what it functionally is: a *leading* — the ruins do not merely *stand*, they *go in front*, and every traveler is their *maʾmūm*: whoever passes Lot's valley and Shuʿayb's thicket *follows*, whether he prays toward the lesson or repeats the crime behind it.

**"So We Took Vengeance": and the Verse the Sūrah Answers with *Ṣafḥ***

The tradition's closing note on this pair of verses is structural and, once seen, unforgettable: the *intiqām* of v. 79 — divine vengeance, full measure, no appeal — stands six verses before the sūrah's last command to its Prophet: "*fa-ṣfaḥ* — turn from them the *fair* turning" (v. 85). The *sūra* thus places, in the same movement of its end-piece, the *settled account* the towns received and the *un-settled grace* the Prophet is told to keep extending — vengeance is *God's* verb, performed, finished, on *record* (v. 92: "by thy Lord, We *will* question them all"); the Prophet's share of the same scene is *ṣafḥ*: the open-palm refusal to collect, *now*, what heaven will collect *then*. The handbooks draw the practical at this verse in one line — *the accounts are not your office*: the man passing between Sodom and Midian, between the town that shamed the guest and the market that shamed the scale, is invited to *read* the imām (v. 79's road) and to *leave* the requital to its Owner (v. 85's *ṣafḥ*): which is why the *two* towns on the *one* clear road are, in the sūrah's final arithmetic, a *single* sign for the *believer* (v. 77) — the vengeance is *written*, the patience is *commanded*, and the road between them is *mubīn*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:80

> **And indeed the inhabitants of al-Ḥijr denied the messengers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Own Land on Trial: *aṣḥāb al-Ḥijr***

*wa-inna aṣḥāba'l-Ḥijri la-kadhdhabū'l-mursalīn* — "and verily, the *people of al-Ḥijr* denied the sent ones." The third town is named, at last, by the sūrah's own title — and the *tafsīr* (Z, in the source's note) gives the geography its teeth: *al-Ḥijr* is the rocky plain between the Ḥijāz and Syria where Thamūd dwelt, "from which they derived their name," and the sūrah that opens with letters (v. 1) and a guarded *dhikr* (v. 9) has been *named* all along for the territory of its audience's ancestors-by-trade: the Meccan caravans that rode the *imām mubīn* of vv. 76-79 rode *through* al-Ḥijr — the stones of Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ, which "the Arabs of the time of the Prophet ﷺ knew well" (the source's note at v. 82) — so that v. 80 is, in the ear of every Qurayshī hearer, an indictment delivered *by the postmark of his own route*: the sūrah's title is its first *ḥujja*, and the town it names is the town it is *speaking to*. The *inna* with its answering *lām* (*la-kadhdhabū*) gives the verse the same oath-solemnity as v. 78's grammar — the sūrah's three closing towns (Lot's, the Ayka's, Thamūd's) are each sworn into fact before they are told: *no town enters this end-piece unnamed, and none unsworn*.

**"Denied the *Messengers*" — Sent One, Pluralized: the Law of the Single Rejection**

*kadhdhabū'l-mursalīn* — plural — and the source records the commentators' question and its two answers: the people of Ṣāliḥ denied *Ṣāliḥ alone* (Bg, R, Z), yet the Qur'an says *the sent ones*, because (Z's alternate) the denial fell on *Ṣāliḥ and the believers with him*, and because, in the sūrah's own jurisprudence rehearsed since its Noah and Lot cycles, *to deny one messenger is to deny the office*: the angels of vv. 51-60 came *as* the *mursalūn* of every age, the one message tested by the one mouth, and the sūrah's earlier verdict verse — "they *do not believe in it*, from the first time to the last" (v. 12's class, *lā yu'minūna bihi*) — shows the grammar: the object of Thamūd's *takdhīb* was, in the end, the same *bihi*: one Book, one camel, one messenger, one rejection: which is why the sūrah's *audience* is indicted in the same breath it hears the indictment of the dead: "did We not *send* among the earlier communities" (v. 10) — the *mursalīn* of v. 80 includes, by the sūrah's economy, the one now speaking to Quraysh: the verse is a mirror with a postmark.

**"Al-Ḥijr": the Forbidden Stone, and the Camel That Was God's *Ḥijma* on Their Land**

And the name of their land says more than geography, for *ḥijr* in the tongue of the *tafsīr*'s lexicographers is at once *stone*, *the forbidding*, and *the inviolable precinct* (the *ḥijr* of the Kaʿba bears the same root: the sacred enclosure one may not violate) — and Thamūd's whole story, which vv. 81-84 will tell, is precisely a *violation of a ḥijr*: God's *sanctuary on legs* in their valley, "the she-camel of God" (7:73), whose *share of the water* was appointed to her by name ("a day of water for her, a day of water for them," 54:28; the source's note at v. 81 records the appointed drinking-day and the command "let her graze in God's earth and seek not to harm her" (7:73)) — a stone people who made their homes in the mountains (v. 82) and their verdict in the sky (v. 83), and whose crime, in the sūrah's triad of the *ḥijr*-word, was *violating what was forbidden them in their own land*: they hamstrung the precinct. The *tafsīr* tradition at this verse loves the irony of the *ḥijr*-name for a second reason — the same sūrah that names a town *the Forbidden* ends its Thamūd-cycle at the Prophet's ﷺ own *ḥajr* command for its ruins when the umma rode past them at Tabūk: "do not enter… except weeping" (at v. 77 above): the land stays *ḥijr*, forbidden to presumption, open only to tears: which is the last lesson of v. 80's *kadhdhabū*: the denial of a *mursal* turns every land of the denyer into a *ḥijr* — a place God *fences off* from His mercy, and that travelers may enter only as mourners.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:81

> **And We gave them Our signs, but they used to turn away therefrom.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We *Gave* Them": the Sign as Gift, the Sin as Refusal of a Relation**

*wa-ātaynāhum āyātinā fa-kānū ʿanhā yuʿriḍūn* — "and We *gave* them Our signs; and they kept turning away from them." The verb of donation — *ātā*, the Qur'an's verb of *givenness*, the same as v. 20's "We *made* for you therein means of living" and v. 21's "We send it down" — frames Thamūd's miracle not as evidence deposited in a courtroom but as a *gift* opened in a house: and the crime the verse names is, accordingly, not *disproof* but *iʿrāḍ* — the turning of the shoulder, the body-language of the offended giver: the sūrah, which has spent its Ādam-section on the *istikbār* that *refused to bow* (v. 31's *aba*, *istakbara*), gives Thamūd the posture of the turning-away as their whole *ʿaqīda* in one verb: *kānū ʿanhā yuʿriḍūn* — they *were* (habitual *kāna* + imperfect: a people whose identity was the *keeping* of aversion, the *dāb* of the neck bent away) — and the commentators' second reading at this verse (the source, Q and Bḍ: "the signs" are *the pregnant she-camel, accompanied by many other signs* — her size, her milking, her rock-born coming forth, Ṭs's general "proofs and miracles") keeps the plural *āyātinā* intact by making the *one* camel a *flock* of signs: the gift was *much* and the turning was *total*.

**The Camel: the Sign That Was Also a Share of Water**

The source's note at this verse gathers the camel's three names in the Qur'an — *burhān* (clear proof, 7:73), *āya* (sign, 11:64), *fitna* (trial, 54:27) — and the sūrah's own ordering at vv. 81-84 keeps the third foremost: the sign *given* was a sign *laid* ("We shall *send* the she-camel as a trial," 54:27: *innā mursalū* — the same verb root as the *mursalūn* of v. 80: the camel *sent* as the messenger's twin), and her terms were *economic* — graze free on God's earth, harm her not, share the well by the drawn day (7:73; 26:155; 54:28's "a day of water for her, a day of water for them") — which is the *tafsīr*'s ground for what the verse means by *iʿrāḍ*: a miracle you *avoid* is a miracle you have *priced*: they did not deny the camel's coming (she was *among* them), they *turned from her* — the refusal of *share* is the refusal of *sign*, and the sūrah has, since v. 12-13, defined *kufr* of the final town by this same arithmetic: "even if We open for them a door of the sky and they *ascend* therein, they would say: only our eyes are dazzled" (v. 14-15): *iʿrāḍ* is the *ʿamh* of v. 72's drunkenness seen from the side of the gift: the gift refused, the eye that will not see, the water-day *not shared*: the camel was, in the commentators' fine phrase at 54:27's *fitna*, the *test made flesh* — and flesh can be hamstringed.

**"They Used to Turn Away": the Habit Broken by the Cry**

The *kānū... yuʿriḍūn* of v. 81 and the *kānū yanaḥṭūna... āminīn* of v. 82 are the sūrah's *two habituals* — the grammar of a way of life, turn and carve, avert and build — and v. 83's *fa-akhāḍhat-hum* ("thereupon the Cry seized them") is the Qur'an's most merciless *faʾ*: the *sudden* upon the *habitual*: what *used to be* every day broke on one morning (v. 83: *muṣbiḥīn*, the very word of Lot's verdict at v. 66: the same dawn's participle welded across two towns). And the verse’s last lesson stands in its twin on the Meccan side of the sūrah: "We send down the angels not but with the truth, and then they would not be respited" (v. 8) — heaven’s errands *descend with ḥaqq* and *without reprieve*, and Thamūd’s camel was the same economy on four legs: a gift that could be turned away from but not put off — the respited night of Lot’s guests (v. 65’s single darkness) and the respited *day of water* of the she-camel (54:28) are, in the sūrah’s arithmetic, the *same* courtesy, one granted and one denied, and vv. 81-84’s four verses read, in the handbooks’ summary, as the *definition of the economy of kufr*: refuse the gift, hoard the gain, hew the shelter, meet the dawn.

**Application: The Signs Still in the Valley**

The scholars draw from this verse the *rule of the passed-by sign*: the people of al-Ḥijr were not destroyed for *failing to solve* a miracle but for *turning from* a gift that had *entered their share* — the camel drank *their* water on *her* day, which is to say: the sign was not a spectacle but a *claim upon the ledger* — and the umma's passing of their dwellings (Tabūk, v. 77's weeping-command) is the same verse read *from the other side of the valley*: the believer who rides past the ruins, or past the mosque, or past the hour of his own morning prayer, is being *given* — *ātaynāhum* — before he is judged, and his *kāna* is being written: *kānū ʿanhā yuʿriḍūn* or *kānū bihā yadhakkarūn*: the sūrah, which "gives signs" to every generation that passes al-Ḥijr, asks one question of every passer: *what is your habitual*: what do you *keep turning from*?

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:82

> **And they would hew dwellings in the mountains, [feeling] secure.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Hewed": the Scribe's Verb, the Grammar of Permanence**

*wa-kānū yanaḥṭūna mina'l-jibāli buyūtan āminīn* — "and they *used to hew*, out of the mountains, houses, *in safety*." *Naḥṭ* is the old verb of *engraved work* — the cut of the line into hard stuff, the root the later lexicographers give to the pen's incised letters (the *khaṭṭ* of the scribe is, in one of its names, *nawaqṭ* and *inḥiṭāṭ*), and the choice is the verse's quiet satire: Thamūd did not *build*, they *inscribed*: they treated their dwellings as their *writing* — the rock-cut facades of Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ, still legible when the source notes that "during the time of the Prophet ﷺ the Arabs were familiar with the remains of these mountain-dwellings," were, in the idiom of the trade, the *book* this people wrote in stone, and the sūrah, whose own title-claim is a *guarded dhikr* (v. 9) written on a *preserved* page, sets its permanence *against* theirs: what God keeps needs no quarry; what the quarry keeps is, by v. 83, already an inscription *of* the Cry *in* the rock: the *naḥṭ*-verb thus carries the whole verse's argument in its phonetics — a people who *cut* into the mountains were *cut* (v. 66's verb, *maqṭūʿ*, the hewing's own root of severance, here turned upon the hewers).

**"Feeling Secure": *Āminīn*, the Word Given Twice in One Sūrah**

And the verse's hinge-word is the one the sūrah has already *spoken from the other side*: at v. 46 the people of the Gardens are bid — "*enter them in peace, āminīn*" — and at v. 82 the people of the rock are described — "*āminīn*" in their hewn houses: the sūrah's two uses of the same participle are its entire theology of security: there, the *gift* of *amn*, promised, eschatological, given by the All-Merciful to the reverent; here, the *feeling* of *amn*, self-manufactured, geological, carved against every weather — and the *tafsīr*'s two readings of the motive (the source, Kl: "because they would be *less vulnerable to destruction*"; Kl and R: or "to *evade God's punishment*") name exactly the illusion the pairing exposes: *safety read as architecture*, *amn* as a property of walls rather than a promise of the *Munʿim*, which is why the handbooks read v. 82 as the Qur’an’s clause in its standing argument with every fortification: the twin tellings keep the verb and lose only the adjective — "and you *hew* of the mountains palaces, *showing off*" (7:74: *ḥāhirīn*), and "you *hew* of the mountains houses, *cunning*" (26:149: *ḥirāʾ*) — skill, cunning, and *amn* are the three moods of the one chisel, and in all three the sequel is the same Cry (7:78: "*the earthquake seized them, and they became in their homes, prostrate*").

**"Out of the Mountains": the Shield of Nūḥ's Son, Rewarded**

The verse's sharpest intertextual edge is held by the *jibāl* — and the tradition of vv. 82-83 reads it beside the drowning of the *other* mountain-trust: "he will *take refuge* on a mountain that will *shield me* from the water" (11:43: *saʾāwi ilā jabalin yaṣimunī min al-māʾ*), and the answer thundered back across the flood: "there is *no shielder* (ʿāṣim) today from the command of God, save him He shows mercy to" (11:43's end): Thamūd's hew is the *same theology* in dry country — elevation, enclosure, *ḥirz* against the decree — and the sūrah, which has now told of *water* (Nūḥ), *stones from the air* (Lot), *fire from a cloud* (the Ayka), completes the set with *mountains that did not move* (Thamūd): every element the refuge-builders trusted becomes, in the telling, the element of the seizure: the flood rose on the hill, the stones fell on the plain, the cloud burned under its shade, and the Cry *entered the rock as a morning enters a room*: which is why v. 84, at a single verse's distance, pronounces the law over all four: "*and that which they used to earn availed them nothing*" — the *ʿāṣim* of 11:43 and the *mughnī* of v. 84 are the same denied noun: *nothing avails, nothing shields*, against the command, save the mercy it excepts.

**Application: The Bunker and the Cradle**

The exegetes' practical at v. 82 is a rule for the believer's own *yanaḥṭūna*: the sūrah does not condemn the house but the *āminīn* — not security *in* the home but security *from* God lodged *in* the home — and the handbooks set the two *āminīn* verses of the sūrah side by side in the student's *muḥāsaba*: the walls you build to *not be reached* are the exact inverse of the walls the reverent are *invited into* ("Gardens of *ʿAdn*, entering them *āminīn*," v. 45-46, whose *salm* is given, not hewn); therefore: *build, and lodge your amn in the Builder* — the Prophet's ﷺ law on the same ground of al-Ḥijr makes the posture exact, for when the umma rode past the hewn facades the command was *not* "admire" nor *even* "fear" but "*weep*, lest what befell them befall you" (cited at v. 77): the only permissible *security-relation* to the houses of the *āminīn*-dead is *tears* — and tears are the one building-material of the *āla* of Lot: vv. 59-60's saved household, hewn from no mountain, carried out of the city in a *qiṭʿa* of night (v. 65): the sūrah's whole architecture of safety, from the tent of Abraham to the valley of Thamūd, is a single sentence: *the saved travel light and weep much; the destroyed hew deep*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:83

> **Yet the Cry seized them in the morning,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shared Hour: *Muṣbiḥīn*, the Word That Crossed from Lot's Verdict to Thamūd's Fall**

*fa-akhāḍhat-humuṣ-ṣayḥatu muṣbiḥīn* — "so the Cry seized them at the breaking of day." The last word is the first treasure of the verse: *muṣbiḥīn* is, exactly, the terminal word of Lot's verdict two dozen lines above — "their root cut off, *muṣbiḥīn*, at morning" (v. 66) — and Thamūd's seizure now takes the very participle of Sodom's decree: what was *decided* for the one town is *dawned* upon the other, and the sūrah has welded its two great morning-cycles into a single clock: Lot's people are seized *mushriqīn* ("at sunrise," v. 73), Thamūd *muṣbiḥīn* ("at daybreak," v. 83), and Hūd's telling of Lot's same morning carries *this* word for *that* town ("the earthquake seized them *muṣbiḥīn*," 11:67) — one participle circulating between two annihilations, the sūrah's grammar of *the one morning of the criminals* which its own warning-verse had promised: "their tryst is the morning — *is not the morning near?*" (11:81, at v. 66), and the nearness-question, here, has been answered a second time for a second town, which is the verse's plain terror: the morning is *always near*, and it is *always the same hour*.

**"The Cry": One *Ṣayḥa*, Three Earths of the Ḥijāz**

The noun too is the shared coin: *ṣayḥa* has now seized, in this sūrah, Sodom (v. 73) and Thamūd (v. 83), and the source's note completes the set from the commentators' harmonization: the *Cry* visited on Thamūd "is said to have been an *earthquake* (7:78) accompanied by a terrible, loud sound," and 11:67's *rajfa* and our *ṣayḥa* are, in al-Ṭabarī's reading, one event with a sound and a shock — the *ṣā'iḥa* that *levels*, the same divine weather the sūrah has been gathering since its *ṭayr* of stones and *shihāb* of guard-stars: heaven's artillery in this sūrah runs to a crescendo: meteors pelting the eavesdroppers of the sky (vv. 16-18), stones raining on the city that refused its guests (v. 74 — the only other *sijjīl* in the Book being the Army of the Elephant's, 105:3-4), and finally, for the third town, the plain *voice*: the *ṣayḥa* is the sūrah's *last* sound-word, and the sūrah's *first* words were *āyāt* of a *recited* Book (v. 1): the end of the sūrah answers its beginning in the register of sheer sound: the sky *speaks* — once, in thunder, to the heedless town; always, in *dhikr*, to the *muʾminūn*. Which is the verse's other, quieter function in its place between the *āya* of the standing road (vv. 76-77) and the next verse's Hour (v. 85): every morning, in the valley this sūrah is named for, is a rehearsed resurrection.

**"Seized": the *Faʾ* of the Sudden upon the Habitual — and the House That Was Already a Tomb**

The *faʾ* binds v. 83 as it bound v. 73, and the sūrah's two *kānū* habituals — *yuʿriḍūn* (v. 81), *yanaḥṭūna... āminīn* (v. 82) — collapse into the aorist of seizure: the *used-to* ended in one *at daybreak*: and the parallel tellings add the tableau the *tafsīr* loves, in one line each: "they became *jāthimīn*, prostrate, in their homes" (7:78), and "as though *they had not prospered therein*" (11:67's *kā-an lam yaghnaw bihā*), and of Thamūd in al-Qamar: "We sent upon them a single Blast, and they became like the crushed stalks of a penned fold" (54:25: *ka-ḥaṣīmi muʿtaḍḍim*): the *buyūt* of v. 82 — hewn, engraved, *inscriptive* — are, in the same breath, the *dār* of the prostration and the *fold* of the crushed stalks: the houses the *āminīn* cut from the living rock became, before dawn was old, the exact shape of a *qabr*, for the Qur'an's burial-images are these: *jāthim, khāwiyy, ḥaṣīm* — and the sūrah's last gift in this verse is its *law of the earned morning*: v. 84, its next word, is the sentence: the *security* and the *seizure* are the same object seen from inside the hewn wall and from the side of the *dhikr*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:84

> **and that which they used to earn availed them not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Which They *Used to Earn*": the Qur'an's Accounting Verb, Closed**

*fa-mā aghnā ʿanhum mā kānū yaksibūn* — "and *that which they used to earn* availed them not." The verse ends the three-town cycle (vv. 78-84) with the noun of *the ledger itself*: *kasb*, the Qur'an's word for *acquisition* — the verb of profit, of the earned, of what a hand lays claim to (the source flags the clause’s twins: "cf. 39:50; 40:82" — the same *aghna* is pronounced over the reprieved enjoyers: "what availed them what they were given to enjoy?" (39:52: *mā aghnā ʿanhum mā kānū yumattaʿūn*) — the formula is the Qur’an’s standing end-note on every destroyed cycle, and Thamūd’s is its last installment in this sūrah) — and the choice of *kasb* over the sūrah's own earlier wealth-words (v. 20's *maʿāyish*, means-of-life; v. 22's *rizq* given from the sky) is the verse's verdict: *God makes means of living; men make earnings* — and at the daybreak (v. 83) the second is weighed against the first and found to be, on its own, nothing: the same sūrah that swore "We give you *therein* means of living" (v. 20, *maʿāyish* — the plural noun of *life itself*, from *ʿāsha*) now swears the earnings of Thamūd *un-availingly cut off* (v. 66's *maqṭūʿ*, the verb of the hewers returned upon their hoard): *mā aghnā* — *availing*, the root *gh-n-y*: to be *rich enough*, to be *a defense* — for in the lexicographers' sense the *mughnī* is that which *suffices and shields* (the *ghināʾ* of the *self-sufficient*), and v. 84 pronounces, over the hewn vaults of al-Ḥijr, that the earned was *neither*: neither rich enough, nor a shield: which answers, to the letter, the son of Nūḥ's mountain ("*a mountain shall shield me* from the water" — 11:43, the same *ghināʾ*-claim in the *ʿiṣma*-verb) and Lot's town's rejoicing mob (v. 67: what did their *ghibṭa* avail, at *muṣbiḥīn*?): three towns, one noun of *the availing*, all three *not*.

**The Earning Was Not Nothing: V. 84 and the Sūrah's Last Oath**

But the verse's two words — *kānū yaksibūn* — must be read beside the sūrah's final oath, two pages ahead, which shares their very grammar: "*by thy Lord, We will question them all about that which they used to do* (*ʿammā kānū yaʿmalūn*)" (vv. 92-93): and the pairing is the sūrah's balance of justice in one typographical fact: the earning (*kasb*) that *availed not* at the morning — the wealth, the hewn houses, the shopped measures — is the *selfsame* earning that *will be asked*: v. 84 kills *kasb* only in its function as *shield*, never in its function as *evidence*: the books of the men of al-Ḥijr were *full* (their *kitāb maʿlūm* of v. 4) while their vaults stood *empty* (*khāwiy*, as at 27:52's sequel to the same story: "*those houses of theirs, standing empty, for that they wronged*") — and the exegetes' practical sentence on this verse, transcribed by the handbooks into the *dhikr*-liturgies of the grave, is the Prophet's ﷺ, which the tradition forever sets beside v. 84: "the dead is followed by three, and two return and one remains: his family, his wealth and his deed follow him; his family and his wealth return, and his deed *remains with him*" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas) — *that which they used to earn availed them not*: except, precisely, the *deed* embedded in the earning, which is the only earning the Qur'an here lets keep its name.

**Application: What Is Sent Ahead Is What Avails**

The verse's *ʿibra*, as the sūrah itself arranges vv. 80-84, is *an economy completed*: Thamūd denied the sent ones (v. 80), turned from the signs *given* (v. 81 — *ātaynāhum*: the camel as *donation*), earned shelter (v. 82 — *yanaḥṭūna*: acquisition as *hewing*), and were *un-shielded* at the morning (vv. 83-84): the cycle is the sūrah's standing warning against the one heresy of trade — that *the balance sheet is the verdict* — and the practical tradition reads v. 84 as Islam's counter-entry in every ledger: the *ghānin* (the enriched) of v. 84's root is *him who sent ahead*, which is the sūrah's own v. 20's quiet rebuke of the hewers — *maʿāyish*, means of *living*, were *made for you in the earth* (v. 20) — and the last word the sūrah puts on the *ākhira*'s exchange rate, "the *ghināʾ* (self-sufficiency) is the *ghināʾ* of the *heart*" (agreed upon, from Abū Hurayra: "wealth is not the abundance of *ʿaraḍ* — but *ghināʾ* is the *ghināʾ* of the soul") — and the handbooks add from the other side of the valley, where the Prophet ﷺ rode past the availed-not and wept (v. 77): the believer's *yaksibūn* is lawful, commanded even — the hewer's sin was never the stone but the *āminīn* lodged in it: *earn, and lodge your ghināʾ in the Ghaniyy*: it is the only *mughnī* the morning has ever seen.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:85

> **And We did not create the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them, save in truth. And surely the Hour is coming; so forbear with beautiful forbearance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Save in Truth": the Sūrah's Cosmology in One Clause, Its Twin in al-Aḥqāf**

*wa-mā khalaqnā's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍa wa-mā baynahumā illā bi'l-ḥaqq* — and the source's first note marks the verse's opening as *verbatim identical* with a clause of Sūrat al-Aḥqāf (46:3), with its company (10:5; 30:8; 16:3; 29:44; and 44:38-39's double formula: "We created not the heavens and the earth and what is between them *in play (bi'l-ʿabath)*; We created them *not* save in truth"): creation has *reality and purpose* and is *not in vain* (the source's gloss, grounded at 3:191's *mā khalaqnā hādhā bāṭilan* and 38:27). And what al-Aḥqāf's identical clause adds is the key this sūrah has been cutting for eighty verses: "illā bi'l-ḥaqqi *wa-ajalin musamman*" — *and a numbered term* (46:3) — which is, of course, precisely v. 4's *ajalin maʿlūm*, the *known term* of every destroyed town of vv. 51-84: the sūrah's whole narrative arc — the towns whose mornings were *written* — was the *proof by history* of v. 85's *proof by cosmology*: the heavens are not idle, and the towns are not accidents; the *bi'l-ḥaqq* of creation and the *ajalin* of decree are one clause in al-Aḥqāf and *two* in this sūrah, the first its creed, the second its eighty-four stories; even the *āla* of v. 31's prostration and the stones of v. 74's morning are *bi'l-ḥaqq*: nothing *between the heavens and the earth* — *wa-mā baynahumā*, the camel of Thamūd included, the she-camel whose *day of water* was *numbered* (v. 81's note, 54:28) — is outside the *ḥaqq*.

**"Surely the Hour Is Coming": the Sworn Imminence, and the Two Fingers**

*wa-inna's-sāʿata la-ātiyatun* — the *lām* of the oath on the sūrah's last town's last morning converts into certainty: not "the Hour may come" — it *comes*, and the *tafsīr* records how the Prophet ﷺ lived the nearness: "I and the Hour were sent thus — and he joined his index and his middle finger" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Sahl b. Saʿd), and the source's second note: he spoke *often* of its imminence, "indicating symbolically that the distance between him and it was as the space between *two fingers*"; and yet the *how-soon* stayed heaven's own: the source's Jibril-ḥadīth — the archangel's man-shaped question "When is the Hour?" and the answer that *is* the doctrine: "the one questioned knows no more than the questioner" (Muslim, from the report of ʿUmar), with the verse of the umma's tongue-script: "*say: knowledge thereof is only with my Lord*" (33:63) — for the Hour's *certainty* is *revealed* (v. 85 swears it) and its *clock* is *withheld* (v. 85 does not number it): which is the exact balance the sūrah struck at vv. 36-38 — Iblīs *asked for the respite to the Day* and was given "a day of *known time*" (v. 38) *whose date he was not told*: the nearness-belief and the date-ignorance are the two rails of the *dhikr*'s *yaqīn*.

**"So Forbear with a Beautiful Forbearance": the *Ṣafḥa* — the Sūrah's Final Turn of Its Own Key**

*fa-ṣfaḥi'ṣ-ṣafḥata'l-jamīlā* — and the command that crowns the cosmology is not the sword but the *shoulder turned gently*: *ṣafḥ*: to turn one's *face* (*ṣafḥat al-wajh*) *away* — and the handbooks' gloss on *jamīlā* (the source's third note, citing the encouragements at 5:13 and 43:89 and 70:5): a *turning without resentment, with a sweet word and an open face* (Ibn ʿAbbās is reported in this sense), *beautiful* because it expects *no collection*: the *ṣāfaḥ* is beautiful exactly when the *intiqām* is left where v. 79 left it — with God; Yaʿqūb's coinage stands behind the adjective — "*a patience, beautiful* (12:18; 12:83: *fa-ṣabrun jamīl*)" — and the *mufassirūn* note that this is the Meccan *ṣafḥ*: the turn-away *before* the *sword-ayah*, and the *sūrah itself* seals the point: at v. 94 it will repeat the gesture (*wa-aʿriḍ ʿani'l-mushrikīn*: "and *turn* from the polytheists"), so that the *sūrah's* whole *daʿwa*-law is enclosed between this *fa-ṣfaḥ* and that *fa-aʿriḍ*: the warner is sent to *warn* (v. 89: *innī anā'n-nadhīru'l-mubīn*) and to *forbear*, and to forbear under the name that owns the turning (v. 86’s *al-Khallāqu’l-ʿAlīm*), and *not* to *avail* (v. 84: the *aghna* belongs to no hand but His): which is the sūrah's answer, at its last hinge, to v. 2's *rubbamā*: *thy* part is the *warning*; *their* part, the *regret*; *the Hour's* part, the *coming*; and *thy* part, meanwhile: the *beautiful* turn.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:86

> **Truly thy Lord is the knowing Creator.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He *It Is*": the Restrictive Pronoun, the Sūrah's Seal on Its Own School**

*inna rabbaka huwa'l-khallāqu'l-ʿalīm* — "verily thy Lord — *He*, *He it is* — the knowing Creator." The double naming with the isolated *huwa* is the restrictive construction the Arabic grammarians call *qaṣr* — "the *khallāq* is *He and no other*, and the *ʿalīm* is *He and no other*" — and the sūrah ends its long argument about *knowledge* exactly where v. 85 left it: the Hour's *clock* (v. 85: withheld) and creation's *measure* (v. 85: *bi'l-ḥaqq*) are both, v. 86 swears, *within one pair of names*: nothing is *made* by another, nothing is *known* to another, and therefore *no making escapes His knowing and no knowing precedes His making*: the *mashāyiikh* of the sūrah read v. 86 as the seal-stamp on its whole school-room: the *ʿAlīm* who *taught* Ādam the names (2:31) is the *ʿAlīm* of this verse, and the sūrah that opened the human story with *names* closes its Meccan towns with the *Named*: the *khalq* that formed the clay the pride of fire refused (vv. 26-29: *ṣalṣāl min ḥamaʾ masnūn* — “sounding clay of moulded black mud”: the passive *masnūn*, “given form,” whose maker stands named at last, in this verse’s intensive pattern: *al-Khallāq* — *ever-forming*).

**The Two Names, and the Two Anxieties They Retire**

And the pairing — *khallāq* before *ʿalīm* — is treated by the handbooks as the sūrah's *counsel in two clauses*, addressed, first, to the man commanded to *forbear* (v. 85's *ṣafḥ*) while his town counted its gains (v. 84's *kasb*): *thy Lord is* — the *rabbaka*, *thy* Lord, the sūrah's tenderest construction — the same *rabbaka* she will swear by ("by thy Lord, We shall question them all," v. 92) and command with ("and worship thy Lord," v. 99) — *the knowing Creator*: if He is the *Khallāq*, the *enjoyments* Quraysh count (v. 88's *azwāj*, the source's note: the *goods of this world given to the Makkan rejecters*) are *made-over* things, and making is *His* ledger, not their boast; and if He is the *ʿAlīm*, the *mockery* threaded through the guilty hearts (v. 12: *naslukuhu fī qulūbi'l-mujrimīn* — He *knows*, and the source's note at v. 92 will show He *asks*) is *known* — and what is *known by the Khallāq* does not need the *warner's* worry: the two names retire, in one verse, the *greed* that measures by *things* and the *grief* that measures by *people* — which is exactly why the sūrah places v. 86 *between* the command to *forbear* (v. 85) and the announcement of the *gift* that out-measures the town (v. 87: *la-qad ātaynāka...*).

**"The Knowing Creator" and the End of the Argument with Fire**

The tradition hears, in *al-Khallāqu'l-ʿalīm*, the sūrah's *final answer to Iblīs*: the claim that damned him was *materialist* — "I would not prostrate to a human You fashioned from sounding clay of moulded black mud" (v. 34), in Sād: "You fashioned me of fire and fashioned *him* of clay" (38:76): *rank in the raw stuff*; and the sūrah's *last word about that claim*, after all the towns it has destroyed, is not a rebuttal but a *name*: *al-Khallāq*: *the making itself is the rank* — the clay is honored not as clay but as *made*, and the making is *of Him*, and therefore *known* — *al-ʿAlīm*: worth is His *art*, not the *matter*: which is the sūrah's whole prostration-scene read backward: the angels bowed *to the made* (v. 30: *fa-kharrū... sājidīn*) because the *making* and the *knowing* were *in it*: "and I *breathed into him* of My spirit" (v. 29: *wa-nafakhtu fīhi min rūḥī*) — and fire, refusing the *name* of the Maker's art, is left to his *aba* (v. 31: *abā an yakūna maʾaʾ as-sājidīn* — *he refused*) as its own eternal verdict. Which is why the sūrah, after v. 86, has *nothing more to say about Iblīs*: the argument ended *with the argument's premise dissolved*; what remains — vv. 87-99 — is the *gift* (v. 87), the *mission* (vv. 88-89), the *questioning* (vv. 90-93), and the *patience* (vv. 94-99): the *knowing Creator* is the sūrah's *khatm* of the *old war*, and its *ibtidāʾ* of the new.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:87

> **And We have indeed given thee the seven oft-repeated, and the Mighty Quran.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We *Gave* Thee": the Consolation-Built on the Accusation's Verdict**

*wa-laqad ātaynāka's-sabʿa mina'l-mathānī wa'l-Qur'āna'l-ʿaẓīm* — "and *We have indeed given* you the seven of the oft-repeated, and the mighty Qur'an." The verb is the sūrah's own witness: *ātā* — *given*, the verb of v. 81: "and *We gave* them (*wa-ātaynāhum*) Our signs, but they kept turning away" — and the juxtaposition of v. 81 and v. 87 is the verse's structure in one glance: *giving* and *giving*, and the difference is everything: Thamūd *turned from* what was given them; what is given *thee*, the sūrah swears, is given *for keeps* — the *laqad*-oath at v. 87's head answers the *junūn* accusation at its v. 6: "O you to whom the Reminder has been sent — you are possessed!" — and the sūrah's last section therefore *begins as an answer*: the man they called mad has been given *the seven and the mighty*: which is why the *mufassirūn* call this verse, at the hinge of the sūrah's movement from *towns destroyed* to *Prophet sustained*, the verse of *taʿjīb* (astonishment at the gift) and *taslī* (consolation): the first is *the Fātiḥa*, the second *the Qur'an*: the *ʿaẓīm* that makes every town's stones, in the scale of this gift, *small* (the source's note at v. 88 transcribes Abū Bakr's sentence at this verse: "whoever is given the Qur'an and thinks *anything of this world* better than what he has been given, *has deemed a great thing small and a small thing great*" — at the level of report it stands beside the Prophet's ﷺ sworn line the source records (R): "God revealed *nothing like it* in the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, or the Qur'an — it is the *Mother of the Book* (43:4) *and* the seven oft-repeated" — Muslim, from Ubayy b. Kaʿb).

**"Seven of the *Mathānī*": the Number That Is This Sūrah's Architecture**

*al-sabʿa mina'l-mathānī* — and the name is the sūrah's: *mathnā* is the *doubling* — "repeated twos," and the source gathers the *tafsīr*'s two readings of the repetition: *recited in every unit of every prayer* (the Fātiḥa is *the* oft-repeated sūrah, Aj, R), and — the grammarians' second sense, in the *sunnah*'s own wording — *the dialogue*: for the *mathnā* is doubled *because God answers in it*: "when the servant says *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*, God Most High says: *my servant has praised Me*… until '…make us not one of those who earn anger nor of those astray' — He says: *this is between Me and My servant, and for My servant is what he asks*" (Muslim, from ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb, of the sūrah's division into halves — the *ḥadīth qudsī* the source cites at v. 87's end: "it is divided between God and His servant"). And the *seven* belongs to this sūrah's cosmology by a name it already knows: "*We built above you* **seven** *firmly-built*" (v. 16) — the sky's seven are the *lamps of guard* (v. 16-18, spent against the eavesdroppers of vv. 17's *rujūm*); the Prophet's seven are the *lamps of converse*, and the sūrah's final gift-verse sets the two sevens *one above the other*: the seven *stars* from which *every stolen word is repelled* — the seven *verses* in which *every asked word is answered*: the heavens of vv. 16-18 and the prayer of v. 87 are, in the sūrah's own imagery, the *two sealed registers*, one refusing the soothsayer, one rewarding the servant, and the *count of seven* (with the basmala, as the source notes the counting goes: Aj, R) is the seal that they match.

**"And the Mighty Qur'an": the Whole in the Part, and the Sūrah's Bracket**

*wa'l-Qur'āna'l-ʿaẓīm* — and the commentators of the source's pages (Ṭ, Z against the rest; and Qm and ʿAj) weigh whether *the seven* are a *part* of the second gift or its *first fruits* — the grammar of *ʿaṭf* that turns on "is the Fātiḥa *within* the Qur'an or *with* it" — and the sūrah's own usage *settles the direction of the verse's intent*, not the jurists' dispute: this sūrah has been, from v. 1, a *Book about being given a Book*: "*āyāt* of the *kitāb* and of a *Qur'an made clear*" — and its *titular* claim (v. 9: "*We* sent down the *dhikr* and *We* are its guardian") is the promise that makes v. 87 possible: *if the Qur'an is not the seven's larger name, it is their guaranteed one*: the *ʿaẓīm* sūrah that *answers* one sūrah's seven verses — that is the bracket the sūrah has built for itself between v. 1's *kitāb* and v. 87's *Qur'ān*: *the Book* is named at the door and *the mighty Qur'an* on the threshold of the Prophet's own last section, and between them the whole parade of towns has *passed* — Lot's morning, Ayka's cloud, Thamūd's daybreak — while the *given* seven remained the sūrah's standing counter to every *earned* vault: "that which they used to earn *availed them not*" (v. 84) — "and *We have indeed given thee*" (v. 87): the end-piece begins by *weighing the two verbs*, and the *mathnā* — the doubled — is the doubling the sūrah has been teaching all along: *every avenged town a mirror, every gifted verse a mercy*, read twice, the second time for *the asking*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:88

> **Strain not thine eyes toward the enjoyments We have bestowed upon certain classes of them, and grieve not for them, and lower thy wing unto the believers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Eye, the Heart, the Wing: One Verse, Three Directions**

*lā tamuddanna baṣaraka ilā mā mattaʿnā bihi azwājin min aṣnāfihim wa-lā taḥzan ʿalayhim wa-ukhfiḍ janāḥaka li'l-muʾminīn* — and the verse is a *physiology of the prophet's temptations* in three clauses: *do not stretch* (the eye, outward, toward the enjoyments of *certain classes — azwājin min aṣnāfihim*, the plural of pluralities: kinds *nested within* kinds, the sūrah's most taxonomic image of luxury); *do not grieve* (the heart, inward, over *them*, the rejecters whose unbelief the warner *feels*); *lower your wing* (the body, downward, *to the believers*): the eye strained after what heaven has *temporarily bestowed* is answered by v. 84 — "and *what they used to earn availed them not*" — and the sūrah's own cousin-telling completes the answer in the words its hearers knew: "strain not thine eyes toward what We have bestowed on *classes (azwāj)* of them, the *splendor of the life of this world*, that We might *try* them therein — *and the provision of thy Lord is better and longer-lasting*" (20:131: *khayrun wa-abqā*): the *tamāʿ*-clause of v. 88 is, in the two sūrahs' shared idiom, *a trial with a timer*: the enjoyments are *mattaʿnā* — *We* bestowed them — *azwāj* — *pairs*, *classes*, *short lives in multiples* — and the *abqā* of the gift of vv. 87 (the seven, the Qur'an) is the verse's unstated second half: the eye that has *received* the oft-repeated has no *mattaʿ* left worth straining for, which is why the source's note at v. 88 says it plainly: the Prophet is admonished *because what he has been given is greater than anything they have* (Aj), and Abū Bakr's sentence (cited there) is the umma's copy of the verse.

**"Grieve Not for Them": the Sūrah's Tenth Mercy-Counsel**

The *nahy* about grief belongs to a whole literature of *tafhīm*, and the source runs the chain to v. 88's head: "the injunction not to grieve on behalf of the Makkan idolaters refers to *the grief he experienced when they would not believe*" (Bḍ), with its cousins: "perhaps you would *undo* yourself, grieving for their trace, if they believe not in this account" (18:6: *tubāqiya nafsaka*); "perhaps you will *undo yourself* (*bākhīʿu nafsika*) because they do not believe" (26:3); "let not their words *grieve* you" (36:76); "and *be not* in *straitness* for what they scheme" (16:127; the source's list: 3:176; 5:41,68; 6:33; 10:65; 35:8; 27:70) — and the *tafsīr* marks here the theological weight of the negation: the grief in the Prophet ﷺ was, in itself, mercy — and God *forbids* it not as a sin but as a *wrong accounting*: the souls are not *his*, the mornings are not *his*, and he has been told (v. 92) that the questioning is *God’s* office — so the verse heals the heart, in its third clause, by *re-direction*, not by callousness: the *believers* are *there*, the *few* who followed: the warner who counts the refusing many is commanded to *turn his whole chest to the believing few* — the sūrah that has spent forty verses on saved *āla* and destroyed towns makes v. 88 its *pastoral*: Lot *wept* for his people and was *sent the morning* — the Prophet ﷺ is told *the morning is the address*, and the *wing* is for *those who are already coming*: grief is for *them*; *amn* was for *them*; vv. 59-60's *āla*-law is now the *muʾminūn*-law: the saved *arrive through a mercy, not through your sorrow*, and *lowering the wing to them is the ʿibāda* left when *nadhīr*-duty is done (v. 89).

**"Lower Thy Wing": the Bird of Ibrāhīm, and the Wing That Was Given to Carry Stones**

*wa-ukhfiḍ janāḥaka li'l-muʾminīn* — *ukhfiḍ*: fold down the *wing*: and the verb is, in the *tafsīr*'s two glosses (the source: Aj's "be humble and gentle toward them"; and the *ʿārifūn*'s *raʾūf raḥīm*-grammar), the *tawāḍuʿ* of the bird *shortening the reach of its flight to its young* — and the sūrah's hearers held, in the same verb's company, two verses they could not miss: the child's prayer — "and *lower for them both* the wing of humility out of mercy" (17:24, cited at the source's note: 17:24c) — and *Ibrāhīm's own command*, which our verse repeats verbatim: "*and lower your wing to the believers who follow you*" (26:215): which is the sūrah's last *twinning*: this sūrah whose *house* (vv. 51-60) was *his* tent, whose *guest-grammar* is the source of the verse above, ends its Prophet's law with *Ibrāhīm's* law — the *wing* Muḥammad ﷺ is bidden to lower is the *wing* Ibrāhīm lowered on the road to the very city whose morning vv. 61-77 told: the *silsila* of the *janāḥ* — Ibrāhīm, Muḥammad, the *muʾminūn* — is the *sunna* of the tent carried into the *ṣalāh*: and the handbooks add, at this verse's foot, the sūrah's *one winged* verse (105:3, cited at v. 74's stones): the birds of *ṭayr abābīl* flew *with stones for the proud*, and the Prophet's ﷺ wing is *lowered with nothing but humility for the lowly*: two wings, one sky, and the *dhikr* chooses the *lowered* one — which is why the verse places *ukhfiḍ* *after* the two *nahys*: the *straining* eye is *stilled*, the *grieving* heart is *emptied*, and *then* the wing *comes down*: a man cannot lower his wing to the *muʾminūn* while his eye is *up* in Quraysh's gardens.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:89

> **and say, "Truly I am the clear warner."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Say": the Office in Three Words — the Formula Every Prophet Shares**

*wa-qul innī anā'n-nadhīru'l-mubīn* — "and *say*: I am *the* plain warner." The command is to *say*, not to *feel*, not to *compel*: the sūrah, which has just retired the straining eye and the grieving heart (v. 88), now *defines* what is left: the *qawl* of warning, plain and public — and the source's note gathers the formula's kin ("for the Prophet as a clear warner, see 22:49; 29:50; 38:70; 46:9; 51:50; 67:26"): *innī lakum nādhirun mubīn* is the messengers' *shared self-naming* — Nūḥ's, Hūd's, Ṣāliḥ's, Lūṭ's, Shuʿayb's (26:104-180's litany: *fa-attakhu'llāha... fa-kayfa kāna nahīr — "how [proved] My warning"*) — and v. 89 therefore *re-titles* the sūrah's *own* six-town lecture as *one* sermon: the warner of al-Ḥijr speaks, in this verse, with *all* the warners' mouth: the *mubīn* — plain, and making plain — binds him to the *kitāb* at v. 1's head (*qur'ānin mubīn*): the man is *one piece* with the recitation; his clarity and the Book's clarity are the same root, and the sūrah, whose angels came with *bi'l-ḥaqq* and whose towns were destroyed *bi'ṭṭīḥānā*, now gives its Prophet, as his *whole* task, the *plainness* of the warning: not the *results*, not the *hearts*, not the *morning* — the *mubīn*.

**The "I" That Echoes the Divine "I": V. 89 Beside VV. 49-50**

And the verse's *innī anā* — *I, I* — is the *twin construction* of the sūrah's center, which the reader has met twice before in identical words: at v. 49 heaven dictated his *qul* in the first person of the *Lord*: "inform *My* servants that *I, I am* — *al-ghafūru'r-raḥīm*"; and v. 50 sealed the same pronoun-shape with the *other* half: "*and* that *My* punishment — *it* is *the* abiding pain"; and now, v. 89: "say: *I, I am* — *the plain warner*": the warner's *anā* is *made of* the two announcements it carries — the *ghafūr* and the *ʿadhāb*, the *bishāra*-voice of the angels at Abraham's tent (vv. 51-56: *mubashshirūka*) and the *akhādha*-voice of the morning (v. 73) — and the sūrah's christening of the Prophet's office at this verse is therefore *not* the warner's *name* but the *name of what he bears*: he is *mubīn* because the *two* announcements are *in* his *anā*: which is why the full title of the Prophet in the Qur’an stands in full ("a warner and a bearer of glad news," 35:24; 41:4 — and here the *warner*-half is named *alone*, because the *glad-news*-half has just been *given* to him — v. 87: *the seven and the mighty Qur'an*): the sūrah's logic at vv. 85-89: *the gift is his bashāra; the task is his nidhār; and the two together are his "say".*

**"Unto the Believers" — and "to Them": the Direction the Verse Keeps**

The verse *does not say*: "say to them"; the *qul* is *bare*: *wa-qul*: say it *out loud, wherever*, and the commentators mark that the *object* of the warner's speech, in this sūrah, has *two directions* set by the two consecutive verses: *v. 88*: the *wing* — *li'l-muʾminīn*, *for* the believers, lowered *to* them; *v. 89*: the *warning* — *their* portion the plain *statement* with *no* second party attached: which is, the handbooks say, the *fiqh of the dāʿī's two audiences*: to the *inside*, *janāḥ*: wing, gentleness, nearness; to the *outside*, *nidhār*: plainness, distance, *no* coercion of the heart — and the *sunnah* of the two verses is preserved in the Prophet's ﷺ own etiquette at the *ḥijr*-gates of the dead (v. 77's weeping at al-Ḥijr) and at the *ṣafā* of the living: "*I am not a guardian over you*" (11:86, Shuʿayb's — the sūrah's own Ayka-prophet's — sentence that *fits* v. 89's office like a seal), and the twin-verse of the *whole* end-piece: "*reminder; you are not over them a ruler*" (88:21-22: *fa-dhakkir, innama anta mudhakkir, lasta ʿalayhim bi-muṣayṭir*): which is how the sūrah reads, at its last hinge, its first Meccan quarrel: they called him *majnūn* (v. 6); God makes him *nadhīr mubīn* and gives him, for his *sanad*, a sūrah whose every town *confirms* the warning with its morning: *the questioning is Ours* (v. 92, next: the sūrah's own last oath will say it) — *the plainness is thine*: this is, in the scholars' summary of vv. 88-89, the *mīzān* of the prophetic office itself, and of every office after it.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:90

> **Such as We have sent down for those who make division,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Like What We Sent Down": the Simile That Makes Quraysh the Next Town**

*kamā anzalnā ʿalā'l-muqtaṣimīn* — "like what We *sent down* upon the dividers." The *kāf* of similitude is the sūrah's *hinge*: the punishment of vv. 90-93's Meccan *muqtaṣimūn* is sworn *like* the punishments just narrated — Lot's rain, Thamūd's Cry — which completes the verse-arc the sūrah has been building since v. 4 ("We destroy no town but it has a known decree"): the *mursalūn* denied in al-Ḥijr (v. 80) and the *Qur'ān* fragmented in Mecca (v. 91) are *one* crime, and the *inzāl* of punishment is *one* weather: as in the source's first note on the trio, v. 90 may hang on v. 89: "I am a clear warner *with a punishment like the punishment that visited the dividers*" (Bg, R) — the *nadhīr*'s v. 89 declaration and the sūrah's v. 90 simile are one sentence, warner to warned. And the verb of *sending down* — *anzalnā* — is the sūrah's most *double-faced* word: the same verb with which "We sent down *the Reminder*" (v. 9) and "We *send down* the angels with the truth" (v. 8) and "We sent down *water* and gave you drink" (v. 22) — *descent* is the sūrah's grammar for whatever comes from heaven, *mercy or stones*, and the dividers will learn, in the direction of their simile, that *what they did to the sent-down Book, the sent-down punishment will do to them*: they *broke the descent* — and the descent *breaks them*.

**"The Dividers": the Watchtowers of al-Walīd, and What "iqtisām" Does to News**

*al-muqtaṣimūn* — and the source's second note (R) records the historical identification: "a band of Meccans whom *al-Walīd b. al-Mughīra* posted along the roads into Mecca, to tell incoming pilgrims: *there is a man in Mecca claiming prophethood — in truth a sorcerer, a soothsayer, a poet*" (the source's cross-reference: 26:224-226c, the *shayāṭīn*-poetry verses) — the *iqtisām*, in this reading, is *the organization of reception*: they *cut up* the pilgrim-season, road by road, mouth by mouth, so that the * Qur'ān* would reach no ear *un-prejudiced*: the *sūrah* has, all along, been describing *this* crime under its own terms — "We thread it into the hearts of the guilty" (v. 12's *naslukuhu*: the *waswasa* too is *posted at the roads of the heart*, and the *muqtaṣimūn* are *men doing Iblīs's logistics*); and *iqtisām* — share-cutting — is, in the *tafsīr*'s deeper sense, the *partition* of a *whole* thing into *portions for us*: the *Qur'ān* they called *siḥr* was, to them, a *claim to be divided up*: one part accepted (the *sanad* of the fathers, the poetry, the *ḥaram* and its trade), one part refused (the *tawḥīd*, the *ākhira*, the *mīzān*) — and v. 91 (its next word: "who *made the Qur'ān into portions*") will name the *portioning* itself, the *bidʿa*-verse, so that vv. 90-91 are the *crime in two registers*: the *street* (the watchtowers) and the *soul* (the ledger that divides revelation by appetite).

**Application: The Road-Block of the Ear**

The *ʿulamāʾ* draw the practical at this verse into the *adab al-talāqqī*: the *muqtaṣimūn* were not, in the first instance, men who *read* the Qur'an and refused it — they were men *posted to see that no one came to it clean* — and the sūrah, which opened by calling the Qur'an "āyāt of a *clear* Book" (v. 1) against "the hearts' *ṭaym*" (v. 12), makes *v. 90* the eternal warning against the *pre-reading frame*: the *watchtower* set over a listener before the first verse reaches him (the *prejudice industry*: "soothsayer, poet, possessed" — v. 6's *majnūn*, the *sūrah*'s own first accusation, turns out, at v. 90, to have had *staff*): and the tradition's companion-rule reads the *reverse* of v. 90's *iqtisām* in the Prophet's ﷺ saying on the Qur'an's *whole-claim*: "God *raises* by this Book a people and *lowers* another" (Muslim, from ʿUmar) — *raises and lowers*, undivided: the Book's *descent* (v. 90: *anzalnā*) does what the posted men could not: *it arrives anyway*, in every age, un-prejudiced or not, and the *dhikr* that v. 9 promised to guard is guarded *past every watchtower*: which is the verse's consolation inside its warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:91

> **who made the Quran into fragments.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Made the Qur'ān *Biḍʿa*": the Portions, and the Philology of Chop**

*alladhīna jaʿalū'l-Qur'āna biḍʿa* — "who *made* the Qur'ān *biḍʿa*." The *jaʿala* is the verb of *making*, and the sentence's scandal is the subject it takes: not *they rejected* the Qur'ān, not *they denied* it — *they made it into pieces*: the sūrah's charge is that the *idolaters acted upon the Book as makers*, apportioning it, and the word *biḍʿa* is the noun of *portions* — *buḍʿa*: bits, chunks of a broken whole — and the source records the lexicographers' alternative root (Q, R): read from *ʿ-ḍ-w* — "*biḍʿa* as *lies* (*aḍāwīʿ*)" — the Qur'an's own label for such books — "fables of the ancients, which he had written down, dictated to him morning and evening" (25:4-5: *asāṭīru'l-awwalīn*), and both readings converge on the verse's verdict: *whether they chopped the true into portions or called the whole a lie, they refused it its unity* — and *unity* is the very claim on which this sūrah has been built since its third verse of the *one-Book* argument (v. 90's *anzalnā*, the *sent-down*: revelation is *one descent*, one hand, one *ḥikma*), so that v. 91's sin is *the sūrah's own subject*: they did to the Qur'ān what the mockers of vv. 95-96 do to its *warner*: they *parted* what God *joined*, and the sūrah's *khatm*-argument (v. 92: "We shall question them *all*, *concerning what they used to do*") arrives *because* the *bidʿa* is the *sin of the ledger*: the *portion-taking* that keeps the comfortable and cuts out the threatening.

**"Fables of the Ancients": the Label and Its Kin, and the *tafsīif*-Verse**

And the source's first note at the pair records the *Meccan label*: "they rejected the Qur'ān *by calling it* sorcery, *fables of the ancients* (e.g. 83:13), or *soothsaying*" — the three-fold accusation-list of vv. 6 and 15 (*siḥr*, *majnūn*, *shāʿir* — 26:224-226, cited at v. 90) — and *biḍʿa*, "portions," means, in the *tafsīr*'s plainest gloss (Q, per the source): *"not a whole, but cobbled from these false sources"* (R's wording) — a quilt of borrowings, and the sūrah lets the *metaphor indict itself*: a *quilted* book has *no author* and therefore *no account to render* — and *that* is precisely why the *bidʿa* is answered, in the next verse, with the *account*: "so, *by thy Lord*, We shall question them *all*" (v. 92): *the Book you stitched from scraps has stitched you into a ledger with a Reader*: which is the sūrah's *law of the cut* — what you *cut* about the revelation, the revelation *keeps* about you: even the verse of *idjāz*, "and We have *indeed* created the heavens and earth *in truth*" (v. 85), has been holding, since four lines back, that *the real is the whole*: creation "*illā bi'l-ḥaqq*" and the *bidʿa* are the sūrah's two *opposite grammars*: God makes *whole*; they make *pieces*; and the *making* in v. 91 — *jaʿalū* — is the *imitatio* that damns them: *they played at making, and the Maker's Book remembers it*.

**"Some Believe Some and Disbelieve Some": the Second Muqtaṣimūn, and the Sūrah's Mirror**

The source's note keeps, beside the Meccan identification, the *other* reading (Q: "*those who believe in the parts of the Qur'ān they like and reject the rest*," cf. 2:85 — *a-mā tu'minū... wa-takfurūna*: "do you believe *part* of the Book and disbelieve *part*?" the *Banū Isrāʾīl* verse whose shame is *exactly* this *bidʿa*): and the *mufassirūn* let both senses stand *on purpose* (the source's *thus* is the point: the *muqtaṣimūn* are *whomever* who *portion* the *dhikr* by appetite), which makes v. 91 the *sūrah's mirror* across the centuries: the *mujmal/muṭayyif* splitter, the *āyāt* that keep *comfort* and cut *cost*, the *sūrah* of mercy kept and the *sūrah* of *mīzān* shelved — and the *tafsīr* tradition reads the bare verb of *making* — *jaʿalū* — as their *usurpation*, not their error: they *made* what God *sent*; v. 85 has just sworn that *making* belongs to the *ḥaqq* alone, and the *āya*-verse of the *believers* (v. 77) stands across from it: *the believers* take the *one āya* from the *one Book*; the *dividers* take *many books* from *one*: and the *sūrah's* arithmetic, since vv. 75-77, has been about *how many signs one makes of a sign*: the *mutawassim* — many; the *muʾmin* — one; the *muqtaṣim* — *portions*: which is the definition, in the *sūrah's own idiom*, of *kufr's* mathematics.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:92

> **So by thy Lord, We shall question them all**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Second Oath Touching the Prophet: From *ʿUmr* to *Rabb***

*fa-rabbika la-nasʾalannahum ajmaʿīn* — "then — *by thy Lord* — We shall question them, *all of them*." The reader who has kept the sūrah's ledger of divine asseverations sees the frame close: the sūrah swore once before, in the middle of the Lot-cycle, by *the Prophet's own life* ("*by thy life*, they were dazedly wandering," v. 72), and now, at the edge of its last quarter, it swears *by his Lord*: *la-ʿumruka* and *fa-rabbika* — the two oaths that touch the Prophet ﷺ in this sūrah, one at the scene of the mob's *suʾkr*, one at the scene of *the questioning*: as if the sūrah said — *his life* was the proof against the *drunk*, and *his Lord* is the proof against the *dividers*; and the *oath-forms of the Book* (sky, night and morning, the *kitāb* and its *dhikr*: vv. 1-9) all lead, at the sūrah's last turn, to an oath whose *mention* is "thy Lord": the God he has served the towns for is *himself* the *maqsam*: the plainest honor in the sūrah's last section, placed *exactly where the threat is sworn*.

**"We Shall Question Them": the Question of Exhibition, and What "the Deed" Means**

*lanasʾalannahum* — and the *tafsīr* tradition asks, here as always, *what* a knowing One questions for: not *information* but *exhibition*: the question of the Day is the *second* clarity — the *mubīn* of the warning (v. 89) and the *mubīn* of the record, which is why the *sūrah's* whole law of *iʿdhār* (8:42, at vv. 67-69) *ends* in this verb: every excuse finished on earth is a question opened in the *ākhira*: the questioning of v. 92 is *the completion of the warning of v. 89*: the *nadhīr* spoke plainly, and the *khabīr* questions *justly*. And *ʿammā kānū yaʿmalūn* (v. 93) fixes, in the source's note, the object's reach by *two* readings of the *ʿamal* questioned: "God's questioning the Makkan idolaters *concerning that which they used to do* refers to *how they responded to the statement 'lā ilāha illa Llāh'* — whether they believed it and acted in accordance with it (Q)"; and Ibn Rajab, at this verse in his *tafsīr*, transcribes the Salaf's split: the *word of tawḥīd* is meant, as transmitted from *Ibn ʿUmar and Mujāhid*, while *Ibn Baṭṭa* and "ṭawāʾif min al-ʿulamāʾ" *refused* that limitation and held the *ʿamal* here to the *deeds of the limbs* — and the two readings *agree on the point the sūrah has been driving at since v. 91*: the *bidʿa*-dividers are questioned about the *whole*: about the *word* they portioned *and* the *limbs* they guided by the portioning: "the answer to *lā ilāha illa Llāh*", in the reading of this verse’s report, is a *whole* deed: a creed whose parts — its affirmations of God, of His Messenger, of Paradise, Fire, Reckoning and the Last Day — cannot be *portioned* the way the *bidʿa* portions a book — and v. 92 swears that *the whole* is what they will be asked.

**"*Ajmaʿīn*": the All-Inclusive Word, Given Twice in Opposite Directions**

And the *ajmaʿīn* is the sūrah's own old coin, spent — at its other great totalizing moment: "We *shall deliver* him and his family *all together* (*ajmaʿīn*)" — Lot's *āla* (v. 59) — and here, *ajmaʿīn* again, now on the *questioned* side of the same verse-pair: the sūrah's *comprehensiveness* runs in *two* directions with *one* word: *all* of the saved brought in, *all* of the guilty stopped — and no soul in either *ajmaʿ* is *missing from the ledger*: the grammar of *salvation's totality* (v. 59) and the grammar of *questioning's totality* (v. 92) are, in this sūrah, *rhymed*: which is its final word about *those two* — the *family* and the *dividers*: the sūrah will *not* leave, in either town, *a remainder*: v. 23's inheritance-law stands behind the rhyme: "It is We who give life and cause death, and *We are the Inheritors*": *return* is *v. 92's* verb in *noun-form*: the *questioning* is the *return's* *first* act: and the handbooks read this verse as the *sūrah's* standing *ḥujja* against the *bidʿa*-divider: whoever *cuts* the Book has, in this oath, cut *his own* *answer*: for *the Questioner is the One whose Book it is*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:93

> **concerning that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They *Used to* Do": the Sūrah's Tense, Final Rehearsal**

*ʿammā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "concerning *that which they used to do*." The clause is, in the sūrah's grammar, the *last* of a long series of *kāna* + imperfects, the sūrah's *habituals*, and the audit of them is the verse's whole meaning: *kānū yamtarūn* — "they *used to* dabble in doubt" (v. 63); *kānū yuʿriḍūn* — Thamūd "*used to* turn away" from the signs (v. 81); *kānū yanaḥṭūna... āminīn* — they "*used to* hew houses, secure" (v. 82); *kānū yaksibūn* — their "*used-to-earn*" (v. 84); and now, over all of them, the Day's clause: *kānū yaʿmalūn* — the questioning is *not* about a crime, a day, a decision, but about *the tense itself*: about what a people *were doing so regularly it had become their name*: which is the sūrah's last lesson on *habit* — what you *use to*, you *will be asked about*, and the *used-to* is the *record*: habits, in this sūrah's theology, are the *only* things that survive death intact (v. 84 proved the rest *does not avail*): the *kānū* that v. 84 *emptied of shelter* v. 93 *fills with questions*: the *used-to* could not *guard*, and it will not be *lost*: the two verses are a single verdict in halves.

**"The Doing" and the Book: the Sūrah's Bracket Closed**

And the sūrah began by naming what it is — "*āyāt* of the *kitāb* and a Qur'ān *made clear*" (v. 1) — and it ends (in this verse, before the two mercy-verses that carry it out, vv. 98-99) by naming what men make of such books: *their* deeds, *asked*: the bracket the sūrah has built — *given* Book (v. 1, v. 87), *refused* Book (v. 6, vv. 90-91: *siḥr*, *bidʿa*), *Question* about the *doing* (v. 93) — is, in the handbooks' reading, the sūrah's definition of *the whole religious situation*: between a *sent* clarity and a *sworn* question there stands exactly one thing, the *kānū yaʿmalūn*: and the *lām al-jawāb* of the oath (v. 92: *la-nasʾalannahum*) resting, in v. 93, on a *common* noun (*mā*, "that which"), makes the question as *plain* as the warning it completes: *no mystery of accounting*: you will be asked about *the things you kept doing* — the *mubīn*-sūrah, whose word *mubīn* has marked its Book (v. 1), its warner (v. 89) and its road (v. 79's *imām mubīn*), ends with a Day whose *ledger* is no fourth thing but the *same* clarity: *the record is the Book, read back*.

**Application: What the Sworn Question Is For**

The *salaf*'s practical on vv. 92-93, transcribed in the handbooks, turns the oath into the preacher's *warrant* and the listener's *medicine* at once: *for the warner* — v. 92's "*by thy Lord*" is given *before* v. 94's "*so proclaim boldly what you are commanded*": the *sadaʿ* (v. 94) is *breathed into* by the *oath* (v. 92): no one *declares* the whole command *openly* who has not first believed that *the questioning is God's own office*, since the moment *he* accepts *that* as his own burden, he will *trim* the message to *lighten* the load: *the swearer will handle the swearer's verdicts* — this is the *ʿilm* of *daʿwa* in this sūrah's last four verses; and *for the audited* — the *bidʿa*-dividers are the sūrah's demonstration that the *portioning* of a Book is *not* a safer *middle* than *acceptance* or *rejection*: the *Question* is sworn over *the whole* of what men *used to do*, and the *heart's arithmetic* — which *āyāt* to keep, which *costs* to skip — is *itself* among the *ʿamal* asked about: the verse therefore ends the sūrah's courts with the law its *center* announced at v. 49-50 — *the Ghafūr and the ʿAdhāb are both true, and both are asked*: "*concerning that which they used to do*": and the *dhālika*, in the sūrah's own idiom, is *āya* — *for the believers* (v. 77).

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:94

> **So proclaim as thou hast been commanded, and turn away from the idolaters.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"*Fa-ṣdaʿ*": The Mountain-Verb, the Ascent of the Open**

*fa-ṣdaʿ bimā tuʾmaru wa-aʿriḍ ʿani'l-mushrikīn* — "so *climb out* with what you are commanded, and turn away from the polytheists." *Ṣadaʿ* is the noun of the *mountain-peak* (*ṣadiʿ*: the high breast of it; the *ṣadʿ* of a camel's neck is its upright), and the verb here — *fa-ṣdaʿ* — means, in the lexicons and the *tafsīr*'s glosses alike, *to raise the voice with the matter, to declare it high and open as a crest*: the command is *an ascent*, and the ascent is *public*: what v. 88 had forbidden — the *strain* of the eye *toward* their enjoyments — v. 94 now commands in the *opposite* vector: *lift the whole affair onto the peak where they can see it*. And the *bi-mā tuʾmar* is the verse's second, heavier word: the *passive*: *that which YOU ARE COMMANDED*: not *your* program, not the *portion* the *bidʿa* men keep (v. 91), not the *weight* the crowd can bear: *the passive of command* is the *ṣadaʿ*'s *license and its limit*: the *warner* climbs *only* the *ordered* mountain, and *all* of it — the Day of al-Ṣafā, which the two Ṣaḥīḥs record when 26:214 came down, shows the *word* *working* as the *history*: "when *And warn thy nearest kin* came down, God's Messenger ﷺ *came up to al-Ṣafā*, climbed it, and cried: *O Fatima bint al-Khaṭṭāb, O Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib — I cannot own you [before God]; ask me of my wealth whatever you will*" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim): *that* is *fa-ṣdaʿ*: the *peak*, the *kin*, the *nameless* on *no* list: the *ṣadaʿ* is *bi*-the-command *all the way up*.

**"And Turn Away from the Polytheists": the Sūrah's Second *Iʿrāḍ*, and the Law of the Whole Hand**

*wa-aʿriḍ ʿani'l-mushrikīn* — the identical clause is on the lips of another Meccan *kitāb*-verse (the source's note: "see 6:106: *Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord — there is no god but He — and turn away from the polytheists*"), and the *pairing* in both verses is the *sunnah*: *proclaim the whole, then let them*: the *iʿrāḍ* is *not* the message's *shortening* but the *result's* *surrender*: v. 92 has just *sworn* that *the questioning is God's own* (*la-nasʾalannahum*), and this *iʿrāḍ* is what *believes that oath*: the *dāʿī* can *release* every soul *because* he never held it: which is *why* the sūrah's *ṣafḥ* (v. 85: *the beautiful turning*) and this *iʿrāḍ* are *two commands of one hand*: *v. 85* rules out *resentment* in the turning; *v. 94* rules out *diminution* in the *telling*: *the hand is open toward them (no collecting), the voice is full toward them (no holding back)* — and the *tafsīr*'s old *naskh*-question ("is not *aʿriḍ* abrogated by the *sword*?") is answered in this sūrah's terms by the commentators who keep *both* verbs *standing*: the *sword* (whenever it comes) *ends a treaty, not a character*: this is the *mīzān* of the end-piece, vv. 85-94: the prophet’s policy in every stage — *full speech, fair turn, and the morning left to its Owner* (v. 66’s law, now v. 94’s permission).

**"Lot Went Where He Was Commanded": the Sūrah's Two Passive Verbs**

And the reader of this sūrah meets, at v. 94, *its* own oldest *tuʾmar* — for Lot, at the night's end, was told: "*go on where you are commanded* (*wa-mḍū ḥaythu tuʾmarūn*, v. 65)": the sūrah's *two passive-command verses* are *its two ages*: the *night* of the *saved* — *travel to the command without a map* (v. 65's law of *sarā*, the *qiṭʿa*-of-night, the no-look-back: all of vv. 60-66) — and the *day* of the *sent* — *climb to the command without an audience* (v. 94): *Lot goes where he is not shown, the Prophet speaks what he is not asked*: and the *dhikr* welds them with *the same sharaf*: the *command* is enough for both, the *obedience* is the *map*: which is why v. 95's promise of *sufficiency* ("*We* suffice you against the mockers") follows *at once*: the *ṣadaʿ* is *not* the *warner's* *army*: *he climbs, he calls, he turns — and the One who commanded is the One who keeps*: the sūrah's last *battle-plan* is *thrice passive*: *commanded, sufficed, guarded* (v. 9: *innā naḥnu* — the *ʿalīm*'s own *ḥifẓ*): the *active* verbs, *all of them*, belong to *the Other*: and the *believer's* part is *the climb*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:95

> **Truly We shall suffice thee against those who mock,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Suffice Thee": the Sūrah's Two *Innā Naḥnu*, Book and Bearer**

*innā kafaynāka'l-mustaḥziʾīn* — "verily *We* — *We* suffice you against the mockers." Set the verse beside the sūrah's first great *inna*: "*Innā naḥnu* nazzalna'l-dhikra wa-*innā* la-hu la-ḥāfiẓūn" (v. 9), and the pair is the sūrah's architecture revealed: *two* asseverations in the same divine first-person plural — heaven's *guarding* (ḥifẓ) of the *Book*, and heaven's *sufficing* (kifāya) of the *man who carries it* — and the *dhikr*'s promise at v. 9 and its Prophet's at v. 95 are one *ʿināya* with two objects: the *mockers* who could not touch the *Reminder* (v. 9's *ḥāfiẓūn* answered v. 6's "*O you upon whom the Reminder is sent — you are mad!*") *cannot touch the Warner either*: the sūrah's bracket, which opened at v. 6 with the first insult of *nuzūl*, closes here with God's own answer to the *insulter* — the *dhikr* needs no defense, and its *bearer* needs no army: *kafaynāka*: *We suffice you*: the verb of *sufficing* — and the source’s own cross-reference points to 2:137c, the *ḥisb* grammar of the Qur’an: "*O Prophet, God is sufficient for you — and for whoever of the believers follows you*" (8:64): the very *kifāya* of v. 95 spoken once before in *ḥisb*-words, and spoken there with its sūra-fullest clause: *sufficiency for you and for your followers*: v. 95’s *kafaynāka* is that verse’s twin, and the sūra’s *muʾeinūn* of v. 88’s lowered wing are the ones *included in the sufficing*.

**Who the Mockers Were: al-Walīd's Band, and the Ditch at Badr**

The *tafsīr* names them, and the source's note transcribes the report at v. 95-96: the verse is "*an assurance to the Prophet that God will destroy a particular group of Makkan idolaters known as the 'mockers,' among whom was al-Walīd b. al-Mughīra* (JJ) — *the most bitterly opposed to his message, who insulted him, spat, and threw animal entrails upon him*" — and *sufficing* is glossed precisely (Kl): "He will *destroy them without need of any effort on the Prophet's part*," with the historical seal: "*it is reported that members of this group all died before Badr*" (Kl, Z). And the *Ṣaḥīḥ*'s own witness stands at this verse in the sūrah's *tafsīr*-chapter: from ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd: when Quraysh *stiffened in opposition* to the Prophet ﷺ, he *called down* upon their nobles one by one — and Ibn Masʿūd swore: "*by the One besides whom there is no god, I saw those whom God's Messenger ﷺ invoked against at Badr, dragged and cast into the ditch (al-qadīb) of Badr*" (al-Bukhārī and Muslim): the *kifāya* of v. 95 is, in the collections' own ordering, *this* verse's *tafsīr*: *God sufficed him with a ditch* — the mockers of the *thrown entrails* were themselves *thrown*, the *entrails-thrown-upon prostration* the source records as these mockers’ signature is returned, in the Ṣaḥīḥ’s own ordering, at its exact measure — the *sujūd* they filled with filth answered by the *qadīb* into which their names were poured — mockery thrown at the warner, thrown back at the law of v. 84: the earning that avails not, even when the earning is laughter.

**"Soon They Will Know" — the Verse That Answers *ḍāqa ṣadrak* Before It Is Asked**

And v. 96's half-clause (*fa-sa-yaʿlamūn*: "then *soon they will know*") performs, in one *faʾ*, the sūrah's entire patience-theodicy: the *soon* of God — the *mawʿid*-vocabulary of vv. 38 (Iblīs's *waqt maʿlūm*) and 66 (Lot's *morning*) — is the *only* answer the warner is given to the mocker, and the warner's *chest* is the *real* audience of the verse: for v. 97, its next line, begins "*qad naʿlamu annaka qad ḍāqa ṣadruka*..." — the *soon* of the mockers' knowledge exists because the *now* of the Prophet's *pain* exists: the verse is *medicine before the diagnosis is complete*: *God sufficing* (95) and *God knowing the chest* (97) bracket vv. 96's *they will know* — and the *dhikr*'s final law of *kifāya* is thus *not* the *ditch* but the *knowing*: at every Badr there are those who *see* the ditch, and at every hour there is the *ʿAlīm* who sees the *ḍābiq* chest, and v. 95's promise, in the handbooks' summary, is given to *both*: *the mocking will end; the wounded warner will be upheld*; and *whoever carries a Book heaven itself guards needs no guard of his own*: *ḥasbuka Llāh*.

---
## Sūrah al-Ḥijr 15:96

> **those who set up another god along with God; yet soon they will know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Name of the Enemy: From Deniers to Portioners to Mockers to Polytheists**

*alladhīna jaʿalū maʿa Llāhi ilāhan ākhara fa-sa-yaʿlamūn* — and the sūrah, at the last, *unmasks* its long cast: the mockers (v. 95) are not, in this verse, defined by the *laughter* but by the *setting-up*: the *mustahziʾūn* are *mushrikūn*. The ladder the reader has climbed now shows its rungs backward: the people denied the *mursalīn* (v. 80), turned from the *āyāt* (v. 81), portioned the *Qur'ān* (vv. 90-91), and *made their mockery a wall* (v. 95) — and at the bottom of the wall stands *another god*: which is the verse's *tafsīr*-jewel, in its very verb: *jaʿalū* — *they made* — is the identical verb of v. 91: "*they* **made** *the Qur'ān into portions* (*jaʿalū'l-Qur'āna biḍʿan*)" — and the two *jaʿala*-verses, back to back, are one sentence in halves: *the Book you chop in pieces is the God you are setting up beside Him*: whoever *portions* revelation has, by that very act, *made* an appetite its co-sovereign: the *shirk* of Sodom's mob (v. 70's self-legislated "we forbade you") and the *shirk* of Mecca's *muqtaṣimūn* are the same *making*, and the sūrah ends its enemy-list where it ended its first enemy's: the same “made-of” theology as Iblīs’s “*I am better than he*” (7:12; 38:76) and his refusal to bow to the made (v. 31: *abā an yakūna maʾaʾ as-sājidīn*) — the *partnership* of self with God: which is why the verse *omits* the mockery entirely: the *sukhriyya* is only *symptom*; the *disease* is *shirk*: the ʿamr that, set beside God, *is* a co-maker — and only *that* is *asked*: v. 93's "*concerning what they used to do*" arrives here at its object.

**"Yet Soon They Will Know": the Sūrah's Bookend, Spoken a Second Time**
